# Adult Attachment: Structure, Dynamics, and Couple Functioning

## Source and Edition Note

This file reconstructs Mario Mikulincer and Phillip R. Shaver's *Attachment in Adulthood: Structure, Dynamics, and Change*, published in 2007 by The Guilford Press. The Project copy is the original 2007 edition represented by the title page, copyright page, preface, contents, appendices, references, and index. It is the earlier Project edition contemplated by the Laboratory architecture, not a later revision. The book's purpose is explicitly integrative: the authors organize the large adult-attachment literature around a model of the attachment behavioral system in adulthood, bringing together theory, measurement, experimental work, correlational research, longitudinal evidence, interpersonal and couple studies, and clinical implications.

The source has five parts and sixteen chapters. Part I establishes the attachment behavioral system, normative processes, measurement, and individual differences. Part II covers mental representations, emotion regulation, behavioral self-regulation, and personal growth. Part III treats interpersonal regulation, couple functioning, caregiving, and sexuality. Part IV considers psychopathology, psychotherapy, and group/organizational applications. Part V is an epilogue on attachment security. Seven appendices reproduce major self-report and caregiving measures: the Adult Attachment Questionnaire (AAQ), Adult Attachment Scale (AAS), Attachment Style Questionnaire (ASQ), Relationship Style Questionnaire (RSQ), Experiences in Close Relationships Scale (ECR), ECR-Revised (ECR-R) items, and a caregiving questionnaire. [Primary source: Mikulincer & Shaver, 2007, Contents and Preface]

The PDF's extraction quality is good enough for detailed reconstruction. Running text is generally reliable. Typical artifacts are line-break hyphenation, occasional broken characters, and degraded table layout when read as plain text. Conceptually important visual material includes Figure 2.1, the authors' model of adult attachment-system activation and functioning; Figure 4.2, the two-dimensional anxiety-avoidance space; Figure 5.1, the prototype view of attachment development; Figure 7.1, an emotion-process flowchart; and multiple evidence-summary tables in Chapters 5-10 and 13. This file reconstructs those figures descriptively rather than reproducing them.

This is a reconstruction of the 2007 evidence base. It does not silently import post-2007 findings, later editions, current popular attachment discourse, or Susan Johnson's later clinical formulations into Mikulincer and Shaver's account. Where later frameworks are compared, they are labelled as later or external to the primary source.

## Evidence and Measurement Note

Adult attachment in this book is simultaneously a theoretical model of a behavioral system and a research program based on multiple methods. Claims therefore need to be routed by evidence type.

**Attachment theory** refers to propositions derived from Bowlby, Ainsworth, and later adult attachment theorizing: evolved behavioral systems, proximity seeking, safe-haven and secure-base functions, internal working models, and alternative regulatory strategies. **Empirical findings** are observations from studies reviewed by Mikulincer and Shaver. Within those findings, **correlational evidence** establishes association but not direction of causation; **experimental evidence** can support causal claims about manipulated states such as threat or security priming within the limits of the manipulation; **longitudinal evidence** helps establish temporal ordering but does not automatically eliminate confounding; **observational dyadic evidence** measures actual couple behavior more directly than self-report but remains context-specific. **Clinical extrapolation** appears most clearly in Chapter 14. **Laboratory synthesis** in this file is an interpretive navigation aid and is explicitly labelled.

The measurement landscape is not unitary. Romantic-attachment self-report instruments and interview-based traditions such as the Adult Attachment Interview (AAI) are related but not interchangeable. Mikulincer and Shaver devote an entire chapter to this problem. Their own research program relies heavily on self-report measures of attachment anxiety and avoidance, especially the Experiences in Close Relationships family of measures. They also review narrative/interview methods, behavioral observation, implicit and experimental measures, and relationship-specific instruments. [Primary source: ch. 4]

A central methodological conclusion is that adult self-report attachment is better represented dimensionally than as a small set of natural categories. The two broad dimensions are **attachment anxiety**, reflecting hyperactivating tendencies and worries about rejection/availability, and **attachment avoidance**, reflecting deactivating tendencies and discomfort with closeness/dependence. Categorical labels can be constructed from combinations of those dimensions, but categorization discards information, obscures within-category variation, and can encourage reification. [Primary source: ch. 4]

## Coverage of This File

The reconstruction prioritizes material most relevant to adult attachment and intimate relationships: activation of the attachment system; proximity seeking; safe haven and secure base; security-based strategies; hyperactivation and deactivation; working models; dimensional measurement; developmental continuity and change; emotion regulation; support seeking; caregiving; interpersonal perception; intimacy; conflict; relationship satisfaction and stability; sexuality; dyadic partner effects; and psychotherapy implications where the authors themselves support them.

Psychopathology, group processes, and organizational applications are reconstructed chapter by chapter but more compactly because they are secondary to the Laboratory's couple-functioning purpose. Cross-framework comparison is confined to clearly separated later sections. The adult-attachment reconstruction itself remains in Mikulincer and Shaver's theoretical vocabulary.

## How to Use This File

Use this file to formulate hypotheses, not identities. A behavior such as reassurance seeking, withdrawal, emotional intensity, silence, self-reliance, delayed responding, conflict escalation, or sexual distancing is not by itself an attachment classification. Attachment constructs are probabilistic, contextual, and usually measured at group level. Any episode analysis should first establish the observable event, actual partner behavior, context, and perceived threat before considering attachment-related processes.

The key scientific question is not, “What attachment type is this person?” It is, “Is an attachment-relevant threat plausibly active here, and is there evidence of security-based regulation, hyperactivation, or deactivation?” The answer can differ across relationships and across time.

# Part I - Foundations of the Adult Attachment System

## 1. Intellectual Lineage

Mikulincer and Shaver present adult attachment research as an extension of Bowlby's evolutionary and control-system account and Ainsworth's empirical study of attachment organization. Bowlby proposed that natural selection favored an attachment behavioral system because proximity to protective others increased survival. Ainsworth operationalized central aspects of infant attachment, especially individual differences in the use of a caregiver as a safe haven and secure base. Adult attachment researchers later asked whether romantic partners can acquire analogous attachment functions and whether individual differences in adult relationships can be organized around attachment-related expectations and regulatory strategies. [Primary source: chs. 1, 3-5]

The adult theory is not simply “childhood attachment carried forward.” The book repeatedly treats adult functioning as the product of early experience, later relationships, current context, multiple working models, and ongoing revision. Adult romantic bonds also combine behavioral systems that are more clearly separable in infancy, especially attachment, caregiving, and sexuality. [Primary source: chs. 5, 10-12]

## 2. What Is an Attachment Behavioral System?

An attachment behavioral system is an evolved regulatory system with a biological function: protection from danger through maintaining or restoring proximity to one or more caring attachment figures. It is a system rather than a single behavior. Crying, calling, approaching, contacting, mentally representing a loved person, or accepting support can all serve the same regulatory goal depending on age and context. The system monitors threat and the accessibility of attachment figures, organizes proximity-seeking behavior, and normally becomes less active when adequate security is restored. [Primary source: ch. 1]

The adult system is therefore goal-corrected and flexible. Physical proximity can matter, but adults also use symbolic and representational forms of proximity. A remembered or imagined attachment figure, a telephone call, or confidence in an available partner can serve regulatory functions that an infant cannot yet perform independently. Adults also have stronger nonattachment coping resources, so activation thresholds and behavioral expression differ from infancy.

Attachment system is not attachment style. The system is the proposed species-typical regulatory architecture. Attachment orientations describe patterned individual differences in how that system tends to operate.

## 3. Safe Haven

A **safe haven** is the attachment figure's protective and comforting function under conditions of threat, distress, fatigue, illness, or vulnerability. The attachment system is activated, proximity or support is sought, and the person's distress can be reduced if the figure is perceived as available and responsive. [Primary source: chs. 1-3]

Safe haven does not mean “all emotional support in all circumstances.” Support between partners can involve instrumental help, companionship, validation, caregiving, sexuality, or ordinary cooperation. The safe-haven construct is specifically tied to the regulation of attachment-relevant threat and distress.

## 4. Secure Base

A **secure base** is the attachment figure's role in enabling exploration, competence, goal pursuit, and engagement with the wider environment. Security does not eliminate dependence. Rather, confidence that support is available when needed can free attentional and behavioral resources for autonomous activity. This is one reason Bowlby's model rejects the equation of mature attachment with pathological dependency. [Primary source: chs. 1, 2, 8, 14]

Secure base and safe haven are related but not identical. Safe haven emphasizes refuge under threat. Secure base emphasizes the support of exploration when threat is sufficiently regulated.

## 5. Proximity Seeking

**Proximity seeking** is the attachment system's primary strategy. When threat is appraised and the system becomes activated, the person seeks actual or symbolic proximity to a preferred attachment figure in ways appropriate to age and context. In adults this can include approaching, calling, disclosing distress, asking for reassurance, accepting help, or mentally activating a representation of a supportive other. [Primary source: chs. 1-3]

Proximity seeking is not inherently pathological dependency. Its adaptive value depends on context, proportionality, the availability of the attachment figure, and whether it ultimately restores security and frees the person to resume nonattachment goals.

## 6. Attachment-System Activation

The system is activated when a person appraises actual or potential threat to safety, well-being, or attachment availability. Bowlby's original domain includes danger, illness, fatigue, separation, and loss. In adult research the triggering field also includes rejection, abandonment cues, relationship threat, vulnerability, and symbolic reminders of mortality or separation. [Primary source: chs. 1-3]

Mikulincer and Shaver's model emphasizes a two-stage process. A threat appraisal can produce relatively automatic or preconscious activation of attachment-related goals and representations. Further processing then evaluates attachment-figure availability and the viability of proximity seeking. Chronic working models bias both stages. Hyperactivating strategies can lower the threshold for detecting threat and sustain activation; deactivating strategies can inhibit or exclude attachment-related information. [Primary source: ch. 2]

Activation is a theoretical and research construct. It is not synonymous with “having a strong feeling.” Anger, fear, shame, sadness, jealousy, sexual desire, or interpersonal frustration can occur without attachment-system activation. Evidence for an attachment formulation is stronger when the episode concerns safety, separation, rejection, availability, protection, or access to a preferred attachment figure and when proximity regulation is implicated.

## 7. Security-Based Strategy

When an attachment figure is appraised as available and responsive, the primary proximity strategy can succeed. Security is restored, distress diminishes, and attention becomes available again for exploration, relationships, work, play, and other goals. Mikulincer and Shaver describe this as a **broaden-and-build cycle of attachment security**: repeated security experiences reinforce positive expectations, constructive affect regulation, confidence in support, and exploration. [Primary source: ch. 2]

A useful heuristic is:

`threat -> attachment-system activation -> proximity/support -> perceived availability/responsiveness -> reduced distress -> renewed exploration`

This is not a rigid universal sequence. Adults may self-regulate effectively, use multiple attachment figures, combine attachment with other coping systems, or experience mixed appraisals.

## 8. Secondary Attachment Strategies

If an attachment figure is repeatedly unavailable, inconsistent, rejecting, or unsafe, proximity seeking may fail to restore security. Mikulincer and Shaver organize the major alternatives as **secondary attachment strategies**. If proximity seeking is still judged viable, the system may be **hyperactivated**. If proximity seeking is judged ineffective, dangerous, or incompatible with the person's learned strategy, the system may be **deactivated**. [Primary source: ch. 2]

“Choice” here need not be conscious. The authors explicitly model these processes as capable of operating automatically through working models, attentional biases, and learned expectations.

# Part II - Anxiety, Avoidance, and Secondary Attachment Strategies

## 9. Attachment Anxiety

Attachment anxiety is the major dimension associated with worries about rejection, abandonment, partner availability, and one's ability to secure sufficient closeness and reassurance. High anxiety is linked theoretically to hyperactivating strategies. The person tends to keep attachment concerns active, monitor potential rejection closely, intensify signals of need, and have difficulty disengaging from attachment-related threat. [Primary source: chs. 2, 4, 7]

The dimension is continuous. It is not an anxiety disorder, and it does not imply that the person is globally anxious in every domain. Nor does it mean that every request for reassurance is insecure. The attachment interpretation becomes stronger when reassurance seeking is persistent, threat-driven, difficult to satisfy, and tied to doubts about availability or worthiness.

## 10. Hyperactivating Strategies

Hyperactivation attempts to compel or restore proximity when the attachment figure is not reliably available but continued proximity seeking still appears potentially effective. Typical mechanisms in the book include intensified monitoring of the attachment figure, vigilance to rejection cues, stronger accessibility of attachment concerns, rumination, persistence of distress, emotional amplification, repeated proximity attempts, and biased interpretation of ambiguous relational information. [Primary source: chs. 2, 6-7, 9-10]

Hyperactivation is not simply “being emotional.” The defining issue is the regulatory organization of cognition, affect, and behavior around continued access to attachment security. A person can be emotionally expressive without hyperactivation, and hyperactivation can occur through cognitive preoccupation even when outward behavior is restrained.

## 11. Hyperactivation and Attention

Experimental studies reviewed by Mikulincer and Shaver show that attachment-related threat can increase the cognitive accessibility of attachment figures and that attachment anxiety is associated with difficulty disengaging from attachment-related concerns. Hyperactivating strategies favor the detection, retention, and elaboration of information that signals rejection, separation, or insufficient support. [Primary source: chs. 2-3, 7]

The evidence is strongest for group-level attentional and memory tendencies under laboratory conditions. It does not license the inference that a particular anxious thought in a specific person is caused by attachment anxiety.

## 12. Hyperactivation and Emotion Regulation

Hyperactivation sustains distress rather than efficiently terminating it. The strategy can magnify threat appraisals, keep negative emotion highly accessible, encourage rumination, and make reassurance difficult to consolidate into a stable sense of security. In couple interactions this can manifest as repeated checking, escalating protest, intensified disclosure, or difficulty allowing a conflict to close. [Primary source: chs. 7, 9-10]

The mechanism is not “feeling more deeply.” It is difficulty terminating attachment-system activation when safety remains uncertain.

## 13. Attachment Anxiety and the Self-Model

Attachment anxiety is often associated with doubts about self-worth and the availability of others. The person may rely more strongly on signs of partner acceptance to stabilize self-evaluation. The source reviews links with lower self-esteem, negative self-representations, self-criticism, and vulnerability to rejection, while also stressing that attachment measures are not direct measures of working models. [Primary source: chs. 4, 6]

This is a probabilistic association. High attachment anxiety is not identical to low self-esteem, shame, dependency, or any psychiatric condition.

## 14. Attachment Anxiety in Couple Conflict

In couple research, anxiety is associated on average with more intense distress, heightened monitoring, negative interpretation, conflict escalation, and stronger demands for reassurance or closeness. Anxious individuals may find it difficult to disengage from unresolved conflict and may respond strongly to ambiguous partner behavior. [Primary source: chs. 9-10]

These are associations, not deterministic predictions. Partner behavior matters. A partner who is actually unreliable, contemptuous, unfaithful, or unavailable can generate justified concern. The existence of an anxious orientation does not make the perceived threat unreal.

## 15. Attachment Avoidance

Attachment avoidance is the major dimension associated with discomfort with dependence and closeness, mistrust of others' availability, reluctance to seek support, and a preference for self-reliance. It is theoretically linked to deactivating strategies. [Primary source: chs. 2, 4, 7]

Avoidance is not introversion. Introversion in Jung's typology concerns the direction of conscious psychic orientation toward the subject rather than the object. Attachment avoidance concerns strategies for regulating security, dependence, vulnerability, and proximity in attachment relationships. A person can be introverted and secure, extraverted and avoidant, or any other combination.

## 16. Deactivating Strategies

Deactivation reduces attachment-system activation by inhibiting proximity seeking and excluding or suppressing information that would intensify felt dependence. The strategy includes distancing, suppression of attachment needs, reduced support seeking, defensive self-reliance, attentional disengagement from attachment cues, and cognitive inhibition of vulnerable states. [Primary source: chs. 2, 7]

Deactivation does not mean absence of attachment needs or absence of distress. The source reviews evidence that avoidant participants can display lower reported distress while physiological or cognitive indicators reveal continuing activation under some conditions. Defensive control can also fail under high cognitive load or intense stress.

## 17. Deactivation and Defensive Exclusion

The Bowlbian idea of defensive exclusion is important to the deactivation account. Information that would activate attachment needs can be kept out of focal awareness because acknowledging it would conflict with a learned strategy of self-sufficiency or with expectations that others will not respond. [Primary source: chs. 2, 7]

This creates a measurement caution: low self-reported distress cannot automatically be taken as evidence of low underlying activation. Yet the opposite inference is also unsafe. One should not assume hidden distress every time a person reports calm. Convergent evidence from behavior, physiology, context, and repeated pattern is needed.

## 18. Avoidance and Emotion Regulation

Avoidant regulation prioritizes suppression, distancing, attentional disengagement, and control of dependency-related cognition. It can reduce overt distress and attachment behavior in the short run. Costs identified in the reviewed literature include reduced support seeking, less emotional disclosure, constrained intimacy, and less flexible processing when attachment needs cannot be successfully excluded. [Primary source: chs. 7, 9-10]

## 19. Secure Autonomy versus Defensive Self-Reliance

Secure autonomy is compatible with depending on others when appropriate. A secure person can seek support, receive it, and return to exploration. Defensive self-reliance is organized around minimizing the importance of attachment needs or other people's availability because proximity seeking is expected to be ineffective, unsafe, or constraining. [Primary source: chs. 1-2, 7-8]

The outward behavior can look similar. Two people may both prefer to solve a problem alone. The distinction lies in flexibility, context, and what happens when genuine vulnerability makes support useful.

## 20. Attachment Avoidance in Couple Conflict

Avoidance is associated on average with withdrawal, disengagement, reduced self-disclosure, lower support seeking, lower responsiveness to a distressed partner, and discomfort with vulnerable discussion. In demand-withdraw patterns, avoidant tendencies can contribute to distancing. [Primary source: chs. 9-11]

Withdrawal is not automatically attachment avoidance. Silence can arise from overload, shame, anger, strategic time-out, introverted processing, fear of escalation, power dynamics, learned communication habits, or unwillingness to engage.

## 21. Anxiety x Avoidance: The Two-Dimensional Structure

The source treats anxiety and avoidance as continuous dimensions. The familiar categorical labels are best understood as approximate regions of this space rather than fixed natural kinds. [Primary source: ch. 4]

| Anxiety | Avoidance | Common descriptive label in the literature | Regulatory emphasis |
|---|---|---|---|
| Low | Low | Secure | Availability trusted; flexible proximity and exploration |
| High | Low | Preoccupied / anxious | Hyperactivation; intensified proximity seeking |
| Low | High | Dismissing / avoidant | Deactivation; defensive self-reliance |
| High | High | Fearful / fearful-avoidant | Strong desire/fear conflict; possible mixture or alternation of hyperactivation and deactivation |

The fourth region should not be casually equated with infant disorganized attachment. Adult self-report categories and infant observational classifications are different measurement traditions.

## 22. Secure Orientation

A secure orientation corresponds approximately to low attachment anxiety and low attachment avoidance. It implies relatively confident expectations of availability, comfort with dependence and closeness, and flexible use of support. [Primary source: chs. 2, 4]

Secure does not mean conflict-free, permanently calm, independent, or always satisfied with a relationship. Secure people can be angry, jealous, hurt, or distressed. The difference lies in the typical organization and recovery of the attachment system, not the absence of negative affect.

## 23. Preoccupied / Anxious Pattern

The preoccupied region combines high anxiety with relatively low avoidance. Categorical language is a shorthand for a dimensional profile. It should not be used as a fixed identity or inferred from one episode. [Primary source: ch. 4]

## 24. Dismissing / Avoidant Pattern

The dismissing region combines relatively low anxiety with high avoidance, often with deactivating regulation and defensive self-reliance. Again, the label summarizes a region in a continuous space. [Primary source: ch. 4]

## 25. Fearful Pattern

The fearful region combines high anxiety and high avoidance. It can involve a conflicted motivational pattern: strong concerns about rejection and availability alongside strong inhibition of dependence and closeness. Mikulincer and Shaver's model allows mixtures or alternation of secondary strategies when neither provides stable security. [Primary source: chs. 2, 4]

## 26. Dimensional versus Categorical Models

The book's scientific preference is dimensional. Taxometric work reviewed in Chapter 4 favors dimensions over discrete self-report attachment types. Dimensions retain information, permit graded estimates, and map more directly onto hyperactivation and deactivation. Categories can be useful for description, teaching, or some analyses, but they impose cut-points, conceal within-category differences, and invite identity language. [Primary source: ch. 4]

# Part III - Internal Working Models and Individual Differences

## 27. Internal Working Models: Core Definition

Internal working models are cognitive-affective representations and expectations built through attachment experience. They concern the accessibility and responsiveness of attachment figures, the self's ability or worthiness to elicit care, and procedures for regulating proximity and security. They influence attention, memory, appraisal, affect, and action. [Primary source: chs. 1-2, 5-6]

They are not merely explicit beliefs. They can operate automatically and can be activated differently by context. Nor are they directly measured by every attachment self-report scale. Chapter 4 explicitly warns that labeling an attachment-style questionnaire a “working model” measure overstates what such instruments establish.

## 28. Model of Self

Repeated experiences of being protected, accepted, and responded to can support representations of the self as lovable, competent, and capable of mobilizing support. Experiences of inconsistency or rejection can contribute to doubts about worth or efficacy. [Primary source: ch. 6]

The book does not reduce adult attachment to a simple positive-self/negative-self dichotomy. Self-representations are multiple, context-sensitive, and dynamically activated.

## 29. Model of Other

Working models of others concern expected availability, responsiveness, trustworthiness, sensitivity, and willingness to provide support. These expectations guide whom people approach, how they interpret ambiguous behavior, and how quickly they seek or inhibit proximity. [Primary source: ch. 6]

## 30. Working Models as Dynamic Structures

Working models persist partly because they guide attention and behavior in self-confirming ways. People may select partners or interpret events in ways that fit existing expectations, and their own anxious or avoidant behavior may evoke partner responses that reinforce those expectations. But Mikulincer and Shaver also emphasize reality testing and update. Working models must remain sufficiently open to social reality to adapt to changed relationships and life circumstances. [Primary source: ch. 5]

## 31. Hierarchical Organization

The book reviews evidence that attachment representations can be organized at multiple levels: broad generalized expectations, relationship-domain models, and relationship-specific models. A person may therefore show one general tendency while being more secure with one partner and less secure with another. [Primary source: chs. 4-5]

The practical consequence is decisive: attachment orientation is not one immutable global style.

## 32. Relationship-Specific Attachment

Relationship-specific measures can capture variation that global measures miss. A person's attachment expectations toward a parent, friend, former partner, or current romantic partner may differ. Current relationship experience can also modify the accessibility of particular models. [Primary source: chs. 4-5]

This is one reason it is poor practice to infer a person's global attachment identity from behavior with one partner in one context.

## 33. Stability and Variability

Adult attachment orientations show meaningful stability, but not perfect stability. Stability is higher when the relational environment is stable; significant changes in relationships, life circumstances, or perceived support can shift attachment expectations. Mikulincer and Shaver favor a prototype model in which early working models retain influence while current models are updated by later experience. [Primary source: ch. 5]

## 34. Internal Working Model versus Jungian Complex

This distinction is developed in Part IX. At this point the primary-source conclusion is simple: an attachment working model is a construct within attachment theory concerning relational expectations and regulation. It is not a Jungian complex and should not be renamed as one.

## 35. Internal Working Model versus Projection

A working model can bias expectation and interpretation without meeting the Jungian concept of projection. An inaccurate expectation can arise from learning, generalization, current anxiety, selective attention, or actual partner behavior. Calling all such errors “projection” collapses distinct theories.

## 36. Internal Working Model versus Archetype

Attachment working models are experience-shaped representational structures. Even though attachment theory uses evolutionary assumptions about the behavioral system, this does not make working models equivalent to Jungian archetypes. Knox later attempts a developmental bridge between Jungian and attachment-informed thinking, but that is Knox's synthesis, not Mikulincer and Shaver's construct.

# Part IV - Development, Stability, and Change

## 37. Developmental Foundations

The developmental account begins with repeated interactions in which caregivers are more or less available, sensitive, and responsive. Such experiences shape the person's confidence in proximity seeking and contribute to working models of self and others. Parental attachment, family stress, temperament, context, and later experiences also matter. [Primary source: ch. 5]

The book explicitly rejects a simple linear determinism. Early experience matters, but it is neither a complete nor an irreversible cause of adult romantic attachment.

## 38. Childhood-to-Adulthood Continuity

The reviewed evidence for continuity from infancy to adult attachment is mixed. Some longitudinal studies support meaningful continuity, especially under stable environmental conditions, while others show substantial change. The prototype model predicts moderate rather than perfect continuity: early representations remain influential, but discrepant later experiences can revise current models. [Primary source: ch. 5]

## 39. Developmental Determinism Guardrail

Adult attachment orientations are influenced by developmental history but are not perfectly determined by infancy or childhood. Inferring a specific childhood history from a current adult behavior is therefore not warranted. The same adult pattern can be reached through different developmental pathways, and later relationships can strengthen or weaken security.

## 40. Parents versus Romantic Partners

Child-caregiver and adult romantic bonds share attachment functions but differ structurally. Infants are strongly asymmetrical care recipients; adult romantic partners often alternate attachment and caregiving roles and integrate sexuality, mutuality, shared goals, and adult self-regulation. [Primary source: chs. 1, 10-12]

## 41. Pair Bonds and Adult Attachment

Romantic partners can become preferred attachment figures by serving proximity, safe-haven, and secure-base functions. The process is gradual: early attraction and dating are not automatically full attachment bonds. Couple formation also recruits caregiving and sexual systems. [Primary source: chs. 3, 10-12]

## 42. Can Adult Attachment Change?

Yes, but change is constrained. The book's developmental model balances **homeothetic** forces that preserve existing organization with **destabilizing** experiences that demand revision. New relationships, major life events, repeated partner responsiveness, and psychotherapy can alter working models. [Primary source: chs. 5, 14]

The best summary is neither “attachment is fixed” nor “attachment is infinitely malleable.” It is relatively stable, context-sensitive, and open to revision.

## 43. Relationship-Induced Change

Responsive relationship experiences can increase security when they are sufficiently repeated and credible to revise expectations. Conversely, loss, betrayal, persistent rejection, or deteriorating relationship quality can reduce security. Directionality can therefore run both ways: attachment orientations shape relationships, and relationships shape attachment orientations. [Primary source: chs. 5, 10]

## 44. Earned Security

The term **earned security** belongs particularly to interview-based developmental traditions in which adults can show coherent, autonomous states of mind despite adverse histories. Mikulincer and Shaver discuss interview and self-report traditions separately. The term should not be used loosely as a generic label for anyone who reports becoming “more secure” in romantic relationships. [Primary source: ch. 4]

## 45. Security Enhancement

The primary source discusses experimentally induced security and therapeutic security provision. Security priming studies show that temporarily activating representations of available, supportive others can influence affect, cognition, and social behavior. Chapter 14 also describes the therapist as providing safe-haven and secure-base functions that may support revision of working models. [Primary source: chs. 2-3, 7-9, 14]

State manipulations do not prove permanent trait change. Experimental security priming establishes that attachment-related representations can be activated and can have short-term causal effects under laboratory conditions.

## 46. Stability versus Plasticity

The evidence is best represented as **structured plasticity**. Existing models guide perception and behavior, producing continuity. Yet models are probabilistic, layered, and revisable. Stability is an empirical tendency, not an identity claim; change requires experiences strong and repeated enough to challenge existing expectations.

# Part V - Emotion Regulation, Support, and Caregiving

## 47. Attachment as an Affect-Regulation System

Attachment is not reducible to emotion regulation, but regulation of distress is one of its central functions. When threat is detected, the attachment system mobilizes access to an attachment figure or to internalized representations of security. If the strategy works, distress becomes more manageable and the person can return to other goals. [Primary source: ch. 7]

Security is associated with flexible regulation rather than suppression of negative emotion. Secure individuals are generally more able to acknowledge distress, appraise it realistically, seek support when useful, use problem-focused coping where possible, and disengage from unproductive distress once the threat is contained. Hyperactivation and deactivation are alternative regulatory organizations when security cannot be confidently restored.

## 48. Security Priming

Mikulincer and Shaver review a substantial experimental program in which security-related representations are activated by words, names, imagery, or reminders of supportive relationships. Compared with neutral or threat conditions, security priming can reduce defensive reactions, increase positive affect or compassion, and alter responses to stress or social information. [Primary source: chs. 2-3, 7-9]

The scientific significance is that attachment security is not only a questionnaire score. Security-related representations can be experimentally activated and can causally influence short-term cognition and behavior. The limitation is equally important: a laboratory prime is a temporary manipulation, not evidence that a person's chronic attachment orientation has been permanently changed.

## 49. Affect Regulation under Security

Security supports what the authors portray as constructive regulation: tolerating negative affect without being overwhelmed by it, seeking support without losing autonomy, using reappraisal and problem solving, and maintaining exploration under manageable stress. [Primary source: ch. 7]

A secure person can experience intense sadness, fear, anger, or jealousy. Security is not emotional flatness. It is better understood as confidence that difficult states can be managed without either endlessly amplifying attachment threat or excluding the need for others.

## 50. Hyperactivation versus Deactivation

| Dimension | Hyperactivation | Deactivation |
|---|---|---|
| Basic problem | Attachment figure not reliably available, but proximity still seems potentially effective | Proximity seeking expected to fail, be rejected, or create unacceptable vulnerability |
| Threat appraisal | Heightened vigilance; lower threshold for attachment-relevant threat | Attenuation or exclusion of attachment-relevant threat where possible |
| Attention | Monitoring, difficulty disengaging | Attentional distancing or disengagement |
| Emotion | Amplified and prolonged distress | Suppression, inhibition, or reduced conscious access |
| Proximity | Intensified or repeated seeking | Inhibited seeking; distance and self-reliance |
| Support seeking | Frequent, urgent, sometimes difficult to satisfy | Reduced or delayed even when useful |
| Cognition | Rumination; negative interpretations; accessibility of rejection concerns | Defensive exclusion; suppression of dependency-related thoughts |
| Interpersonal risk | Escalation, reassurance loops, intrusive monitoring | Withdrawal, low disclosure, reduced responsiveness |
| Attachment dimension | Primarily anxiety | Primarily avoidance |
| Common misreading | “Too emotional” | “Does not care” |

[Primary source: chs. 2, 7, 9-10]

## 51. Attachment versus General Emotion Regulation

Attachment theory does not explain every form of emotion regulation. People regulate emotion through attention, reappraisal, problem solving, behavioral control, social norms, temperament, learned coping, substances, distraction, sleep, exercise, and many other processes. Attachment theory is most relevant when regulation is organized around security, availability, proximity, rejection, separation, or dependence on attachment figures.

## 52. Support Seeking

Support seeking is one adult form of proximity regulation. Security is generally associated with willingness to seek help when needed and confidence that support can be used effectively. Anxiety can intensify support seeking while also making reassurance harder to consolidate. Avoidance is associated with reduced support seeking and a stronger preference for self-reliance. [Primary source: chs. 7, 9]

Support seeking is not equivalent to dependency and is not evidence of anxiety by itself. In the secure-base model, appropriate dependence is compatible with mature autonomy.

## 53. Support Provision

Adult attachment bonds are reciprocal. A partner can be an attachment figure when one is distressed and a caregiver when the other is distressed. The book therefore treats caregiving as a separate behavioral system rather than explaining all couple support through the care-seeker's attachment orientation. [Primary source: ch. 11]

## 54. The Caregiving Behavioral System

The caregiving system organizes detection of another person's need and actions intended to protect, soothe, or support that person and foster his or her exploration and growth. Its set-goal is the welfare of the other, not the caregiver's own proximity. Sensitive caregiving requires accurate appraisal of need, responsiveness, and the ability to provide the right amount and kind of help. [Primary source: ch. 11]

Attachment and caregiving are functionally linked but distinct. Attachment behavior asks, in effect, “Can I obtain protection and security?” Caregiving behavior asks, “Can I detect and respond to another's need?” A person can be relatively secure as a care-seeker yet still vary in caregiving skill.

## 55. Attachment and Partner Responsiveness

The evidence distinguishes **actual responsiveness** from **perceived responsiveness**. Working models can bias perception, but partners also really differ in sensitivity, reliability, and support. A complete analysis therefore needs both levels: what the partner did and how the behavior was interpreted. [Primary source: chs. 6, 9-11]

Anxiety can increase monitoring and make ambiguous support feel insufficient. Avoidance can reduce willingness to receive or provide support. Neither tendency means the partner's actual behavior is irrelevant.

## 56. Support Seeking versus Dependency

Normal adult interdependence includes asking for help, relying on a partner under genuine threat, and allowing another person to matter. Insecure dependency is better conceptualized as a rigid or dysregulated strategy, not the mere existence of need. A secure person can be strongly bonded and dependent in specific circumstances while remaining capable of exploration and self-directed action.

# Part VI - Intimacy, Love, Sexuality, and Couple Functioning

## 57. Intimacy

Intimacy involves self-disclosure, responsiveness, closeness, trust, and the experience of being known without excessive threat. Secure attachment generally supports disclosure and receptivity because closeness is less likely to be interpreted as dangerous or precarious. Anxiety can intensify disclosure and the search for reassurance; avoidance can inhibit disclosure or responsiveness. [Primary source: chs. 9-10]

Intimacy is not fusion. Mature adult attachment allows closeness alongside exploration and differentiated goals.

## 58. Trust

Attachment-related trust concerns expectations that significant others will be available and responsive when needed. Secure working models make such expectations easier to maintain, while anxiety and avoidance can bias interpretation toward unreliability for different reasons. [Primary source: ch. 6]

Not all trust problems are attachment insecurity. Deception, broken agreements, coercion, neglect, and incompatibility are first-order realities. Attachment theory should not be used to psychologize justified mistrust.

## 59. Romantic Love as a Multi-System Organization

Mikulincer and Shaver follow the adult attachment tradition in treating romantic love as involving at least three behavioral systems: attachment, caregiving, and sexuality. These systems interact but are not interchangeable. One partner can be the target of attachment, the recipient or provider of caregiving, and a sexual partner, with different motives and regulatory processes active at different moments. [Primary source: chs. 10-12]

This multi-system model matters because it prevents a common reduction: not every sexual problem is an attachment problem, not every caregiving failure reflects the care-seeker's insecurity, and not every bond problem is about sexual attraction.

## 60. Dependency and Interdependence

The source's secure-base logic treats adult interdependence as compatible with autonomy. Confidence in another person's availability can reduce the need for defensive self-sufficiency and enable exploration. Attachment security therefore does not mean independence from others. [Primary source: chs. 1-2, 8]

## 61. Autonomy and Exploration

Chapter 8 extends attachment theory beyond distress regulation. Security is associated with exploration, openness to information, persistence, goal pursuit, and personal growth. When defensive resources are not consumed by attachment threat, the person has more psychological capacity for learning and self-directed action. [Primary source: ch. 8]

The causal claim must remain bounded. Attachment security is one contributor to exploratory functioning, not a complete theory of autonomy, achievement, personality development, or individuation.

## 62. Attachment and Sexuality

Chapter 12 treats sexuality as a distinct behavioral system whose proximal goals can include sexual arousal and pleasure and whose distal evolutionary function concerns reproduction. Sexual behavior can occur without attachment, and attachment can exist without sexual activity. In adult romantic life the systems frequently interact. [Primary source: ch. 12]

Attachment orientation is associated with sexual motives, comfort with intimacy, communication, and the relationship between sexual experience and relationship quality. The evidence is heterogeneous and often correlational. Attachment does not determine sexual orientation, sexual functioning, or the meaning of a sexual act.

## 63. Sexuality and Attachment Security

Security is generally associated with greater integration of sex, affection, mutuality, communication, and relationship intimacy. Secure individuals are more likely to approach sexual activity without needing either to use it as a primary reassurance strategy or to detach it defensively from emotional closeness. [Primary source: ch. 12]

## 64. Attachment Anxiety and Sexuality

Anxiety is associated in the reviewed literature with motives tied to reassurance, closeness, partner approval, fear of rejection, and relationship maintenance. Sex can acquire attachment-regulatory significance when it is used to confirm that the partner is still available or committed. [Primary source: ch. 12]

This is not a stereotype about all highly anxious individuals, and sexual desire itself is not evidence of attachment anxiety.

## 65. Attachment Avoidance and Sexuality

Avoidance is associated on average with greater separation of sex from emotional intimacy, discomfort with dependence, and in some studies more permissive or uncommitted sexual motives. It can also be associated with reduced sexual communication or less use of sex as an expression of closeness. [Primary source: ch. 12]

Again, these are group-level associations and are not sufficient to infer a person's attachment orientation from sexual choices.

## 66. Sexual Experience and Relationship Quality

The chapter reviews diary and couple research showing reciprocal links between sexual experience and relationship quality. One 42-day diary study found that positive sexual experiences predicted more positive relationship-related feelings or behavior the following day, with associations moderated by attachment orientations. The broader evidence base remains largely observational, so reciprocal causation is plausible. [Primary source: ch. 12]

# Part VII - Conflict, Dyadic Regulation, and Relationship Outcomes

## 67. Attachment and Conflict

Conflict is attachment-relevant when it threatens availability, acceptance, safety, or the integrity of the bond. Security generally supports more constructive engagement, better problem solving, and greater confidence that disagreement need not imply abandonment. Anxiety can intensify threat perception and protest. Avoidance can promote disengagement or suppression of vulnerability. [Primary source: chs. 7, 9-10]

The same observable conflict can also be driven by values, money, sexuality, household labor, betrayal, family obligations, personality, stress, power, coercion, or communication skill. Attachment is a layer of analysis, not a universal master explanation.

## 68. Pursuit and Withdrawal

Adult attachment research includes patterns that resemble pursuit and withdrawal. Anxious hyperactivation can look like pursuit, demands for reassurance, or difficulty ending a conflict. Avoidant deactivation can look like withdrawal, silence, or reduced disclosure. [Primary source: ch. 10]

But **pursuit is not attachment anxiety** and **withdrawal is not attachment avoidance**. Pursuit and withdrawal are observable interactional positions. Anxiety and avoidance are attachment-regulatory dimensions inferred from broader patterns and usually measured with validated methods.

## 69. Anger

Mikulincer and Shaver distinguish more secure, relationship-preserving anger from insecure forms. Security can support anger that identifies a problem while preserving hope of repair. Hyperactivation can intensify anger and keep it active when reassurance fails. Deactivation can suppress anger or turn it into distancing, resentment, or low-engagement hostility. [Primary source: ch. 7]

Anger is not itself proof that the attachment system is active.

## 70. Conflict Behavior

Attachment orientations predict tendencies, not scripts. Observational studies of conflict show links between insecurity and less constructive communication, but actual behavior reflects both partners, the topic, timing, stress, communication habits, and relationship history. [Primary source: chs. 9-10]

A useful analytic order is: observable behavior first; context and legitimate grievance second; dyadic sequence third; attachment hypotheses only after those facts are clear.

## 71. Relationship Satisfaction

Across many self-report studies, attachment anxiety and avoidance are generally associated with lower dating and marital satisfaction. The relationship is not perfectly consistent across methods. Studies using the Adult Attachment Interview have produced a less uniform pattern than relationship-focused self-report measures. [Primary source: ch. 10]

This is partly a measurement point: a current romantic attachment measure is closer to current relationship satisfaction than an interview assessing state of mind with respect to childhood attachment. Shared method and construct proximity can therefore inflate associations.

Insecurity does not guarantee dissatisfaction. The reviewed effects are generally moderate, and many other determinants of relationship quality remain important.

## 72. Relationship Stability

The book reviews links between insecurity and breakup, commitment, instability, and relationship persistence. Avoidance can undermine commitment and closeness; anxiety can produce instability through intense concerns and conflict. Yet persistence is not synonymous with health, and insecurity does not mechanically predict dissolution. [Primary source: ch. 10]

Relationship stability is affected by alternatives, investment, children, finances, social norms, values, abuse, compatibility, and many factors outside attachment theory.

## 73. Partner Effects

Attachment is dyadic. One person's orientation can affect the partner's experience of support, intimacy, conflict, and satisfaction. Chapter 10 reviews many partner-effect studies in which partners of more insecure individuals tend, on average, to report lower satisfaction. Most studies are cross-sectional, though prospective studies provide some temporal evidence. [Primary source: ch. 10]

The direction is not one-way. A chronically unresponsive partner can also increase insecurity. Couple processes are recursive.

## 74. Actor and Partner Processes

Dyadic research distinguishes **actor effects**, where a person's own attachment orientation predicts his or her own outcome, from **partner effects**, where one person's orientation predicts the partner's outcome. This logic is often implemented through interdependence-aware statistical models even when the book does not make the modeling technique itself the conceptual focus. [Primary source: ch. 10]

The important point is that two observations from a couple are not independent. Relationship processes belong partly to the dyad, not only to isolated individuals.

## 75. Dyadic Regulation

Partners can co-regulate attachment distress. A responsive partner can reduce threat, facilitate disclosure, and help restore exploratory functioning. Conversely, one partner's hyperactivation can trigger the other's deactivation, or deactivation can intensify the other's pursuit. [Primary source: ch. 10]

Co-regulation is not fusion. Security ideally allows mutual dependence without erasing personal agency or separateness.

## 76. Couple-Type and Interaction Effects

Chapter 10 reviews pairings of attachment orientations. Anxious-avoidant pairings can be difficult because one person's intensified proximity seeking can meet the other's distancing. Anxious-anxious pairings can amplify distress. Some studies suggest a secure partner can buffer insecurity, but findings are not uniform. [Primary source: ch. 10]

The evidence does not justify ranking partner combinations as inherently compatible or incompatible. Couple-level effects are heterogeneous and context-dependent.

## 77. Jealousy

Jealousy appears in the adult attachment literature as one possible response to perceived partner loss, rivalry, or rejection. Anxiety can heighten monitoring and concern about abandonment. Sexual jealousy also intersects with the sexual behavioral system. [Primary source: chs. 10, 12]

Jealousy is not equivalent to anxious attachment. It can be proportionate to actual infidelity risk, shaped by values or prior betrayal, or arise through other psychological processes.

## 78. Separation, Loss, and Grief

Separation and loss are canonical attachment-system activators. Bowlby's theory treats protest, searching, distress, and eventual reorganization as central responses to loss. Adult research reviewed in Chapter 3 examines how attachment orientation influences mourning and whether people can reorganize attachment after loss. [Primary source: ch. 3]

Hyperactivation can prolong searching and preoccupation; deactivation can suppress attachment-related grief; security can support acknowledgment and eventual reorganization. These are broad tendencies rather than rules for individual grief.

## 79. Breakup

Romantic breakup activates attachment processes when the former partner served attachment functions. Attachment anxiety is associated with more persistent distress and preoccupation in many studies, while avoidance can be associated with distancing or suppression. [Primary source: chs. 3, 10]

Individual predictions remain weak without context. Relationship meaning, initiator status, betrayal, social support, and concurrent stress all matter.

# Part VIII - Methods, Evidence, and Causal Limits

## 80. Self-Report Research

Self-report measures provide efficient, psychometrically useful estimates of attachment anxiety and avoidance. They are well suited to dimensional analysis, relationship-specific assessment, and large samples. Their limitations include conscious-report bias, shared-method variance, response style, social desirability, and the fact that a questionnaire score is not a direct observation of unconscious processes. [Primary source: ch. 4]

## 81. Interview Traditions

The Adult Attachment Interview and related narrative measures assess the organization and coherence of discourse about attachment history rather than simply asking for romantic anxiety and avoidance ratings. Mikulincer and Shaver review evidence that interview and self-report traditions overlap imperfectly. [Primary source: ch. 4]

They should not be treated as interchangeable tests of the same latent thing. Differences in target, method, scoring, and representational level matter.

## 82. Experimental Methods

Experimental attachment research manipulates threat, separation-related cues, or security representations and measures outcomes such as reaction time, memory accessibility, affect, defensive behavior, support seeking, or social judgment. These studies are valuable because they can test process and short-term causation. [Primary source: chs. 2-3, 7-9]

Their limitation is ecological scope. A laboratory prime or lexical-decision effect does not by itself establish a person's chronic relational pattern.

## 83. Observational Couple Studies

Conflict discussions, support tasks, and coded interactions provide behavior that is less dependent on self-report. Observational studies reviewed in Chapters 10 and 11 link attachment orientations with communication, conflict, support provision, and caregiving. [Primary source: chs. 10-11]

Observation remains situation-bound. A 10-minute laboratory conflict is not the whole relationship.

## 84. Longitudinal Evidence

Longitudinal studies contribute to questions of stability, change, and temporal direction. Chapter 5 uses them to examine developmental continuity; Chapter 10 uses prospective studies to assess whether insecurity predicts later partner or relationship outcomes. [Primary source: chs. 5, 10]

Temporal precedence strengthens causal inference but does not eliminate third variables, measurement error, or reciprocal influence.

## 85. Clinical versus Community Samples

The source integrates studies from student, community, married, clinical, and other populations. Findings should not be transferred mechanically across samples. Attachment distributions, stress levels, relationship duration, psychopathology, and demographic context can differ substantially.

## 86. Effect Size and Practical Significance

Mikulincer and Shaver often summarize broad directions of association across many studies. The existence of a statistically reliable association does not mean attachment explains most of the variance in a relationship outcome. Many effects are moderate and some are inconsistent across measures, samples, or genders. [Primary source: especially chs. 9-10]

This file therefore avoids invented effect sizes and emphasizes direction, design, and limitation.

## 87. Causal Inference Guardrail

“Attachment anxiety is associated with lower satisfaction” does not automatically mean “attachment anxiety causes low satisfaction.” Plausible causal paths include anxiety influencing perception and behavior, poor partner responsiveness increasing anxiety, relationship distress changing both attachment reports and satisfaction reports, and third factors affecting both. [Primary source: chs. 5, 10]

Causal confidence is higher when multiple designs converge: longitudinal temporal ordering, experimentally manipulated security or threat, observational behavior, and theoretically specific mediators. Even then, causation can remain reciprocal.

## 88. Developmental Evidence Guardrail

Adult attachment self-report does not allow one to reconstruct childhood with certainty. Associations between early caregiving and later orientation are meaningful but imperfect. Memory, current relationships, temperament, later adversity, and current context all influence adult functioning. [Primary source: chs. 4-5]

## 89. Culture

The book's foundational theory assumes a species-typical attachment system, but much adult romantic attachment research in the 2007 evidence base comes from Western and university/community samples. Cross-cultural work exists, but cultural norms about dependence, emotional expression, marriage, family obligation, and sexuality complicate simple universalization. [Primary source: chs. 3-5, with limitations across empirical chapters]

## 90. Gender

Mikulincer and Shaver review sex/gender differences but do not support a simple theory in which men are inherently avoidant and women inherently anxious. Mean differences are generally smaller and less theoretically central than the anxiety and avoidance dimensions themselves, and many effects vary by sample or context. [Primary source: chs. 4, 9-12]

## 91. Life Stage

Adult attachment can be studied in dating, committed partnership, marriage, parenthood, caregiving to aging parents, bereavement, and other stages. Attachment functions and preferred figures can shift across the life course. The general system remains, but relationships and role demands change. [Primary source: chs. 3, 5, 10-11]

# Part IX - Comparison with Jungian and Post-Jungian Frameworks

The following comparisons are deliberately bounded. They are not claims that adult attachment research can be translated into analytical psychology or vice versa. The frameworks ask different questions, use different methods, and define their constructs differently.

## 92. Attachment System versus the Jungian Psyche

Adult attachment theory focuses on an evolved system for regulating security through proximity, availability, and responsive care. Its empirical program examines individual differences in anxiety and avoidance, working models, attention, affect regulation, support, caregiving, and relationship functioning. Jungian theory is broader and differently constituted. It addresses ego-consciousness, complexes, archetypes, projection, psychological type, the collective unconscious, symbolic meaning, and individuation.

An attachment episode and a Jungian episode can coincide. For example, perceived rejection can activate attachment concerns and constellate an affectively charged complex. But the theories do not identify those events by the same criteria. [Comparative source: Jung, CW 8, “A Review of the Complex Theory”; Mikulincer & Shaver, 2007, chs. 1-2]

## 93. Internal Working Model versus Jungian Complex

| Dimension | Internal working model | Jungian complex |
|---|---|---|
| Theoretical tradition | Bowlby/Ainsworth and adult attachment research | Analytical psychology, rooted in association experiments and Jung's complex theory |
| Primary problem | Expectations and procedures concerning attachment availability, self-worth, and proximity regulation | Affectively charged psychic organization with relative autonomy from the ego |
| Development | Built and revised through attachment interactions and later relational experience | Arises around emotionally significant experience; may include personal and archetypal dimensions |
| Representation | Cognitive-affective models of self, other, and relationship | Cluster of images, memories, affects, ideas, bodily reactions, and behavioral tendencies |
| Affect | Organizes threat, security, and relational affect | Defined by affective charge; can seize or alter consciousness |
| Autonomy | Can operate automatically but is usually modeled as representational/regulatory structure | Jung explicitly emphasizes partial autonomy and “splinter psyche” qualities |
| Triggering | Attachment-relevant cues, threats, availability judgments | Associative cues linked to the complex's affective nucleus |
| Relationship specificity | Can be global, domain-specific, or relationship-specific | May be relational but is not defined by attachment relationship structure |
| Change | Updating through discrepant experience, new relationships, therapy, security | Conscious relation to complex, differentiation, integration, withdrawal of projection, symbolic work |
| Scientific tradition | Psychometric, experimental, longitudinal, dyadic | Clinical, phenomenological, association-experimental, symbolic |

The overlap lies in automaticity, affective bias, memory, and patterned response. The non-equivalence lies in theoretical architecture. A working model need not have the autonomous “partial personality” quality Jung attributes to a complex, and a complex need not be organized around attachment security. [Primary attachment source: chs. 1, 5-6; comparative Jung source: CW 8]

## 94. Attachment Activation versus Complex Constellation

**Attachment activation** requires an attachment-relevant threat or cue and mobilizes security-seeking regulation. **Complex constellation** occurs when a cue activates an affectively charged complex, producing disproportionate affect, associative narrowing, bodily response, and partial loss of ego freedom. The two can co-occur without being identical.

A delayed message, for example, might activate the attachment system if it signals possible unavailability. It might also constellate an abandonment, inferiority, betrayal, or shame complex. Or it might do neither and merely be irritating. The hypothesis must be tested against the episode rather than assumed from the behavior.

## 95. Attachment Expectation versus Projection

Attachment working models bias expectation and interpretation. A person who expects rejection may read ambiguity pessimistically. Jungian projection, by contrast, refers to subjective psychic content experienced as belonging to the object rather than the subject. Projection is therefore not simply “an inaccurate expectation.” [Comparative source: Jungian projection material in CW 6, CW 7, CW 9ii, and CW 16]

An attachment expectation can be accurate, partially accurate, or inaccurate. It becomes a candidate for Jungian projection only when the evidence supports the specific subject-object dynamics of projection.

## 96. Attachment Security versus Individuation

Attachment security concerns confidence in availability, the capacity to seek and receive support, and freedom to explore without chronic hyperactivation or deactivation. Individuation concerns the differentiation and development of the personality through a changing relation between ego, unconscious, and Self. [Comparative source: Jung, CW 7 and CW 9ii]

A person can be secure yet psychologically conventional and little engaged with individuation. A person engaged in individuation can remain attachment-insecure. Security may provide relational conditions that help psychological development, but it is not the same construct and should not be treated as evidence of individuation.

## 97. Attachment Avoidance versus Introversion

Jung's introversion is an attitude in which psychic orientation is directed primarily by the subject and subjective factor. Attachment avoidance is a regulatory strategy concerning closeness, dependence, vulnerability, and access to attachment figures. [Comparative source: Jung, CW 6; primary attachment source: chs. 2, 4]

The two constructs differ in domain, function, origin, and measurement. Quietness, preference for solitude, or a need to process privately cannot be taken as evidence of attachment avoidance.

## 98. Attachment Anxiety versus Extraversion or Feeling

There is no conceptual equivalence between attachment anxiety and Jungian extraversion, nor between attachment anxiety and the feeling function. Extraversion concerns the orientation of consciousness toward objects; feeling is a rational evaluative function in Jung's typology. Attachment anxiety concerns hyperactivating regulation of attachment security. [Comparative source: Jung, CW 6]

## 99. Attachment versus Fordham's Developmental Self

Michael Fordham's developmental model proposes an original or primary Self and processes of **deintegration** and **reintegration** through which infant experience, ego differentiation, and object relations develop. Attachment theory instead describes a behavioral system organized around proximity, protection, working models, and secure-base/safe-haven functions. [Comparative source: Fordham, *Explorations into the Self*, chs. 2-3]

Both take early relationship seriously and reject a purely adult-centered account of psychological development. But deintegration is not attachment-system activation, reintegration is not security restoration, and object relation is not synonymous with attachment bond.

## 100. Attachment versus Knox

Jean Knox is especially relevant because *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis* explicitly integrates developmental psychology and attachment research with a revised Jungian account. Her Chapter 4 treats the formation of internal working models; other chapters address image schemas, trauma and defense, reflective function, and analytic change. [Comparative source: Knox, 2003]

Knox should be treated as a bridge author, not as evidence that Mikulincer and Shaver are covertly Jungian. Her “emergent mind” approach revises classical archetypal assumptions by emphasizing development, implicit organization, and relational experience. Where she uses internal working models, she is importing an attachment/developmental construct into a post-Jungian synthesis. The attachment construct retains its own empirical lineage.

## 101. Attachment versus Young-Eisendrath

Polly Young-Eisendrath's *Love between Equals* emphasizes equality, mutual recognition, dialogue, mature dependence, mindfulness, and the work of seeing through projection and projective identification. She explicitly argues that adult human love cannot be comprehensively reduced to attachment theory. [Comparative source: Young-Eisendrath, 2019]

The difference in emphasis is useful. Attachment theory asks how security, availability, and regulation operate within bonds. Young-Eisendrath asks how two adults become differentiated equals capable of dialogue and recognition rather than idealization, control, or projection. Secure attachment can facilitate such work, but **secure attachment is not love between equals**.

## 102. Attachment versus Contemporary Jungian Couples Therapy

Contemporary Jungian couples approaches commonly foreground complexes, projection, shadow, individuation, symbolic meaning, transference, and the relational field. Attachment-informed couple therapy foregrounds security, attachment threat, availability, responsiveness, and regulation. [Comparative source: Nelson & Delmedico, *The Art of Jungian Couples Therapy: An Introduction*, 2026]

A couple's withdrawal can therefore receive multiple hypotheses: deactivation in attachment terms; complex constellation or projection in Jungian terms; typological processing difference; shame or overload; or a strategic response to actual conflict. The frameworks should be layered rather than collapsed.

# Part X - Adult Attachment Research versus EFCT and Emotion-Focused Therapy

## 103. Adult Attachment Research versus Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy

Mikulincer and Shaver synthesize an empirical/theoretical research tradition. Susan Johnson's Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy (EFCT) is a clinical model that uses attachment theory to understand couple distress and guide therapeutic change. The relationship is substantive but not identity. [Comparative sources: Johnson, 2004; Johnson, 2019]

EFCT adds a structured clinical account of negative interaction cycles, primary attachment-related vulnerability, protective positions, enactments, de-escalation, bonding events, attachment injuries, and stages/steps of treatment. These are not automatically constructs in Mikulincer and Shaver's 2007 research model.

## 104. Hyperactivation versus EFCT Pursuing

Hyperactivation is a regulatory strategy inferred from a person's attachment-system organization. **Pursuing** in EFCT is an interactional position in a particular couple's negative cycle. A pursuer may be attachment-anxious, but pursuit can also be situational, justified, angry, controlling, values-driven, or a response to a partner's specific behavior. [Comparative source: Johnson, 2004]

Overlap is plausible when the pursuit involves escalating attempts to restore availability and reassurance under attachment threat. Identity is not justified.

## 105. Deactivation versus EFCT Withdrawal

Deactivation inhibits attachment needs, proximity seeking, and vulnerable affect. **Withdrawal** in EFCT is an interactional move or position that can protect against conflict, shame, failure, criticism, or feared attachment consequences. [Comparative source: Johnson, 2004]

A withdrawing partner is therefore not automatically attachment-avoidant. Context, within-person pattern, relationship specificity, and validated measurement remain necessary.

## 106. Attachment Security versus EFCT Bonding Event

Attachment security is a broad theoretical and individual-differences construct. An EFCT bonding event is a therapeutic sequence in which partners access and communicate vulnerable attachment-related needs and respond in a new way that reorganizes interaction. [Comparative sources: Johnson, 2004; Johnson, 2019]

A bonding event may increase experienced security, but the two constructs operate at different levels.

## 107. Attachment Injury

Mikulincer and Shaver briefly reference attachment-injury language in their discussion of couple functioning, but the developed clinical construct belongs to Johnson's EFCT tradition. It concerns a significant incident in which a partner is experienced as unavailable or betraying at a critical moment of need. [Primary source: ch. 10; comparative source: Johnson, 2004, ch. 12]

It should not be retrofitted as a core construct of the general adult attachment research model.

## 108. Attachment versus Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy

Leslie Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy is an experiential-humanistic model centered on emotion as adaptive information, emotion schemes, primary adaptive and maladaptive emotion, secondary emotion, awareness, symbolization, and transformation of emotion with emotion. [Comparative source: Greenberg, 2015]

Attachment theory asks how security and proximity regulation organize relationships. Greenberg's model asks how emotional experience is processed and transformed. An attachment episode can contain primary fear, secondary anger, shame, or other emotion-process dynamics, but an **attachment strategy is not an emotion scheme**. “EFT” is therefore ambiguous and should be qualified in the Laboratory: EFCT for Johnson's couple model; Emotion-Focused Therapy for Greenberg's emotion-process lineage.

# Part XI - Applied Episode Analysis, Misreadings, and Guardrails

## 109. How to Use Attachment Theory in Episode Analysis

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Start with the episode rather than the label. Record what happened, what each person actually did, and what relationship context preceded the episode. Then ask whether the event plausibly involved attachment-relevant threat: separation, rejection, unavailability, loss, vulnerability, or uncertainty about support. Only then consider whether behavior is consistent with security-based regulation, hyperactivation, or deactivation.

A disciplined question set is:

1. What was directly observable?
2. What did each person report feeling or fearing?
3. Was safety, rejection, separation, or availability at stake?
4. What response from the partner was desired?
5. Was proximity or support sought, accepted, refused, or suppressed?
6. What did the partner actually do?
7. Was reassurance effective, temporarily effective, or unable to reduce activation?
8. Is there repeated evidence of hyperactivation?
9. Is there repeated evidence of deactivation?
10. What evidence argues against either attachment hypothesis?
11. Does the pattern recur across relationships or only in this one?
12. Could actual incompatibility, poor behavior, power, stress, or values explain the event more directly?
13. Could a Jungian complex have been constellated separately?
14. Could projection be operating separately?
15. Could typology, shame, trauma, neurodivergence, fatigue, or learned communication habits be relevant?
16. What remains unknown?

## 110. Attachment Episode Protocol

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Use the following sequence for structured research notes:

1. **Observable event:** behavior and timing only.
2. **Relationship context:** history, commitments, current stressors, actual reliability.
3. **Perceived threat:** what loss, rejection, danger, or unavailability was perceived?
4. **Affect:** fear, anger, shame, sadness, jealousy, numbness, confusion, etc.
5. **Desired partner response:** closeness, reassurance, space, information, apology, protection.
6. **Proximity/support behavior:** approach, message, disclosure, checking, distancing, silence.
7. **Partner response:** actual accessibility, responsiveness, rejection, uncertainty, escalation.
8. **Hyperactivation hypothesis:** evidence for and against.
9. **Deactivation hypothesis:** evidence for and against.
10. **Security-based hypothesis:** evidence for flexible support seeking and recovery.
11. **Recurrence:** one-off event or stable pattern?
12. **Relationship specificity:** same behavior with other attachment figures?
13. **Complex hypothesis:** separate Jungian formulation if warranted.
14. **Projection hypothesis:** separate subject-object formulation if warranted.
15. **Reality test:** what partner behavior is objectively problematic or incompatible?
16. **Uncertainty:** state what cannot be inferred.
17. **No fixed label:** do not convert the episode into an identity claim.

## 111. Couple Attachment Map

[ATTACHMENT-BASED HEURISTIC]

```text
perceived threat
    -> attachment-system activation
    -> assess attachment-figure availability
        -> if available/responsive:
              proximity/support can succeed
              -> increased security
              -> reduced activation
              -> return to exploration
        -> if unavailable/uncertain:
              assess viability of proximity seeking
                  -> hyperactivation
                  OR
                  -> deactivation
    -> behavior toward partner
    -> partner interprets/responds
    -> security increases OR threat intensifies
    -> recursive interaction
```

This is an attachment heuristic, not an EFCT negative cycle.

## 112. Hyperactivation Map

[ATTACHMENT-BASED HEURISTIC]

```text
attachment-relevant threat
-> increased monitoring of availability/rejection
-> intensified distress and proximity seeking
-> reassurance obtained, ambiguous, or absent
-> reassurance fails to consolidate
-> further vigilance and renewed proximity attempts
```

## 113. Deactivation Map

[ATTACHMENT-BASED HEURISTIC]

```text
attachment-relevant threat
-> expectation that proximity will fail or be unsafe
-> inhibit attachment need / vulnerable affect
-> distance, suppress, self-rely
-> reduce support seeking and attachment-related attention
-> outward activation may fall
-> underlying threat may or may not remain active
```

The last line is deliberately conditional. Outward calm does not prove hidden distress, and hidden distress should not be assumed without evidence.

## 114. Security Map

[ATTACHMENT-BASED HEURISTIC]

```text
threat
-> acknowledge need and/or seek appropriate support
-> perceive sufficient availability/responsiveness
-> distress becomes manageable
-> attachment activation subsides
-> exploration, problem solving, and ordinary relationship activity resume
```

## 115. Generic Case: Delayed Reply

A partner does not answer a message for several hours.

**Situational formulation:** the partner is working, driving, asleep, or has no phone access. No attachment process is required.

**Attachment-anxiety hypothesis:** the delay is appraised as possible rejection; monitoring and checking intensify; alternative explanations are hard to hold; reassurance is urgently sought. Evidence would require repeated threat-linked patterns, not the single delay.
