**Attachment-avoidance hypothesis:** the recipient notices concern but suppresses it and refuses to seek clarification because dependence feels unacceptable. Evidence would require repeated deactivation, not the absence of a reply itself.

**Complex hypothesis:** the delay constellates a pre-existing abandonment, betrayal, humiliation, or inferiority complex with affect disproportionate to the available facts.

**Projection hypothesis:** subjective content is attributed to the partner as though it were an objective property, for example certainty that the partner is contemptuous without supporting evidence.

**Ordinary irritation:** the delay violates a practical expectation and causes annoyance without deeper attachment significance.

No diagnosis can be made from the event alone.

## 116. Generic Case: Conflict Withdrawal

A partner becomes silent during an argument.

Possible formulations include attachment deactivation; physiological overload; shame; fear of making the conflict worse; anger; strategic time-out; introverted processing; a learned family communication pattern; refusal to engage; coercive stonewalling; or a response to actual verbal aggression. The evidence needed includes what the silence means to the person, whether attachment needs are being suppressed, how often it occurs, and what happens when safety is restored.

## 117. Generic Case: Reassurance Seeking

A request for reassurance is normal when uncertainty is real. Possible hyperactivation becomes more plausible when reassurance is repeatedly sought, has only brief regulatory effect, the threshold for threat is low, and the person remains vigilant despite credible partner availability. Context and frequency matter.

## 118. Generic Case: Strong Self-Reliance

Strong self-reliance can represent secure autonomy, temperament, competence, cultural value, practical habit, or attachment deactivation. Secure autonomy remains flexible: help can be sought when useful. Defensive deactivation becomes more plausible when support is consistently avoided precisely under vulnerability and when dependence is treated as unsafe or unacceptable.

## 119. Common Misreadings

| Misreading | Correction |
|---|---|
| Everyone has one fixed attachment style. | Adult attachment is better represented dimensionally and can vary by relationship and context. |
| Attachment style is a personality type. | Attachment orientation concerns security regulation in close relationships; personality is broader. |
| Anxious people are emotionally needy. | Anxiety refers to hyperactivating attachment regulation, not a moral or personality judgment. |
| Avoidant people do not have attachment needs. | Deactivation inhibits or distances from attachment needs; it does not prove their absence. |
| Avoidant people do not feel distress. | Lower reported distress can coexist with other signs of activation, but hidden distress should not be presumed automatically. |
| Secure people do not need reassurance. | Secure attachment includes flexible support seeking and dependence when appropriate. |
| Secure attachment means independence. | Security supports interdependence and exploration, not detachment from others. |
| Secure couples do not fight. | Security concerns regulation and repair, not the absence of conflict. |
| Attachment anxiety is an anxiety disorder. | They are different constructs and measurement traditions. |
| Attachment avoidance is introversion. | Avoidance concerns closeness/dependence; introversion is a Jungian attitude of consciousness. |
| Pursuers are anxious. | Pursuit is interactional behavior; anxiety is an attachment dimension. |
| Withdrawers are avoidant. | Withdrawal has many possible functions and causes. |
| A delayed reply activates attachment in everyone. | Activation depends on appraisal, context, relationship, and individual differences. |
| Attachment style is caused by parents. | Development is probabilistic and includes later relationships, context, temperament, and change. |
| Childhood attachment determines adult relationships. | Continuity exists but is incomplete and revisable. |
| Internal working models are Jungian complexes. | They differ in theoretical lineage, structure, and explanatory purpose. |
| Attachment expectations are projections. | Expectations can be accurate or biased without constituting Jungian projection. |
| Insecurity explains incompatibility. | Values, behavior, life goals, abuse, power, and practical incompatibility remain separate realities. |
| A responsive partner makes insecurity disappear. | Responsiveness can support security, but established models can persist and relationships are reciprocal. |
| One secure partner can regulate the whole relationship. | Buffering can occur, but findings are inconsistent and one partner cannot mechanically determine the dyad. |
| Attachment theory explains all couple conflict. | It is one explanatory framework among many. |
| Adult attachment research and EFCT are the same theory. | EFCT is a later clinical application and elaboration of attachment principles. |
| Secure attachment is individuation. | Security and Jungian individuation are distinct constructs. |
| Satisfaction proves security. | Satisfaction is an outcome influenced by many variables and can correlate with attachment measures without being identical to them. |

## 120. Anti-Flattening Dictionary

- **attachment system != attachment style:** system is species-typical regulatory architecture; style/orientation is individual difference.
- **attachment style != personality type:** attachment is relationship-security regulation; personality and typology cover different domains.
- **attachment dimension != categorical identity:** anxiety and avoidance are continuous variables.
- **attachment anxiety != anxiety disorder:** one is an attachment dimension; the other is a psychiatric diagnostic domain.
- **attachment avoidance != introversion:** defensive regulation of closeness is not Jung's inward psychic orientation.
- **hyperactivation != emotionality:** hyperactivation specifically sustains attachment-related monitoring and proximity regulation.
- **deactivation != absence of feeling:** it can involve suppression, exclusion, or distancing.
- **proximity seeking != pathological dependency:** proximity seeking is the primary attachment strategy and can be adaptive.
- **self-reliance != security:** self-reliance may be flexible or defensive.
- **internal working model != Jungian complex:** different traditions, structures, and explanatory functions.
- **internal working model != archetype:** working models are experience-shaped representations, not Jungian archetypal structures.
- **attachment expectation != projection:** expectation can be learned or accurate without unconscious attribution to the object.
- **attachment activation != complex constellation:** one organizes security; the other is activation of an affectively charged psychic complex.
- **attachment activation != inferior-function activation:** the constructs belong to different theories.
- **secure attachment != independence:** security supports flexible interdependence.
- **secure attachment != individuation:** relational security is not Jung's process of personality development in relation to the Self.
- **secure attachment != relationship satisfaction:** security predicts satisfaction imperfectly; constructs remain distinct.
- **secure attachment != love between equals:** Young-Eisendrath's equality/dialogue model includes ethical and developmental claims beyond attachment security.
- **avoidance != EFCT withdrawal:** one is an attachment dimension/strategy; the other is an interactional position.
- **anxiety != EFCT pursuit:** one is a dimension; the other is observable relational behavior.
- **attachment security != EFCT bonding event:** broad construct versus therapeutic change event.
- **attachment theory != EFCT:** empirical/theoretical tradition versus clinical treatment model.
- **attachment theory != Emotion-Focused Therapy:** attachment security regulation versus Greenberg's emotion-processing framework.
- **association != causation:** co-variation does not establish direction or mechanism.
- **group-level finding != individual diagnosis:** average research tendencies cannot classify a person from isolated behavior.

# Chapter-by-Chapter Reconstruction

This section follows the source's actual order. Page ranges use the book's printed pagination as recoverable from the contents and chapter boundaries.

## Chapter 1 - The Attachment Behavioral System: Basic Concepts and Principles

**Main research question.** What did Bowlby and Ainsworth mean by attachment as a behavioral system, and which concepts are required before adult individual differences can be studied?

**Central theoretical claim.** Attachment is an evolved, goal-corrected behavioral system whose biological function is protection through proximity to caring others. The system has activating conditions, a primary strategy of proximity seeking, a set-goal of felt security/protection, and relations with other behavioral systems. Adult attachment must be understood from this normative architecture before styles or dimensions are introduced.

**Core constructs.** Behavioral system; biological function; proximity seeking; attachment figure; safe haven; secure base; felt security; attachment-exploration balance; working model; attachment style; individual differences.

**Main evidence reviewed.** Bowlby's evolutionary/control-system theory; Ainsworth's observational work; infancy and developmental evidence; early adult romantic-attachment research. The chapter also uses the biographies and intellectual backgrounds of Bowlby and Ainsworth to explain why their theory diverged from drive-reduction accounts of dependency.

**Measurement approach.** Primarily conceptual and historical here; measurement becomes the focus of Chapter 4. The chapter introduces the distinction between normative system functioning and individual-difference assessment.

**Couple / relationship implications.** Adult romantic partners can become attachment figures if they acquire proximity, safe-haven, and secure-base functions. Mature attachment is not the persistence of infantile dependency; security can support autonomous exploration.

**Important empirical findings.** The adult literature supports the usefulness of attachment concepts beyond infancy, but adult bonds differ in reciprocity, symbolic regulation, caregiving, and sexuality.

**Conflicting or qualified evidence.** The same observable behavior can serve different behavioral systems. Physical closeness alone does not prove attachment. An adult can seek a partner for affiliation, sex, habit, or practical support without attachment being the dominant system.

**Causal limitations.** The chapter lays out theory rather than establishing every proposition through adult experiments.

**Relation to other chapters.** Chapters 2-3 convert the architecture into a dynamic adult model; Chapter 4 measures individual differences; Chapter 5 addresses development.

**Relevance to the Laboratory.** It prevents “attachment style” from replacing the broader attachment-system model and prevents normal proximity seeking from being pathologized.

**Source location.** Mikulincer & Shaver, 2007, ch. 1, printed pp. 3-28.

## Chapter 2 - A Model of Attachment-System Functioning and Dynamics in Adulthood

**Main research question.** How does the attachment system operate moment to moment in adults, and how do security, hyperactivation, and deactivation emerge from the same basic architecture?

**Central theoretical claim.** Adult attachment-system functioning can be modeled as a sequence of appraisals: detect threat; assess attachment-figure availability; if availability is sufficient, use security-based strategies; if not, assess whether proximity seeking remains viable; if viable, hyperactivate; if not, deactivate. These processes are influenced by current reality and by chronic working models.

**Core constructs.** Threat appraisal; preconscious activation; availability; security-based strategy; broaden-and-build cycle; hyperactivating strategy; deactivating strategy; attachment anxiety; attachment avoidance; working models; defensive exclusion.

**Main evidence reviewed.** Experimental priming, cognitive accessibility studies, self-report individual-differences research, and developmental/clinical observations supporting the component processes.

**Measurement approach.** The chapter links dimensional anxiety and avoidance to the two major secondary strategies, while also discussing categorical traditions.

**Couple / relationship implications.** Relationship behavior is shaped by both actual partner responsiveness and attachment-related expectations. The model is therefore neither purely intrapsychic nor purely situational.

**Important empirical findings.** Hyperactivation is associated with vigilance, rumination, intense proximity seeking, and difficulty terminating activation. Deactivation is associated with suppression, distancing, self-reliance, and inhibited access to attachment-related thoughts. Security supports flexible proximity and exploration.

**Conflicting or qualified evidence.** High anxiety and high avoidance can produce conflicted or alternating strategies. The model should not be read as three rigid boxes.

**Causal limitations.** Chronic orientations are mostly inferred from measured individual differences; experimental work more directly establishes state activation or priming effects than lifelong causal trajectories.

**Relation to other chapters.** This is the book's central process model. Chapters 3 and 7 test normative activation and emotion regulation; Chapters 9-12 extend it interpersonally.

**Relevance to the Laboratory.** It provides the cleanest basis for distinguishing attachment activation from complex constellation and EFCT interactional positions.

**Source location.** ch. 2, printed pp. 29-50. Figure 2.1 is the central schematic model.

## Chapter 3 - Normative Attachment Processes

**Main research question.** Which attachment processes appear broadly across people independent of chronic attachment orientation?

**Central theoretical claim.** Threat tends to activate mental representations of attachment figures and proximity-related goals; attachment figures are hierarchically organized; actual or symbolic availability can reduce distress and broaden functioning; prolonged unavailability and loss organize predictable attachment responses.

**Core constructs.** Normative activation; preconscious accessibility; preferred attachment figure; attachment hierarchy; monotropy; safe haven; secure base; loss; mourning; reorganization.

**Main evidence reviewed.** Subliminal threat studies, lexical-decision and Stroop tasks, security priming, mortality-salience paradigms, developmental research on attachment hierarchies, and work on separation/loss.

**Measurement approach.** Experimental reaction-time paradigms are important because they probe processes not dependent on conscious self-description.

**Couple / relationship implications.** A romantic partner who has become a principal attachment figure can become especially cognitively accessible under threat. Partner availability can function as a regulator even when the threat is not caused by the relationship itself.

**Important empirical findings.** Threat cues can increase accessibility of attachment-figure representations. The authors are careful to require evidence that proximity has a protective function before calling a response attachment-system activation.

**Conflicting or qualified evidence.** Closeness can serve sex, affiliation, cultural obligation, or shared-interest motives. A death reminder that increases desire for closeness does not by itself prove attachment-system activation.

**Causal limitations.** Laboratory activation paradigms establish short-term process effects, not the full ecology of adult attachment relationships.

**Relation to other chapters.** Chapter 3 supplies the normative baseline against which Chapters 4-5 define individual differences.

**Relevance to the Laboratory.** It gives a strong anti-overinterpretation rule: do not infer attachment merely from closeness or distress; demonstrate security/protection relevance.

**Source location.** ch. 3, printed pp. 51-80.

## Chapter 4 - Measurement of Attachment-Related Constructs in Adulthood

**Main research question.** How should adult attachment be measured, and what do different methods actually assess?

**Central theoretical claim.** Adult attachment has been measured through multiple traditions that are related but not identical. Romantic self-report research is most usefully organized around continuous anxiety and avoidance dimensions. Narrative/interview methods such as the AAI assess different representational phenomena and should not be treated as interchangeable with self-report romantic attachment.

**Core constructs.** Strange Situation categories; self-report attachment style; AAQ; AAS; ASQ; RSQ; ECR; ECR-R; anxiety; avoidance; AAI; relationship-specific measures; general attachment; implicit measurement; discriminant validity.

**Main evidence reviewed.** Psychometric studies, factor analyses, taxometric studies, scale comparisons, interview/self-report convergence studies, and observational validation work.

# Attachment Theory, Affect Regulation, and Therapeutic Change

## Source Status and Evidence Note

This file is an exhaustive structured reconstruction of Susan M. Johnson's *Attachment Theory in Practice: Emotionally Focused Therapy (EFT) with Individuals, Couples, and Families*, published by The Guilford Press in 2019. The Project PDF supplies the verified title, subtitle, author, publisher, copyright year, chapter order, appendices, and primary textual evidence. The Project EPUB was used only as a technically cleaner representation of the same book when chapter structure or extraction was easier to follow there. It is not treated as a second intellectual source.

Johnson's book is simultaneously theoretical, clinical, and argumentative. It presents attachment theory as a developmental theory of personality and affect regulation; argues that attachment science offers psychotherapy a parsimonious, empirically anchored framework; connects attachment science with a humanistic-experiential method of intervention; and then shows how the resulting approach is implemented across individual, couple, and family therapy. This reconstruction therefore distinguishes several evidentiary levels that Johnson herself moves between:

- **Johnson's theoretical proposition**: her claims about attachment, personality, health, dysfunction, emotion, relationship, and change.
- **Johnson's use of attachment research**: empirical findings that she cites to justify or elaborate the model.
- **Johnson's clinical inference**: the way she translates attachment concepts and empirical findings into a formulation of distress.
- **Johnson's therapeutic procedure**: stages, interventions, therapist stance, and the EFT Tango.
- **Johnson's case illustration**: clinical examples showing the model in action.
- **Johnson's efficacy claims**: her summary of outcome and process research, especially for Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy.

The book itself is not a systematic review or comparative meta-analysis. Accordingly, this corpus file reconstructs Johnson's use of the research without treating every empirical claim as independently re-verified by the Laboratory. Case examples are treated as demonstrations of clinical reasoning and intervention, not as proof of general propositions.

**Primary provenance:** [Johnson, 2019, Preface; chs. 1-10; appendices 1-3].

## Coverage of This File

The reconstruction follows Johnson's own sequence before offering a cross-cutting retrieval architecture. It covers:

1. attachment as a proposed unifying framework for psychotherapy;
2. attachment as a developmental theory of personality and affect regulation;
3. safe haven, secure base, proximity, bonding, and constructive dependency;
4. accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement as the defining qualities of a secure bond;
5. insecurity, anxious hyperactivation, avoidant deactivation, and protective strategies;
6. working models of self and other as affectively charged procedural expectations rather than merely detached beliefs;
7. the reciprocal relation between internal experience and interactional process;
8. the therapeutic alliance as a secure-base and safe-haven relationship;
9. emotion as both a target of change and an agent of change;
10. the EFT Tango as Johnson's recurring metaframework for intervention;
11. Emotionally Focused Individual Therapy (EFIT);
12. Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy (EFCT);
13. Emotionally Focused Family Therapy (EFFT);
14. Johnson's account of corrective emotional experience and corrective interaction;
15. the role of case formulation, assessment, and measurement;
16. Johnson's concluding argument about the promise of attachment science for psychotherapy.

The file does not attempt to provide the full detailed couple-treatment manual that belongs in File 24, nor does it reconstruct Leslie Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy, which belongs in File 25. It also does not substitute for the broader empirical adult-attachment architecture represented in File 22.

## Terminology and Therapy-Lineage Note

Johnson uses **EFT** as an umbrella term for her attachment-centred, emotionally focused approach. In this file, modality-specific acronyms are used where clarity requires them:

- **EFIT**: Emotionally Focused Individual Therapy.
- **EFCT**: Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy.
- **EFFT**: Emotionally Focused Family Therapy.

Johnson's use of "emotionally focused" must not be collapsed into Leslie Greenberg's later, separate **Emotion-Focused Therapy** lineage. Johnson and Greenberg share important historical and experiential roots, and their earlier collaboration is part of the history of the field, but Johnson's 2019 book explicitly differentiates her attachment-saturated EFIT from process-experiential/emotion-focused therapy. In particular, Johnson privileges attachment working models, constructive dependency, co-regulation, systemic feedback loops, and the EFT Tango, and she rejects the idea that the core of change can be reduced to changing one emotion with another or working through a catalogue of task markers. [Johnson, 2019, Appendix 3].

## How to Use This File

Use this file when the question is specifically about Johnson's 2019 attachment-centred psychotherapy model: how she understands personality, insecurity, affect regulation, therapeutic alliance, emotion, corrective experience, and change across modalities. For narrower empirical questions about adult attachment structure and measurement, activate File 22. For the detailed technical architecture of couple therapy, activate File 24. For Greenberg's emotion-processing model, activate File 25. For later Jungian integration, use Files 31-33 rather than importing Jungian concepts into Johnson's own framework.
# Part I - Attachment as a Framework for Psychotherapy

## Chapter 1 - Attachment: An Essential Guide for Science-Based Practice

### Johnson's starting problem: fragmentation in psychotherapy

Johnson opens by framing contemporary psychotherapy as intellectually and practically fragmented. The field contains a proliferating number of named therapies, disorder-specific protocols, and in-session techniques. Her concern is not merely that there are many interventions, but that practitioners can lose a coherent picture of the human being while trying to match an ever-expanding list of techniques to an ever-expanding list of diagnostic categories. Symptom relief can become detached from personality, developmental context, intimate relationships, and the processes that organize distress. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 1, "Four Routes Out of Chaos"].

She considers four broad routes toward greater coherence. The first is **dedicated empiricism**, in which the therapist attempts to master the research and select the optimal protocol for each disorder and presentation. Johnson sees value in empirical discipline but doubts that a clinician can realistically become a technician who masters hundreds of increasingly specialized protocols. The second is a focus on **common factors**, especially therapeutic alliance and client engagement. She treats these as important but insufficient. Alliance matters, but an alliance without a theory of what should change and how it changes cannot by itself orient intervention. The third route is to identify **common structures across disorders**, as in transdiagnostic models of emotional disorders that focus on threat sensitivity, avoidance, catastrophizing, and negative affect rather than on dozens of diagnostic labels. Johnson considers this direction useful but still incomplete. The fourth route is the one she endorses: an empirically grounded, developmental and relational theory of human functioning that explains how people thrive, how they become constrained, and what processes generate durable change.

Her requirement is ambitious. A useful theory for psychotherapy must describe observable patterns, support prediction, provide explanatory links, apply across individual, couple, and family modalities, and address emotion, cognition, behavior, development, relationships, health, and dysfunction in one coherent frame. It should orient the therapist to core organizing variables rather than merely to presenting symptoms. Johnson argues that modern attachment theory is the strongest available candidate for this role. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 1].

### Attachment theory as Johnson's model of the person

Johnson presents attachment theory as far more than a theory of childhood bonding. In her 2019 formulation, it is a developmental theory of personality, a theory of affect regulation, a theory of intimate relationship, and a model of resilience and vulnerability across the lifespan. Its most basic premise is that humans are a bonding species. Our nervous systems, expectations, emotion-regulation strategies, and behavioral repertoires develop in a relational ecology in which proximity to trusted others has survival significance.

Three general features organize Johnson's presentation. First, attachment is fundamentally **interpersonal**. It places the person in the context of important relationships and treats bonding as a primary survival strategy rather than an optional social preference. Second, attachment is centrally about **emotion and emotion regulation**, especially fear and the management of vulnerability, helplessness, separation, and loss. Third, attachment is **developmental**. It asks how repeated patterns of response from important others shape the individual's expectations, regulation strategies, sense of self, capacity for exploration, and ability to adapt as circumstances change. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 1, "Principles of Attachment Theory"].

Johnson's preferred image of the human being is therefore relational from the start. The individual does not first become a self-contained unit and only later enter relationships. Self-regulation, self-confidence, exploration, and autonomy are scaffolded by experiences of responsive connection. The secure person is not the person who needs nobody. The secure person can depend effectively, can use others as resources without losing agency, and can move outward into exploration because connection is sufficiently reliable.

### The ten core principles Johnson highlights

Johnson condenses modern attachment theory into ten interrelated principles. The following table reconstructs those principles in compact form while preserving her conceptual sequence.

| Principle | Johnson's formulation and clinical significance |
|---|---|
| **1. Proximity seeking is primary** | Across the lifespan, people seek physical and emotional proximity to a small number of irreplaceable others, especially under threat, pain, uncertainty, or loss. The felt sense of connection is not secondary to another motive. It is a primary survival-oriented goal. |
| **2. Responsive others create a safe haven** | Predictable accessibility and responsiveness calm the nervous system. The attachment figure becomes a place to turn toward for comfort, reassurance, and restoration of emotional balance. |
| **3. Security supports an integrated self** | When distress can be regulated in connection, inner experience becomes more coherent. Needs can be recognized and expressed more congruently, and the person can organize experience without having to deny, distort, fragment, or amplify it. |
| **4. Dependable connection creates a secure base** | A secure bond supports exploration, risk taking, autonomy, competence, and curiosity. Johnson repeatedly frames effective dependency as a source of strength rather than as a developmental failure. |
| **5. Secure bonds are defined by A.R.E.** | The decisive qualities are **Accessibility, Responsiveness, and Engagement**. The attachment question in close relationships is effectively: can I reach you, will you respond, and are you emotionally present with me? |
| **6. Threatened connection triggers separation distress** | Loss, rejection, unresponsiveness, and emotional isolation activate the attachment system. Distress around a threatened attachment bond has a distinctive intensity because the threat is registered as danger and helplessness, not merely disappointment. |
| **7. Security is organized through interaction and encoded in working models** | Repeated interactions become affectively charged expectations about whether others are dependable and whether the self is worthy of care. These models include beliefs, memories, perceptual biases, emotional tendencies, and procedural expectations about what to do in close relationships. |
| **8. Security supports direct reaching and receiving** | Secure people can acknowledge need, send relatively clear signals, ask for connection, receive care, and use it to regain balance. Security buffers stress because the person has an effective relational strategy rather than needing to intensify or suppress attachment needs. |
| **9. Insecurity organizes secondary protective strategies** | When responsiveness is unreliable or dangerous, people adopt hyperactivating strategies, deactivating strategies, or mixed fearful patterns. The problem is not that protection exists; it is that once-flexible protection can become rigid and self-confirming. |
| **10. Working models and strategies can change** | Attachment orientations are relatively stable but not fixed essences. New relationships and emotionally powerful disconfirming experiences can revise expectations, regulation patterns, and interactional repertoires. Therapy can therefore target attachment organization rather than simply manage symptoms. |

[Johnson, 2019, ch. 1, "Principles of Attachment Theory"].

### Safe haven, secure base, and constructive dependency

Two attachment functions structure much of Johnson's later clinical model. **Safe haven** refers to the availability of an attachment figure in moments of threat or distress. The secure person can turn toward another, signal need, receive a response, and regain emotional equilibrium. **Secure base** refers to the platform of confidence created by this dependable availability. Once the person knows that help can be reached when needed, attention and energy can shift from threat monitoring to exploration, play, work, sexuality, creativity, and independent action.

Johnson uses these concepts to challenge a common therapeutic ideal of self-sufficiency. In her model, maturity is not the elimination of dependency. It is the transformation of dependency from anxious, coercive, hidden, or disowned need into flexible **constructive dependency**. A person can turn to others without collapse, receive comfort without shame, and move back into autonomous action. Self-reliance and relational reliance are not opposites. Secure attachment allows them to support each other.

### Accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement

Johnson operationalizes secure connection through the A.R.E. triad:

- **Accessibility**: the other can be reached and is not chronically absent, closed, or inaccessible when attachment need is activated.
- **Responsiveness**: the other registers the signal, grasps something of its emotional meaning, and responds contingently rather than mechanically or dismissively.
- **Engagement**: the response carries emotional presence. The other is not merely physically available but affectively involved.

These qualities define both the attachment bond and Johnson's preferred therapeutic alliance. A therapist who is technically competent but emotionally unavailable does not reproduce the security-promoting relational conditions that Johnson considers central to change. Likewise, a partner or parent may offer instrumental solutions while remaining inaccessible to the vulnerable emotional signal that actually organizes the distressed interaction.

### Working models of self and other

Johnson treats internal working models as dynamic, affect-laden procedural maps rather than as isolated cognitive beliefs. They answer questions such as: Can I count on others? Am I entitled to care? What happens if I show need? What should I do when I am afraid? Is closeness soothing or dangerous? The model of self and the model of other are reciprocal. A repeated experience of unresponsiveness can organize a self that expects to be too needy, unworthy, incompetent, or alone. A repeated experience of reliable responsiveness can support a self that expects distress to be manageable and relationships to be usable sources of support.

These working models operate through selective attention, interpretation, memory, emotional response, and action. When rigid, they can become self-confirming. A person who anticipates rejection may intensify bids for reassurance until the partner withdraws, confirming the expectation of abandonment. A person who anticipates intrusion or disappointment may suppress need and distance from others, eliciting less engagement and confirming the expectation that others cannot be relied upon. Johnson therefore does not locate dysfunction solely "inside" the individual or solely in the relationship. The model is circular. Inner expectation shapes action; action shapes the partner's response; the response feeds back into the expectation.

### Insecurity and protective strategies

Johnson's nonpathologizing stance is clearest in her treatment of insecurity. Anxious and avoidant strategies are understood as adaptations to relational environments in which direct reaching was unreliable, costly, frightening, or ineffective. **Anxious or hyperactivating strategies** amplify signal, vigilance, protest, and urgency. The person becomes highly sensitive to cues of rejection and may have difficulty taking in reassurance once alarm has escalated. **Avoidant or deactivating strategies** minimize awareness and expression of need, dampen emotion, turn away from dependence, and prioritize control or self-sufficiency. **Fearful avoidant patterns** combine longing with threat: the attachment figure is desperately needed and simultaneously experienced as dangerous.

The clinical problem is not that these responses once existed. In Johnson's language, survival strategies can become prisons when they generalize, rigidify, and prevent new information from being encoded. Therapy must therefore validate the protective logic of the response while opening the client to new emotional and interpersonal experience.

### Affect regulation as the centre of attachment health

For Johnson, secure attachment is inseparable from effective affect regulation. Security does not mean an absence of negative emotion. It means that fear, sadness, anger, shame, and uncertainty can be noticed, tolerated, symbolized, communicated, and moved through without either runaway escalation or rigid suppression. Secure people can stay engaged with distress long enough to learn from it and recover from it. Insecure regulation is organized more often around hyperarousal, compulsive monitoring, catastrophizing, avoidance, numbing, suppression, or oscillation between these states.

This gives attachment a direct clinical relevance to anxiety and depression. Johnson summarizes evidence linking insecurity to vulnerability across a range of problems, but she is careful to frame insecurity as a **risk factor**, not a deterministic diagnosis. She emphasizes multifinality and context: similar histories or orientations can lead to different outcomes, depending on present relationships, stress, temperament, loss, trauma, and other moderating variables.

### Secure attachment and mental health

Johnson's review of attachment research connects security with broad indices of functioning: resilience under stress, self-esteem, optimism, curiosity, capacity to tolerate ambiguity, empathy, social support, coherent meaning making, and the ability to recover from negative affect. At the interpersonal level, security is associated with sensitive caregiving, openness to difference, compassion, and more constructive responses to conflict. Her clinical emphasis is less on any single correlation than on the pattern: emotional regulation, coherent information processing, and confidence in self and others tend to reinforce one another.

In contrast, attachment insecurity is presented as a vulnerability factor for depression and anxiety. Anxious attachment is especially relevant to loneliness, abandonment fear, helplessness, rejection sensitivity, and interpersonal forms of distress. Avoidant attachment is associated with suppression, compulsive self-reliance, reduced help seeking, and forms of distress organized around achievement, self-criticism, or emotional distance. Johnson does not treat these as one-to-one diagnostic mappings. Rather, the attachment frame explains why threat, isolation, regulation, and relationship quality deserve a central place in case formulation.

### Common misconceptions Johnson addresses

Johnson spends substantial space correcting what she sees as misunderstandings of attachment theory.

**Attachment is not childhood determinism.** Early experience matters, but working models are open to revision. Present relationships can repeatedly confirm insecurity or provide disconfirming experiences that shift it.

**Attachment is not the opposite of autonomy.** Secure dependency makes exploration and independent action more possible because the individual does not need to devote so much energy to threat monitoring or defensive self-sufficiency.

**Attachment is not reducible to a fixed style label.** People vary across dimensions, relationships, contexts, and phases of life. Johnson repeatedly warns against reifying styles into immutable traits.

**Attachment does not exclude sexuality.** In adult pair bonds, sexuality and attachment often become integrated. Johnson argues that security can support sexual exploration because safety permits relaxation, risk taking, communication, and play.

**Attachment is not merely analytic or intrapsychic.** Johnson emphasizes Bowlby's systemic sensibility. Inner emotional organization and interpersonal feedback loops continuously shape each other. Attachment gives systemic therapy an account of the lived emotional experience inside the dance, while systems theory gives attachment a language for recursive patterns between people.

### Attachment change in psychotherapy

Johnson ends Chapter 1 by turning explicitly to change. She notes that adult attachment can be assessed in several ways: self-report measures of anxiety and avoidance, behavioral coding of secure-base interactions, and interview-based assessments of attachment state of mind and narrative coherence. These measures capture different aspects of attachment and should not be treated as interchangeable.

The clinical point is that psychotherapy clients are often insecure when treatment begins, but attachment is changeable. Johnson cites work showing movement toward security in psychodynamic therapy, attachment-based family therapy, and EFCT. The implication is not that every therapy must become attachment therapy, but that change in attachment organization is a meaningful outcome variable rather than an impossible ambition.

The chapter's final proposition is therefore programmatic: if psychotherapy wants a parsimonious map of the whole person, the field should treat attachment science as a central organising framework rather than as a specialist theory of relationships.

---

## Chapter 2 - Attachment Theory and Science as a Model for Therapeutic Change

### From a theory of bonding to a theory of intervention

Johnson begins Chapter 2 from a gap in Bowlby's work. Bowlby developed a powerful account of bonding, development, separation, loss, and working models, but he did not leave a fully specified, session-by-session model of psychotherapy. His late clinical writings nevertheless identify several tasks: provide a secure base; examine how the client's way of relating shapes painful situations; use the therapeutic relationship as a live microcosm of attachment organization; explore the origins of current patterns and the emotions excluded by them; and help the client see how past experience constrains present perception and action.

Johnson argues that two implications of attachment science are especially important and are not captured by a purely interpretive reading of Bowlby. First, attachment working models are "hot": they are organized through emotion, bodily response, action tendencies, and relational expectation, not merely through abstract belief. Second, change is inherently interpersonal. New emotional messages in dialogue with another person can disconfirm old models more powerfully than detached insight alone. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 2].

### Johnson's script for healthy adaptation

Her model of healthy adaptation is circular rather than linear. A felt sense of secure connection supports emotional balance. Emotional balance permits fuller exploration of inner experience and more coherent models of self and other. Coherent inner models support flexible engagement with people and the environment. Responsive interactions then strengthen the felt sense of safe connection. Regulation and relationship therefore continually reinforce one another at both micro and developmental levels.

This is the logic that allows Johnson to treat attachment theory as a clinical model rather than simply a description of bond quality. Therapy can intervene anywhere in the cycle, but its most powerful interventions aim at emotionally charged moments in which inner regulation, meaning, and interpersonal response shift together.

### Six ways Johnson aligns attachment science and EFT

Johnson identifies six major points of convergence between attachment science and contemporary EFT.

| Convergence | Clinical meaning in Johnson's model |
|---|---|
| **1. Active processing and regulation of emotion** | Therapy helps clients identify, assemble, tolerate, deepen, and integrate emotional experience. Regulation includes co-regulation; top-down coping skills alone are insufficient. |
| **2. In-session emotional safety** | The therapist functions as a responsive attachment figure, creating a safe haven and secure base from which difficult emotion and new action can be explored. |
| **3. Integration of within and between** | Internal experience and interactional pattern are treated as mutually determining. Self and system are addressed together rather than assigned to separate theories. |
| **4. Shared picture of health and dysfunction** | Health means flexible regulation, revisable working models, and an ability to reach and respond. Dysfunction means rigid protection, constricted processing, and blocked engagement. |
| **5. Present process is the main arena of change** | History matters, but the therapist works with how old expectations are confirmed or revised in current emotion and current interaction. |
| **6. Empirical orientation** | Johnson presents both attachment science and EFT as grounded in observation, pattern recognition, prediction, outcome research, and process research rather than charismatic technique alone. |

[Johnson, 2019, ch. 2, "EFT: Attachment-Driven Psychotherapy"].

### The therapeutic alliance as safe haven and secure base

Johnson's alliance is more specific than a generic warm bond. The therapist aims to be accessible, responsive, and emotionally engaged. The client is repeatedly given the experience that vulnerability can be brought into relationship without humiliation, abandonment, or coercive control. This safety allows emotion to be approached rather than avoided and makes it possible to take risks at the edge of the client's usual repertoire.

The therapist is therefore neither a neutral interpreter nor mainly a coach. Johnson describes a relationally present practitioner who tracks the client's nervous system, validates protective responses, offers soothing when necessary, and actively titrates risk. Empathy is not simply a preliminary condition that makes technique possible. The secure relational experience is itself part of the mechanism of change.

At the same time, Johnson does not reduce therapy to alliance. The alliance creates the conditions for specific experiential and interactional processes. It must be paired with a coherent map, active emotional processing, and the shaping of new encounters.

### Health, dysfunction, and protective organization

Johnson defines health through flexibility rather than through the absence of difficult emotion. Healthy functioning involves the capacity to recover balance, stay open to new information, revise working models, communicate need, and respond to the needs of others. Dysfunction emerges when strategies that once reduced danger become rigid. An avoidant client may maintain emotional control by suppressing need; an anxious client may amplify threat and demand reassurance. Both strategies can become self-reinforcing because they limit access to disconfirming relational information.

This produces one of Johnson's most consistent therapeutic moves: **validate the function, then expand the repertoire**. The therapist recognizes that a defensive strategy helped the person survive or preserve connection under earlier conditions. Change does not begin by declaring the strategy irrational. It begins by making its logic understandable and then helping the client discover that more flexible alternatives are now possible.

### The present as the place where the past is maintained or revised

Johnson acknowledges developmental history, trauma, and early attachment, but her intervention model is strongly present-focused. The important question is not only what happened long ago, but how the past is being re-enacted now through attentional bias, bodily response, emotion regulation, expectation, and interaction. Present encounters continually reconfirm or challenge working models. Therapy therefore tries to generate emotionally vivid new information in the present.

This is why Johnson privileges corrective emotional experience. A client may intellectually know that a partner is caring or that a therapist is nonjudgmental, yet remain unable to *take in* that information under attachment threat. Change requires an experience with enough emotional salience to be encoded at the level where the old model operates.

### Change events rather than generic improvement

Johnson's clinical research orientation directs attention toward identifiable **change events**: moments in which a cascade of shifts occurs in emotion, meaning, action, and relationship. Her interest is not merely whether a client reports feeling better after therapy, but what happens in the session when entrenched organization loosens and a new response becomes possible.

Such events involve the creation of safety, deeper emotional engagement, clearer articulation of attachment fear or need, a new interpersonal signal, a different response from the other, and integration of the encounter. The importance of these events is that they link intrapsychic and interpersonal change. A new feeling is not left private. It becomes a new action, invitation, boundary, or reach, and the response to that action supplies further emotional information.

### The nature of emotion in Johnson's model

Johnson treats emotion as an organizing process with several functions. Emotion:

1. **orients attention** toward what is relevant to survival, need, threat, and value;
2. **shapes meaning making**, giving thought a felt direction and binding information into coherent experience;
3. **motivates action**, preparing the organism to approach, flee, fight, hide, seek comfort, or explore;
4. **communicates with others**, rapidly organizing reciprocal response through facial expression, tone, posture, and other signals.

[Johnson, 2019, ch. 2, "The Nature of Emotion"].

This gives emotion a double role in therapy. It is a **target** because stuck forms of regulation must become more flexible. It is also an **agent** because new emotional experience reorganizes perception, meaning, action tendency, and relationship in ways that detached explanation often cannot.

Johnson does not conceptualize the therapeutic task as simply increasing emotional intensity. The aim is **balanced engagement**. Emotion can be too distant, too chaotic, too undifferentiated, or too overwhelming. The therapist helps clients move from abstract description or reactive surface emotion toward a level of engagement in which bodily feeling, meaning, and action tendency become coherent enough to use.

### Corrective emotional experience

A corrective emotional experience is central to Johnson's theory of change. The phrase refers to more than catharsis or the release of pent-up emotion. Correction occurs when a client enters an old emotional territory and experiences it in a new relational and regulatory context. Vulnerability is approached rather than avoided; the therapist or attachment figure remains available; the client discovers a new way of signalling or responding; and the encounter produces information that does not fit the old model.

A corrective emotional experience may therefore alter several levels at once:

- bodily expectation of threat;
- emotional regulation strategy;
- meaning assigned to the self;
- expectation of the other;
- action tendency;
- interpersonal pattern.

Johnson's insistence that change is both **within and between** is most visible here. The new experience is intrapsychic, but its power often comes from what happens in relationship.

---

## Chapter 3 - Intervention: Working with and Using Emotion to Construct Corrective Experiences and Interactions

### Emotion as target and agent of change

Chapter 3 turns the broad attachment model into a method of intervention. Johnson contrasts experiential therapy with approaches in which emotion is merely information to be managed, reduced, or cognitively reappraised. In EFT, emotion becomes the central working medium. Therapists track it, evoke it, deepen it, organize it, regulate it, and use it to generate new action and connection. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 3, "Emotion and Change in Experiential Therapy"].

This does not mean that all emotion should be intensified. Johnson's target is a more coherent, flexible and usable emotional process. Reactive anger may need to be slowed down so fear or hurt becomes accessible. Numbing may need to soften so bodily cues can be noticed. Chaotic affect may need to be assembled into recognizable components. The therapist continuously adjusts intensity so that engagement remains within a tolerable range.

### Attachment security and regulation capacity

Johnson links secure attachment to better regulation at every point in the emotional sequence. Secure people are less easily triggered, less likely to construe ambiguous events as catastrophic, less likely to remain trapped in hyperarousal or numbing, more able to explore what an emotion means, and more able to use it as information. They can communicate emotion more clearly and take in another person's response.

The clinical implication is that therapy should not aim only to teach solitary self-control. A major route to regulation is **co-regulation**. The client learns to remain with emotion in the presence of an attuned other, to send clearer signals, and to use contact as a resource. This process then becomes internalized as greater confidence and flexibility.

### Deepening emotional engagement

Johnson draws on experiential research showing that the depth of engagement with experience is associated with better outcomes. Clients at shallow levels talk about events from a distance, explain, generalize, or intellectualize. At deeper levels they can identify bodily experience, articulate nuanced emotion, connect feeling with meaning, and use the emerging experience to revise how they understand themselves and what they do next.

The therapist's task is therefore to move from generic labels such as "angry" or "anxious" toward an assembled experience. Johnson uses a simple affect map derived from Magda Arnold. The therapist explores:

- **trigger or cue**;
- **initial perception**;
- **bodily response**;
- **meaning creation**;
- **action tendency**.

The effect is to transform an emotion from a vague force that seems to simply happen into a coherent process that can be recognized and worked with. Naming does not eliminate emotion. It makes the emotion navigable.

### The EFT Tango: Johnson's metaframework for intervention

The core procedural contribution of Chapter 3 is the **EFT Tango**, a five-move sequence used across individual, couple, and family modalities. Johnson emphasizes that it is a flexible metaframework rather than a rigid script. A session may move back and forth between moves, and one move can occupy much of a session. The sequence nevertheless gives the therapist a reliable orientation when the clinical process becomes confusing.

| Tango move | Core therapist task | Intended change process |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Mirroring present process** | Track and reflect the client's current cycle of emotion regulation and, where relevant, the interactional cycle with others. Describe without blame. | The client begins to see the process rather than merely experience its consequences. Automatic inner and interpersonal patterns become observable. |
| **2. Affect assembly and deepening** | Piece together trigger, perception, bodily feeling, meaning, and action tendency; stay with emerging vulnerable emotion and deepen it safely. | Fragmented or reactive emotion becomes coherent, owned, and more differentiated. New layers of fear, sadness, shame, longing, or need may become available. |
| **3. Choreographing engaged encounters** | Turn newly accessed emotional experience into a structured message or encounter with the therapist, a partner, family member, imagined attachment figure, or part of self. | Inner change becomes interpersonal action. The client risks a different signal and opens the possibility of a different response. |
| **4. Processing the encounter** | Explore what it was like to send the new message, hear the response, and take it in. Work with blocks to receiving or responding. | New relational information is metabolized rather than dismissed. Working models can begin to update. |
| **5. Integrating and validating** | Summarize the change sequence, highlight courage and competence, reinforce positive emotion and new meanings. | The experience becomes coherent, memorable, and available as a building block for future action and a more competent sense of self. |

[Johnson, 2019, ch. 3, "The Heart of EFT Intervention: The Tango"].

### Move 1: Mirroring present process

The therapist first makes the process visible. This can be an internal cycle, such as numbing followed by rage followed by shame and withdrawal, or an interpersonal cycle, such as one person's protest leading to another's withdrawal, which escalates the protest further. Johnson's emphasis is on **process over content**. The question is not initially who is correct, what the final explanation is, or which party has the more legitimate complaint. The therapist tries to capture what happens next, how one move triggers another, and how the sequence acquires a life of its own.

This move is nonpathologizing because the problem is increasingly located in the constraining cycle rather than in a defective person. The client can begin to stand beside the therapist and look at the pattern instead of being wholly immersed in it.

### Move 2: Affect assembly and deepening

The second move identifies the emotional structure underneath the presenting reaction. Johnson notes that clients often arrive with either highly reactive states, especially anger, or with detached and intellectualized descriptions. The therapist slows the process down and assembles the emotional sequence piece by piece.

A central clinical skill is to work at the **leading edge** of experience: not so far ahead that the client feels invaded or interpreted, but far enough to help make implicit meaning explicit. The therapist may repeat a phrase, focus on a bodily cue, introduce an evocative image, or offer a tentative conjecture. Repetition serves not as drill but as a way of helping the nervous system remain with previously avoided material long enough for it to become familiar and tolerable.

### Move 3: Choreographing engaged encounters

Once new emotional experience becomes sufficiently clear, Johnson turns it into an interaction. In EFIT this may be an imagined conversation with an attachment figure, a direct disclosure to the therapist, or an encounter with a part of self. In EFCT and EFFT it is often an actual interaction between people in the room.

Johnson's logic is that experience changes most powerfully when the client does something different with another person. A private realization is distilled into a concrete attachment-relevant message and then enacted. The therapist helps the client stay emotionally engaged, prevents retreat into abstract explanation, and slices the risk thinner if the task becomes overwhelming.

### Move 4: Processing the encounter

The new interaction is then explored. The therapist asks what it was like to send the message, what happened in the body, what the other's response meant, and whether the client can actually receive that response. This last point is crucial. Insecurity often includes an inability to *take in* comfort or reassurance, even when it is offered. The therapist therefore works with blocks to receiving new information, not just with blocks to expressing vulnerability.

In couple and family work Johnson sometimes describes the therapist as needing to "catch the bullet": if one person responds to a vulnerable reach with dismissal, hostility, or retreat, the therapist interrupts the sequence, contains the negative response, and helps make the block understandable so the emerging corrective encounter is not immediately destroyed.

### Move 5: Integrating and validating

The fifth move consolidates the change. The therapist steps back with the client and reviews what just happened: the old pattern, the emotional discovery, the risk taken, the new encounter, and the different outcome. Johnson sees positive emotion here as clinically useful because relief, pride, warmth, hope, and connection broaden attention and reinforce exploration. The client leaves not only with a new insight but with evidence of agency: "I can face this feeling; I can send a clearer message; I can survive the risk; I can shape what happens next."

### Therapist stance: the relational platform of the Tango

Johnson describes the therapist as operating on several levels simultaneously. The therapist is emotionally present and uses his or her own responses to support empathy. The therapist actively monitors safety and repairs alliance ruptures. The therapist is a curious process consultant who explores rather than imposes a diagnosis. The therapist also maintains a metaperspective on stages, goals, and the structure of the change process.

The attachment ideal is A.R.E. in the therapy relationship itself: accessible, responsive, engaged. The therapist uses tone as an intervention. Johnson's shorthand **RISSSC** captures a recurring style: repeat; imagery; simple words; slow pace; soft voice; and the client's words. The principle is not aesthetic. When a client is near the edge of vulnerability, pace, prosody, and linguistic complexity directly affect whether the experience can be tolerated and integrated.

### General experiential and interactional techniques

The Tango organizes a set of more specific microinterventions. These include empathic reflection, validation, evocative questions, heightening, repetition, imagery, tentative interpretation at the leading edge, tracking interactional sequences, attachment-oriented reframing, and direct choreographing of interactions. Johnson treats these techniques as ingredients whose function depends on context rather than as isolated tricks.

Reframing is especially important. A hostile-looking move may be reframed as a desperate attempt to restore connection; a withdrawal may be reframed as protection against anticipated failure; an adolescent's defiance may be reframed as protest or despair. The purpose is not to excuse harmful behavior. It is to reveal the emotional logic that must be addressed if the pattern is to change.

### The core change mechanism across Chapters 1-3

The first three chapters yield a coherent clinical architecture:

**attachment threat -> protective regulation -> constricted meaning and action -> self-reinforcing interaction -> greater insecurity**

Therapy attempts to reverse this through:

**relational safety -> deeper and more coherent emotional engagement -> new attachment-relevant meaning -> new signal or action -> responsive encounter -> integration -> greater security and flexibility**

This architecture becomes the template for EFIT, EFCT, and EFFT in the chapters that follow.
# Part II - Emotionally Focused Individual Therapy

## Chapter 4 - Emotionally Focused Individual Therapy in the Attachment Frame: Expanding the Sense of Self

### The individual is always relationally situated

Johnson's EFIT begins from a premise that sharply differentiates her attachment model from therapies that conceptualize the client as a mostly self-contained mind. A coherent sense of self, emotional balance, and flexible agency develop within important relationships. The individual's inner world cannot be understood apart from the history and present organization of attachment bonds.

From this perspective, personality development involves at least three interacting processes: the establishment of habitual emotion-regulation strategies; the formation of affectively charged expectations and meaning frames about self and others; and the development of behavioral protocols for moving toward, away from, or against others under conditions of vulnerability. These processes are reciprocal. How a person regulates emotion affects how others respond; those responses become new evidence for the person's working models; those working models in turn shape future perception and action. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 4].

The desired endpoint of EFIT is therefore not merely symptom reduction. Johnson describes a person who is more emotionally balanced, more open and flexible, more able to reflect, more alive and engaged, more capable of learning from experience, and more able to use supportive relationships. Secure selfhood and secure connection are treated as two sides of the same developmental process.

### EFIT and attachment-oriented views of depression and anxiety

Johnson focuses the individual-therapy discussion primarily on depression and anxiety, grouped broadly as emotional disorders. She does not claim that attachment insecurity maps neatly onto particular diagnoses. Instead she emphasizes **multifinality**: similar attachment histories or strategies can contribute to different outcomes, depending on later relationships, current stress, temperament, trauma, loss, and other factors.

The attachment lens nevertheless changes how symptoms are understood. Responses that appear dysfunctional in the present may be disordered or overgeneralized versions of originally adaptive responses. Withdrawal can protect someone who once depended on an unpredictable or frightening attachment figure. Hypervigilance and anger can be functional if rejection is likely. Suppression can reduce exposure to pain. The problem arises when the response becomes global, inflexible, and unable to update in response to safer present conditions.

Johnson links depression to patterns of loss, helplessness, failure, isolation, self-criticism, and negative models of worth. She follows Bowlby in emphasizing themes of loneliness, being unwanted or unlovable, and helplessness. Depression is sustained by feedback between inner organization and interpersonal process: a negative model of self may produce withdrawal or reassurance seeking; the resulting interpersonal difficulties then intensify hopelessness and confirm the negative model.

Anxiety is organized around threat, unpredictability, vigilance, and attempts to avoid or control emotional activation. Johnson draws on transdiagnostic research to highlight four recurring processes: frequent and intense negative affect with low clarity or acceptance; threat-biased information processing and intolerance of uncertainty; avoidant and suppressive responses that paradoxically maintain arousal; and fear of the anxious response itself. What matters clinically is not simply whether fear exists but how the person relates to fear. A rigid struggle to eliminate anxiety can become one of the forces that maintains it.

Johnson finds common ground here with transdiagnostic approaches such as the Unified Protocol for emotional disorders. Both attend to threat, perceived uncontrollability, avoidance, emotional exposure, and new regulation strategies. She nevertheless argues that attachment offers the more complete explanatory framework because it places regulation inside a theory of relationship, development, and the human need for safe connection.

### Case formulation in EFIT

Johnson's case formulation is process based. The therapist does not begin by trying to fix the presenting symptom or by imposing a diagnostic explanation. The therapist joins the client as a **process consultant**, enters the painful experience with the client, and collaboratively discovers the patterns that organize it. Diagnosis and questionnaires may orient the clinician, but the assessment develops through the therapy itself. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 4, "Case Formulation with Emotional Disorders"].

The therapist pays attention not only to **what** the client reports but to **how** the report is organized. Relevant observations include:

- emotional intensity or flatness;
- bodily activation or dissociation;
- the degree of specificity and granularity in emotional language;
- coherence or fragmentation of narrative;
- how the client handles uncertainty and vulnerability;
- how the client responds to the therapist's interest, empathy, and availability;
- how the self and significant others are represented;
- whether the client turns toward, away from, or against others under stress.

This approach makes assessment continuous and relational. The therapy relationship itself supplies information. A client who shifts the topic whenever vulnerability arises is demonstrating a process in the room, not merely reporting a historical pattern.

Johnson describes the therapist's stance through the three D's: **discover, distill, disclose**. The therapist and client discover the relevant emotional and relational process; distill the core fear, need, meaning, or pattern; and then disclose or enact that new experience in a way that creates different relational information.

### Inclusion, limits, and safety

Johnson presents EFIT as particularly suited to depression, anxiety, trauma-related sequelae, relationship difficulties, and existential problems organized around connection, loss, worth, and vulnerability. She also names important clinical limits. Because the therapy is experiential and relatively focused, clients need enough stability to maintain a therapeutic alliance and engage with emotion. Severe psychosis, significant antisocial patterns, uncontrolled addiction, chronic severe depression, or high suicide risk may require other specialized treatments, medication, or coordinated multidisciplinary care. The therapist's task is not to force emotional intensity, but to work inside the client's window of tolerance and titrate risk.

Case formulation therefore includes specific attention to:

- cycles of hyperreactivity, numbing, or dissociation;
- somatic signs of dysregulation;
- blocks to coherent meaning making;
- blocks to adaptive action and agency;
- negative models of self, including unworthiness, failure, helplessness, or exclusion from human connection;
- negative models of others as dangerous, unreliable, unpredictable, rejecting, or abandoning;
- the interaction between these internal models and current relationship patterns.

A recurring clinical problem is loss of choice. When negative models dominate, the client may oscillate between hyper- and hypoarousal, react automatically to threat cues, and lose the reflective space needed to choose differently. Corrective emotional experience restores choice by making emotion and meaning more ordered and by opening new action possibilities.

### The three stages of EFIT

Johnson organizes EFIT into three stages. These same broad stages recur in couple and family work, though their implementation differs by modality.

#### Stage 1: Stabilization

Stabilization establishes sufficient emotional balance and relational safety for deeper work. The therapist collaborates with the client to formulate problems and goals in the context of developmental history, current relationships, and present process. The alliance is explicitly shaped as a safe haven and secure base. Ambivalence about closeness to the therapist is expected and worked with rather than treated as resistance in a pejorative sense.

The therapist then maps self-perpetuating patterns in two linked rings. The **inner ring** concerns emotional regulation: how the client turns emotion up, down, or off; how bodily cues are interpreted; and how meaning develops. The **outer ring** concerns interpersonal response: how the client turns toward, away from, or against others and what those responses evoke in return.

Emotion is made more granular. Vague, overwhelming, or disowned reactions are broken into trigger, perception, body response, meaning, and action tendency. Johnson uses the mnemonic spirit of an "E" process: evoke, engage, explore and expand, elucidate, and encounter emotion. Emerging emotional material is validated and, where appropriate, taken into imagined or direct encounters with significant others.

By the end of stabilization the client is expected to show greater emotional balance, greater openness to discovery, more ability to recognize patterns, more tolerance for the leading edge of experience, and more capacity to weave emotions, meanings, interactions, and symptoms into a coherent narrative. Hope and efficacy begin to replace helplessness.

#### Stage 2: Restructuring attachment

In restructuring, the therapist moves more deeply into core fears, longings, wounds, and disowned aspects of experience. The work becomes more existential and emotionally absorbing. The therapist may use stronger heightening, repetition, imagery, and tentative proxy-voice formulations, while continuing to titrate risk.

The client now encounters previously excluded experience more fully and takes greater relational risks. An imagined attachment figure may be confronted; an abandoned or despised part of self may be acknowledged; an old trauma may be revisited from a position of greater agency. The aim is not to relive pain for its own sake. The aim is to transform the organization of the experience so that vulnerability can be owned without collapse and a different response becomes possible.

Johnson uses the idea of **flow** to describe successful deep engagement. The client becomes absorbed in the experience, but not helplessly overwhelmed by it. Emotion, meaning, and action become integrated. Old narratives lose their absolute quality. The client can say, in effect, "This happened to me; this is how I learned to survive; but it does not define the limits of who I can now be."

At the end of restructuring, improvement is visible not only in symptom reduction but in a more secure, coherent, and compassionate sense of self; more direct ownership of attachment fear and need; increased ability to tolerate vulnerability; and more empowered interactions with others.

#### Stage 3: Consolidation

Consolidation translates therapeutic discoveries into everyday life. The client approaches practical decisions and relationships from revised working models and a greater ability to use emotion as information. Problems that were previously paralyzing often become easier to solve because the organizing emotional and relational constraints have shifted.

The therapist and client create a coherent account of the therapeutic journey: what the old pattern was, how it developed and maintained itself, what new emotional experiences altered it, what strengths the client demonstrated, and how the client will respond when old patterns return. The therapist helps the client mentally retain the secure-base function of the therapeutic relationship while preparing for separation from the therapist.

Relapse prevention in this model is not primarily a list of coping techniques. It is the consolidation of a new way of regulating, making meaning, relating, and recovering balance.

### The Tango in EFIT

The EFT Tango operates in EFIT with one important modification: the significant other is often not physically present. Move 3, choreographing engaged encounters, may therefore involve:

- a direct disclosure to the therapist;
- an imagined encounter with a partner, parent, sibling, or other attachment figure;
- an encounter with a vulnerable or critical part of self;
- occasionally, an actual attachment figure invited into a session.

The crucial principle remains the same. Newly assembled emotion is turned into a relationally meaningful act. The client does not merely understand fear, shame, sadness, or longing; the client learns to *do something different with it*.

### The Dave example: panic, rage, control, and disconfirming connection

Johnson's extended illustration of Dave shows how the attachment formulation transforms apparently disparate symptoms into a coherent process. Dave presents as chronically anxious, controlling, indecisive, rageful, and ashamed. He attempts to eliminate uncertainty through exhaustive checking and control. When certainty proves impossible, anxiety escalates toward panic. Anger briefly restores a sense of strength, but the aftermath produces shame, withdrawal, and self-attack. Similar dynamics organize his marriage: he monitors contact with his wife, reacts strongly to signs of disconnection, demands reassurance, and then experiences her withdrawal as proof of his inadequacy and danger.

The formulation is a feedback loop rather than a trait label. The more Dave tries to secure himself through control, the more he scans for danger; the more danger he finds, the more rigidly he controls; the more rigid he becomes, the more his wife withdraws; the more she withdraws, the more he experiences abandonment and failure.

In Tango Move 2, Johnson slows the sequence down until Dave can identify the fear and helplessness that precede rage. The body's panic, the thought that he will make a disastrous mistake, the impulse to escape, and the shame about being weak are assembled into one coherent emotional process. In Move 3, the vulnerability is turned into an imagined encounter with his wife. Instead of demanding reassurance through anger, Dave acknowledges uncertainty, fear of not being good enough, and terror of losing her. The imagined wife's comforting response is then processed and repeated until he can take it in. The result is not merely an insight that "anger covers fear." It is a new experience of vulnerability, reception, and emotional regulation that can compete with the old panic-control sequence. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 4, "The Tango Moves in EFIT"].

---

## Chapter 5 - EFIT in Action

### Why the Fern case matters

Chapter 5 provides a full clinical illustration of EFIT through Johnson's work with Fern, a 46-year-old woman referred because she was too emotionally overwhelmed to participate effectively in couple therapy. Fern presents with moderate scores on depression and anxiety measures, marked agitation, intense shame, intrusive recollection, inability to sleep, self-condemnation, and a long-standing hunger for affirmation and belonging.

Her central crisis concerns an affair that followed years of feeling emotionally excluded in her marriage. The affair partner then abruptly rejected her, and her own and her husband's families condemned her. Fern experiences the affair as a moral failure and evidence of stupidity. She tries to suppress the pain, judges herself for not being "over it," and withdraws from people who might judge her. Johnson's attachment formulation does not deny responsibility for the affair. It places the behavior inside a larger emotional and relational context: a history of striving for approval, chronic deprivation of validation, intense need to feel seen and special, a sudden experience of attention, then abandonment, shame, and social exclusion.

### Phase 1 of the work: making pain coherent and safe to share

In the early sessions Johnson prioritizes alliance, validation, and the first two Tango moves. Fern is given an experience of being understood rather than judged. Johnson repeatedly reframes her distress as understandable in the context of unmet needs, loss, exclusion, and a harsh internalized standard rather than as proof that Fern is defective.

This reframing creates a decisive shift in the relationship to the symptom. Fern has been treating pain itself as evidence of failure: she should no longer hurt, and the fact that she does is shameful. Johnson instead treats the pain as meaningful information. The emotional sequence becomes visible: longing for affirmation -> risky reach -> apparent connection -> betrayal and abandonment -> public condemnation -> shame -> self-attack -> isolation -> increased anxiety and depression.

The therapist's acceptance becomes a live disconfirming experience. Fern explicitly tests whether Johnson is judging her. Johnson uses the moment to help Fern notice that she can reveal shame and remain connected. The alliance is therefore already functioning as a corrective attachment encounter.

### Phase 2: from global self-condemnation to differentiated experience

Across subsequent sessions Fern begins to distinguish several emotional strands that had previously collapsed into one undifferentiated sense of being bad.
