Grief over the loss is distinguished from shame about the affair; hunger for validation is separated from moral worth; anger at deception is distinguished from anger at herself; fear of family rejection is differentiated from her own evolving judgment about her behavior.

Johnson's work repeatedly moves between the client's internal "judge," historical attachment figures, present family members, and the therapy relationship. The emotional logic of each position is explored. The aim is not to create a positive affirmation that overrides shame. It is to give Fern enough contact with pain, need, and self-compassion that the punitive organization no longer monopolizes her identity.

Imagined encounters are used to convert the new experience into action. Fern addresses important figures who have defined her as unacceptable and experiments with a different position: her pain matters; she can regret an action without condemning her entire self; other people's disapproval does not have unquestioned authority over her identity.

### Phase 3: selective vulnerability and realistic boundaries

One important feature of the case is that increased security does not require indiscriminate openness. Fern recognizes that one sister is unlikely to become emotionally accepting. Johnson helps her differentiate **security** from **exposure**. Fern can choose not to disclose vulnerable material to someone who is predictably judgmental while also refusing to internalize that person's judgment as the final definition of who she is.

This is clinically important. Attachment-based growth is not represented as universal reconciliation or the abandonment of boundaries. The client becomes more able to choose whom to trust, what risks to take, and how to maintain self-respect when another person cannot respond.

### The outcome in Johnson's presentation

By the final session Fern reports markedly less anxiety and depression, greater self-compassion, more ability to take in her husband's care, increased connection with her mother, less destabilization around her sister, and a willingness to return more fully to couple therapy. Her scores on the Beck Anxiety and Depression Scales fall dramatically. Johnson explicitly considers whether demand characteristics might have influenced the self-report scores, but concludes that the changes fit Fern's observed presentation and behavior across sessions.

The case illustrates several aspects of Johnson's change model at once:

- the therapist's secure-base function;
- normalization of attachment need without removing accountability;
- clarification of regulatory cycles;
- emotional deepening rather than advice giving;
- use of imagery and imagined encounters;
- revision of models of self and other;
- selective rather than indiscriminate relational risk;
- integration of symptom change with changes in relationship and identity.

### Case-process map: Fern

| Clinical level | Initial organization | Intervention | Emerging organization |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Trigger** | memories of affair, car dealerships, family judgment, husband's distance | slow exploration and validation | triggers become specific and tolerable rather than globally contaminating |
| **Emotion** | shame, agitation, grief, anger collapsed together | affect assembly and deepening | more differentiated grief, hurt, longing, anger, and self-compassion |
| **Protective strategy** | secrecy, self-punishment, emotional masking, avoidance of family | therapist provides safe disclosure and imaginal encounters | selective openness, clearer boundaries, less self-attack |
| **Model of self** | foolish, bad, condemned, undeserving | reframing and repeated validation of pain and need | flawed but worthy, entitled to care, able to forgive herself |
| **Model of other** | others judge, abandon, exclude | corrective alliance and selective relational testing | some others can respond; some cannot; discernment replaces global expectation |
| **Action** | hide, ruminate, withdraw, plead for approval | choreograph new messages and positions | return to couple work, reconnect selectively, speak with more agency |

### What Chapter 5 does not establish

The Fern case is a clinical illustration, not a controlled test of EFIT efficacy. Johnson's report shows how she formulates and intervenes, and the symptom scales provide outcome-relevant data for this client. They do not by themselves establish comparative superiority, mechanism specificity, or generalizability to all clients with similar presentations. The value of the case for this corpus is procedural: it makes the attachment-change model concrete.
# Part III - Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy

## Chapter 6 - Getting to Safe and Sound in Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy

### Romantic love as an attachment bond

Johnson's couple model begins with a clear definition of the relationship itself. Romantic partnership is not primarily a contract, a friendship with added sexuality, or a negotiation problem. It is an adult attachment bond. Partners function as major sources of comfort, protection, emotional regulation, and confidence in facing the world. When this bond is reliable, partners can use the relationship as a safe haven under threat and as a secure base for exploration. When responsiveness is uncertain, relationship conflict is amplified because the underlying issue is experienced as danger to a survival-relevant bond. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 6].

This formulation changes what counts as the core problem in relationship distress. Johnson looks beneath the explicit topic of the argument and asks whether the relationship has become emotionally inaccessible, unresponsive, or unsafe. Chronic fights about money, sex, parenting, chores, time, or family can become organized by an implicit question about the bond: can I reach you, do I matter, will you respond when I need you, and can I risk depending on you?

### Johnson's use of relationship research

Johnson links the attachment frame to findings in relationship science that emphasize process rather than the surface content of arguments. She highlights the predictive power of negative emotion, the toxicity of demand-withdraw and stonewalling patterns, the importance of mutual soothing, and the protective role of positive emotional connection. Attachment gives these findings a unifying explanation: unresponsiveness is not merely poor communication; it threatens the bond and activates separation distress.

Johnson also presents EFCT as a strongly researched couple treatment. She summarizes randomized trials, follow-up studies, direct treatment comparisons, process-of-change research, and applications to populations facing depression and trauma. She reports substantial rates of recovery from relationship distress and improvement among treated couples, and she cites evidence of shifts in attachment security and physiological threat response after therapy. In this corpus file these are retained as **Johnson's efficacy claims** from the 2019 book. They should not be read as an independently updated systematic review or as a ranking of all couple therapies.

### Distress as deprivation and negative cycle

Johnson's central formulation of couple distress is **emotional deprivation within a self-reinforcing cycle**. Each partner has a legitimate need for safe connection, but attachment alarm triggers protective moves that make connection less likely. One partner may protest, criticize, pursue, interrogate, or demand. The other may defend, shut down, retreat, rationalize, or stonewall. Each move becomes evidence for the other's fear.

The therapist therefore makes the cycle, rather than either partner, the immediate problem. A typical pattern can be represented as:

**perceived distance -> attachment alarm -> protest or criticism -> partner feels failure/attack -> withdrawal -> increased alarm -> more protest**

or:

**anticipated conflict -> deactivation/withdrawal -> partner experiences abandonment -> escalation -> greater threat -> deeper withdrawal**

The point is not that all couples fit one template. Johnson names several recurrent cycles, including **Find the Bad Guy**, **Criticize and Withdraw**, **Freeze and Flee**, and **Chaos and Ambivalence**. The names are shorthand for different organizations of pursuit, attack, retreat, paralysis, and contradictory approach-avoidance. The specific sequence must be discovered with the couple rather than imposed in advance.

### Assessment and the first sessions

Assessment in EFCT is ongoing and process oriented. Relationship-satisfaction measures, attachment questionnaires, and symptom scales may be used, but the heart of assessment is the observation of how partners regulate emotion and respond to each other in session.

The therapist attends to:

- each partner's explicit goal for therapy;
- the couple's recurring negative cycle;
- attachment triggers and raw spots;
- how each partner regulates fear, shame, sadness, anger, and helplessness;
- whether signals are clear, distorted, amplified, suppressed, or mixed;
- the ability to take in reassurance and care;
- injuries, betrayals, or losses that block trust;
- safety issues, including active violence or coercion;
- individual vulnerabilities such as trauma, depression, anxiety, or addiction;
- strengths, positive moments, and existing signs of connection.

The first task is to create a collaborative alliance with both partners. The therapist must be experienced as allied with the relationship rather than as a judge deciding who is correct. Johnson repeatedly redirects the conversation from content and blame toward the process of disconnection.

### Stage 1: Stabilization and de-escalation

In couple therapy, Johnson commonly calls Stage 1 **de-escalation**. The couple learns to see the cycle and the attachment alarm that drives it. Partners begin to recognize how their protective moves inadvertently create the very response they fear.

The therapist mirrors the dance in Tango Move 1 and reframes it in attachment terms. Criticism can be reframed as protest against disconnection; withdrawal can be reframed as a response to anticipated failure, attack, or helplessness. This does not erase accountability for harmful behavior. It makes the behavior intelligible enough that the couple can oppose the cycle rather than each other.

Emotion is then deepened gradually. Reactive anger, rational argument, or flat withdrawal are slowed down until softer attachment emotions become available. A blaming partner may discover fear of not mattering. A withdrawn partner may discover shame and fear of failing the other. As each begins to recognize the emotional logic of the other's position, the cycle loses some of its automatic power.

The goal of Stage 1 is not yet the full creation of secure bonding. It is enough that the couple can identify the negative process, reduce escalation, and begin to send less threatening signals. The relationship becomes sufficiently safe for deeper restructuring.

### Couple-specific interventions: changing channels, slicing risk, catching the bullet

Working with two distressed partners changes how the Tango is implemented. The therapist often needs to **change channels**: away from factual content toward process; away from labels toward concrete actions; away from global character judgments toward present emotion; and away from proving who is right toward the cycle that organizes both partners.

Johnson also uses the phrase **slice it thinner**. If a new attachment message is too risky, the therapist reduces the demand. A partner who cannot yet say "I need you" may be able to say, "It is hard for me to tell you what I need." The smaller risk preserves forward movement without flooding the client.

**Catch the bullet** refers to protecting an emerging vulnerable interaction from an automatic negative response. If one partner finally risks a new message and the other responds defensively, the therapist interrupts the old sequence and explores the receiver's block rather than allowing the vulnerable disclosure to be immediately punished. This is one of the practical ways the therapist maintains the secure-base conditions necessary for corrective experience.

### Stage 2: Restructuring attachment

Stage 2 is the heart of Johnson's couple-change model. Partners deepen access to attachment fear, shame, hurt, longing, and need, and then communicate these emotions in a way that permits a new response. The therapist intensifies Tango Moves 2 through 4: affect assembly and deepening, choreographing engaged encounters, and processing the encounter.

Johnson describes two broad kinds of change events. A more withdrawn partner may move toward **re-engagement**, becoming emotionally present and able to express need rather than retreat. A more blaming or pursuing partner may move toward **softening**, replacing accusation or coercive protest with a direct, vulnerable reach. In both cases the partner is moving from defensive protection to accessible attachment communication.

These events culminate in what Johnson calls **Hold Me Tight conversations** or bonding events. Both partners can tolerate and name vulnerable emotion; one reaches in a clear attachment-relevant way; the other responds with accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement; and both can take in the new interaction. The relationship becomes experientially redefined as a safer bond.

Johnson's model of change here is highly specific. It is not enough for partners to learn communication skills while the attachment system remains organized by danger. It is also not enough to produce insight into childhood patterns. The couple must enact a new relational event that changes emotional expectation in the present.

### Corrective interaction and working-model revision

The couple modality shows most clearly Johnson's distinction between **corrective emotional experience** and **corrective interaction**. A partner accesses a new emotion internally, but the model is updated when that emotion is expressed and receives a new response from the significant other.

The sequence may look like:

**old cue -> old fear -> deeper vulnerable emotion -> new direct message -> responsive partner -> relief/connection -> revised expectation**

Repeated bonding events gradually weaken the dominance of the old model. The partner who expected rejection learns that vulnerability can evoke care. The partner who expected inevitable failure learns that he or she can remain present and be useful rather than escape. The relationship itself becomes a stronger regulatory resource.

### Attachment injuries

Johnson gives special attention to attachment injuries: moments in which a partner was unavailable, rejecting, or betraying at a time of intense need, violating the assumption that the bond would provide safety. Such injuries may remain active even after the general negative cycle has de-escalated.

Healing requires more than deciding to move on. The injury is revisited in an attachment frame. The hurt partner articulates the emotional impact; the other partner hears and acknowledges the pain and takes responsibility for his or her role; the context of the original event is clarified without erasing the injury; the injured partner is supported to state the attachment meaning of what happened; and the other responds with remorse, care, and present accessibility. The goal is a new bonding event around the old wound.

### Stage 3: Consolidation

Consolidation stabilizes the new cycle. Partners identify how they now interrupt old patterns, how they reach and respond differently, and how the revised bond changes practical problem solving. The therapist helps the couple create a coherent narrative of change and prepare for predictable future triggers.

Pragmatic issues that once generated major escalation are often easier to address because the attachment alarm no longer dominates. Johnson's logic is that better problem solving often follows secure connection rather than creating it.

### Sexuality and caregiving

Johnson rejects the idea that attachment security necessarily dulls eroticism. In her account, secure connection can support sexual exploration because safety makes vulnerability, communication, play, and risk taking more tolerable. Avoidant organization, by contrast, may split sex from emotional intimacy and prioritize performance or sensation. Anxious organization may use sex primarily as reassurance of love and may heighten sensitivity to rejection.

Similarly, caregiving is part of attachment functioning. Partners alternate between careseeking and caregiving positions. Secure functioning requires both the capacity to signal need and the capacity to respond to another's vulnerability without becoming consumed by one's own alarm.

### Broader applications of the couple attachment model

Johnson extends the couple attachment frame into relationship education, health psychology, and work with diverse couples. She presents the Hold Me Tight educational program as a preventive translation of the same bonding science used in therapy. She also discusses applications to cardiac recovery, chronic illness, trauma, sexuality, same-sex relationships, and other contexts in which the quality of intimate support affects coping.

The broad proposition is consistent: intimate connection regulates physiology and emotion, and relationship interventions should target the quality of that connection directly rather than treating it as a secondary lifestyle variable.

---

## Chapter 7 - EFCT in Action

### Sarah and Galen: a difficult Stage 2 block

Chapter 7 presents a consultation with Sarah and Galen, a chronically distressed couple with a long history of severe conflict and significant trauma. By the time of the consultation, their earlier escalation has largely reduced. The problem is no longer simply de-escalation. Galen is beginning to reach, but Sarah cannot trust or take in his new signals. Her trauma history and decades of relational injury leave her organized around vigilance, independence, sarcasm, and rapid movement into protective attack or withdrawal.

Johnson conceptualizes this as a **block to receiving** rather than merely stubbornness. Sarah longs for connection but experiences dependence itself as dangerous. Galen, meanwhile, fears making another mistake and waits for signs that it is safe to approach. The couple therefore reaches a kind of truce without secure engagement: both are less openly combative, but neither knows how to move into closeness.

### The consultation process

Johnson first mirrors the paradox. Sarah no longer pursues but remains hurt that Galen does not come toward her. Galen waits for permission because he fears renewed conflict. Neither has what is wanted. Johnson validates Sarah's vigilance in light of earlier betrayal and abuse while also heightening the attachment meaning underneath it: she needs evidence that Galen will come toward her without forcing her to make the first vulnerable move.

The work then centers on whether Sarah can risk receiving his presence. Johnson repeatedly stays with the protective logic of the block rather than arguing that Galen has changed. The therapist cannot talk the client into security. The client has to experience enough safety to register a different relational event.

The case demonstrates several clinical principles:

- **De-escalation is not the endpoint.** A calmer relationship can remain emotionally distant.
- **Insecurity can block reception as well as expression.** A partner may be unable to believe or metabolize care.
- **Trauma narrows the window for relational risk.** The therapist must move slowly and titrate exposure to dependency.
- **New signals must be repeated and processed.** A single vulnerable reach does not automatically override decades of danger learning.
- **The therapist validates protection while inviting contact.** Sarah's independence and vigilance are treated as understandable survival strategies, not character pathology.

### Trauma and the attachment frame

Johnson explicitly notes that trauma work often requires additional caution. The therapeutic alliance may be more difficult to establish; emotional storms are more intense; substance use or violence may complicate treatment; setbacks are common; and risks have to be sliced thinner. Psychoeducation about trauma may be necessary, and safety concerns must be addressed directly.

Even with those qualifications, Johnson sees attachment-oriented couple work as uniquely positioned to address traumatic isolation. The corrective experience is not simply remembering the trauma differently. It is discovering that vulnerability can now occur in the presence of a loved other who remains engaged.

### What the case adds to the model

Sarah and Galen demonstrate that the central EFCT task is not generic conflict resolution. The key transition is from **protection against the partner** to **use of the partner as a resource**. The partner who was encoded as dangerous or unavailable must gradually become a viable safe haven. That shift occurs through lived interaction, not through reassurance alone.
# Part IV - Emotionally Focused Family Therapy

## Chapter 8 - Restoring Family Bonds in Emotionally Focused Family Therapy

### Family distress through an attachment-systemic lens

Johnson's family model integrates attachment and systems theory. The family is treated as a network of reciprocal emotional signals in which one person's regulation strategy alters the options available to everyone else. A child's distress cannot be understood only as an individual symptom if the child's bids for connection, the parents' own regulation difficulties, and the couple relationship are part of the process that maintains the problem. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 8].

The systems contribution is crucial. Causality is circular rather than linear. A withdrawn parent can intensify an adolescent's protest; protest can make the parent feel ineffective or attacked; the parent may then become more controlling or more distant; the adolescent becomes more dysregulated. The family settles into a self-maintaining pattern. A healthy system, by contrast, is open enough to update and adapt.

Johnson adds attachment content to this process map. The critical organizing variable in many distressed families is whether caregivers can function as a safe haven and secure base when a child or adolescent is vulnerable. The therapist therefore does not stop at altering hierarchy, rules, boundaries, or communication. The therapist works directly with the emotional signals that organize distance, threat, protest, and care.

### EFFT differs from couple EFT in the goal of the bond

Couple EFCT seeks mutual accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement between adult partners. EFFT is asymmetrical in an important respect: the therapist's primary goal is to help the parent become more accessible, responsive, protective, and emotionally available to the child. The child is then helped to risk reaching, receive the care, and use it to regulate emotion.

This means that EFFT does not simply reproduce couple intimacy inside the parent-child bond. Parents are expected to provide guidance, limits, and caretaking appropriate to the child's developmental position. They should seek their own adult emotional support primarily from other adults rather than reversing the caregiving hierarchy.

### Differences from other family-therapy models

Johnson identifies several differences between EFFT and other systemic or behavioral family approaches.

First, EFFT remains explicitly **systemic** but does not reduce change to skills training. Parenting strategies can be useful, but the therapist asks what emotional process determines whether those strategies can be delivered and received.

Second, the central target is not simply power, coalition, or structural organization. It is **distance and disconnection that interfere with caregiving**. The therapist tracks whether a parent can see the child's vulnerability and whether the child can trust and use the parent's care.

Third, EFFT uses flexible subsystem sessions. The whole family may begin and end treatment together, while intermediate sessions may involve parents alone, a child with one parent, both parents with the child, or siblings. The configuration serves the attachment task rather than a rigid rule about who must always attend.

Fourth, EFFT is intensely experiential. Emotion is evoked, distilled, deepened, and used to restructure the interaction. The therapist is interested in the experience of the people inside the family dance, not only in the visible pattern.

### Assessment in EFFT

Assessment widens to the family system while retaining the same attachment-regulation questions used elsewhere in Johnson's model. The therapist considers:

- the child's or adolescent's presenting symptoms and developmental position;
- which relationships are safest and which are experienced as dangerous or unavailable;
- the family's recurrent interactional cycles;
- parental emotion-regulation capacities;
- parental fears of failure, rejection, or loss of the child;
- marital or coparenting conflict that weakens the caregiving system;
- whether children are drawn into parental emotional roles;
- how limits and discipline are delivered and received;
- the family's ability to repair after rupture;
- existing strengths, affection, and caregiving motivation.

Johnson treats the parental urge to protect as a major therapeutic resource. Even parents who appear angry, rigid, or defeated are often terrified that they are failing their child. If that fear is regulated and reorganized, the protective system can become more accessible.

### The stages of EFFT

The broad sequence parallels the other EFT modalities.

**Stage 1: Stabilization and de-escalation.** The therapist builds alliance with family members, maps the cycle, reduces blame, and frames the problem in attachment terms. Parents are helped to understand the child's disruptive behavior as carrying vulnerability and attachment meaning rather than as proof of bad character. The therapist also helps parents recognize their own protective reactions.

**Stage 2: Restructuring attachment.** Parents deepen contact with their fear, grief, guilt, inadequacy, or anger and become more able to see the child's underlying vulnerability. The child is helped to move beneath defensive protest, shutdown, or defiance and express needs in a way the parent can respond to. The therapist choreographs direct encounters in which caregiving and reaching can occur differently.

**Stage 3: Consolidation.** The family integrates the new secure-base pattern into everyday parenting, conflict, rules, and transitions. The therapist helps members identify how they can recognize and repair old cycles when they return.

### The EFFT Tango

The same five Tango moves operate, but the attachment goal is parent-child security.

1. The therapist mirrors the family process without blame.
2. Emotion is assembled and deepened, especially parental fear and child vulnerability.
3. The therapist structures a new encounter, often a parent reaching toward a child or the child expressing a previously hidden need.
4. The response is processed, including any blocks to receiving care.
5. The therapist validates and integrates the new family experience.

Johnson's interventions frequently reframe apparently oppositional behavior as a protest against disconnection or as a dysregulated attempt to secure attention. Likewise, a parent's rigid control can be reframed as an expression of fear, helplessness, or a desperate wish to protect. The reframe is intended to lower threat so that a more functional signal becomes possible.

### Parenting from the bottom up

A distinctive feature of Johnson's family approach is that she does not primarily coach parenting from the top down. She aims first to create enough emotional balance and connection that more effective parenting can emerge. A limit set by a regulated, emotionally available parent is different from the same rule delivered through panic, contempt, or withdrawal. Similarly, a child who feels fundamentally unseen may experience even sensible guidance as rejection or domination.

The secure-base relationship therefore becomes the context in which discipline, problem solving, and development can work.

### The "dark triad" and its antidote

Johnson draws attention to a recurring triad associated with insecurity and dysfunction:

- difficulties regulating emotion;
- heightened vigilance for threat;
- low perceived responsiveness from others.

The attachment antidote is also triadic: accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement. In family terms, the child needs enough evidence that the caregiver can be reached, can understand the distress, and will remain emotionally present. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 8].

### Confidence and hope as developmental outcomes

Johnson repeatedly returns to resilience rather than obedience as the ultimate family outcome. Securely attached children are portrayed as more able to recover from stress, persist after failure, ask for help, explore, and move outward into the world. The parent-child bond is therefore not merely a source of comfort. It is a developmental platform for confidence and agency.

---

## Chapter 9 - Emotionally Focused Family Therapy in Action

### Josh and his family: the systemic attachment problem

Chapter 9 follows an 11-year-old, Josh, whose family is alarmed by severe aggression, suicidal threats, school failure, anxiety, and repeated crises. The family contains several interacting problems: Josh and his father Sam are trapped in coercive conflict; Josh is closer to his mother Emma but can overwhelm her with threats and dysregulation; Sam and Emma have a distressed pursue-withdraw marriage; the older sibling stays largely outside the family turmoil; Sam works long hours and also uses work to escape the emotional intensity at home.

Johnson's formulation deliberately avoids locating the entire problem in Josh. His behavior is serious and requires safety management, but it is also embedded in a family with low emotional security. Josh is highly sensitive and anxious, his parents struggle to regulate themselves and each other, and the parental team is weakened by marital disconnection. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 9].

### Working with the parents first

Before deepening father-son work, Johnson meets with Sam and Emma to map their couple cycle and its impact on parenting. Emma feels alone, overwhelmed, and panicked; Sam feels incompetent and retreats into work and rational direction. The more he withdraws, the more desperate and angry Emma becomes; the more she escalates, the more he wants to escape. This cycle undermines their capacity to act as a coordinated secure base for Josh.

Johnson helps them create brief daily periods for sharing the emotional burden of parenting rather than only solving problems. This is a typical example of her emphasis on co-regulation. The parents need to become resources for each other if they are to function as stronger, wiser caregivers for their child.

### Working with Josh and Sam

The father-son session demonstrates the move from behavioral conflict to attachment meaning. At the surface, Sam gives instructions and Josh refuses. Each experiences the other as unreasonable. Johnson maps the cycle but then moves beneath it. Sam reveals the pain and fear that he is losing his son; Josh encounters a father who is emotionally affected rather than merely controlling.

The pivotal intervention is a structured encounter in which Sam tells Josh that their relationship is precious and that being shut out hurts. Josh is surprised. His previous working model of his father has emphasized toughness, distance, work, and authority. The new message introduces a different father: one who cares, fears disconnection, and can reach emotionally.

Johnson monitors Josh's window of tolerance carefully. She uses play, humor, brief detours, and a lower emotional intensity when needed. This is important to the model: emotional depth is not synonymous with relentless intensity. Effective experiential work depends on the client's ability to remain engaged rather than flooded.

### Working with Sam and Emma as parents

In a later session Johnson returns to the couple relationship but keeps the focus on parenting. Both parents recognize that they feel useless, frightened, and alone. Sam uses schedules, tasks, work, and withdrawal to manage helplessness. Emma uses escalating anger and pursuit. Each sees the other's surface defense and misses the fear underneath.

When Sam can name his panic and inadequacy rather than defend or disappear, Emma can respond with less anger. When Emma can describe feeling deserted and overwhelmed rather than only attacking, Sam can hear her need for partnership. The couple develops a shared language for moments when the family emotional system is becoming overwhelmed and experiments with standing beside each other rather than blaming.

Johnson explicitly notes that she is not primarily giving parenting techniques. She is shaping a **corrective emotional experience of parenting support** so that the couple can regulate each other and then offer their son a steadier secure base.

### Systemic outcome in Johnson's account

At follow-up, the family reports fewer crises, improved school functioning for Josh, greater parental cooperation, and more connection between father and son. Johnson also recommends couple therapy because the marital attachment system remains relevant to the family's longer-term stability.

The case illustrates how change at one level can propagate through the system. Sam's greater accessibility changes Josh's experience of him. Improved coparental connection changes Emma's regulation. Lower parental panic changes the emotional climate in which Josh's behavior is interpreted. The family therefore becomes less organized around danger.

---

# Part V - Attachment Science and the Wider Psychotherapy Model

## Chapter 10 - The Promise of Attachment Science

### Johnson's integrating thesis

Johnson's postscript returns to the argument with which the book began. Psychotherapy needs an integrated picture of the person. Attachment science, joined with experiential intervention, provides such a picture because it links biology, emotion, development, interpersonal connection, working models, and systems of interaction.

Her most general claim is that the **between** must always be part of therapy. The self and the relational system continuously define one another. Treating one while excluding the other distorts the problem and limits the available routes to change. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 10].

### Uniting science and practice

Johnson identifies several priorities that follow from an attachment-experiential integration.

**Psychotherapy is relational.** The therapist is not simply a provider of techniques. The therapeutic relationship can function as a safe haven and secure base in which vulnerability becomes manageable and new exploration becomes possible.

**Emotion is central.** Attachment is a theory of affect regulation as well as a theory of relationship. Emotion must be identified, organized, deepened, and used as a motivator of new action rather than treated only as a symptom to suppress.

**Inner and interpersonal processes are inseparable.** The self is expressed in interaction; interaction feeds back into self. Lasting change therefore involves both.

**Therapy touches existential realities.** Isolation, vulnerability, loss, mortality, belonging, and meaning naturally emerge when deeper attachment fear and longing are engaged. Johnson regards emotional isolation as especially traumatic because the attachment system makes connection a primary resource for facing threat.

**Therapy should target constriction rather than defect.** Stuck responses usually had protective value. The therapist validates their original logic while opening a wider range of possibilities.

**The model should remain empirically accountable.** Attachment science and EFT are presented as attempts to observe process, identify patterns, test predictions, and refine explanations rather than as closed doctrinal systems.

### The practitioner: presence rather than reductionism

Johnson warns against an increasingly mechanical psychotherapy in which practitioners deliver coping tips, symptom-management packages, or decontextualized techniques. She does not deny that skills and digital or pharmacological interventions can have value. Her argument is that they cannot substitute for an attuned human encounter when the problem involves shame, loss, fear, attachment insecurity, or entrenched relational organization.

The attachment therapist's self is therefore part of the clinical instrument. Genuine empathy requires enough self-awareness that the therapist can notice personal reactivity without becoming controlled by it. Training and supervision should provide therapists with their own secure-base contexts in which presence, emotional awareness, and attunement can develop.

### Honoring emotion

Johnson also criticizes approaches that treat emotion mainly as something to regulate from above through reappraisal, suppression, detachment, or habituation. Her complaint is not that these processes never help. It is that they underuse the organizing and motivational functions of emotion and neglect co-regulation.

Her alternative is an active journey **into and through** emotion. The therapist helps the client discover what the emotion is signalling, how it is being assembled, what action it prepares, how it is communicated, and how relationship can transform its regulation. Emotional experience becomes a pathway into new meaning and new connection.

### The value of relationship

Johnson closes with an explicitly value-laden position: connection is not incidental to human life. Attachment science, in her reading, gives empirical grounding to the proposition that relationships are central sources of meaning, resilience, and development. She extends this beyond the consulting room, arguing that greater social isolation, weak communal bonds, and the replacement of embodied relationship with increasingly impersonal forms of contact are public-health and cultural concerns.

This is the broadest claim in the book and should be kept distinct from the narrower clinical evidence. Johnson is moving here from research and clinical theory into a normative vision of psychotherapy and society. The value she wants the field to cultivate is humane, responsive connection.

---

## Appendix 1 - Measuring Attachment

Johnson's measurement appendix begins with an important clinical warning: attachment responses exist on continua and should not be used as rigid identity labels. Observation of process in session is often more clinically useful than assigning a person a fixed style.

She illustrates observational assessment through a distressed couple. Indicators of anxious organization include highly chaotic affect under threat, catastrophic interpretation, confused or escalating bids for reassurance, inability to take in a partner's comfort, and continued hypervigilance after immediate conflict has subsided. Movement toward security is visible when the same person can experience anger without losing coherence, articulate fear more directly, use support, send clearer signals, accept reassurance, and recover emotional balance more quickly.

Johnson then presents two formal self-report approaches: the **Relationship Scales Questionnaire (RSQ)**, which yields secure, preoccupied/anxious, dismissing-avoidant, and fearful-avoidant style scores; and the **Experiences in Close Relationships Scale-Revised (ECR-R)**, which assesses attachment anxiety and avoidance dimensionally. She notes that general orientation and relationship-specific attachment can differ.

The main Laboratory implication is methodological: measurement instruments operationalize different aspects of attachment and should not be treated as interchangeable with clinical observation, interview-based narrative assessment, or behavioral coding.

---

## Appendix 2 - General Factors and Principles in Therapy

Johnson acknowledges client factors, therapist factors, alliance variables, and general technique factors, but rejects the conclusion that these make specific models irrelevant.

Client characteristics affect treatment. Avoidant attachment may make alliance formation more difficult; severity, chronicity, social support, relationship quality, and comorbidity also matter. Johnson uses these findings to support adaptation of pace and intensity rather than abandonment of a coherent model.

The therapeutic alliance is necessary but not sufficient. Johnson notes that alliance-outcome associations are meaningful but limited and that the nature of alliance varies by treatment. In EFT, the relevant alliance is itself attachment-shaped: the client needs to feel that the therapist is emotionally present and that the therapeutic tasks are directly relevant to the client's experience. She draws attention to research suggesting that the **task** component of alliance can be particularly predictive, which she interprets as the client's felt sense that the therapist is on target.

Johnson also argues that named techniques are difficult to isolate from the relational and experiential processes in which they are embedded. Mindfulness, for example, can mean different things in different models. In an EFT-consistent form, present-centered nonjudgmental attention resembles longstanding experiential practices and helps the client observe experience as an unfolding process rather than as an immutable fact.

The overall conclusion is that common factors matter, but they do not provide enough directional specificity for therapy. A theory of the person and a model of how change occurs are still needed.

---

## Appendix 3 - Emotionally Focused Individual Therapy and Other Empirically Tested Models That Include the Attachment Perspective

Johnson compares EFIT with other individual therapies that incorporate attachment ideas, especially interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT) and process-experiential/emotion-focused therapy (PE/EF).

She views IPT as having important interpersonal and attachment-compatible elements but as using attachment more as a background than as a fully active map of affect regulation and change. In her reading, IPT pays less attention to deepening emotional experience and using newly processed emotion as a route into new action.

The comparison with PE/EF is more consequential because of the potential terminology confusion. Johnson recognizes strong similarities: both approaches are humanistic-experiential, collaborative, client centered, emotionally engaged, and interested in how immediate experience is constructed. Both use reflection and evocative questioning and both seek greater integration and agency.

She nevertheless draws several boundaries. EFIT is more thoroughly organized by attachment science, more systemic and relational, more explicitly concerned with constructive dependency and co-regulation, and less centered on task-specific markers such as chair-work sequences. Johnson does not adopt PE/EF's language of maladaptive emotion or emotion schemes. She prefers attachment working models and focuses on problematic **regulation and organization of emotion** rather than classifying an emotion itself as maladaptive. She also does not frame the core mechanism as changing one emotion with another. Her preferred sequence is assembling, deepening, distilling, disclosing, co-regulating, and enacting new attachment-relevant responses.

This appendix is the strongest source in the book for distinguishing Johnson's 2019 model from Greenberg's separate Emotion-Focused Therapy lineage.
# Part VI - Cross-Cutting Conceptual Architecture

The chapter sequence above preserves Johnson's own movement from attachment science to a general model of therapeutic change and then into individual, couple, and family applications. This final part reorganizes that material for retrieval. It is not a second reconstruction of the book. Its purpose is to make the recurring architecture of Johnson's 2019 model explicit while keeping theory, evidence, clinical inference, procedure, and case illustration distinct.

## Attachment as a Theory of Personality and Development

Johnson treats attachment as more than a theory of childhood bonds or adult relationship style. She presents it as a developmental theory of personality that explains how repeated experiences of availability, responsiveness, rejection, unpredictability, or disconnection organize expectations about self and others, the regulation of emotion, attention under threat, and characteristic ways of seeking or avoiding support. [Johnson, 2019, chs. 1-2].

The person is therefore not conceptualized as a sealed unit. The self develops and operates within relational systems. A felt sense that significant others are available provides a safe haven in distress and a secure base for exploration. In Johnson's formulation, dependence and autonomy are not opposites. Reliable connection supports exploration, confidence, and flexible action. She uses the language of **constructive dependency** to oppose the assumption that maturity means self-sufficiency or freedom from attachment needs.

Working models provide the continuity in this system. They contain emotionally charged expectations about whether others can be counted on and whether the self is worthy of care, together with perceptual biases, memories, beliefs, and procedural response tendencies. They are not static internal objects. They are maintained, confirmed, challenged, and revised through ongoing interactions. Johnson accordingly sees personality as relatively patterned but open to reorganization when new attachment-relevant experience is sufficiently vivid, repeated, and integrated.

This developmental account leads directly to her clinical stance. Therapy is not primarily a project of correcting irrational ideas in isolation or teaching the client to become independently self-regulating. It is a process of expanding emotional flexibility and relational security so that the client can engage experience more openly, reach for help more directly, receive care, and act with greater coherence and agency.

## Attachment as an Affect-Regulation System

Affect regulation is central rather than secondary in Johnson's use of attachment theory. Security is associated with the capacity to notice distress, remain engaged with it, recover emotional equilibrium, and use emotion as information about needs, threats, and goals. The secure person does not have to eliminate vulnerability in order to function. Distress can be tolerated, symbolized, communicated, and, where appropriate, shared with another person. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 1].

Insecurity alters this process. Under attachment threat, a person may intensify distress and proximity seeking, or suppress need and deactivate attachment signals. Johnson presents these as organized protective strategies rather than random symptoms. Anxious strategies tend toward hyperactivation: heightened monitoring of threat, urgent bids for reassurance, catastrophic meaning, difficulty settling, and difficulty taking in reassurance. Avoidant strategies tend toward deactivation: suppression or dismissal of vulnerability, compulsive self-reliance, distancing, and reduced openness to support. These descriptions are dimensional and contextual, not rigid identities.

Johnson's preferred image of effective regulation is movement **with and through** emotion. The goal is neither uncontrolled discharge nor top-down suppression. Regulation involves enough safety to approach an emotion, distinguish its components, understand its action tendency and attachment meaning, and allow it to guide adaptive action. Co-regulation is therefore not a fallback for people who have failed to self-regulate. It is a normal part of human functioning and a core mechanism through which emotional balance is restored.

A compact representation of Johnson's distress cycle is:

**attachment threat -> dysregulation -> protective strategy -> constricted signal or action -> reciprocal response from others -> confirmation of threat and working model**

The corresponding therapeutic change cycle is:

**secure therapeutic connection -> deeper emotional engagement -> affect assembly and meaning -> clearer vulnerable signal or need -> new action or enactment -> responsive encounter -> processing and integration -> increased security and flexibility**

These sequences are Laboratory summaries of Johnson's recurring model, not quotations or independent causal claims.

## Emotional Connection and Co-Regulation

Johnson repeatedly positions emotional connection as a regulatory resource. The presence, mental representation, or responsiveness of an attachment figure can reduce perceived threat and make difficult experience more manageable. This claim underlies her emphasis on the therapist as an active attachment-relevant presence and on significant others as potential participants in change.

Co-regulation means that affective balance can be supported through responsive contact. In couple work, a partner can become a source of comfort rather than danger. In family work, a parent can become more available as a safe haven and secure base. In individual therapy, the therapist can provide a relationship in which previously disowned or overwhelming experience can be approached without the client being left alone with it.

Johnson does not equate connection with agreement, reassurance, or the removal of conflict. Secure connection is compatible with difference. Its regulatory value comes from confidence that vulnerability can be expressed and met with enough accessibility and responsiveness that the person does not have to escalate, shut down, or organize around anticipated abandonment.

## Accessibility, Responsiveness, and Engagement

Johnson condenses the qualities of secure attachment into **A.R.E.**:

- **Accessibility**: Can I reach you? Are you emotionally available when I need you?
- **Responsiveness**: Will you notice and respond to my signals in a way that fits my need?
- **Engagement**: Are you emotionally present and involved with me rather than merely physically nearby or technically helpful?

A.R.E. functions across the book at several levels. It describes secure intimate bonds; it helps the therapist identify where relationships fail under threat; and it specifies the quality of the therapeutic alliance Johnson wants to create. The model is therefore more specific than a generic recommendation to be empathic. The therapist's emotional presence is part of the intervention because safety changes what clients can attend to, feel, disclose, and risk.

A.R.E. also helps clarify why reassurance sometimes fails. If a distressed person cannot register the other as accessible or responsive, the literal content of reassurance may not be taken in. This is central in the Sarah and Galen consultation, where the problem is not simply a lack of supportive behavior from the partner but Sarah's difficulty trusting and receiving that support in the context of trauma and established insecurity. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 7].

## Security, Insecurity, and Protective Strategies

Johnson's model is organized around **strategies**, not personality defects. A protective response usually had an intelligible function: reducing exposure to rejection, forcing proximity when connection seemed uncertain, preventing humiliation, controlling overwhelming arousal, or avoiding feelings associated with helplessness. The clinical problem arises when the strategy becomes rigid, overgeneralized, or self-confirming.

This perspective supports a non-shaming form of accountability. Reframing a harsh attack as attachment protest does not make the attack harmless. Reframing withdrawal as protection from anticipated failure does not make withdrawal relationally neutral. The attachment formulation asks what threat gives the strategy its urgency and how the strategy, once enacted, shapes the other person's response and thereby perpetuates the very insecurity it attempts to manage.

Security is consequently defined less by calmness than by flexibility. A secure person can move toward and away from others as circumstances require, tolerate dependency without collapse, act autonomously without cutting off connection, remain emotionally engaged during distress, and revise expectations when new evidence appears. Therapy aims to increase this repertoire rather than install a single preferred behavior.

## Emotion in Therapeutic Change

Johnson assigns emotion four linked clinical functions. Emotion orients attention; organizes meaning; prepares action; and communicates motivational information to other people. For these reasons it is both an object of therapeutic exploration and an active mechanism of change. [Johnson, 2019, chs. 2-3].

Her intervention model works from immediate experience rather than from abstract explanation alone. The therapist helps identify the trigger, the first appraisal or perception, bodily activation, the meaning being made, and the action tendency that follows. This **affect assembly** turns a vague global state into a more differentiated process. The work then deepens toward the attachment significance of the experience and toward what the person is trying to protect, obtain, or avoid.

Emotion becomes especially transformative when the new experience is enacted. The client may say something previously unsayable, risk reaching, set a boundary, ask directly, or take in another person's response. The emotional event then needs to be processed and integrated so that it is not merely a dramatic moment but a new reference point for future perception and action.

Johnson's use of emotion should not be reduced to catharsis. Heightening serves precision and engagement, not discharge for its own sake. Nor does she treat all intense emotion as inherently authentic. The therapist tracks how emotion is assembled, how it regulates attachment threat, and what happens when a client becomes more able to stay with experience without either escalation or avoidance.

## Therapeutic Alliance and Therapist Stance

The therapeutic alliance is a change context, not a neutral delivery system for techniques. Johnson describes the therapist as a temporary or surrogate attachment figure who offers a safe haven and secure base. This does not mean creating dependence on the therapist as an end state. It means establishing enough relational safety for exploration, emotional risk, and new action.

The stance is active, engaged, and process oriented. The therapist tracks moment-to-moment experience and interaction, validates the logic of protective responses, reflects emotion, asks evocative questions, heightens key moments, uses imagery where useful, and invites new encounters. Johnson's **RISSSC** shorthand captures the tone she recommends near vulnerable experience: repeat key words; use imagery; keep language simple; slow the pace; soften the voice; and stay close to the client's own words. [Johnson, 2019, ch. 3].

Alliance is continuously monitored. Avoidant clients may experience emotional focus or dependency language as intrusive; anxious clients may fear that the therapist will not stay emotionally available; trauma survivors may need smaller increments of risk. Johnson therefore treats pacing and titration as model-consistent clinical judgment rather than as deviations from protocol.

## Corrective Emotional Experience

A corrective emotional experience is a new event with enough emotional salience to challenge an old organization of self, other, and relationship. It is not simply a new thought. The client encounters an experience that conflicts with the old expectation and is able to remain engaged long enough for the contradiction to matter.

In EFIT, such an event may occur in the relationship with the therapist or through an evocative/imaginal encounter that reorganizes how the client relates to vulnerability, self-criticism, or significant others. In EFCT, the experience often involves a partner becoming accessible and responsive to a vulnerable reach. In EFFT, a parent and child can encounter one another in a way that changes the emotional meaning of caregiving and need.

The term **corrective** should not imply that one dramatic session permanently erases earlier learning. Johnson's cases show a process of preparation, risk, enactment, processing, repetition, and consolidation. The event gains developmental significance as it becomes part of a revised working model and a more available response repertoire.

## Corrective Interaction

Johnson's model is not complete when change remains intrapsychic. New experience is often most powerful when it changes an actual relational sequence. A corrective interaction therefore includes both a transformed signal from one person and a transformed response from another.

The logic is circular. A person who previously attacked may disclose fear more directly; a partner who previously withdrew may remain present and respond; the first person then has an opportunity to take in that response rather than continue escalating. The sequence creates evidence that a different relational future is possible. In family therapy, a parent who previously corrected or controlled may instead reveal concern and become emotionally available; a child may then risk showing fear or need rather than defiance.

This is why enactment is central in Johnson's couple and family work. Talking about a relationship is not always equivalent to changing the relationship. The therapist helps create a new exchange in the room and then slows down to process what each participant experienced and what the event means.

## EFIT, EFCT, and EFFT Compared

| Dimension | EFIT | EFCT | EFFT |
|---|---|---|---|
| Primary therapeutic field | Client-therapist relationship plus the client's internalized and current attachment relationships | Reciprocal adult intimate bond | Parent-child and wider family attachment system |
| Core clinical focus | Constricted self-organization, dysregulated affect, negative models of self/other, blocked engagement | Insecure bond and self-reinforcing negative interaction cycle | Disrupted caregiving/care-seeking and family cycles that maintain disconnection |
| Relational asymmetry | Therapist carries clinical responsibility; other attachment figures may be imaginal or external | Partners are adult attachment figures in a substantially mutual bond | Parent-child relationship is developmentally asymmetric; parent is asked to provide secure-base/safe-haven caregiving |
| Stage 1 emphasis | Stabilize, build alliance, map inner/relational cycles, expand emotional granularity | De-escalate negative cycle and clarify its attachment logic | Stabilize family system, identify cycles, strengthen parental accessibility and coordinated caregiving |
| Stage 2 emphasis | Deepen vulnerability, create new self/other encounters, transform habitual responses | Restructure interaction through withdrawer re-engagement, softening, reaching, and responsive bonding events | Restructure care-seeking/caregiving exchanges and create new parent-child engagement |
| Stage 3 emphasis | Consolidate new narrative, choices, relationships, and agency | Consolidate secure connection and new problem-solving capacity | Consolidate new family patterns and secure-base functions |
| Typical Move 3 encounter | Client with therapist, imagined other, or aspects of self depending on case | Partner-to-partner enactment | Parent-child or relevant family enactment |
| Desired change | Greater emotional flexibility, coherent sense of self, capacity to reach and receive | More secure bond, flexible responsiveness, constructive dependency | More secure caregiving/care reception and a family climate that supports development |

The modalities share the same attachment map, experiential method, three-stage arc, and EFT Tango. What differs is where attachment organization is most directly enacted and who is available to participate in corrective interaction. [Johnson, 2019, chs. 4-9].

## Clinical Change Processes Across Modalities

Across EFIT, EFCT, and EFFT, Johnson's clinical process can be summarized in seven linked movements:

1. **Create sufficient safety.** Establish an A.R.E. alliance and regulate the intensity of the work so that experience can be approached rather than avoided or overwhelmed.

# Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy: Cycles, Bonding, and Repair

## Source and Edition Note

This file reconstructs Susan M. Johnson's attachment-oriented model of couple therapy from two Project sources. The procedural spine is the second edition of *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy: Creating Connection*, published by Brunner-Routledge in 2004. Its fourteen chapters move from the field and attachment philosophy, through theory of change and core interventions, into assessment, de-escalation, Stage 2 restructuring, bonding events, consolidation, clinical difficulties, family applications, attachment injuries, and two extended couple-session illustrations. The printed chapter starts are pp. 1, 23, 41, 53, 113, 131, 147, 171, 187, 201, 243, 267, 275, and 297 respectively.

The later companion source is Johnson's *Attachment Theory in Practice: Emotionally Focused Therapy (EFT) with Individuals, Couples, and Families*, published by The Guilford Press in 2019. That book presents attachment science as a general clinical map and extends the model across individual, couple, and family modalities. It introduces and names the five-move **EFT Tango** as a transdiagnostic intervention metaframework. The Tango is therefore a **later formulation**. It should not be retroactively treated as a named construct in the 2004 couple manual, although many of its component moves are clearly continuous with the older manual's practice.

Johnson normally uses the abbreviation **EFT**. In this Laboratory, **EFCT** is used when necessary to make clear that the subject is Johnson's attachment-oriented couple model. **Emotion-Focused Therapy** refers to Leslie Greenberg's separate, mature emotion-process model. Their histories overlap, but their mature organizing theories, intervention maps, and technical vocabularies must not be conflated.

The supporting attachment comparison uses the Project's 2007 first edition of Mikulincer and Shaver's *Attachment in Adulthood*. It is a broad research synthesis of adult attachment, not an EFCT manual and not the final word on the post-2007 evidence base. The supporting Emotion-Focused Therapy comparison uses Greenberg's 2015 second edition. Jungian and post-Jungian sources are used only to control conceptual boundaries and support later comparison. They do not reinterpret Johnson from inside a Jungian framework.

### Extraction and provenance note

The Johnson PDFs were text-extractable and generally clean, but ordinary PDF artefacts remain, including line-break hyphenation, page headers, and occasional character noise. Chapter and printed-page locations were checked against the contents pages and extracted text. This reconstruction paraphrases the sources rather than reproducing long passages. Short source terms and chapter titles are retained when necessary for technical precision. No paragraph or page number has been invented. The later Jungian couples-therapy source in the Project is the 2026 Routledge edition by Elizabeth Eowyn Nelson and Anthony Delmedico, despite an earlier planning reference to 2025; this file uses the source's actual publication data where that comparison appears.

## Coverage

This source is designed to answer, on Johnson's terms, four linked questions:

1. What makes an intimate relationship function as an attachment bond?
2. How does attachment threat become organized into repetitive negative couple cycles?
3. What does the EFCT therapist do, in sequence, to transform those cycles?
4. What counts as a meaningful therapeutic change in EFCT: de-escalation, re-engagement, softening, bonding, repair, and consolidation?

The file therefore gives priority to adult attachment as used clinically in EFCT; accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement; negative interaction cycles; pursue-withdraw and related patterns; primary vulnerability and reactive emotion; attachment protest; withdrawal and disengagement; de-escalation; restructuring interaction; enactments; withdrawer re-engagement; pursuer or blamer softening; bonding events; attachment injuries; forgiveness and repair; therapist stance; and the creation of a more secure bond.

It does **not** treat every couple problem as an attachment problem, does not diagnose partners by cycle position, does not turn EFCT into Jungian theory, and does not infer that the source manuals alone establish comparative treatment superiority.

## How to Use This File

For questions about Johnson's model, start with Parts I-VII and the chapter-by-chapter reconstruction. For questions comparing frameworks, use Part VIII. For applied reflection, use Part IX only after reading the guardrails in Part X. The applied sections are explicitly marked as Laboratory synthesis and are not presented as Johnson's own protocol.

When this file and another source disagree about what Johnson said, the Johnson source controls. When a claim concerns empirical adult attachment rather than EFCT procedure, route it to Mikulincer and Shaver or later attachment evidence. When a claim concerns Jungian concepts, route it to Jung or the relevant post-Jungian author. When a claim concerns Greenberg's emotion taxonomy or marker-guided process, route it to Greenberg.

---

# Part I. EFCT's Core Clinical Model

## 1. The clinical problem EFCT is trying to solve

[EFCT]

Johnson's starting point is not that distressed couples lack communication techniques in the abstract. Nor is the primary problem framed as irrational beliefs, poor negotiation, deficient insight, or a deficit of conflict-management skills. The central problem is that partners who matter profoundly to one another become caught in interactions that repeatedly signal **inaccessibility, unresponsiveness, danger, rejection, abandonment, failure, or disconnection**. The more important the relationship, the more emotionally consequential these signals become.

In this model, distress is maintained by a reciprocal loop between **inner emotional experience** and **interpersonal organization**. A partner's perception of threat activates bodily arousal, meanings about self and other, and action tendencies. Those responses appear in the interaction as criticism, pursuit, demand, defense, withdrawal, silence, distancing, attack, appeasement, or chaotic approach-avoidance. The other partner then reacts to those moves through his or her own attachment sensitivities. Each response becomes the next person's trigger. A self-reinforcing dance develops.

This is why Johnson repeatedly works on two levels at once. She enters the individual's emotional world while also tracking the couple's sequence. The 2004 manual describes this as integrating the experiential and systemic traditions: the therapist uses a close-focus lens for internal emotional experience and a wide-angle lens for the interactional system. Neither alone is enough. Emotional change that never enters the relationship may leave the couple's dance intact; restructuring behavior without changing the emotional signals may produce brittle or short-lived compliance.

The therapeutic target is therefore a more secure form of connection in which partners can make themselves emotionally available, respond to one another's attachment signals, and remain engaged when vulnerability rises. The change is not simply from "bad communication" to "good communication." It is from rigid protection against expected relational danger toward safer, more flexible emotional engagement.

### 1.1 What this formulation does and does not claim

Johnson's attachment frame gives a powerful account of many moments in couple distress, especially those in which the apparent topic carries a deeper question about dependability, importance, acceptance, care, or availability. But the model should not be extended into the proposition that every dispute is secretly attachment distress. Money, fidelity, parenting, work, sex, substance use, religion, values, health, caregiving burdens, migration, unequal labour, coercion, and material scarcity can be substantive realities in their own right. EFCT often changes how partners can face these realities together; it does not make the realities disappear.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

The Laboratory therefore distinguishes **process significance** from **content reality**. A dispute about money may become an attachment alarm and a negative cycle. It can also remain a genuine disagreement about risk, fairness, power, or resource allocation. A responsible formulation asks both questions.

## 2. Attachment as the organizing framework

[EFCT]

Johnson uses attachment theory to explain why adult intimate bonds have such regulatory power. The 2004 manual presents attachment as a lifespan motivational system rather than an infantile phase that should be outgrown. Mature autonomy is not defined as freedom from dependence. The model instead treats effective dependence on reliable others as compatible with exploration, agency, and self-confidence.

The key attachment functions are familiar from the broader attachment tradition:

- **Safe haven:** in threat or distress, a trusted other can reduce alarm and help regulate affect.
- **Secure base:** felt security supports exploration, risk taking, learning, play, and autonomy.
- **Proximity and connection under threat:** fear and uncertainty intensify the need for dependable contact.
- **Separation distress:** perceived unavailability of an attachment figure can provoke protest, pursuit, despair, detachment, or other defensive strategies.
- **Working models:** repeated interactions become affect-laden expectations about whether others are dependable and whether the self is worthy of care, effective in reaching, or likely to be rejected.

Johnson's clinical emphasis is relational and procedural. She is interested in how these expectations are enacted in actual moments between partners and how new moments can revise them. The 2019 text makes the three security-defining qualities especially explicit as **accessibility, responsiveness, and emotional engagement**, abbreviated **A.R.E.** Johnson uses the acronym to condense the attachment question that erupts in couple conflict: in effect, "Are you there for me?" [Johnson, *Attachment Theory in Practice*, 2019, Chapter 1].

The three terms are related but not identical:

- **Accessibility** means the partner can be reached emotionally rather than being functionally absent, shut off, or unreachable.
- **Responsiveness** means the partner registers the attachment signal and responds in a way that conveys relevance, care, and attunement.
- **Engagement** means the partner is emotionally present and involved, not merely performing a correct behavior while remaining detached.

A partner can be physically present but emotionally inaccessible. A partner can hear a message but respond defensively rather than responsively. A partner can agree intellectually while remaining unengaged. EFCT therefore works at the level of lived relational signals, not only stated intentions.

## 3. Couple distress as attachment threat

[EFCT]

In Johnson's account, intimate partners often become each other's primary attachment figures. That does not mean adult bonds are identical to parent-child bonds. Adult relationships are more reciprocal, commonly include sexuality and caregiving in both directions, and rely heavily on symbolic and representational processes. Yet the underlying need for a dependable other in moments of vulnerability gives relational events disproportionate emotional force.

A cue that would be minor in another relationship can become highly charged when it is read as evidence that an attachment figure is unavailable or rejecting. The cue may be a facial expression, delayed response, tone, silence, sexual rejection, dismissive joke, forgotten promise, refusal to discuss a topic, or an argument over a practical issue. The decisive question is not the objective size of the cue but its meaning in the bond.

When partners cannot restore safety, they tend to rely on protective strategies. Some intensify the signal, protest, demand, criticize, pursue, or attempt to force responsiveness. Others suppress attachment needs, shut down arousal, become task-focused, rationalize, stonewall, leave, or minimize the importance of the bond. These strategies may reduce immediate exposure or create a momentary sense of control, but in a distressed relationship they often generate exactly the response each partner fears.

Thus, a critical pursuit may be received as proof that one can never succeed or be accepted, prompting withdrawal. Withdrawal may be received as proof of indifference or abandonment, prompting more urgent pursuit. The cycle becomes stronger than either person's declared intention.

## 4. Attachment protest

[EFCT]

Johnson treats some anger, criticism, demanding behavior, clinging, and escalation as **attachment protest**: an attempt, however ineffective, to re-establish contact when connection feels endangered. This is a functional formulation, not an excuse for harmful conduct. The therapist asks what the behavior is trying to accomplish in the bond and what fear or longing makes the protest so urgent.

The 2004 manual differentiates forms of anger by their relational function. Anger can still contain hope that the other will respond, or it can become the anger of despair when the partner is expected never to respond. As despair grows, active protest can give way to numbness, grief, or detachment. Clinically, this matters because loud conflict is not always the most dangerous state of a relationship. Exhausted disengagement can signal that one partner is beginning to give up on the bond.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

To call anger "protest" does not imply that the anger is unreal, invalid, or merely secondary. It can contain legitimate information about violation, injustice, betrayal, or boundary crossing. EFCT's task is to widen the emotional picture, not to disqualify anger by searching for a supposedly more authentic softer feeling underneath every angry response.

## 5. Withdrawal and disengagement

[EFCT]

Withdrawal is similarly treated as an organized attempt to regulate threat. A withdrawing partner may be trying to prevent escalation, avoid failing again, contain shame, reduce physiological overload, protect the other from anger, or escape an interaction experienced as impossible. The surface message can look like indifference while the internal process is fear, helplessness, defeat, or a belief that nothing one says will work.

Johnson's therapist therefore does not assume that distance means lack of investment. The question is what happens immediately before the withdrawal, what the withdrawing partner perceives, what occurs in the body, what self-meaning is activated, what action tendency follows, and what effect the move has on the other partner.

At the same time, withdrawal is not romanticized. Chronic unavailability can be deeply injurious to the other partner and can become part of a coercive or neglectful pattern. Functional understanding and accountability must remain together.

## 6. Secure dependence and differentiation

[EFCT]

A recurrent misunderstanding is that attachment-oriented therapy promotes fusion, constant closeness, or regression into dependency. Johnson's position is the opposite: reliable connection can make autonomy safer. When a person trusts that support is available, exploration does not have to be defended against relational catastrophe. The relationship can support two distinct people rather than requiring defensive pseudo-self-sufficiency or anxious fusion.

This is one important point of compatibility, though not equivalence, with differentiation-oriented and Jungian perspectives. EFCT's secure bond is not individuation, but secure dependence need not undermine individuation. A person can be more psychologically separate precisely because the bond is not constantly at risk.

---

# Part II. The Negative Interaction Cycle

## 7. The cycle is the main unit of couple distress

[EFCT]

The signature EFCT move is to locate the recurring **negative interaction cycle**. Johnson externalizes the cycle so that the couple can begin to experience it as the shared process that captures them rather than as proof that one partner is the defective problem.

A clinically useful cycle map is concrete and sequential. It identifies:

1. the immediate cue or trigger;
2. each partner's perception of the cue;
3. bodily arousal or numbing;
4. rapid attachment-relevant meaning;
5. the more visible reactive emotion;
6. the protective action tendency;
7. the observable interpersonal move;
8. the impact of that move on the other partner;
9. the other partner's meaning and response;
10. the feedback loop that confirms each partner's fear.

The therapist does not need a perfect historical theory before working with the cycle. The most useful material is often visible in the session as it unfolds. A sigh, averted gaze, tight jaw, accelerated speech, long silence, sarcastic laugh, sudden intellectualization, or change in posture can mark the moment the cycle takes over.

Johnson's circular causality is important here. Saying that A's criticism triggers B's withdrawal and B's withdrawal intensifies A's criticism is not the same as saying both are equally responsible for everything that has occurred. Circular interaction describes **mutual influence**, not equal moral responsibility. Power, coercion, betrayal, violence, exploitation, and unilateral decisions require separate assessment.

## 8. Pursue-withdraw / criticize-withdraw dynamics

[EFCT]

The criticize-withdraw pattern is the most familiar EFCT cycle. One partner experiences distance, uncertainty, or nonresponse and moves toward the other in a heightened form: questioning, criticizing, demanding, protesting, instructing, or insisting on immediate engagement. The other experiences this approach as criticism, failure, engulfment, danger, or evidence that any response will be wrong and therefore backs away, goes silent, rationalizes, leaves, or narrows the conversation to facts.

Each move is both response and trigger. Pursuit increases withdrawal; withdrawal increases pursuit. Over time the partners stop seeing the protective logic of the other's behavior and instead assign characterological meanings: controlling, needy, cold, selfish, impossible, weak, irrational, uncaring, or incompetent. These labels harden the cycle because they transform a situational pattern into a theory of the other person's essence.

Johnson's 2019 text calls this common pattern **Criticize and Withdraw**, and also notes the familiar EFCT nickname **Protest Polka**. The later name should not be used to imply that the 2004 manual lacked the process. The underlying pursue-withdraw formulation is central throughout the 2004 treatment model.

### 8.1 Pursuer is not a diagnosis

A pursuer position is a **cycle position**, not an attachment-style diagnosis.
