Attachment anxiety can make hyperactivating strategies more likely, but a generally secure or avoidant person can pursue in a particular relationship or under a particular threat. Likewise, a person can pursue one topic and withdraw on another.

### 8.2 Withdrawer is not a diagnosis

A withdrawer position is not synonymous with avoidant attachment, introversion, emotional incapacity, or indifference. Deactivating attachment strategies can contribute, but the actual cycle must be observed. A highly anxious person may withdraw in despair; a generally avoidant person may pursue when the bond is threatened in a specific way.

## 9. Other negative cycles

[EFCT]

The 2019 couple chapter names four recurring distressed patterns. These names belong to Johnson's later teaching vocabulary and are useful as heuristic maps rather than exhaustive diagnostic categories.

### 9.1 Find the Bad Guy

This is a short, escalating attack-attack sequence in which both partners attempt to establish who is at fault or who is the more injured, unlovable, or wronged party. Blame offers a temporary sense of control but keeps both people inside threat and defensiveness. The cycle can move rapidly and may conceal fear of humiliation, rejection, or abandonment beneath escalating anger.

### 9.2 Criticize and Withdraw / Protest Polka

One partner amplifies the signal in response to disconnection while the other distances or shuts down. It is the most familiar EFCT pattern and the clearest example of a self-confirming loop.

### 9.3 Freeze and Flee

Both partners retreat, stonewall, or cease reaching. The relationship can look calmer than an attack-attack couple while being more emotionally depleted. Johnson notes that for a formerly pursuing partner this may mark grief and movement toward detachment.

### 9.4 Chaos and Ambivalence

One partner approaches for closeness but becomes alarmed by the very vulnerability of connection and then retreats, rejects, attacks, or disorganizes the encounter. The partner may oscillate rapidly between demanding connection and defending against it. Johnson links this later formulation especially to fearful-avoidant patterns and histories in which attachment figures were simultaneously sources of safety and threat.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

The four-cycle typology is a clinical simplification. Real couples can shift among patterns, and the same partner can change positions across contexts. The cycle label should follow observed process rather than precede it.

## 10. Negative Cycle Map

```text
TRIGGER / CUE
    ↓
RAPID PERCEPTION
"What does this signal about me, you, or us?"
    ↓
BODY / AROUSAL
activation, constriction, heat, freezing, numbness, collapse
    ↓
ATTACHMENT-RELEVANT MEANING
"I do not matter" / "I will fail" / "You are leaving" / "I am trapped"
    ↓
PRIMARY OR DEEPER EMOTION
fear, hurt, sadness, shame, helplessness, longing, grief, anger at violation
    ↓
REACTIVE / PROTECTIVE EMOTION
anger, irritation, contempt, panic, flatness, defensive certainty
    ↓
ACTION TENDENCY
pursue / demand / criticize / attack / appease / explain / withdraw / freeze
    ↓
VISIBLE MOVE A
    ↓
PARTNER B'S TRIGGER AND MEANING
    ↓
VISIBLE MOVE B
    ↓
FEEDBACK TO A
"See, I was right: you are unavailable / impossible / unsafe / rejecting."
    ↺
CYCLE STRENGTHENS
```

A good EFCT map is specific enough that partners can recognize the sequence in real time and broad enough that neither is reduced to a caricature.

## 11. Externalizing the cycle without erasing agency

[EFCT]

Johnson often frames the cycle as the shared enemy. This reduces shame and adversarial blame and helps partners gain a metaperspective. Yet externalization can be misused if it becomes a way of saying "the cycle made us do it." The therapist still asks each person to recognize his or her moves, impact, choices, and responsibilities.

The strongest formulation is therefore dual: **the cycle is bigger than the partners' immediate intentions, and each partner participates in maintaining or changing it**. This balance protects both compassion and agency.

---

# Part III. Emotion in EFCT

## 12. Emotion is not noise around the real problem

[EFCT]

Emotion is central because it organizes perception, meaning, action, and interaction. Johnson does not treat emotion merely as an output to be calmed before rational problem solving begins. Emotional responses orient people toward what matters, signal needs, mobilize action, and communicate to others. In attachment relationships they are part of the regulatory system through which partners seek and provide safety.

The 2004 manual describes emotion as an integrated process involving appraisal of a cue, bodily activation, meaning, and action tendency. The therapist listens not only to the story but to how the client is experiencing it now: pace, breath, posture, facial expression, voice, bodily sensation, images, impulses, and shifts in attention. These details often reveal the cycle before the couple can describe it conceptually.

EFCT does not aim at indiscriminate emotional intensity. The therapist titrates experience. Affect must be vivid enough to reorganize experience but not so overwhelming that the client is flooded or dissociated. Naming feelings from a remote intellectual position is usually insufficient, but uncontained catharsis is not the goal either.

## 13. Primary vulnerability and reactive emotion

[EFCT]

The 2004 manual uses the experiential distinction among primary, secondary, and instrumental emotions that belonged to the shared historical roots of emotionally focused work. In couple practice, Johnson is especially interested in the way highly visible reactive emotions can obscure or defend against more vulnerable attachment-relevant experience.

A typical pursuing sequence may begin with a cue of distance, followed by fear, hurt, loneliness, or a sense of not mattering. Because those states feel exposed or powerless, the person may rapidly organize into anger, accusation, or insistence. A typical withdrawing sequence may begin with a cue of criticism, followed by fear, shame, helplessness, or a sense of inevitable failure. The person may then become flat, logical, silent, or absent.

The therapist does not impose these hidden states by formula. Johnson repeatedly treats them as hypotheses to be discovered with the client. The same outward behavior can arise from different inner processes. The question is not "What feeling should a pursuer have?" but "What happens in you, right here, just before you move into this position?"

### 13.1 Why accessing vulnerability matters

When previously hidden experience becomes explicit, the interpersonal signal changes. "You never care" invites a different response from "When you turn away while I am trying to reach you, I panic and I feel as though I do not matter." "Leave me alone" communicates differently from "When I hear how disappointed you are, I become sure I am failing you and I shut down because I do not know how to fix it."

The clinical value is not softness for its own sake. More congruent disclosure gives the partner better information and can evoke a different response. It also enables the person speaking to experience agency in expressing a need rather than only defending against threat.

### 13.2 Reactive anger remains meaningful

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

An EFCT therapist may deepen an angry response toward hurt, fear, shame, grief, or longing, but that does not make anger merely a disguise. Anger may be primary and adaptive when a boundary has been violated. It may also coexist with vulnerability. A sophisticated formulation preserves both the protective function and the informational value of anger.

## 14. Attachment meaning

[EFCT]

EFCT constantly asks what an interaction means **in the bond**. A practical event becomes clinically potent when it is encoded as evidence about availability, worth, reliability, abandonment, failure, or safety. The therapist reframes behavior in this attachment context.

Examples of attachment meanings include:

- "If you do not respond, I disappear for you."
- "If I try and get it wrong, I will be exposed as inadequate."
- "If I need you, you will use my need against me."
- "If I let you close, you will leave when I am most vulnerable."
- "If I show how much this matters, I will lose all control."
- "If you are disappointed, I have already failed."

These examples are generic reconstructions, not diagnostic templates. The actual meaning must be elicited from the partner's experience.

## 15. Body, emotional experience, and action tendency

[EFCT]

The body is clinically useful because attachment threat is often registered before the person has a polished story about it. Johnson's therapist may slow a moment down and ask what happens in the chest, throat, stomach, shoulders, breath, face, or limbs. A bodily constriction can be linked to a perception and an action tendency. This turns a global statement such as "I just get mad" into a differentiated process: cue, tightening, fear, meaning, urge to attack or leave.

The 2019 Tango's second move later systematizes this deepening as **affect assembly**: therapist and client piece together the trigger, initial perception, bodily response, meaning, and action tendency into a coherent emotional whole. This language is later than the 2004 manual, but it describes a process already central to the earlier work.

---

# Part IV. The EFCT Treatment Map

## 16. The 2004 three-stage, nine-step model

[EFCT]

The 2004 manual organizes treatment into three stages and nine steps. The sequence is a map rather than a rigid script. Steps overlap, are revisited, and unfold at different speeds for different partners.

### Stage 1: De-escalation of negative cycles

1. **Create an alliance and identify the conflict issues in the core attachment struggle.**
2. **Identify the negative interactional cycle in which those issues are expressed.**
3. **Access unacknowledged emotions underlying the partners' interactional positions.**
4. **Reframe the problem in terms of the negative cycle, underlying emotion, and attachment needs.**

### Stage 2: Changing interactional positions

5. **Promote identification with disowned attachment emotions, needs, and aspects of self, and integrate them into relationship interactions.**
6. **Promote each partner's acceptance of the other's experience and new responses.**
7. **Facilitate direct expression of needs and wants and create new emotional engagement and bonding events.**

### Stage 3: Consolidation and integration

8. **Facilitate new solutions to old relationship problems.**
9. **Consolidate new positions and new cycles of attachment behavior.**

Johnson identifies three especially important shifts: de-escalation at the end of Stage 1; withdrawer re-engagement in Stage 2; and blamer softening in Stage 2. De-escalation is a first-order shift in the existing pattern. Re-engagement and softening are deeper second-order reorganizations of emotional position and relationship structure.

## 17. EFCT Change Map

```text
DISTRESS / ATTACHMENT THREAT
        ↓
ASSESS SAFETY + BUILD ALLIANCE
        ↓
TRACK AND NAME NEGATIVE CYCLE
        ↓
ACCESS UNDERLYING EMOTION AND ATTACHMENT MEANING
        ↓
REFRAME CYCLE AS SHARED ENEMY
        ↓
STAGE 1 DE-ESCALATION
partners recognize the dance; reactivity falls; hope and agency rise
        ↓
DEEPEN DISOWNED FEARS, HURTS, LONGINGS, NEEDS
        ↓
SHAPE ACCEPTANCE OF NEWLY EXPRESSED EXPERIENCE
        ↓
CHOREOGRAPH DIRECT ENACTMENTS
        ↓
WITHDRAWER RE-ENGAGEMENT
        ↓
PURSUER / BLAMER SOFTENING
        ↓
BONDING EVENTS / RESPONSIVE REACHING
        ↓
REPAIR ATTACHMENT INJURIES WHERE NECESSARY
        ↓
NEW POSITIVE CYCLE OF ACCESSIBILITY, RESPONSIVENESS, ENGAGEMENT
        ↓
STAGE 3 CONSOLIDATION
practical problem solving + integration + maintenance of new bond
```

## 18. Stage 1: De-escalation

[EFCT]

Stage 1 stabilizes the relationship by helping the couple see and interrupt the negative dance. The therapist creates a working alliance with both partners, assesses safety and suitability, identifies the recurrent cycle, and begins to access the emotion that fuels each position.

### 18.1 Step 1: alliance and assessment

The alliance is not a preliminary courtesy before the real technique begins. Johnson treats it as the secure base from which clients can risk contact with vulnerable experience. The therapist must be experienced as nonjudgmental, emotionally present, competent, and able to protect the session from destructive escalation.

Assessment includes the couple's presenting issues, relationship history, significant attachment events, typical cycle, strengths, goals, commitment to the work, and barriers to safe participation. The therapist also screens for ongoing violence, intimidation, coercion, active affairs, untreated addiction, severe instability, and other conditions that may make vulnerable couple work unsafe or unproductive.

### 18.2 Step 2: map the cycle

The therapist tracks sequences rather than debating who is objectively right in every exchange. The map is built collaboratively from real episodes and in-session interactions. The therapist notices the order of moves, the triggers, the emotional signals, and the effects on the partner.

A strong cycle formulation is symmetrical at the level of process without becoming morally symmetrical. It shows how the interaction sustains itself while leaving room to identify unilateral harms.

### 18.3 Step 3: access unacknowledged emotion

The therapist slows the visible position and explores what is happening immediately beneath it. Johnson's classic examples often involve a critical partner discovering fear, abandonment panic, hurt, loneliness, or longing, and a withdrawn partner discovering intimidation, shame, helplessness, fear of failure, or a belief that any response will be wrong. These are common patterns, not fixed assignments.

The therapist uses reflection, validation, evocative questions, heightening, and empathic conjecture to make the experience more specific and alive. Nonverbal responses are used as data. A client who says "I don't care" while tearing up, gripping a chair, or looking away may be invited to stay with that discrepancy rather than being confronted as dishonest.

### 18.4 Step 4: attachment reframe

The therapist links each partner's protective move to underlying emotion and attachment need, then links the two partners' moves into the negative cycle. The problem becomes the cycle of insecurity and protection rather than one defective individual.

The reframe can transform the meaning of behavior. Withdrawal may become understandable as an attempt to prevent failure or escalation rather than proof of indifference. Criticism may become visible as desperate protest rather than proof of hostility. Understanding does not automatically make the behavior acceptable; it makes the process workable.

### 18.5 End point of Stage 1

By the end of de-escalation, partners can usually recognize the cycle while it is occurring, recover more quickly, and see the other's behavior as less personally malicious. Previously hidden emotions have become more accessible. The relationship has not yet been fully restructured, but there is enough safety to take greater relational risks.

## 19. Stage 2: Restructuring attachment

[EFCT]

Stage 2 aims at a deeper reorganization. The task is no longer simply to stop the old dance. Partners develop new emotional positions and enact new forms of reaching and responding. Johnson's later 2019 language describes this stage as shaping **constructive dependency** and interactions that are attuned, accessible, responsive, and engaged.

### 19.1 Step 5: deepen and own disowned experience

A partner learns to tolerate and claim attachment-related fear, hurt, shame, longing, grief, need, and desire for connection. The therapist helps the client move from a defensive position into a more integrated first-person experience: not merely "You make me..." but "This is what happens in me, this is what I fear, this is what I need, and this is what I do when I am afraid."

Johnson describes this not as turning someone into a different personality but as helping the person become more fully what he or she already is. Previously disowned experience becomes available for choice and communication.

### 19.2 Step 6: facilitate acceptance by the partner

The receiving partner must be able to hear the new signal. Because couples have long histories, this is not automatic. A partner may distrust a new vulnerable disclosure, dismiss it, become confused, or hear it through the old cycle. The therapist slows the response, validates difficulty, and helps the receiving partner take in what is different.

This step is where the interactional nature of EFCT becomes unmistakable. An intrapsychic breakthrough is not yet the full change event. The newly accessed experience has to enter the relationship and meet a response.

### 19.3 Step 7: direct expression and new bonding interaction

Partners are helped to ask more directly for closeness, reassurance, comfort, contact, respect, space-with-connection, or other attachment-relevant responses. The request is shaped from a more empowered and vulnerable position rather than as accusation, mind reading, or threat.

The therapist then choreographs the actual encounter. This is the setting for withdrawer re-engagement, pursuer/blamer softening, and bonding events.

## 20. Withdrawer re-engagement

[EFCT]

In many criticize-withdraw couples, Johnson encourages the more withdrawn partner to re-engage before expecting the critical or pursuing partner to soften fully. This is a clinical sequence, not an invariant law. In withdraw-withdraw couples, whichever partner can begin to risk engagement may move first.

Re-engagement involves more than talking more. The withdrawn partner becomes emotionally present in the relationship and able to disclose the fear, hurt, shame, helplessness, or longing that had been hidden behind distance. The person begins to define the relationship actively rather than participating mainly through absence or defense.

A re-engaged withdrawer may move from "Whatever you want" to "When you come at me that way I panic because I am sure I will disappoint you. I pull away, but I do not want to lose you. I need you to slow down and let me stay with you." The important shift is from mute defensive retreat to embodied, emotionally consequential participation.

## 21. Pursuer or blamer softening

[EFCT]

Once the formerly withdrawn partner is more accessible and responsive, the pursuing or blaming partner can risk letting go of protective anger, control, or relentless monitoring and reveal the attachment need that made pursuit urgent. Johnson calls the major change event **softening**.

Softening does not mean submission, becoming agreeable, forgiving prematurely, suppressing anger, or abandoning legitimate complaints. It means the person can speak from the place that the defensive strategy was protecting and make a direct relational reach. A demand for proof can become a request for reassurance; attack can become an explicit statement of fear of abandonment; controlling instruction can become a request to be included and valued.

The receiving partner must be sufficiently engaged for this risk to be viable. Softening is therefore a dyadic event, not simply an individual emotion-regulation skill.

## 22. Enactments

[EFCT]

An **enactment** is a structured, real interaction in the session. The therapist does not merely ask partners to talk about how they communicate. The therapist helps one partner turn toward the other and express newly accessed experience directly, then helps the other receive and respond.

Enactments differ from role play. The client is not acting a script or practicing a generic communication formula. The material is drawn from live emotional experience that has just been deepened. The therapist may slice the risk thinner if direct reaching is too difficult: for example, the first enactment may simply be to tell the partner that speaking is frightening or that the client cannot yet ask for comfort.

Enactments serve several functions at once:

- they consolidate newly accessed emotion;
- they expose partners to a new relational risk in a protected setting;
- they generate new information about the partner;
- they reveal blocks immediately;
- they create opportunities for corrective emotional experience;
- they revise models of self, other, and relationship through lived interaction rather than argument.

## 23. Bonding events

[EFCT]

A bonding event occurs when a partner can make a vulnerable, coherent reach and the other responds with accessibility, care, engagement, reassurance, or comfort. The event matters because it contradicts the old procedural expectation. The formerly isolated partner experiences the other as available; the formerly helpless partner experiences a capacity to reach and have an effect.

The 2019 book often calls the culminating Stage 2 encounters **Hold Me Tight conversations**. It links them to the same restructuring process: partners discover and distill attachment fears, share them coherently, receive a more accepting response, express attachment needs, and obtain comfort or reassurance.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

A bonding event is not a mystical union, permanent cure, or Jungian **coniunctio**. It is a clinically shaped interaction that can become a new learning event in the attachment relationship. Repetition and consolidation matter.

## 24. Stage 3: Consolidation and integration

[EFCT]

Once the relationship has greater security, practical problem solving becomes easier because ordinary differences are less likely to be encoded as existential threats to the bond. Stage 3 therefore returns more explicitly to content issues and collaborative decisions.

Step 8 supports new solutions to old problems. The therapist does not assume all disagreements vanish. Some remain genuine differences that require negotiation, trade-offs, boundaries, or acceptance. The point is that partners can address them without the old negative cycle dominating the conversation.

Step 9 consolidates the new positions and positive interaction patterns. The couple develops a coherent story of how the old cycle worked, how they now recognize it, how they repair when it returns, and how they maintain accessibility and responsiveness across ordinary transitions, separations, reunions, caregiving demands, sexuality, parenting, work pressure, and other stresses.

The therapeutic endpoint is not absence of conflict. It is a more secure and flexible regulatory system in which conflict does not automatically become attachment catastrophe.

---

# Part V. The 2019 EFT Tango as a Later Metaframework

## 25. Status of the Tango in this corpus

[EFCT - LATER FORMULATION]

The **EFT Tango** is explicit in the 2019 *Attachment Theory in Practice*. It is not a named construct in the 2004 second-edition couple manual. The correct corpus treatment is therefore continuity-with-development: the five moves formalize recurrent interventions already visible in the earlier model, but the named metaframework belongs to the later source.

Johnson presents the Tango as a repeating five-move sequence used across emotionally focused individual, couple, and family therapy. The pacing and intensity vary by stage and modality.

## 26. Tango Move 1: Mirroring present process

The therapist tracks and reflects the client's present emotional and interpersonal process without evaluation. In couple work this usually means distilling the negative cycle to its essential sequence and attachment consequences. The therapist helps partners observe how they participate in a drama that has developed momentum of its own.

This move corresponds closely to earlier EFCT cycle tracking and de-escalation work.

## 27. Tango Move 2: Affect assembly and deepening

The therapist helps the client assemble the components of emotion into a coherent experience: trigger, initial perception, bodily response, meaning, and action tendency. The process often reveals deeper or previously unacknowledged emotion.

This is a later, more systematic language for the earlier manual's work of accessing and expanding primary emotion.

## 28. Tango Move 3: Choreographing engaged encounters

Newly deepened emotional experience is brought into an encounter with a significant other. In couple therapy, the other partner is physically present. The therapist structures direct disclosure and response so that a new inner experience becomes a new interpersonal act.

This move formalizes the enactment process central to 2004 Stage 2.

## 29. Tango Move 4: Processing the encounter

After the interaction, therapist and partners examine what it was like to reach, hear, respond, accept, reject, freeze, or become confused. Blocks are processed rather than treated as failure. If the receiving partner cannot take in the new signal, the therapist helps slow and explore that response.

## 30. Tango Move 5: Integrating and validating

The therapist reflects the significance of what occurred, validates the risk and competence involved, and helps the new experience become part of the couple's evolving story of self and relationship. Positive emotional shifts are highlighted so they can become building blocks for further change.

## 31. Tango and the three stages

The 2019 text renames the general stages across modalities as **stabilization**, **restructuring attachment**, and **consolidation**. In the couple literature, stabilization corresponds to the familiar de-escalation stage. The same Tango moves recur throughout treatment, but their function changes with stage. Early on they help expose and tame the negative cycle. Later, Moves 2-4 are intensified to produce bonding encounters. In consolidation, the couple uses the new security to integrate change and solve practical problems.

---

# Part VI. Attachment Injuries, Rupture, Forgiveness, and Repair

## 32. What Johnson means by an attachment injury

[EFCT]

The 2004 manual introduces a focused clinical formulation of **attachment injuries** as relationship traumas that can block Stage 2 change. They are not simply any hurt or disagreement. They involve abandonment or betrayal at a crucial moment of need and are experienced as a violation of basic assumptions about the partner's availability and the safety of the bond. [Johnson, 2004, Chapter 12, printed pp. 267-274]

The injury can be objectively dramatic or appear small to an outsider. Its power depends on context and attachment meaning. Johnson gives examples involving absence at childbirth, sexual or relational betrayal, and other moments in which a partner was acutely vulnerable and experienced the attachment figure as unavailable. Affairs can function as attachment injuries, but the category is determined by meaning and context rather than by a mechanical rule that every affair is the same phenomenon.

These injuries often reappear when therapy asks the injured partner to risk closeness. The memory can become vivid and immediate, resembling a traumatic reactivation. The injured partner may become hypervigilant for recurrence, numb, or unwilling to reach. The other partner may minimize, defend, explain, or distance, which reinforces the injury.

## 33. The 2004 seven-step injury repair sequence

[EFCT]

Johnson places focused attachment-injury repair in Stage 2, after sufficient de-escalation. The sequence can be reconstructed as follows from Chapter 12:

1. **The injury becomes alive in the session.** As the injured partner is asked to risk engagement, a specific abandonment or betrayal event emerges with intense, often disorganized affect. The other partner may initially discount, deny, minimize, or defend.
2. **The injured partner stays with the experience and articulates its attachment significance.** Outrage and anger are expanded into the hurt, helplessness, fear, shame, grief, or loss carried by the event. Its link to the current negative cycle becomes clearer.
3. **The injuring partner begins to hear the event in attachment terms.** With therapist support, the person recognizes the depth of the other's suffering and explains his or her own state of mind in a way that makes the behavior comprehensible without erasing its impact.
4. **The injured partner offers a more integrated account of the wound.** The person can express grief and fear about the threatened attachment bond and allow the other to witness vulnerability directly.
5. **The injuring partner becomes emotionally engaged and accepts responsibility for his or her part.** Empathy, regret, or remorse can now be expressed.
6. **The injured partner risks asking for the care or comfort that was unavailable at the time of injury.** This is a new attachment reach rather than only retrospective accusation.
7. **The partner responds with care.** The response functions as an antidote to the original abandonment and enables the couple to construct a new, coherent narrative of the event.

The sequence is not a courtroom procedure and does not require the injured partner to accept an implausible account. Nor does the other's explanation nullify responsibility. The therapeutic work combines acknowledgment of impact, comprehensibility, responsibility, emotional engagement, and a new responsive encounter.

## 34. Rupture and repair

[EFCT]

EFCT treats repair as more than returning to the pre-conflict status quo. If the original relationship already contained chronic inaccessibility, simple restoration is insufficient. A successful repair should produce new relational knowledge: "I can tell you when I am hurt; you can stay; you can acknowledge your impact; I can ask; you can respond; and we can build a shared account of what happened."

Repair therefore has prospective as well as retrospective value. It becomes evidence for a different future bond.

## 35. Forgiveness in EFCT

Johnson's attachment-injury work enters the territory of forgiveness and reconciliation, but the model does not reduce forgiveness to a decision to let go. The sequence depends on relational response. The injured person's experience must become coherent and witnessed; the injuring partner must take in the impact and assume responsibility; and a different caring response must become possible.

This is why premature pressure to forgive is inconsistent with the model. Forgiveness is an emergent outcome of repair, not a moral requirement imposed before safety or accountability exists.

## 36. Attachment explanation does not excuse harm

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

An attachment frame can help explain why a person behaved defensively, withdrew, panicked, attacked verbally, concealed, or sought control. Explanation does not convert harmful behavior into harmless behavior. It also does not establish symmetry between partners. Abuse, coercive control, intimidation, stalking, threats, sexual coercion, and repeated unilateral betrayal require explicit safety and accountability assessment; they should not be softened into a neutral "mutual cycle."

---

# Part VII. Therapist Stance and Core Interventions

## 37. Therapist stance

[EFCT]

The therapist is active but not authoritarian, emotionally engaged but not engulfed, and theoretically directed without imposing a predetermined story on the couple. Johnson's stance is rooted in humanistic-experiential values: respect for clients' capacity for growth, accurate empathy, validation, collaboration, and attention to present-moment experience.

The therapist holds several maps simultaneously:

- the individual's unfolding emotional process;
- the interpersonal cycle;
- the attachment meaning of the interaction;
- the current treatment stage;
- the immediate change task;
- safety and the quality of the therapeutic alliance.

The therapist repeatedly moves "within" and "between": within each person's experience and between the partners' interactive moves. Skill lies in knowing when to deepen an individual emotion and when to turn that experience into an encounter.

## 38. Validation is not agreement

[EFCT]

Validation communicates that a person's response has an intelligible logic in context. It can normalize fear, shame, protest, or withdrawal without endorsing every conclusion or behavior that follows from those states.

A therapist can validate that a partner became terrified when a spouse did not come home, while not endorsing surveillance or threats. A therapist can validate that a withdrawn partner felt overwhelmed by criticism while not endorsing days of punitive silence. Validation reduces shame and defensiveness so the underlying process can be examined.

| Validation | Agreement |
|---|---|
| "I can see how that landed as abandonment given what this moment meant to you." | "Your interpretation is objectively correct." |
| Makes emotional logic coherent. | Decides the facts or merits of a dispute. |
| Can coexist with challenge and accountability. | May imply endorsement of behavior or claim. |
| Aims to create safety for exploration. | Aims to settle who is right. |

## 39. Core intervention families in the 2004 manual

[EFCT]

The 2004 manual organizes much of the therapist's work around experiential and interactional interventions. Key experiential moves include:

- **Empathic attunement and reflection:** accurately track and reflect emotional experience.
- **Validation:** make the response understandable in its context.
- **Evocative responding:** use focused questions and reflections to make implicit experience more vivid.
- **Heightening:** repeat, simplify, slow, or intensify a key phrase, image, bodily cue, or emotional statement so the client can stay with it.
- **Empathic conjecture:** tentatively offer a possible meaning or feeling that is implied but not yet explicit, always as a hypothesis to be corrected by the client.

Key interactional moves include:

- tracking and reflecting the cycle;
- reframing behavior in attachment terms;
- externalizing the negative dance;
- shaping enactments;
- supporting the partner's reception and response;
- processing what happened in the encounter.

The later 2019 text places many of these inside the Tango. It also emphasizes the therapist's accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement with clients and the familiar EFCT use of slow, simple, evocative, client-near language.

## 40. Content versus process

[EFCT]

Johnson strongly prioritizes process. The therapist repeatedly redirects abstract argument about content toward the emotional and interactional process occurring now. This prevents sessions from becoming endless debates over details that simply reproduce the cycle.

The process focus asks: What happened between you when this topic appeared? What did you see in your partner? What did that mean? What did you feel? What did you do? What happened in the other person then? Where did the cycle take you?

[LABORATORY SYNTHESIS]

A process-first stance should not become **content blindness**. Facts and material conditions can determine what repair requires. If one partner has spent shared savings without consent, maintained an affair, repeatedly failed caregiving obligations, or used threats, the content is not merely a carrier wave for attachment insecurity. Good formulation holds both levels: the interactional process and the substantive issue.

## 41. Responsibility, mutuality, and asymmetry

[EFCT]

EFCT's circular account helps partners see how they mutually cue and reinforce one another. This can reduce self-righteous blame and make change possible. Yet mutual influence is not the same as equal responsibility.

A clinically sound formulation can say:

- Partner A's criticism increases Partner B's withdrawal.
- Partner B's withdrawal increases Partner A's protest.
- Partner A remains responsible for insults or threats used during protest.
- Partner B remains responsible for punitive silence or deception used during withdrawal.
- A unilateral affair, assault, financial coercion, or intimidation is not morally split 50:50 because the relationship also has a cycle.

The therapist therefore externalizes the cycle without externalizing accountability.

## 42. Therapist use of self and emotional presence

[EFCT]

Johnson's therapist is not a detached observer. Tone, pacing, facial expression, emotional resonance, and confidence in the clients' capacity to change are part of the intervention. The therapist's presence can become a temporary secure base that supports risk.

The therapist must also monitor personal reactions. A highly pursuing client may evoke pressure or irritation; a highly withdrawn client may evoke rescue attempts or frustration. Unexamined therapist responses can reproduce the couple's polarization. Competence therefore includes awareness of one's own attachment sensitivities and habitual positions.

---

# Part VIII. Comparative Boundaries

## 43. Adult attachment theory versus EFCT

| Dimension | Adult attachment theory / research | Johnson's EFCT |
|---|---|---|
| Primary purpose | Describe and test attachment-system functioning, individual differences, working models, regulation strategies, relationship processes. | Treat distressed relationships by reorganizing emotion and interaction in an attachment frame. |
| Key Project source | Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood* (2007 first ed.). | Johnson 2004 couple manual; Johnson 2019 later clinical framework. |
| Typical constructs | Attachment anxiety, avoidance, security, hyperactivation, deactivation, safe haven, secure base, working models. | Negative cycle, attachment protest, de-escalation, re-engagement, softening, enactment, bonding event, injury repair. |
| Unit of analysis | Individuals and relationships across empirical studies. | Live couple process in therapy. |
| Evidence form | Experimental, correlational, longitudinal, measurement, relationship research. | Clinical model plus its own outcome/process research literature. |
| Direction of inference | Attachment findings can inform hypotheses about regulation and relationship behavior. | Uses attachment constructs to guide therapeutic intervention. |
| Key caution | Anxiety/avoidance dimensions do not mechanically predict one person's cycle position. | A pursue/withdraw pattern does not by itself diagnose attachment style. |

[ATTACHMENT THEORY]

Mikulincer and Shaver's research synthesis is especially useful for the concepts of hyperactivating and deactivating regulation, internal working models, safe haven, secure base, and the relation between attachment orientation and emotion regulation. EFCT translates related principles into a change process. The translation is theoretical and clinical; it is not a one-to-one derivation from every attachment study.

## 44. Johnson EFCT versus Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy

| Dimension | Johnson's EFCT | Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy |
|---|---|---|
| Mature organizing frame | Adult attachment and the security of close bonds. | Emotion processing and transformation in a neohumanistic-experiential model. |
| Primary clinical unit in this corpus | Couple interaction and bond. | Person's emotion-processing difficulties; also applied to couples in a distinct framework. |
| Main distress map | Attachment threat becomes a negative interaction cycle that reinforces insecurity. | Maladaptive emotional processing and emotion schemes require differentiated access, evaluation, and transformation. |
| Emotion distinctions | 2004 manual uses primary, secondary, and instrumental distinctions in couple work, especially to access attachment-relevant vulnerability beneath reactive positions. | Systematically distinguishes primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, and instrumental emotion; uses process diagnosis to guide differential tasks. |
| Change mechanism | De-escalate cycle; deepen attachment emotion; restructure reaching/responding; create bonding and repair events; consolidate security. | Access and symbolize emotion; distinguish adaptive from maladaptive states; use marker-guided tasks; transform maladaptive emotion with new adaptive emotion; create new meaning/narrative. |
| Structured interventions | Cycle tracking, attachment reframing, enactments, re-engagement, softening, bonding, Tango in later model. | Marker-guided interventions such as focusing, two-chair and empty-chair work, plus emotion coaching and transformation tasks. |
| Relationship role | Relationship is the direct field of change in EFCT. | Relationship with therapist supports emotional processing; couple applications have their own tasks but are not reducible to Johnson's attachment map. |
| Key term not interchangeable | Attachment vulnerability. | Primary adaptive emotion / primary maladaptive emotion. |
| Historical relation | Johnson and Greenberg jointly developed an earlier emotionally focused couple approach; Johnson's mature model increasingly organizes around attachment. | Greenberg's mature Emotion-Focused Therapy develops a broader emotion-process model. |
| Corpus rule | Use EFCT for Johnson's attachment-oriented couple model. | Spell out Emotion-Focused Therapy when referring to Greenberg. |

[EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY]

Greenberg's 2015 model describes emotion schemes as core affective-cognitive processing structures and treats different emotional states differently. Primary adaptive emotion provides useful action information; primary maladaptive emotion is an immediate but historically learned response that no longer fits the present; secondary emotion is a response to another emotional process; instrumental emotion is expressed, consciously or not, for interpersonal effect. This taxonomy is more elaborated and technically central in Greenberg's mature model than in Johnson's attachment-centered EFCT.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

Do not convert Johnson's "deeper vulnerability" into Greenberg's "primary adaptive emotion" by default. A vulnerable fear can itself be maladaptive in Greenberg's taxonomy, and Johnson's question is often attachment-functional rather than taxonomic: what emotion organizes this person's defensive position in this bond, and what new interaction can transform it?

## 45. Negative cycle versus Jungian complex constellation

| Dimension | EFCT negative cycle | Jungian complex constellation |
|---|---|---|
| Core construct | Recurring dyadic sequence of emotional signals and protective responses. | Activation of an affectively charged psychic complex with relative autonomy. |
| Primary location | Between and within partners simultaneously; observable interaction is central. | Primarily a dynamic of the psyche, though it strongly affects perception and relationship. |
| Trigger | Relational cue interpreted through attachment significance. | Stimulus touches the affective nucleus and associated material of a complex. |
| Repetition | Maintained through reciprocal partner responses. | Repetition follows autonomous associative organization of the complex. |
| Change emphasis | De-escalate cycle; access emotion; restructure interaction; create secure bonding responses. | Differentiate ego from complex, make unconscious material more conscious, transform relation to the complex. |
| Non-equivalence | A couple cycle is not itself a complex. | A complex can help explain why a particular cue has unusual charge, but does not replace the dyadic map. |

[JUNGIAN COMPARISON]

Jung's complex theory in CW 8 gives a model of feeling-toned, relatively autonomous psychic organizations that can disrupt conscious intention. EFCT gives a model of reciprocal couple process. The two can overlap phenomenologically: a partner's move may constellate a complex, intensifying the cycle. But the explanatory levels differ.

## 46. Attachment expectation versus Jungian projection

| Dimension | Attachment expectation / working model | Jungian projection |
|---|---|---|
| Basic idea | Procedural expectation about self, other, and availability built through attachment experience. | Unconscious psychic content is experienced as belonging to an external object. |
| Typical question | "What do I expect will happen if I need or reach for you?" | "What am I seeing in the other that may partly belong to my own unconscious psyche?" |
| Reality relation | Can be accurate, biased, or overgeneralized; revised by new relational experience. | Projection may contain a 'hook' in the object but involves unconscious attribution. |
| Clinical change | New responsive interaction updates expectation. | Recognition and withdrawal/integration of projected content changes subject-object relation. |
| Non-equivalence | Expecting rejection is not automatically projection. | Projection is not simply a negative belief or attachment prediction. |

Jung and C. A. Meier both emphasize that projection cannot be reduced to fantasy without any relation to the object. EFCT likewise avoids assuming that every threat perception is inaccurate. A partner may in fact be unavailable. The task is to distinguish present evidence, learned expectation, and reciprocal process.

## 47. Negative cycle versus Schwartz-Salant's interactive field

| Dimension | EFCT negative cycle | Schwartz-Salant interactive field |
|---|---|---|
| Main image | Repeating dance / loop. | A third, in-between field with emergent qualities. |
| Clinical emphasis | Track concrete sequence and attachment meaning; change moves and responses. | Attend to relational field phenomena, chaos, mutual unconscious participation, transformation. |
| Causality | Reciprocal systemic sequence plus attachment regulation. | Field may have emergent qualities not reducible to linear subject-object causation. |
| Therapist task | Name and externalize cycle; deepen affect; enact new bond. | Enter, experience, and symbolize field processes without reducing them to one person's projection. |
| Non-equivalence | Cycle is a structured interactional pattern. | Field is a broader relational/process ontology. |

[POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT]

Schwartz-Salant's *The Mystery of Human Relationship* describes an interactive "third area" that can transform both participants. That is richer and less behaviorally specific than Johnson's cycle construct. A cycle can occur within an interactive field, but the terms should not be substituted for one another.

## 48. Pursuer/withdrawer position versus attachment style

| Cycle position | What EFCT can say | What EFCT cannot infer from position alone |
|---|---|---|
| Pursuing / criticizing | The person is using an escalating strategy in this interaction, often to protest distance or force engagement. | That the person has a globally anxious attachment orientation. |
| Withdrawing / stonewalling | The person is reducing contact or emotional exposure in this interaction, often to regulate threat or anticipated failure. | That the person has a globally avoidant attachment orientation. |
| Oscillating approach-avoid | The person alternates between bids and defense under threat. | A fixed fearful-avoidant classification without independent assessment. |

## 49. Primary vulnerability versus reactive / secondary emotion

| Level | EFCT clinical use | Caution |
|---|---|---|
| Deeper / primary experience | Fear, hurt, shame, loneliness, helplessness, grief, longing, anger at violation, or other immediate experience organizing the position. | Do not assume the deeper emotion is always soft or always fear. |
| Reactive / secondary emotion | Anger, irritation, panic, flatness, defensive certainty, contempt, or other responses that may organize around the deeper state. | Reactive does not mean fake or meaningless. |
| Protective action | Pursuit, criticism, explanation, withdrawal, appeasement, attack, silence. | Behavior still carries responsibility and consequences. |

## 50. Validation versus agreement

| Validation | Agreement |
|---|---|
| Recognizes intelligibility of emotion in context. | Endorses a factual claim, judgment, or position. |
| Reduces shame and supports exploration. | Settles or aligns with one side of a dispute. |
| Can coexist with boundaries. | May imply behavior is acceptable. |
| Is a core therapeutic stance. | Is not required for empathy. |

## 51. Mutual influence versus equal responsibility

| Mutual influence | Equal responsibility |
|---|---|
| Describes reciprocal effects in a system. | Allocates moral or causal responsibility evenly. |
| Often true in ordinary negative cycles. | Often false when there is unilateral harm or power asymmetry. |
| Useful for identifying leverage points for change. | Can become victim-blaming if imposed mechanically. |
| Compatible with explicit accountability. | Should never be inferred merely from circular causality. |

## 52. Bonding event versus coniunctio

| EFCT bonding event | Jungian coniunctio |
|---|---|
| Concrete therapeutic encounter of vulnerable reaching and responsive engagement. | Symbolic/alchemical image of the union of opposites with intrapsychic and transpersonal meanings in Jung's later work. |
| Evaluated by new interaction and attachment security. | Interpreted within analytical psychology and alchemical symbolism. |
| Can be repeated and consolidated behaviorally and emotionally. | Not a synonym for a successful couple interaction or romantic union. |
| Clinical couple-process term. | Symbolic depth-psychological construct. |

## 53. Secure attachment versus individuation

| Secure attachment | Jungian individuation |
|---|---|
| Greater confidence in accessibility and responsiveness of attachment figures; flexible dependence and exploration. | Long developmental process of differentiation and relation between ego and the wider psyche/Self. |
| Primary relational-regulatory outcome. | Broader personality-development process. |
| Can support autonomy and exploration. | Can include conflict with collective adaptation and relationship patterns. |
| Not equivalent to psychological wholeness. | Not equivalent to relationship satisfaction or secure attachment. |

## 54. Repair versus return to status quo

| Repair | Return to status quo |
|---|---|
| Integrates injury, responsibility, emotional meaning, and a new responsive experience. | Restores prior routine without changing the attachment expectation. |
| Produces new relational knowledge. | May leave original vulnerability intact. |
| Can reorganize the cycle. | May simply suppress conflict until next trigger. |

## 55. EFCT versus Young-Eisendrath's *Love Between Equals*

[POST-JUNGIAN COMPARISON]

Polly Young-Eisendrath's 2019 *Love Between Equals* is organized around personal love, projection/falling in love, commitment and containment, dialogue, and relationship as a developmental or spiritual practice. Its emphasis on equality and mindful dialogue asks partners to encounter one another as separate subjects rather than as instruments of personal fantasy.

EFCT overlaps phenomenologically in valuing responsiveness and genuine encounter, but its clinical map is different. EFCT asks how attachment threat organizes a negative cycle and how a therapist can create corrective bonding interactions. Young-Eisendrath's dialogue framework is more explicitly concerned with equality, witnessing, and the developmental task of seeing the actual other. Neither should be reduced to the other.

## 56. EFCT versus contemporary Jungian couples therapy

[CONTEMPORARY JUNGIAN COUPLES]

Nelson and Delmedico's *The Art of Jungian Couples Therapy: An Introduction* (Routledge, 2026 in the Project source) organizes couple work through Jungian concepts such as complexes, typology, shadow, archetypal dynamics, alchemy, and the psyche of the couple. It explicitly resists reducing couple experience to a single standardized mechanism.

EFCT provides something different: a highly specified, stage-based map of emotional regulation and interaction, with observable change events. A Laboratory that uses both can ask two different questions of the same episode: "What cycle of attachment threat and protection is happening?" and "What complexes, projections, typological asymmetries, symbols, or developmental tasks may be constellated?" The answers should be compared, not fused.

---

# Part IX. Applied Laboratory Use

## 57. Comparative Couple Map

```text
ONE COUPLE EPISODE
       |
       +--> JUNG / ANALYTICAL PSYCHOLOGY
       |      What complex is constellated?
       |      What projection may shape perception?
       |      What shadow material or typological one-sidedness appears?
       |      What does the episode ask of individuation?
       |
       +--> JEAN KNOX / DEVELOPMENTAL-ATTACHMENT JUNGIANA
       |      What internal working model or developmental representation
       |      organizes meaning?
       |      How are archetypal expectations and relational development linked?
       |
       +--> SCHWARTZ-SALANT
       |      What is emerging in the interactive field between the partners?
       |      What shared chaos, mutual influence, or third-area process appears?
       |
       +--> JOHNSON EFCT
       |      What attachment cue occurred?
       |      What deeper emotion and meaning followed?
       |      What protective move did each partner make?
       |      What negative cycle formed?
       |      What reachable/responsive interaction would reorganize it?
       |
       +--> GREENBERG EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY
              What kind of emotion is present: primary adaptive,
              primary maladaptive, secondary, instrumental?
              What emotion scheme is activated?
              What marker-guided task might transform the process?
```

No single branch has automatic priority for every research question. The Laboratory should route evidence to the theory being tested.

## 58. Reconstructing a couple episode in EFCT terms

[REFLECTIVE APPLICATION]

A disciplined episode reconstruction should stay close to observable sequence before assigning attachment style or deeper theory.

### Minimum episode record

- **Context:** Where and when did the interaction occur?
- **Concrete cue:** What did one partner actually say, do, fail to do, or signal?
- **Immediate perception:** What did the other partner think the cue meant in that moment?
- **Body:** What changed physically?
- **First accessible emotion:** What feeling was consciously noticed?
- **Possible deeper emotion:** What became visible if the moment was slowed down?
- **Attachment meaning:** What did the moment seem to say about availability, worth, safety, abandonment, failure, or dependence?
- **Protective move:** What did the person do next?
- **Impact on partner:** How did the partner receive that move?
- **Partner's meaning and move:** What happened next in the loop?
- **Cycle name, if useful:** pursue-withdraw, attack-attack, withdraw-withdraw, approach-avoid, or another idiosyncratic sequence.
- **Substantive issue:** What real-world content remains even after the cycle is understood?
- **Asymmetry check:** Was there coercion, threat, deception, unilateral harm, or unequal power that should not be neutralized as mutual process?

This record is descriptive. It does not establish an attachment diagnosis.

## 59. Self-reflection implications

[REFLECTIVE APPLICATION]

EFCT is especially useful for self-reflection when it helps a person separate **what happened** from **what the nervous system and relationship history rapidly made it mean**, then reconnect that meaning to behavior and interpersonal effect.

Useful questions include:

- What cue do I treat as evidence that I do not matter, will be abandoned, will fail, or will be controlled?
- What happens in my body before I become certain of my interpretation?
- What do I do to protect myself?
- What signal does that protection send to my partner?
- What response does it tend to pull from the partner?
- How does that response confirm my original expectation?
- What would a more direct statement of my experience sound like without giving up the substantive issue?
- What kind of response from my partner would help, and is that response reasonable and safe to ask for?
- What responsibility remains mine even if my protective move makes sense?

## 60. [LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS] Eighteen-step EFCT-informed reflection protocol

The following protocol is a Laboratory tool derived from Johnson's model. It is **not** presented as a standardized Johnson assessment or therapy procedure.

1. **Choose one bounded episode.** Avoid global judgments such as "we always" or "you never."
2. **Record observable facts first.** Separate words/actions from interpretation.
3. **Name the substantive issue.** Money, sex, parenting, reliability, fidelity, time, work, housework, values, etc.
4. **Identify the first attachment-relevant cue, if there was one.** Do not force an attachment reading if none fits.
5. **Record the first automatic meaning.** What did the cue seem to say about self, other, or bond?
6. **Notice bodily activation or deactivation.** Heat, tension, collapse, numbness, racing heart, shallow breath, urge to leave.
7. **Name the visible/reaction emotion.** Anger, anxiety, irritation, contempt, flatness, certainty, despair.
8. **Explore one layer deeper without invalidating the visible emotion.** Fear, hurt, shame, grief, helplessness, longing, anger at violation, or something else.
9. **Identify the protective action tendency.** Pursue, attack, explain, persuade, demand, appease, freeze, withdraw, shut down.
10. **Describe the actual move.** What did you do or say?
11. **Infer the likely signal received by the partner, then verify rather than assume.**
12. **Map the partner's response and your next move.** Build the loop.
13. **Externalize the loop.** Give it a neutral name if that helps both people see it.
14. **Run the responsibility/asymmetry check.** Mutual cycle does not erase unilateral harm.
15. **Formulate a direct first-person message.** Include trigger, emotion, meaning, and need without making the other responsible for regulating everything.
16. **Formulate a specific, bounded reach.** Ask for a response that is observable and realistic.
17. **Process the response.** Did the partner hear it? What blocked reception? What new information emerged?
18. **Consolidate learning.** What is one early warning sign of the cycle and one agreed move that can interrupt it next time?

This protocol is for reflective inquiry and research. In situations involving abuse, coercion, severe instability, or fear of retaliation, joint vulnerability work may be unsafe and professional assessment takes priority.

---

# Part X. Guardrails, Limits, and Common Misreadings

## 61. EFCT guardrails

1. **Not all conflict is attachment conflict.** Some disagreements are primarily practical, ethical, sexual, financial, cultural, or value-based.
2. **A pursuer is not automatically anxiously attached.** Position and orientation are different levels of description.
3. **A withdrawer is not automatically avoidantly attached.** Withdrawal can arise from many states, including anxiety, shame, overwhelm, grief, or strategic disengagement.
4. **Vulnerability does not invalidate anger.** Anger may be protective, reactive, or directly adaptive to violation.
5. **Attachment explanation does not excuse harmful behavior.** Understanding function and assigning responsibility are separate tasks.
6. **Externalizing the cycle does not eliminate agency.** Partners still choose and remain accountable for actions.
7. **Mutual influence does not mean equal responsibility.** Coercion, violence, deception, and exploitation require asymmetry-sensitive formulation.
8. **Responsiveness does not mean agreement.** A partner can respond empathically while disagreeing with a conclusion or request.
9. **Secure attachment does not mean constant closeness.** Healthy bonds tolerate separateness, exploration, privacy, and ordinary frustration.
10. **Secure dependence is compatible with differentiation.** Closeness need not imply fusion.
11. **A bonding event is not a final state.** New patterns require repetition and consolidation.
12. **An attachment injury is not every hurt feeling.** Johnson uses the concept for abandonment or betrayal at a crucial moment of need that reorganizes trust in the bond.
13. **Couple therapy is not always suitable.** Ongoing violence, coercion, intimidation, active uncontained affairs, severe instability, or other safety concerns may require a different sequence of intervention.
14. **Abuse is not simply a mutual negative cycle.** A circular process formulation must not erase victimization or power.
15. **EFCT is not a personality typing system.** Its cycle positions are dynamic and contextual.
16. **EFCT is not Jungian psychology.** It can be compared with Jungian concepts, but its explanatory language is attachment and experiential-systemic process.
17. **EFCT is not Greenberg's mature Emotion-Focused Therapy.** Shared historical roots do not erase later divergence.
18. **Practice manuals do not by themselves establish comparative efficacy.** Treatment-ranking claims require direct outcome evidence and appropriate reviews.

## 62. What EFCT adds to the Jung, Self and Relationship Laboratory

EFCT adds a disciplined **micro-process map**. Jungian interpretation can be rich in meaning but may move too quickly from an emotionally charged episode to complex, projection, shadow, anima/animus, typology, or symbolic significance. EFCT forces the Laboratory to first ask what actually happened between two people in sequence.

It adds a practical distinction between:

- cue;
- attachment meaning;
- emotional activation;
- protective action;
- partner impact;

---

# Emotion-Focused Therapy: Emotion Schemes, Processing, and Transformation

## Source and Edition Note

The principal source for this file is Leslie S. Greenberg, *Emotion-Focused Therapy: Coaching Clients to Work Through Their Feelings*, Second Edition, published in Washington, DC by the American Psychological Association in 2015. The title page identifies Greenberg as author and labels the work the second edition. The copyright page gives 2015 as the publication year, identifies the American Psychological Association as publisher, and supplies ISBN 978-1-4338-1995-7 and DOI 10.1037/14692-000. The source is a 15-chapter practice-and-theory book organised into four parts: Foundations; The Arriving and Leaving Phases; Working With Specific Emotions; and Emotional Intelligence in Specific Contexts. It also contains an epilogue, appendix of exercises, references, and index. [Greenberg: title and copyright pages; Contents]

The Project PDF is a strong digital-text source rather than a scan-only document. Text extraction is generally reliable, including chapter structure, prose, tables, and most task terminology. Minor defects include line-break hyphenation, occasional encoding artefacts, and loss of some visual formatting. Tables and figures were checked against their surrounding text where conceptually important. The PDF reports 417-418 digital pages depending on the page-counting method, while the book's printed pagination runs through the index and author material. This file therefore uses the book's printed page references only where they are clear from the source and otherwise cites chapters or named sections.

The book is a contemporary experiential and emotion-process psychotherapy source. It is not a Jungian text, not an attachment research handbook, and not Susan Johnson's attachment-centred Emotionally Focused Therapy / Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy. Greenberg does discuss attachment, couple process, developmental adversity, and affect regulation, but those concepts are nested within his broader theory of emotional processing. [Greenberg: Introduction; chs. 2, 13]

The empirical claims reconstructed below are claims reported in Greenberg's 2015 book. The book cites randomized trials, process-outcome studies, task analyses, and a then-existing meta-analysis, but it is not a 2026 systematic review. Statements about treatment efficacy should therefore be read as a reconstruction of the evidence base Greenberg presented at that time, not as a current comparative efficacy verdict. [Greenberg: ch. 2, pp. 61-65]

## EFT Terminology and Identity Note

Throughout this file:

- **Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy** means the emotion-process, experiential model reconstructed from Greenberg's 2015 book.
- **Johnson attachment-centred EFT / EFCT** means Susan M. Johnson's attachment-centred Emotionally Focused Therapy and, where the couple modality is intended, Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy.
- The bare acronym **EFT** is avoided where the referent could be ambiguous.

Greenberg's book itself sometimes uses *EFT* and *emotion coaching* interchangeably to describe the therapeutic principles and their extension beyond formal psychotherapy. That source usage is preserved when relevant, but this Laboratory file does not imply that clinical Emotion-Focused Therapy is equivalent to nonclinical coaching.

## Evidence and Epistemic Status

This reconstruction distinguishes seven evidence levels:

1. **Emotion theory**: Greenberg's account of emotion as embodied, meaning-generating, action-organising information.
2. **Clinical formulation**: Greenberg's taxonomy of emotional responses, emotion schemes, needs, and process diagnosis.
3. **Therapeutic task/intervention**: marker-guided focusing, evocative unfolding, two-chair work, empty-chair work, compassionate self-soothing, and related methods.
4. **Process-outcome claim**: propositions about arousal, productive processing, adaptive emotion, and therapeutic change.
5. **Case illustration**: clinical examples used to show how the model operates. These illustrate but do not by themselves prove causal efficacy.
6. **Empirical evidence**: studies and findings cited by Greenberg.
7. **Laboratory synthesis**: cross-framework comparisons and applied maps created here. These are labelled explicitly and are not attributed to Greenberg.

Greenberg's emotion taxonomy is treated as a clinical model, not as settled universal neuroscience. Similarly, the comparisons with attachment and Jungian constructs are conceptual comparisons, not demonstrations that the constructs are identical or reducible to one another.

## Coverage of This File

This file reconstructs: emotion as an adaptive information-processing and action system; emotion schemes; needs and action tendencies; primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, and instrumental emotions; productive emotional processing; awareness, symbolisation, differentiation, regulation, acceptance, agency, and arousal; changing emotion with emotion; case formulation and marker-guided tasks; focusing, systematic evocative unfolding, two-chair and empty-chair work; self-criticism, unfinished business, fear, shame, anger, sadness, guilt where covered; therapist empathy and process guidance; narrative meaning; couple relevance; and the principal distinctions from Johnson, adult attachment theory, and Jungian concepts.

## How to Use This File

Use Greenberg's own sections first when asking what Emotion-Focused Therapy means by a term. Use the cross-framework sections only for comparison. In applied analysis, classify an emotion provisionally and functionally rather than by surface appearance. A strong emotion may be an adaptive response to a current event, an old maladaptive organisation, a secondary reaction, an instrumental expression, an attachment response, a Jungian complex constellation, or some combination. The observable event and actual interpersonal context remain part of the evidence.

---

# Part I - Greenberg's Theory of Emotion

## 1. Source and Edition Note

The source's actual chapter sequence is:

1. *Emotional Intelligence and the Purpose of Emotion*
2. *The Nature of Emotions*
3. *Distinguishing Among Varieties of Emotional Expression*
4. *The Therapeutic Relationship, Steps of Emotion Coaching, and Coach's Own Emotional Awareness*
5. *Case Formulation and Marker-Guided Interventions*
6. *Arriving at a Primary Emotion*
7. *Evaluating Whether a Painful Primary Emotion Is Healthy*
8. *Working With Primary Emotions*
9. *Accessing New Healing Emotions and Creating New Narratives*
10. *Lessons About Anger and Sadness From Emotion Coaching*
11. *Transforming Fear and Shame in Emotion Coaching*
12. *Working With Emotional Injuries: Letting Go and Forgiveness*
13. *Coaching for Emotional Intelligence in Couples*
14. *Coaching for Emotional Intelligence in Parenting*
15. *Coaching for Emotional Intelligence in Leadership*

The organisation matters. Greenberg first establishes a theory of emotion, then differentiates kinds of emotional response, then specifies therapist stance and marker-guided work, then describes how to arrive at and evaluate primary emotion, then how to transform maladaptive primary emotion, and only after that applies the framework to particular emotions and contexts. [Greenberg: Contents]

## 2. Greenberg's Core Clinical Proposition

Greenberg's central proposition is not simply that emotion should be expressed. Emotion is a fast, embodied system that evaluates what matters for well-being, prepares action, communicates significance, and organises attention and meaning. Because this system can be adaptive, maladaptive, defensive, or socially instrumental, therapy requires discrimination rather than indiscriminate emotional deepening. [Greenberg: Introduction; chs. 1-3]

A person's emotional response can carry useful information about threat, loss, violation, attachment, identity, goals, or needs. Yet experiencing an emotion does not itself establish that the emotion's appraisal is accurate in the present. The therapeutic task is to help clients become aware of emotion, regulate it when necessary, symbolize it, identify its meaning and need, determine its function, and either use it adaptively or transform it. [Greenberg: chs. 1, 3, 4, 7-9]

Greenberg therefore rejects two crude alternatives: rational control of emotion as though emotion were noise, and cathartic discharge as though venting were curative. The model instead combines an empathic relationship with process-guided work on emotion. The therapist follows the client's experience while also guiding particular processes when recognizable markers indicate that a structured task may be useful. [Greenberg: Introduction; chs. 3-5]

## 3. Emotion as an Adaptive System

Emotion is described as an evolved adaptive system that rapidly appraises events in relation to personal well-being. It can operate before deliberate reasoning. Fear can orient attention toward possible danger and ready protection or escape; anger can mobilize approach, boundary-setting, and resistance to violation; sadness can orient the person to loss, conserving resources and recruiting comfort; interest opens attention to novelty. These are tendencies, not compulsory behaviors. [Greenberg: Introduction; ch. 1]

Greenberg repeatedly links emotion to bodily change, attention, motivation, action readiness, and social communication. Emotion changes what is salient. Fear biases attention toward danger; anger toward violation or obstruction; sadness toward loss. This gives emotion organising power, but it also explains why an old emotion scheme can distort present processing when the implicit appraisal was learned under different circumstances. [Greenberg: chs. 1-3]

The adaptive origin of emotion does not imply that every present emotional response is adaptively accurate. Evolutionary usefulness at the level of the system and current usefulness of a specific response are separate questions.

## 4. Emotion as Information

Greenberg treats emotion as information about the person's relation to the environment and to his or her own goals and needs. Emotion can signal, among other things:

- danger or threat;
- violation or obstruction;
- loss;
- the state of an intimate bond;
- the state of self-worth or recognition;
- progress or failure in relation to a goal;
- a need for comfort, safety, validation, autonomy, protection, connection, or agency.

This informational role is one basis for emotional intelligence. The client learns to attend to emotion and let it inform reflection and action. But emotion does not mechanically dictate truth. In Greenberg's nine-step model, the client must still evaluate whether a primary response is healthy in the current situation. A feeling of violation is evidence that the person experiences a violation; it is not proof that the other person objectively violated him or her. [Greenberg: chs. 1, 4, 7]

**Permanent distinction:** **emotion is data, not fact.**

## 5. Emotion and Action Tendency

An emotion includes a readiness to act. In Greenberg's examples, fear tends toward protection, escape, or withdrawal from threat; anger tends toward approach, resistance, boundary-setting, or assertion; sadness can support crying, withdrawal for recuperation, or reaching for comfort after loss; disgust tends away from intrusion or contamination; interest moves toward exploration. [Greenberg: chs. 1-2]

Action tendency is part of an emotion's meaning, but it is not behavioural destiny. Emotional intelligence involves integrating bodily information with reflection, context, values, and anticipation of consequences. Greenberg is explicit that awareness and expression are different tasks. A person may need to know that he or she is angry without immediately expressing the anger, and even adaptive anger does not justify aggression. [Greenberg: ch. 10]

## 6. Emotion and Need

Needs are central in Greenberg's change model, but he does not reduce them to a fixed list of innate drives. He treats needs as emerging from affective experience, biological requirements, developmental learning, relationships, and the human processes of regulation and meaning creation. In practice the clinically important question is often: what does this painful emotion say was missing, threatened, violated, or longed for? [Greenberg: chs. 2, 8-9]

Examples in the source include needs for connection, validation, safety, protection, support, recognition, autonomy, competence, and agency. Attachment-related needs are important, especially in intimate relationships and developmental injury, but Greenberg's category of need is broader than attachment need. [Greenberg: chs. 2, 11, 13]

A useful three-level distinction is:

1. **Need signalled by emotion**: for example, protection, comfort, recognition, autonomy.
2. **Strategy for meeting the need**: for example, asking, withdrawing, asserting, seeking reassurance, self-soothing.
3. **Behaviour enacted**: the actual interpersonal or self-directed action.

The need can be legitimate while a particular strategy is ineffective or harmful. A need also does not automatically create an obligation for another person to satisfy it.

---

# Part II - Emotion Schemes

## 7. Emotion Scheme: Core Definition

The **emotion scheme** is Greenberg's fundamental unit of emotional processing. In Chapter 2 he describes it as an internal organisation shaped by experience that rapidly processes the personal significance of a situation and links perception with affective, bodily, motivational, cognitive, and behavioural response tendencies. It is not merely a belief. [Greenberg: ch. 2, "Emotion Schemes"]

The scheme operates substantially at an automatic and preconscious level. A cue is attended to and appraised; an emotion scheme is activated; and conscious emotion and need, thought, action tendency, and behaviour emerge in interaction. Greenberg's Figure 2.1 is explicitly a conceptual aid rather than a rigid linear chain. The components recursively affect one another. [Greenberg: ch. 2, Figure 2.1]

An emotion scheme may include:

- bodily sensations and visceral patterns;
- affective responses;
- perceptual and attentional tendencies;
- episodic and affective memory;
- images and sensory qualities;
- cognitive meanings and appraisals;
- action tendencies;
- needs and goals;
- expectations about self, other, and world;
- habitual behavioural responses.

Greenberg describes such schemes as partly preverbal, affective, and embodied. They can function like wordless narratives or scripts, becoming consciously articulated only when experience is symbolized. This is why therapy attends not just to propositional thought but to felt bodily experience, imagery, emotion, and action readiness. [Greenberg: ch. 2]

## 8. Emotion Scheme versus Cognitive Schema

| Dimension | Greenberg emotion scheme | Cognitive schema, in the narrower conventional sense |
|---|---|---|
| Core medium | Embodied affective processing | Primarily beliefs, rules, assumptions, expectancies |
| Bodily component | Constitutive | May be secondary or not central |
| Emotion | Constitutive | Often treated as consequence or associated response |
| Memory | Affective, episodic, sensory, semantic | Often emphasises semantic or propositional organisation |
| Action tendency | Built into emotional organisation | May be inferred from cognition |
| Need/goal | Closely linked | May be represented cognitively |
| Level | Often preconscious/preverbal as well as conscious | Commonly articulated at conscious or potentially verbal level |
| Change emphasis | Access, regulate, symbolize, activate adaptive emotion, create new meaning | Cognitive reappraisal, belief testing, behavioural learning, depending on model |

This comparison is conceptual. Greenberg's scheme includes cognition, and cognitive therapies can also incorporate embodiment and affect. The point is narrower: Greenberg does not reduce emotional organisation to thoughts. [Greenberg: ch. 2]

## 9. Emotion Scheme versus Internal Working Model

Adult attachment theory uses **working model** for stored cognitive-affective representations of attachment interactions that guide prediction, appraisal, proximity seeking, emotion regulation, and expectations of self and attachment figures. Mikulincer and Shaver describe working models as representations of attachment figures' responsiveness and of one's own value and efficacy, containing episodic memories, beliefs, affective memories, and procedural knowledge. [Mikulincer & Shaver: *Attachment in Adulthood*, chs. 1, 6-7]

An emotion scheme is broader in domain. It is an emotion-organising structure that can concern threat, shame, anger, loss, identity, competence, violation, or other personally significant themes whether or not the attachment system is central. An internal working model is attachment-specific. Both can contain affective memory, expectation, appraisal, and action tendencies, so they may interact strongly in the same episode, but they are not synonymous.

## 10. Emotion Scheme versus Jungian Complex

A Greenberg emotion scheme and a Jungian feeling-toned complex can look similar because both can be affectively charged, triggered rapidly, linked to memory, and experienced as organising perception and behaviour. The theoretical architectures are nevertheless different.

| Dimension | Greenberg emotion scheme | Jungian complex |
|---|---|---|
| Tradition | Experiential / emotion-process psychotherapy | Analytical psychology |
| Basic definition | Internal organisation of emotional processing linking appraisal, body, meaning, memory, need and action | Emotionally accentuated image of a psychic situation, relatively autonomous and partly incompatible with habitual conscious attitude |
| Formation | Lived learning, memory, affective organisation, development; can be adaptive or maladaptive | Personal experience and affective history, with Jungian theory also relating complexes to deeper archetypal organisation |
| Affective charge | Central | Central, classically "feeling-toned" |
