| Autonomy | Automatic and can feel compelling; Greenberg does not make relative psychic autonomy the defining principle | Relative autonomy is defining; active complexes can disrupt conscious intention and behave like partial personalities |
| Body | Constitutive in embodied appraisal and felt experience | Affect and physiological disturbance can accompany constellation, but body is not specified in the same emotion-scheme architecture |
| Need/action tendency | Explicitly central | Not organised by Greenberg's need/action-tendency taxonomy |
| Symbolic/archetypal dimension | Not required | Potentially central in Jungian development, especially where archetypal patterns constellate |
| Change | Emotional processing, regulation, adaptive emotion, new meaning, new narrative | Making the complex conscious, differentiating ego from it, integrating its meaning, working with projection and symbolic material within individuation |

Jung defines a feeling-toned complex as the image of a psychic situation that is strongly emotionally accentuated, has inner coherence, and possesses a relatively high degree of autonomy; he also remarks that complexes can "have us" and disturb conscious intention. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "A Review of the Complex Theory," pars. 200-202]

**Permanent distinction:** **emotion scheme ≠ Jungian complex.**

## 11. Emotion Schemes and Experience

Once activated, an emotion scheme can influence what a person notices, how a cue is interpreted, what memories are retrieved, how the body responds, what need becomes salient, and what action seems immediately compelling. Because schemes are experience-shaped, the same external event can activate different organisations in different people. [Greenberg: ch. 2]

Greenberg's model is probabilistic, not deterministic. Schemes can be revised through new experience, reflection, regulation, and repeated adaptive emotional processing. Current context also matters. A scheme-generated appraisal may be wrong, partly right, or completely appropriate to present reality.

## 12. Maladaptive Emotion Schemes

Maladaptive emotion schemes are learned organisations whose automatic responses no longer fit current circumstances well enough to support adaptive action. Greenberg especially links them to painful developmental and traumatic learning, repeated invalidation, and failures of affect regulation. Such a scheme may recreate shame, fear, abandonment pain, rage, or helplessness in contexts that only partly resemble the original conditions. [Greenberg: chs. 2-3, 7-9]

The problem is not that emotion as such is defective. The problem is that the organised response is based on old meanings and action tendencies that are not adequate to the present. Therapy therefore needs more than suppression. The old organisation has to become accessible enough to be processed and brought into contact with new emotional resources, needs, meanings, and actions.

---

# Part III - Primary, Secondary and Instrumental Emotion

## 13. Primary Emotion

In Greenberg's taxonomy, **primary emotion** is the person's first, core, immediate visceral emotional response to a situation or its implicit meaning. Primary does not mean metaphysically deepest, morally best, or factually truest. It describes process position: the first direct emotional organisation rather than a later reaction to another emotion or an expression used primarily for interpersonal effect. [Greenberg: ch. 3]

Primary emotion is divided into **adaptive** and **maladaptive** forms. This division is indispensable. A primary response can be immediate and genuinely felt while still being an old maladaptive response to the present.

## 14. Primary Adaptive Emotion

A primary adaptive emotion is an automatic first response whose implicit appraisal, expression, action tendency, and regulation fit the current stimulus sufficiently well to support adaptive action and need satisfaction. Greenberg's examples include sadness at loss that recruits comfort, fear in response to threat, anger at violation, grief that permits letting go, disgust at intrusion, and hopelessness that supports relinquishing a need that cannot be met. He also allows complex emotions to be primary if they are the first response. [Greenberg: ch. 3, pp. 73-74]

Primary adaptive emotions are generally situation-responsive. When the situation is resolved, the emotion tends to change or fade. Clinically, the task is usually to help the client become aware of the emotion, symbolize it, accept it, extract its information, and use its action tendency intelligently rather than automatically acting it out.

## 15. Primary Maladaptive Emotion

A primary maladaptive emotion is also an automatic first response, but it reflects old unresolved organisation more than current reality. Greenberg describes these responses as having often developed under traumatic, invalidating, neglectful, or dysregulated relational conditions. They may once have represented the best available adaptation but now fail to guide effective current action. [Greenberg: ch. 3, pp. 74-75]

Examples in the source include debilitating fear, insecurity, lonely abandonment sadness, shame and humiliation, destructive rage, and unresolved grief. These states can feel old, familiar, stuck, disorganising, and disproportionately persistent. They may be evoked by external cues or internal memories and can feel as if they have a life of their own.

Greenberg at one point calls such responses a person's most fundamental or "true" feelings. That wording must not be converted into the claim that primary maladaptive emotion is epistemically truthful. Within his own clinical logic, the decisive question is whether the emotional response fits present reality and supports adaptive action. A maladaptive primary emotion can be completely genuine as experience and simultaneously misleading as a guide to the current situation.

## 16. Secondary Reactive Emotion

A **secondary emotion** is an emotional response to another emotion, thought, appraisal, or situation. It often functions defensively or reactively and can obscure a primary process. Greenberg gives examples such as hopelessness covering anger, resentment covering hurt, anger covering fear or shame, and sadness covering anger. He is equally clear that these are examples, not fixed formulas. Any surface emotion, including anger or sadness, can be primary, secondary, or instrumental depending on its function in that moment. [Greenberg: ch. 3]

Secondary emotion is clinically important because treating only the surface response can leave the primary need or maladaptive core untouched. Yet the secondary response must first be acknowledged rather than dismissed, because it is part of the client's actual experience and may have a protective function.

## 17. Secondary Emotion Is Not False Emotion

Secondary emotion is a real emotional event. "Secondary" indicates its relation to another process, not falsity. It may be intense, embodied, and subjectively compelling. The therapist's task is to understand what function it serves, what preceded it, and whether it is blocking, regulating, communicating, or reacting to another emotional state.

Calling secondary emotion "fake" would distort Greenberg's model and encourage therapists or self-observers to devalue exactly the experience that needs to be understood.

## 18. Instrumental Emotion

**Instrumental emotion** is expressed, displayed, or sometimes experienced in a way that functions to influence another person's response. Greenberg includes deliberate and non-deliberate forms. Instrumentality can become habitual and does not always imply conscious deception. [Greenberg: ch. 3]

Examples in the source include crying to obtain sympathy or emotional display used to obtain a desired interpersonal outcome. The appropriate therapeutic response is not to shame the person for "manipulation" but to clarify the interpersonal function, increase awareness of the impact on others, and support more direct expression of the underlying goal or need where possible.

## 19. Four-Way Emotion Taxonomy

| Emotion type | Definition | Adaptive status | Typical function | Therapist response | Main risk of misclassification |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Primary adaptive | First/direct emotional response that fits present situation and mobilises useful action | Adaptive in current context | Inform, orient, protect, connect, grieve, assert | Access, accept, symbolize, use information and action tendency | Assuming strong emotion is maladaptive because it is unpleasant |
| Primary maladaptive | First/direct response organised by old unresolved learning and poorly fitted to present | Maladaptive in current context | Re-enact old fear, shame, abandonment, rage, grief, insecurity | Regulate if needed, access fully enough for processing, identify need, transform with adaptive emotion | Treating intensity or immediacy as proof of current truth |
| Secondary reactive | Emotion about or in reaction to another emotion, thought, or process | Variable | Defend, cope, cover, protest, react | Validate, track sequence, explore what precedes or is obscured | Calling it fake or assuming one fixed underlying emotion |
| Instrumental | Emotion expression functioning to influence another | Variable | Shape interpersonal response or obtain outcome | Clarify function, impact, goal and more direct alternatives | Equating instrumentality with conscious manipulation |

[Greenberg: ch. 3]

---

# Part IV - Awareness, Regulation and Emotional Processing

## 20. Emotional Awareness

Awareness begins with attending to what is actually happening in the body and affective field. Clients may display emotion nonverbally without consciously knowing what they feel. Greenberg therefore directs attention to facial expression, posture, gesture, voice, breath, visceral sensation, and emerging impulses. The next step is to differentiate and describe the experience rather than collapse it into a global "bad" feeling. [Greenberg: chs. 3-4]

Awareness is not the same as expression. A person can accurately register anger and choose not to express it immediately. Awareness provides information; expression is an interpersonal action with consequences.

## 21. Symbolising Emotion

Symbolisation means putting felt experience into words or another representational form so that it can be reflected on, communicated, and organised. Greenberg explicitly allows that symbolisation can occur through words, image, movement, or other expressive media, although verbal symbolisation is central in therapy. [Greenberg: ch. 3, "Symbolization"]

Symbolisation is not mere labeling. The person searches for a formulation that fits the felt experience. In Greenberg's dialectical-constructivist account, the bodily-emotional experience constrains meaning but does not mechanically determine it. The act of symbolising helps create meaning. This makes emotional processing neither purely bottom-up nor purely cognitive.

**Distinction:** Greenbergian symbolisation is not Jungian amplification. It is the articulation and meaning-making of lived emotional experience, not a method of relating an image to mythological, cultural, or archetypal parallels.

## 22. Emotion Differentiation

Differentiation is the movement from global, undifferentiated distress toward more specific emotional meaning. "I feel awful" may differentiate into hurt, fear, shame, grief, anger, disappointment, loneliness, guilt, or combinations. The value of differentiation is functional: different emotions imply different appraisals, needs, and action tendencies. [Greenberg: chs. 3-4]

Differentiation is not a demand to force every experience into a rigid emotion label. Greenberg treats emotional experience as complex, layered, and sometimes blended.

## 23. Bodily Felt Experience

Greenberg's approach is explicitly embodied. Primary emotion is accessed through visceral experience, bodily sensation, nonverbal expression, imagery, and action readiness, not only through verbal report. The book draws directly on Eugene Gendlin's focusing tradition when describing work with vague or unclear felt sense and the therapeutic value of finding words or images that "fit" bodily experience. [Greenberg: ch. 5, Experiential Focusing]

The body is therefore an important source of data, but bodily intensity does not establish psychological truth. A bodily response can be adaptive, maladaptive, secondary, or part of an interruption process. It must be interpreted in context.

## 24. Experiencing versus Intellectualising

Greenberg repeatedly contrasts live, embodied emotional experiencing with talking *about* emotion from a detached distance. Intellectualising can function as avoidance when it keeps the person from contacting the relevant emotional process. [Greenberg: Introduction; chs. 3-6]

This is not an anti-cognitive position. Thought, appraisal, narrative, semantic knowledge, and reflective meaning are components of emotion schemes and therapeutic change. Greenberg's process research argument is specifically that arousal plus reflective processing is more useful than arousal alone. Emotion needs to be experienced and then made sense of.

## 25. Emotional Arousal

Emotional activation matters because an emotion cannot be transformed if it is never sufficiently active to be accessed, symbolized, and revised. Greenberg's 2015 review reports process-outcome studies in which higher emotional arousal, especially when coupled with reflection or deeper processing, predicted better outcomes in certain treatments. [Greenberg: ch. 2, pp. 62-64]

But Greenberg is equally explicit that arousal alone is inadequate. Some arousal is unproductive, stale, disorganising, or retraumatising. The clinical target is productive emotional processing, not maximum intensity.

## 26. Optimal Emotional Arousal

The source describes a workable level of activation rather than using the later popular term "window of tolerance." The person needs enough emotional contact that the experience is felt and informative, but not so much that attention, symbolisation, relationship contact, and reflective capacity collapse. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 9]

Greenberg cites process work suggesting a non-linear relation between arousal and benefit and emphasises moderate-to-high arousal that remains facilitative. This is best understood as an empirical and clinical claim within the 2015 model, not as a universal threshold.

## 27. Overwhelm

When a client is flooded, cannot maintain contact with the therapist, cannot respond to intervention, is in crisis, has a history of losing control or aggression, or uses destructive coping such as self-harm or substance misuse, Greenberg argues for down-regulation rather than immediate intensification. [Greenberg: ch. 3, Table 3.1]

Methods in the book include slowing the process, breathing, creating working distance, self-soothing, shifting attention, and establishing safety. In trauma work, evocation without sufficient regulation can become nonproductive or retraumatising.

## 28. Under-Engagement

At the other pole, some clients avoid, intellectualise, constrict, distract from, or interrupt emotion. Here the task may be to approach and deepen the experience. Markers such as vague felt sense, problematic reaction, blocked feeling, self-interruption, or unfinished business guide specific interventions. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 5-6]

The key decision is functional: does the client need more contact with emotion or more regulation of it?

## 29. Emotional Regulation

Emotion regulation in Greenberg is the capacity to keep emotional experience workable enough to be used. It can involve awareness, acceptance, breathing, self-soothing, working distance, attentional shifts, imagery, cognitive orientation to emotion as information, and behavioural strategies. Regulation can occur before, during, or after deeper experiential work. [Greenberg: chs. 1, 3, 9]

Regulation is not always reduction. Sometimes an underregulated state needs calming; sometimes an overcontrolled person needs greater access and permission. Regulation serves flexible engagement with emotion.

## 30. Regulation versus Suppression

| Dimension | Regulation | Suppression |
|---|---|---|
| Aim | Keep emotion usable, tolerable and context-sensitive | Inhibit or conceal emotional experience/expression |
| Relation to awareness | Usually increases awareness and choice | Can reduce awareness or push experience out of focal attention |
| Typical Greenberg use | Working distance, breathing, self-soothing, acceptance, reorientation | Sometimes strategically useful for immediate behaviour, but not equivalent to processing |
| Risk | Over-regulation can become avoidance | Chronic suppression can preserve unresolved processes and require effort |
| End point | Flexible experiencing and action | Absence or concealment of expression does not itself produce transformation |

[Greenberg: chs. 1, 3, 9]

## 31. Acceptance of Emotion

Acceptance is an open and exploratory stance toward emotional experience: allowing oneself to notice what is present without immediately condemning the self for feeling it or trying to eradicate it. Greenberg treats acceptance as one criterion of productive emotional processing. [Greenberg: ch. 3]

Acceptance is not approval of every interpretation or behaviour. A person can accept "I feel furious" while rejecting aggression; accept "I feel abandoned" while investigating whether abandonment actually occurred; accept shame while challenging the self-condemnation embedded in it.

---

# Part V - Changing Emotion With Emotion

## 32. "Changing Emotion with Emotion"

This is one of the defining propositions of Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy. A maladaptive emotional response is not expected to change durably through argument, suppression, positive thinking, or insight alone. The old emotion scheme must be activated sufficiently for its organisation to become accessible, while a new adaptive emotional response is also evoked and brought into contact with it. [Greenberg: Introduction; ch. 9]

Greenberg identifies empowered anger, grief/sadness, and compassion or self-compassion as especially important adaptive responses for transforming core shame, fear, and the sadness of lonely abandonment. The exact transforming emotion depends on the person's meaning and need. Shame related to violation may be transformed through protective anger and self-compassion; fear may be changed through empowered protection and anger; abandonment pain may be changed through grieving, self-soothing, connection, or compassionate care. [Greenberg: chs. 9, 11]

The mechanism is not emotional substitution in the sense of simply "thinking positive." The client first contacts and accepts the maladaptive state, identifies its need and negative organisation, then accesses an alternative embodied response with a different action tendency and meaning. Repeated new experience can become integrated into memory and narrative, altering how future situations are organised. Greenberg links this to memory reconsolidation research, but his clinical argument does not require treating reconsolidation as a complete neuroscientific explanation of psychotherapy. [Greenberg: ch. 9]

A common process sequence described in the source is:

**global or secondary distress → primary maladaptive fear/shame/pain → heartfelt need → primary adaptive anger, grief, compassion or self-soothing → new meaning and action tendency.**

This is a model of change, not a mandatory sequence for every client.

## 33. Transformation versus Regulation

Regulation and transformation overlap but are not the same operation.

- **Regulation** changes the intensity, distance, duration, or manageability of an emotional state so the person can remain engaged and functional.
- **Transformation** changes the organisation of a maladaptive response by activating a different adaptive emotional response, need, meaning, and action tendency while the old state is accessible.

Regulation often makes transformation possible. A person overwhelmed by trauma-related fear may first need breathing, distancing, therapist coregulation, and self-soothing. Once the fear is tolerable, it can be explored, and adaptive anger or compassion may become available. Conversely, transformation itself can improve later regulation by creating new memories, self-positions, and action tendencies. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 9]

## 34. Adaptive Emotional Resources

Source-supported adaptive resources include:

- **empowered/assertive anger** at violation, which supports protection, boundary, entitlement to respectful treatment, and agency;
- **sadness and grief**, which support acknowledgment of loss, relinquishment, comfort-seeking, and reorganisation after what cannot be recovered;
- **compassion/self-compassion**, which counter harsh self-attack and support soothing of suffering;
- **disgust**, in some trauma examples, which helps reject degrading or violating treatment;
- **warmth, affection, joy, interest, pride, strength, and calm**, where accessible as alternative emotional states;
- **a felt sense of need and deservingness**, which can itself mobilise new emotion.

Greenberg does not prescribe one "resource emotion" for one problem. The therapist searches for an adaptive response whose appraisal and action tendency alter the person's position.

## 35. Anger as Transforming Emotion

Adaptive anger is particularly important when fear, shame, helplessness, or victimisation have organised the self into submission. In Greenberg's trauma examples, anger at violation can replace or combine with fear's tendency to flee, supporting protection, assertion, and a changed view of self. [Greenberg: ch. 9]

The distinction from aggression is fundamental. Greenberg's Chapter 10 states that awareness of anger and expression of anger are different tasks; venting can escalate conflict. Healthy anger informs a boundary or a wrong. How, whether, when, and toward whom it is expressed requires judgement.

## 36. Compassion / Self-Compassion

Compassion is treated as a distinct adaptive emotional response involving sensitivity to suffering and a wish to relieve it. Greenberg uses compassionate self-soothing when clients are overwhelmed by self-condemnation, emotional anguish, or deprivation. A client may find it easier first to feel compassion toward a suffering child or another person, then transfer that stance toward the self. [Greenberg: chs. 5, 9]

In Greenberg's terms, compassion can change shame, fear, and painful sadness by bringing a different emotional organisation into contact with the suffering state. This is not an importation of a separate compassion-focused therapy model; it is the use of compassion inside Greenberg's own transformation framework.

## 37. Sadness and Grieving

Adaptive sadness acknowledges loss. It can support crying, reaching for comfort, withdrawing to recuperate, and ultimately letting go. Greenberg repeatedly distinguishes healthy grieving from overwhelmed distress or stale depressive suffering. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 7, 10]

Grieving is not emotional intensification for its own sake. It involves allowing loss to be emotionally real, identifying what mattered, receiving or creating comfort where possible, and reorganising goals when something cannot be restored.

## 38. Fear Transformation

Fear can be adaptive when it tracks current danger. It becomes a target of transformation when old threat learning dominates current situations. Greenberg's fear work therefore first asks whether danger is present and whether the fear is healthy. If the fear is maladaptive, it is evoked in a safe enough context, regulated, linked to its underlying need for safety/protection, and brought into contact with new protective resources such as anger, disgust, self-soothing, adult agency, or supportive relationship. [Greenberg: chs. 7, 9, 11]

## 39. Shame Transformation

Shame is treated substantially in Chapter 11. In Greenberg's case material, chronic invalidation, ridicule, abuse, and internalised derogation produce a maladaptive sense of defectiveness and unlovability. Shame can itself interrupt emotional awareness by making need, vulnerability, or pain feel humiliating. [Greenberg: ch. 11]

Transformation involves empathic safety; explicit recognition and symbolisation of the shame; access to the pain and legitimate unmet need beneath it; and adaptive responses such as anger at unfair treatment, self-compassion, self-validation, protection, and connection. Greenberg's example of a client abused and invalidated by parents shows shame becoming less dominant as she accesses anger at violation, grief at lost care, compassion for the deprived child, and a new sense of worth.

## 40. Emotional Transformation Is Not Catharsis

Greenberg expressly rejects the idea that venting or intense expression automatically cures emotion. Expression can heighten contact, communicate intensity, recruit validation, or make action tendencies explicit, but change requires productive processing. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 10]

Catharsis is therefore not the mechanism. The clinically relevant sequence includes awareness, regulation, symbolisation, need, adaptive emotional response, new meaning, and consolidation. In Chapter 10 Greenberg notes that intense expression may sometimes interfere with experiencing and that the therapist should return attention inward after expression to ask what is felt and what the experience means.

---

# Part VI - Markers, Tasks and Process Guidance

## 41. What Is a Marker?

A **marker** is a recognizable in-session process indicator that identifies a particular kind of emotional-processing difficulty and suggests that a corresponding therapeutic task may be useful. Marker-guided work is a defining feature of Greenberg's model. The marker is not a psychiatric diagnosis and does not classify the person. It identifies what is happening in the client's process now. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

Examples include a vague or unclear felt sense, a puzzling problematic reaction, a self-critical split, a self-interruption, unfinished business with a significant other, and states of vulnerability or anguish. Each points toward a different process rather than toward a generic instruction to "feel more."

## 42. Marker-Guided Intervention

The clinical logic is:

**client process → marker → task offer → experiential engagement → resolution process.**

The therapist listens for a marker, judges whether the alliance and client readiness are sufficient, offers a task rather than imposing it, then tracks the moment-to-moment process. The task is adjusted according to the client's response. This makes the model structured without being a fixed protocol. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

Greenberg's marker logic is one answer to the question, "How does the therapist know whether to access, regulate, express, symbolize, or transform an emotion?" The answer is not an abstract rule about emotion categories alone. It requires observing the client, the alliance, level of arousal, emotional productivity, readiness, and the specific process problem.

## 43. Tasks

A **task** is a structured experiential form of work designed for a particular process marker. Tasks organise attention and action so that the client can do something psychologically different in session. They are not techniques to be applied mechanically regardless of client state. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

Greenberg's major task families in this source include empathic affirmation, experiential focusing, systematic evocative unfolding, two-chair dialogue for self-evaluative conflict, two-chair enactment for self-interruption, empty-chair work for unfinished business, and compassionate self-soothing for anguish. The book also notes additional narrative and rupture-related markers developed elsewhere, but it does not present them as a complete task manual.

## 44. Task Resolution

For the principal tasks in Chapter 5, Greenberg gives characteristic resolution end states rather than a single universal definition of completion. Examples include a bodily felt shift after focusing, a new view of self-in-world after evocative unfolding, self-acceptance and integration after self-critical two-chair work, expression and empowerment after self-interruption work, and affirmation plus understanding, forgiveness, or accountable letting-go after unfinished-business work. [Greenberg: ch. 5, Table 5.1]

A resolution marker is therefore task-specific. Emotional intensity alone is not resolution.

## 45. Two-Chair Work: Core Purpose

Greenberg uses two-chair methods when different processes within the self are in conflict or when one part of the self interrupts another. Two-chair work makes an internal relationship experiential and observable by assigning different positions to different parts or voices. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

The most developed applications in this source are:

- **self-evaluative split**, especially self-criticism;
- **self-interruption split**, where a person actively constricts, suppresses, or blocks emotion;
- variants such as decisional conflict, attributional splits, internalised critical voices, anxiety-related catastrophising, and depression-related self-attack.

These are not all the same task. The process and resolution differ depending on the marker.

## 46. Self-Critical Split

A self-critical split involves one part of the person attacking, judging, controlling, or contemptuously evaluating another part, which Greenberg calls the experiencing self. The critic may repeat internalised parental or social messages, but the clinical focus is on the current self-to-self process. [Greenberg: ch. 5, pp. 126-129]

The therapist helps make the criticism concrete and specific enough to evoke the experiencing self's actual emotional reaction. The experiencing self may initially collapse into hopelessness, powerlessness, shame, or fear. The crucial movement is not merely arguing against the critic. It is accessing the primary maladaptive pain, identifying the need inside it, and helping the experiencing self assert that need. As the self becomes more resilient and assertive, the critic can soften into concern or compassion. Resolution is associated with greater self-acceptance and integration.

**Non-equivalence:** the critical voice is not automatically the Jungian shadow. It is a specific self-evaluative process within Greenberg's formulation.

## 47. Two-Chair Process

A source-faithful simplified sequence is:

1. identify the self-evaluative split;
2. place the critical position in one chair and encourage concrete criticism;
3. move to the experiencing-self chair and attend to the immediate bodily-emotional reaction;
4. differentiate secondary global distress from more primary shame, fear, hurt, anger, or need;
5. access the need inside the painful state;
6. support assertion of the experiencing self toward the critic;
7. continue the dialogue until the critic softens or the conflict reorganises;
8. consolidate a more self-accepting position.

This is a reconstruction of the flow described in Chapter 5, not a substitute for clinical training. Greenberg explicitly notes that chair work can sometimes be done conversationally rather than through enactment when enactment is too difficult or the critic is too harsh.

## 48. Self-Interruption Split

Self-interruption differs from self-criticism. The marker is an action against experience rather than primarily a negative evaluation: tightening, choking back tears, numbing, going blank, holding the breath, shutting down, or imposing an injunction such as "don't feel." [Greenberg: ch. 5, pp. 129-131]

The therapist helps the client enact how the interruption is accomplished, including muscular or verbal aspects, so the automatic block becomes conscious. The protective intention of the interruption is respected. Once the client experiences himself or herself as the agent doing the interrupting rather than as a passive victim of a mysterious block, the suppressed experience can be approached. Resolution involves de-automatisation, access to the blocked emotion, and increased capacity to express or use it.

## 49. Conflict Split

Greenberg's split framework includes opposing internal positions, such as decisional conflict. A person may be torn between staying and leaving a relationship, pursuing and withdrawing, risking and protecting, or other incompatible courses. The therapeutic aim is not to have the therapist choose. The dialogue helps each position become emotionally explicit so their underlying concerns and needs can be differentiated and reorganised. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

Not every internal conflict is best treated with two chairs. The presence of a recognisable split marker and the client's readiness guide task selection.

## 50. Two-Chair versus Jungian Active Imagination

Two-chair work can superficially resemble dialogue with inner figures, but its theory and task logic differ sharply from Jungian active imagination.

| Dimension | Greenberg two-chair work | Jungian active imagination |
|---|---|---|
| Starting point | Identified process marker such as self-criticism, conflict or interruption | Affect, dream image, fantasy, inner figure, mood, or spontaneous unconscious material |
| Theory | Experiential task analysis and emotion-process psychotherapy | Analytical psychology; relation between ego-consciousness and unconscious contents |
| Parts/figures | Functionally defined self-positions or voices in a current task | Images or figures are approached as products of the unconscious with relative autonomy and symbolic meaning |
| Structure | Therapist actively guides chair positions and process | Ego engages material while allowing the unconscious to develop; form may be image, dialogue, writing, movement, painting |
| Goal | Access emotion/need, reorganise self-process, soften conflict or interruptive pattern | Confront and integrate unconscious material, alter relation between conscious and unconscious, contribute to transcendent function/individuation |
| Resolution | Task-specific experiential change | Not a pre-specified chair-task end state; integration includes symbolic and moral implications |
| Safety | Requires alliance and readiness | Jung explicitly warns the method can be dangerous and, where possible, should be used with expert supervision |

[Greenberg: ch. 5] [Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "The Transcendent Function," pars. 131, 167-171, 189]

## 51. Unfinished Business

**Unfinished business** is Greenberg's term for lingering unresolved emotion toward a developmentally significant other, commonly a parent, but also other important people. The marker often appears as hurt, blame, complaint, resentment, longing, anger, sadness, abandonment pain, invalidation, or diminished identity. [Greenberg: ch. 5, pp. 131-135]

The term is functional and relational. It does not mean that every unresolved relationship constitutes a Jungian complex, and it does not imply that the current partner is simply a substitute for a parent.

## 52. Empty-Chair Task

In empty-chair work the significant other is imagined in an empty chair and the client speaks directly to that representation. The task activates the client's internal representation of the other and the associated emotional response. Depending on readiness, the client may role-reverse and speak from the imagined other's position. [Greenberg: chs. 5, 12]

The process commonly includes:

- activating the relational memory;
- differentiating secondary blame or complaint from primary anger, sadness, fear, shame, or pain;
- expressing adaptive emotion where appropriate;
- accessing unmet needs and what the person deserved or required;
- developing a more empowered self-position;
- revising the view of self and/or other;
- moving toward accountable letting-go, understanding, forgiveness, or some combination.

In trauma-related work, Greenberg emphasises readiness, safety, and regulation. If the imagined other is overwhelming, role reversal may be inappropriate. The therapist can maintain distance, work with the client's own experience, or support selected statements without forcing a full dialogue.

## 53. Resolution of Unfinished Business

Resolution is not synonymous with reconciliation or forgiveness. Greenberg's Table 5.1 explicitly includes several possible outcomes: affirming the self, understanding the other, forgiving, or holding the other accountable. Chapter 12 distinguishes forgiveness from letting go and recognises that the injured person may need to release an impossible demand without declaring the harm acceptable. [Greenberg: chs. 5, 12]

A central process is that the client moves from repetitive complaint or injury toward differentiated primary emotion, explicit unmet need, and a changed position toward self and other. The injury can remain morally wrong while becoming less psychologically controlling.

## 54. Empty Chair versus Active Imagination

Both methods can involve an absent or imaginal figure, but the resemblance should not obscure differences.

- **Greenberg empty chair** begins with a defined unfinished-business marker, uses an imagined significant other, and aims at emotional resolution through expression, need access, changed self-position, and revised representation of the relationship.
- **Jungian active imagination** begins from unconscious material and seeks a sustained encounter between ego-consciousness and the autonomous symbolic process. The figure need not represent an actual person, and its significance may extend beyond biographical relationship repair.

The empty-chair task is therefore a structured psychotherapy task. Active imagination is a broader Jungian method of engaging the unconscious. [Greenberg: chs. 5, 12] [Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "The Transcendent Function"]

## 55. Empty Chair versus Johnson Enactment

Greenberg empty-chair work and Johnson enactment both make emotional experience active in the room, but their interpersonal units differ.

- **Greenberg empty chair**: the client dialogues with an imagined significant other to process unfinished emotion, unmet need, injury, and representation.
- **Johnson enactment**: a newly deepened attachment-related experience is shaped into direct communication to an actual partner or family member, or sometimes an imagined attachment figure in individual work, so the interaction itself becomes a corrective attachment event.

In Johnson's couple model, enactments are embedded in restructuring a negative interactional cycle and building accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement. Greenberg's empty-chair resolution can occur entirely within an individual therapy session without restructuring a live dyadic cycle. [Greenberg: chs. 5, 12] [Johnson: *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, chs. 4, 8; *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 2-3, 6]

## 56. Focusing

Experiential focusing is offered for a marker of **unclear feeling**: the client senses that something is wrong but experience remains vague, external, abstract, or difficult to symbolize. The therapist invites attention to the bodily felt sense, helps the client describe it, tests words or images against the felt experience, and tracks whether a bodily "felt shift" occurs. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

The goal is not to impose an emotion label. It is to move from an unclear embodied whole toward a more symbolised and usable meaning. Greenberg explicitly situates this task in the Gendlin focusing tradition.

## 57. Evocative Unfolding

**Systematic evocative unfolding** is used for a **problematic reaction point**: a specific episode in which the client reacted in a puzzling, surprising, or disproportionate way. The therapist helps the client re-enter the episode vividly, slow it down, reconstruct situational and internal details, and locate the point at which the reaction emerged. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

The process accesses implicit meaning and memory. The end state in Greenberg's task table is a new view of self-in-the-world functioning. It is not a search for an abstract hidden cause detached from the lived episode.

## 58. Problematic Reaction Points

A problematic reaction point is identifiable when the client effectively says, "I don't understand why I reacted that way." The puzzlement itself is the marker. Rather than explaining it from theory, the therapist unfolds the sequence until the emotional trigger, appraisal, bodily response, and meaning become experientially accessible. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

## 59. Narrative Retelling / Meaning Work

Greenberg is not anti-narrative. Emotion schemes contain narrative organisation, and Chapter 9 makes new narrative a final consolidation step. Emotional experience gains full personal meaning when the client can tell what was felt, toward whom, in relation to what issue or need, and what this now means for identity and future action. [Greenberg: chs. 2, 9]

The sequencing matters: meaning-making should not replace emotional contact. Greenberg's clinical preference is often to evoke and process emotion first and then ask what the experience means. The book also notes narrative markers such as the same-old story, untold story, empty story, and broken story, each with different process implications. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

---

# Part VII - Therapist Stance, Case Formulation and Change Processes

## 60. Empathy

Empathy is both a relationship condition and an active information-processing stance. Greenberg's therapist listens for the client's felt meaning, reflects and conjectures tentatively, tracks nonverbal affect, validates vulnerable experience, and helps put words to what is only partly formulated. [Greenberg: Introduction; ch. 4]

The empathic relationship is considered curative in its own right and also the necessary context for deeper task work. Emotional work without a sufficient relational base can be harmful, especially when clients are reluctant, traumatised, or poorly regulated.

## 61. Process Guidance

Greenberg combines **person-centred following** with **process-directive guidance**. The therapist does not claim expert knowledge of what the client "really" feels or what an experience ultimately means. The expertise lies in recognising process and facilitating methods that can help clients access, regulate, symbolise, differentiate, and transform emotion. [Greenberg: Introduction; ch. 4]

This is why the model can be both collaborative and directive. The therapist may suggest a chair task, focus attention on bodily expression, slow a sequence down, or ask for a need, while treating the client's response as the next piece of data.

## 62. Following versus Leading

| Following | Leading/process guidance |
|---|---|
| Stay close to the client's current experience | Invite attention to a productive edge of experience |
| Reflect meaning and feeling | Propose a task when a marker is present |
| Respect pace and readiness | Heighten, focus, differentiate or regulate when needed |
| Let client meaning remain primary | Use process knowledge to structure exploration |
| Correct through client feedback | Remain only slightly ahead, recalibrating continually |

Greenberg's Chapter 10 formulation is useful: follow the client's experience and lead only as necessary, remaining roughly "half or one step" ahead rather than imposing an interpretation.

## 63. Therapist Responsiveness

Intervention is fitted to the moment. Important variables include the marker, whether emotion is over- or under-regulated, whether expression is fresh or stale, whether arousal is productive, whether primary emotion has been reached, whether the primary response is adaptive or maladaptive, the state of the alliance, and the client's willingness to engage. [Greenberg: chs. 3-6]

Responsiveness means that identical surface behaviour can call for different intervention. Anger can need validation, deeper exploration, regulation, assertive expression, or transformation depending on its function.

## 64. Therapeutic Alliance

Greenberg treats the alliance as more than a platform for techniques. Empathic attunement, acceptance, collaboration, and presence are change processes. The therapist can offer a new emotional experience of being understood and can help coregulate difficult affect. [Greenberg: Introduction; chs. 2, 4]

The alliance is not automatically an attachment bond in the technical sense of attachment theory. Attachment can be relevant, but Greenberg's relationship model is broader and rooted in experiential-humanistic psychotherapy.

## 65. Safety and Emotional Work

Safety means enough relational security and emotional regulation for the client to approach difficult experience without becoming overwhelmed or coerced. It does not mean the absence of challenge. Greenberg's therapist can heighten emotional contact, but only within collaboration and with continual attention to the client's capacity to use the intervention. [Greenberg: chs. 3-5, 11]

## 66. Greenberg's Case Formulation

Greenberg describes a process-sensitive formulation that follows what is most painful or poignant rather than constructing a fixed explanatory narrative in advance. He uses the metaphor of a **pain compass**. Initial formulation attends to presenting problems, poignant/painful experience, emotional-processing style, and the unfolding emotion-based narrative. [Greenberg: ch. 5, pp. 119-121]

As therapy progresses, formulation focuses on six elements remembered as **MENSIT**:

- **M**arkers;
- **E**motion, especially core schemes;
- **N**eeds;
- **S**econdary emotions;
- **I**nterruptions;
- **T**hemes.

The formulation links the presenting problem with triggering stimuli, behavioural avoidances, and consequences, while remaining revisable as new emotional meanings emerge.

## 67. Process Diagnosis versus Psychiatric Diagnosis

Greenberg explicitly privileges **process diagnosis** over **person diagnosis** for task selection. Process diagnosis asks what is occurring in the client's emotional process now. A marker such as self-interruption or unfinished business is not a disorder label. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 5]

This does not imply that psychiatric diagnosis is irrelevant to clinical care. It means that Greenberg's moment-to-moment intervention logic is organised around process states rather than around applying the same technique to everyone who shares a diagnostic category.

## 68. Moment-by-Moment Assessment

The therapist continually tracks voice, facial expression, gesture, semantic content, bodily signals, emotional shifts, interruptions, and the effect of interventions. Greenberg also includes the therapist's own emotional response as one possible source of information, provided it is used reflexively rather than projected onto the client. [Greenberg: chs. 3-5]

## 69. Selecting Interventions

A source-grounded selection map is:

**unclear felt sense → experiential focusing**  
**puzzling specific reaction → systematic evocative unfolding**  
**self-critical evaluation → two-chair dialogue**  
**active blocking of experience → two-chair self-interruption enactment**  
**lingering unresolved emotion toward significant other → empty-chair work**  
**vulnerable self-depletion → empathic affirmation**  
**anguish with little self-soothing → compassionate self-soothing**

Selection remains collaborative and contingent on safety, readiness, and how the process unfolds. [Greenberg: ch. 5, Table 5.1]

## 70. What Changes in Emotion-Focused Therapy?

Successful change in Greenberg's model can occur at several linked levels:

- increased awareness of emotional signals;
- improved differentiation and symbolisation;
- greater acceptance and regulation;
- access to primary emotion beneath reactive distress;
- discrimination between adaptive and maladaptive primary responses;
- access to unmet need and adaptive action tendency;
- softening of self-criticism and interruption;
- transformation of maladaptive emotion by adaptive emotion;
- new self-soothing, assertion, grieving, or relational capacities;
- changed self/other meanings and emotion-schematic organisation;
- new autobiographical narrative;
- altered behaviour in the world.

The model therefore does not define change as "feeling more." It defines change as increasingly flexible, productive emotional processing and reorganisation. [Greenberg: chs. 2-9]

## 71. Emotional Processing

**Emotional processing** is not synonymous with experiencing strong affect. Greenberg's most explicit operational account is **productive emotional processing**, in which primary emotion is contacted in a way that permits either use of adaptive information or transformation of maladaptive emotion. [Greenberg: ch. 3]

The seven criteria of productive processing are:

1. attending;
2. symbolisation;
3. congruence;
4. acceptance;
5. regulation;
6. agency;
7. differentiation.

All need to be more present than absent for the process to count as productive in the measure described by Greenberg. This formulation is important because it directly separates emotional processing from unstructured arousal or catharsis.

## 72. Meaning Creation

Meaning is created when affective experience is symbolized and organised into an intelligible relation among event, self, other, need, and action. Greenberg's dialectical-constructivist position treats emotion as constraining meaning without mechanically dictating it. [Greenberg: chs. 2-3]

New emotional experience changes what meanings are available. A client who moves from shame to protective anger may construct a different account of the same history: from "I was defective" to "I was treated unfairly." Narrative consolidation then helps stabilise the new organisation.

## 73. Need Articulation

Accessing a heartfelt need is one of the pivotal steps between maladaptive pain and adaptive emotion. The need gives direction to what the person lacked or required and can mobilise anger, grief, compassion, connection, or agency. [Greenberg: chs. 8-9]

Need articulation does not mean that the therapist promises the need can now be satisfied externally. In many injuries the original need cannot be met retroactively. Change may require grieving the loss, self-soothing, seeking present support, setting a boundary, or accepting that another person cannot provide what is wanted.

## 74. Agency

Agency is one of Greenberg's seven criteria for productive processing. The person experiences "I am having this emotion" rather than being wholly possessed by it or assigning total responsibility for it to another. Agency also means becoming an active participant in change. [Greenberg: ch. 3]

Greenberg does not use agency to deny actual mistreatment. A person can recognise that another behaved abusively and still develop agency in relation to his or her own response, choices, and protective action.

## 75. Self-Compassion / Self-Acceptance

Self-acceptance is a recurring end state in self-critical work; self-compassion is an adaptive emotional resource in self-soothing and transformation. Both oppose internal contempt and allow the person to respond to suffering with care rather than attack. [Greenberg: chs. 5, 9, 11]

These states are not equivalent to approving every behaviour. They alter the relationship to the self so corrective action can occur without global self-condemnation.

## 76. Integration

Greenberg uses integration in a practical experiential sense: opposing self-positions can become less polarised; new adaptive emotion can be integrated with previously maladaptive organisation; cognition and affect can be brought together; and a new narrative can incorporate the emotional change. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 5, 9]

This is not automatically Jungian integration. Jungian integration refers to a different theoretical architecture involving ego, unconscious contents, complexes, archetypes, opposites, and individuation.

---

# Part VIII - Specific Emotions

## 77. Fear

Fear is adaptive when it responds to present danger and supports protection. It becomes maladaptive when past threat learning is automatically reactivated in safe or materially different circumstances. Greenberg distinguishes, for example, traumatic fear that drives escape from danger from abandonment-related fear that can drive approach toward the feared loss of connection. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 7]

Clinically, fear requires reality assessment, regulation if overwhelming, access to its need for safety or support, and, if maladaptive, transformation through protective anger, adult agency, compassion, safety, or other adaptive resources. Chapter 11 shows the process in a client whose childhood fear remained dominant in adulthood.

## 78. Anger

Greenberg's discussion of anger is a standing warning against surface classification. Anger can be:

- **primary adaptive**, signalling current violation, obstruction or boundary infringement;
- **primary maladaptive**, as in destructive rage organised by old injury;
- **secondary**, covering fear, hurt, shame, sadness or another primary response;
- **instrumental**, functioning to influence or control another.

[Greenberg: chs. 3, 10]

Even healthy anger does not prescribe expression. Greenberg distinguishes awareness from expression and warns that venting can escalate attack-counterattack cycles. Adaptive use of anger involves extracting the information and need, often moving from destructive blame toward assertive boundary-setting.

## 79. Sadness

Sadness can be primary adaptive grief at loss, primary maladaptive abandonment/deprivation sadness, or secondary emotional collapse. Healthy sadness connects the person to what has been lost and can invite comfort or support withdrawal for recuperation. Grieving helps relinquish what cannot be recovered. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 7, 10]

Greenberg often treats anger as a separating/boundary emotion and sadness as a connecting emotion, but this is a functional shorthand rather than a rigid law. The same person can need anger and sadness in sequence.

## 80. Shame

Shame can be adaptive in limited contexts of social evaluation or wrongdoing, but Greenberg's clinical focus is often on maladaptive shame: a core sense of defectiveness, unlovability, inferiority, exposure, or worthlessness. Chapter 11 shows how shame can develop through ridicule, invalidation, abuse, and internalised contempt. [Greenberg: chs. 7, 11]

Shame is transformed not by telling the person simply to think better of himself or herself, but through safe contact with the painful experience, recognition of unmet needs and unfair treatment, adaptive anger or disgust at violation, compassion, validation, and a new embodied sense of worth.

## 81. Guilt

Guilt appears in the source but is not developed as one of the four principal treatment emotions to the same degree as anger, sadness, fear, and shame. Greenberg distinguishes guilt from shame in functional terms where relevant: guilt is more closely tied to action and wrongdoing, while shame is more closely tied to negative evaluation of the self. Guilt can also function secondarily, for example as anxiety or guilt about anger that interrupts an otherwise adaptive emotional response. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 7, 9]

Because the book's dedicated clinical chapters concentrate on anger, sadness, fear, and shame, this file does not construct a more elaborate guilt taxonomy than the source supports.

## 82. Emotional Categories Are Functional, Not Moral

**Adaptive does not mean pleasant or morally good.** Grief, fear, anger, and even hopelessness can be adaptive in specific contexts.  
**Maladaptive does not mean morally bad or deliberately irrational.** A maladaptive response may be a once-protective organisation learned under adversity.  
**Secondary does not mean fake.**  
**Instrumental does not necessarily mean conscious deception.**

The taxonomy asks what a response is doing, where it comes in the emotional sequence, how well it fits current reality, and what action it supports.

---

# Part IX - Greenberg versus Johnson and Adult Attachment Theory

## 83. Historical Relationship between Greenberg and Johnson

Greenberg and Susan M. Johnson share an important early clinical history. The Project sources identify Greenberg and Johnson as coauthors of the earlier couple model and cite their joint 1988 work in the development of emotion-focused couple therapy. Greenberg's 2015 book still refers to couple work arising from Greenberg and Johnson and cites Johnson's later 2004 couple manual. Johnson's 2004 book, in turn, uses the primary/secondary/instrumental distinction and cites Greenberg and Johnson's earlier work. [Greenberg: chs. 2, 13] [Johnson: *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, ch. 4]

The later models should nevertheless not be treated as interchangeable. Johnson increasingly made attachment theory the central organising theory of human distress and therapeutic change. Greenberg retained a broader emotion-process theory organised around emotion schemes, emotional response categories, marker-guided tasks, adaptive needs, and transformation of maladaptive emotion by adaptive emotion.

## 84. Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy versus Johnson Attachment-Centred EFT

| Dimension | Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy | Johnson attachment-centred EFT / EFCT |
|---|---|---|
| Main organising theory | Emotion-processing and experiential-humanistic theory; emotion schemes, needs, adaptive/maladaptive processing | Attachment theory and science as overarching developmental and relational map |
| Primary unit of explanation | Individual emotional process and emotion scheme, situated in relationship | Attachment system, emotion regulation, working models, and interactional patterns, especially the couple negative cycle |
| Role of attachment | Important developmental and relational domain among several | Central organising framework |
| Emotion classification | Primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary reactive, instrumental; productive vs unproductive processing | Uses primary/secondary distinctions historically, but clinically prioritises deeper/core attachment-related vulnerability beneath reactive positions |
| Meaning of primary | First/direct response, then assessed as adaptive or maladaptive | Direct/core emotion beneath reactive responses, interpreted in attachment context; fear, hurt, sadness, shame, loss and attachment alarm often central |
| Needs | Broad emotional needs: protection, validation, agency, connection, recognition, autonomy, support, etc. | Attachment needs for accessibility, responsiveness, engagement, comfort, safe haven, secure base, belonging and connection are central |
| Case map | MENSIT; pain compass; process diagnosis; markers and schemes | Attachment history, regulation strategies, working models, negative cycle, attachment fears and longings |
| Therapist stance | Empathic following plus process-directive task guidance | Attachment-informed attunement plus experiential deepening and shaping of new interactions |
| Signature structured work | Focusing, evocative unfolding, two-chair, empty-chair, self-soothing | EFT Tango / enactments, cycle tracking, deepening attachment emotion, restructuring interaction |
| Couple process | Emotion, attachment, identity and attraction/liking; work may be individual or dyadic | Negative interactional cycle is a central systemic unit; change involves new bonding events |
| Change mechanism | Productive processing and transformation of maladaptive emotion by adaptive emotion, plus new meaning/narrative | Corrective emotional and relational experience that reorganises attachment regulation, working models and interaction toward security |
| Resolution emphasis | Flexible emotional intelligence, adaptive self-organisation and relational action | Secure connection, accessibility/responsiveness/engagement, new attachment interaction and regulation |

[Greenberg: Introduction; chs. 3-9, 13] [Johnson: *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, chs. 2-4, 6-9; *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 1-3, 6]

The overlap is real: both value embodied emotion, empathic attunement, direct experiencing, primary emotion, and new emotional experience. The difference is explanatory centre of gravity. Greenberg asks, "What kind of emotional process is this, what need is embedded in it, and what adaptive emotion can transform it?" Johnson asks, "How is attachment threat being regulated and enacted in the relationship, and what corrective bonding experience would reorganise that pattern?"

## 85. Primary Emotion in Greenberg versus Johnson

The same phrase can conceal a significant difference.

In Greenberg, **primary** is a process classification: the first/direct visceral response. It is then divided into **adaptive** and **maladaptive**. A primary shame response can therefore be immediate and still be a maladaptive organisation from earlier experience. [Greenberg: ch. 3]

Johnson's 2004 couple manual also defines primary emotion as a direct here-and-now response and secondary emotion as a reaction to that response, reflecting the shared historical lineage. In practice, however, Johnson uses the distinction inside an attachment formulation. The therapist often moves from reactive anger, blame, numbing or withdrawal toward the attachment-relevant fear, hurt, sadness, shame, helplessness, longing, or sense of failure that organises the partner's position in the cycle. [Johnson: *Practice of EFCT*, ch. 4]

By 2019 Johnson's language of **core affect** and **attachment vulnerability** is even more prominent. The therapist deepens beneath automatic reactive responses toward fears, losses, longings and attachment significance, then renders the new experience into interpersonal action. [Johnson: *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 2-3]

Therefore, one must not assume that "primary emotion" performs exactly the same theoretical work in both systems.

## 86. Vulnerability in Greenberg versus Johnson

Greenberg uses vulnerability both descriptively and as a marker. A vulnerability marker may involve painful self-depletion, shame, helplessness, or despair and calls for empathic affirmation. More broadly, accessing painful primary emotion exposes unmet needs and enables transformation. [Greenberg: ch. 5]

Johnson uses vulnerability within an attachment map. Deepening vulnerability commonly means contacting attachment fears and longings beneath protective positions so these can be owned and communicated in a relationship. The vulnerability is clinically important because it changes the interpersonal signal and permits a new attachment response. [Johnson: *Attachment Theory in Practice*, ch. 2; *Practice of EFCT*, chs. 6-8]

## 87. Need in Greenberg versus Attachment Need in Johnson

Greenberg's needs are broad and emerge from emotional appraisal and lived development. They can concern attachment, identity, autonomy, validation, protection, recognition, competence, support, or agency. Johnson's principal clinical needs are more specifically attachment-system needs: to know that the other is available, responsive and engaged; to obtain contact, comfort, reassurance, protection, and secure connection. [Greenberg: chs. 2, 8-9, 13] [Johnson: *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 1-3]

**Permanent distinction:** emotional need ≠ attachment need automatically.

## 88. Emotion Transformation versus Attachment Restructuring

Greenberg's transformation model brings a maladaptive emotional response into contact with a new adaptive emotional response and integrates the new organisation into meaning and narrative. Johnson's restructuring model changes the person's attachment experience and the dyadic interaction so vulnerability can be expressed and responded to in ways that revise working models and increase security. [Greenberg: ch. 9] [Johnson: *Practice of EFCT*, chs. 7-9]

In a couple, the processes can interact. A partner who transforms shame into assertive self-protection may communicate differently; a responsive attachment interaction may in turn generate new emotion. But individual emotional transformation is not by itself the same as restructuring the couple's attachment cycle.

## 89. Empty Chair versus Johnson Enactment

Greenberg empty-chair work is an imaginal task directed toward unfinished emotion with a significant other. Johnson enactment is a therapist-shaped interpersonal event in which newly accessed emotion is communicated to a significant other, most characteristically the partner who is present in couple therapy. [Greenberg: chs. 5, 12] [Johnson: *Practice of EFCT*, ch. 8]

The Greenberg task can end in holding the other accountable, changed self-position, understanding, forgiveness or letting go. The Johnson enactment is evaluated partly by whether it alters the attachment interaction and helps build a new cycle of engagement.

## 90. Johnson EFT versus Greenberg EFT Terminology Warning

> **TERMINOLOGY WARNING**  
> **Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy** and **Johnson Emotionally Focused Therapy / Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy** are historically related but distinct models. Do not use the acronym **EFT** without clarifying which lineage is intended when the distinction matters.

Greenberg's model is organised around emotion schemes, emotion taxonomy, marker-guided experiential tasks, and changing emotion with emotion. Johnson's later model is explicitly attachment-centred and organises clinical work around attachment regulation, working models, negative cycles, enactments, and corrective bonding experiences.

## 91. Emotion Scheme versus Internal Working Model

Both constructs describe organisations that carry prior experience into present appraisal, but they answer different theoretical questions.

| Dimension | Emotion scheme | Attachment internal working model |
|---|---|---|
| Domain | General emotion processing | Attachment-system functioning |
| Core issue | Personal significance, emotion, need, action | Availability of attachment figures, self-worth/efficacy in obtaining care, security |
| Contents | Body, affect, memory, meaning, action tendency, need, self/world organisation | Episodic and affective memory, beliefs about self/other, relational expectations, procedural regulation knowledge |
| Activation | Any personally significant cue | Threat, separation, proximity, availability, attachment-relevant cues |
| Regulatory strategy | Depends on emotion and task | Primary proximity seeking or secondary hyperactivating/deactivating strategies |
| Change | Emotion processing and transformation | New attachment interactions, revised representations and regulation strategies |

Mikulincer and Shaver emphasise that working models are "working" in the sense of dynamic, provisional simulations, not immutable templates. They are cognitive-affective structures and can be revised. [Mikulincer & Shaver: ch. 1, "The Cognitive Substrate of the Attachment System"; "Attachment Working Models"]

## 92. Emotion Regulation versus Attachment Regulation

Greenberg's emotion regulation is a general capacity to manage, tolerate and use emotional states across domains. Attachment theory is concerned specifically with regulation of threat and security through proximity, availability of attachment figures, and secondary strategies when security cannot be obtained. [Greenberg: chs. 1, 3, 9] [Mikulincer & Shaver: chs. 1-2, 7]

A breathing or self-soothing intervention can regulate fear without engaging the attachment system. Conversely, a partner's availability can down-regulate attachment alarm through social coregulation. The mechanisms may interact without being identical.

## 93. Emotional Need versus Proximity Need

Attachment proximity and security needs are a subset of the wider domain of needs Greenberg works with. A person may need recognition, justice, autonomy, competence, protection, meaning, or self-respect in a situation where proximity is not the key issue. In intimate relationships, several systems can be active simultaneously. Greenberg's Chapter 13 explicitly distinguishes attachment, identity, and attraction/liking as important motivational systems in couples. [Greenberg: ch. 13]

## 94. Greenberg and Attachment

Attachment is neither absent nor the master theory of Greenberg's 2015 source. Greenberg invokes attachment in developmental accounts of affect regulation, in discussions of unmet needs and abandonment, in trauma-related cases, and extensively in the couple chapter. He describes attachment-related fear and sadness and draws on attachment research. [Greenberg: chs. 2-3, 11, 13]

Yet the broader clinical architecture remains emotion-process based. The same marker/task system is used for non-attachment themes, and identity, validation, agency, self-criticism, and other emotional concerns receive independent attention.

---

# Part X - Greenberg versus Jungian and Post-Jungian Models

## 95. Emotion versus Jungian Feeling Function

This distinction is mandatory because ordinary language makes the terms sound closer than they are.

Jung's **feeling function** is one of four basic psychological functions. In *Psychological Types* he defines feeling as a process that imparts value in the sense of acceptance or rejection and treats differentiated feeling, like thinking, as a rational judging function. He distinguishes ordinary feeling from **affect**, in which feeling intensity is accompanied by marked physical innervation. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 6, pars. 723-730]

Greenberg's **emotion** is an embodied adaptive or maladaptive response system involving appraisal, action readiness, bodily change, need, motivation, memory and meaning. It is not a typological valuation function.

Therefore:

**Greenberg emotion ≠ Jungian feeling function.**  
**Emotional intensity ≠ strong or differentiated Jungian feeling.**  
**Difficulty identifying emotion ≠ inferior feeling function.**

## 96. Emotion Scheme versus Complex

The closest phenomenological bridge is recurring affective organisation. Both constructs can explain why a small cue evokes a large, patterned response involving memory, appraisal and behaviour. But Jung's complex theory adds a distinctive account of relative psychic autonomy and, in later analytical psychology, a relation between personal complexes and archetypal patterns. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "A Review of the Complex Theory"]

Greenberg's emotion scheme is more explicitly componential and functional: bodily sensation, appraisal, emotion, need, action tendency, memory, meaning, and behaviour. Its clinical taxonomy asks whether the emotional response is primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, or instrumental and whether it is being processed productively. Jung's complex theory does not use that taxonomy.

## 97. Emotional Activation versus Complex Constellation

A single episode can be described differently depending on evidence:

- **emotion-scheme activation** if a familiar embodied emotional organisation is triggered;
- **complex constellation** if a relatively autonomous Jungian affective configuration disrupts the ego's habitual attitude and produces stereotyped thought/behaviour;
- **attachment-system activation** if threat to security/proximity activates attachment strategies;
- **ordinary adaptive emotion** if the response fits a present event without needing a deeper historical explanation.

These are not mutually exclusive. The same event can activate an old emotion scheme, an attachment working model, and a Jungian complex. But using multiple labels adds value only if each explains something distinct.

## 98. Primary Maladaptive Emotion versus Complex

Primary maladaptive shame is not automatically a "shame complex." Greenberg is classifying the position and adaptive status of an emotional response. Jungian complex language makes a claim about the structure and relative autonomy of a feeling-toned psychic organisation. The overlap must be established, not assumed.

## 99. Shadow versus Disowned Emotion

Jung's shadow concerns disowned or insufficiently conscious aspects of personality and, in *Aion*, is presented as a moral problem involving recognition of qualities that the ego resists owning. Jung also notes the emotional, relatively autonomous and sometimes possessive quality of shadow characteristics. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 9ii, ch. 2, "The Shadow"]

A suppressed or disowned emotion can participate in shadow material, but **shadow is not simply a bucket of repressed feelings**. Greenberg's self-interruption work can uncover blocked anger or sadness without implying that the emotion constitutes the Jungian shadow as a whole.

## 100. Projection versus Emotional Meaning

Jung describes projection as an unconscious process in which a psychic content appears to belong to the object; the person encounters projections rather than consciously choosing them. Projection can distort relation to the actual other. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 9ii, ch. 2]

Greenberg's model asks first what emotion, appraisal, need and action tendency are active. Strong conviction or emotional intensity does not itself establish projection. A person can be accurately angry about real mistreatment. Conversely, an old emotion scheme can bias interpretation without meeting every Jungian criterion one might use for projection.

## 101. Active Imagination versus Chair Work

| Dimension | Jungian active imagination | Greenberg chair work |
|---|---|---|
| Theory | Analytical psychology; conscious-unconscious relation | Experiential emotion-process psychotherapy |
| Starting point | Mood, affect, image, fantasy, dream residue, inner voice or spontaneous unconscious material | Marker such as self-criticism, self-interruption or unfinished business |
| Figures/parts | Autonomous or semi-autonomous products of unconscious process may appear | Functionally defined self-positions or representation of significant other |
| Therapist role | Often supports ego engagement, containment and integration; Jung warned of risks | Directly structures task, chairs, focusing, enactment and emotional sequence |
| Goal | Conscious encounter with unconscious contents; integration and changed attitude | Resolve specific emotional-processing task and reorganise emotion/need/self-position |
| Symbolic autonomy | Central potential feature | Not required; the chair position is clinically constructed for task work |
| Emotion | Often a starting affect and part of symbolic process | Central target of differentiation and transformation |
| Integration/resolution | Emergent relation between conscious and unconscious, potentially transcendent function | Task-specific end states such as self-acceptance, empowerment, letting go or changed representation |

Jung's active imagination is broader and less task-predefined. He explicitly says the method is not a plaything and may be unsafe when unconscious contents have high energy. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "The Transcendent Function"]

## 102. Transcendent Function versus Emotion Transformation

Jung's **transcendent function** arises from bringing conscious and unconscious contents into relation. In his account, tension between opposing positions can generate a new "third" attitude or psychological position that is not merely a logical compromise. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, pars. 131, 145-148, 189]

Greenberg's **emotion transformation** is more specific: a maladaptive emotional response is changed through activation and integration of adaptive emotional experience, need and action tendency. [Greenberg: ch. 9]

Both involve change through bringing different states into contact, but the similarity is formal. The contents, theory, method, and endpoint differ.

**Emotion transformation ≠ transcendent function.**

## 103. Emotion-Focused Change versus Individuation

Jung defines individuation as becoming an individual or "coming to selfhood," a process of psychological development that differentiates and fulfils the individual's unique organisation without reducing the person to individualism or withdrawal from collective obligations. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 7, pars. 266-269]

Greenberg's successful emotional processing can contribute to maturity, agency, self-acceptance, and better relationships, but it does not by itself constitute individuation. Individuation concerns a wider Jungian developmental relation among ego, persona, unconscious, archetypal material, complexes and Self.

---

# Part XI - Relationship and Couple Relevance

## 104. Emotion Schemes in Intimate Relationships

Intimate partners are powerful cues because their responses matter strongly to attachment, identity and affection. Greenberg's Chapter 13 therefore treats relationships as major generators and regulators of emotional experience. A partner's expression can activate an emotion scheme involving fear of abandonment, shame, invalidation, anger at violation, or longing for closeness. [Greenberg: ch. 13]

But a relational formulation must preserve the actual behaviour of the partner. An emotion scheme can amplify or shape an interpretation, yet the partner may also genuinely be unavailable, contemptuous, deceptive, coercive, neglectful, or violating. The clinical question is not "scheme or reality" but how present evidence and historical organisation interact.

## 105. Secondary Emotion in Couple Conflict

Greenberg and Johnson both describe patterns in which secondary reactive emotion can obscure more vulnerable primary experience. A frequently cited example is angry protest or blame over hurt, fear, sadness or attachment threat. [Greenberg: chs. 3, 9, 13] [Johnson: *Practice of EFCT*, ch. 4]

This is not a formula that anger is secondary. In some conflicts anger is primary adaptive because an actual boundary has been violated. In others it is primary maladaptive, secondary, or instrumental. Surface emotion cannot be classified from appearance alone.

## 106. Self-Criticism in Relationships

Self-criticism can shape couple behaviour indirectly. A person who attacks himself or herself after conflict may move into shame, withdrawal, defensiveness, reassurance-seeking, resentment, or collapse. A partner may then respond to the defensive surface rather than to the underlying shame or need. [Greenberg: ch. 5; Laboratory extrapolation to couple process]

This does not mean every withdrawal or reassurance request originates in self-criticism. Attachment deactivation, fear of escalation, actual incompatibility, fatigue, or other factors may also explain the behaviour.

## 107. Unfinished Business and Current Partner
