# Applied Complex Activation, Affect, Defences, and Hijacking

## Purpose

This file is the Laboratory's applied synthesis for episodes in which a person appears to become affectively taken over, narrowed, defensive, repetitive, or temporarily unlike their more reflective baseline. Its task is not to label an individual. Its task is to improve observation, conceptual discrimination, reality testing, ownership, and repair.

The central proposition is narrow. Jung's complex theory gives a useful account of occasions when an affectively charged psychic organization is constellated, gains relative autonomy, interferes with conscious intention, narrows the field of consciousness, recruits associations and memories, and shapes behaviour. That account can illuminate recurrent episodes. It cannot, by itself, establish why an episode occurred, prove trauma or dissociation, determine attachment style, identify an inferior function, diagnose a disorder, decide whether a partner is projecting, or excuse conduct.

This is therefore an observation-first document. The sequence is:

**observable event -> subjective experience -> affect -> body -> thought or interpretation -> behaviour -> recurring pattern -> possible formulation.**

The reverse sequence is prohibited. One should not begin with a theory label and reinterpret all evidence until it fits.

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

## Evidence hierarchy

This file keeps five levels separate.

1. **Primary theoretical proposition.** Claims about Jung's complex theory are anchored first in the relevant published Jung texts, especially *A Review of the Complex Theory* and the relevant passages in *On Psychic Energy* and *On the Nature of the Psyche* in CW 8. Projection and shadow distinctions are checked against CW 9ii. Psychotherapy and transference claims are checked against CW 16. Typological distinctions are checked against CW 6.
2. **Later Jungian or post-Jungian development.** Fordham, Knox, von Franz, Beebe, and Schwartz-Salant are treated as later developments, not as covert summaries of Jung.
3. **Contemporary comparative formulation.** Adult attachment theory is represented principally by Mikulincer and Shaver, Susan Johnson's attachment-centred clinical formulation by her work on Emotionally Focused Therapy, and emotion-process theory by Leslie Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy.
4. **Laboratory applied synthesis.** Episode maps, heuristics, confidence indicators, reconstruction protocols, and generic cases are explicitly labelled as Laboratory constructions.
5. **Limits and alternatives.** Ordinary situational explanations, actual relationship conduct, medical or psychiatric explanations, trauma and dissociation formulations, substance effects, fatigue, pain, stress, power, coercion, and safety remain possible alternatives or additional layers.

The source architecture follows the Laboratory constitution: later clinical frameworks may compare with Jung, but they may not be used to rewrite what Jung originally meant.

## Terminology: constellation, autonomy, and "hijacking"

The word **constellation** is Jung's. In CW 8 he describes an outward situation as releasing a psychic process in which certain contents gather and prepare for action. To say that a person is constellated is to say that a definite readiness to react has formed. The process is automatic and involuntary. The constellated contents are complexes with their own specific energy. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 198-199]

The phrase **relative autonomy** is also faithful to Jung. The complex is not simply a thought voluntarily chosen by the ego. Jung describes the feeling-toned complex as a strongly affectively accentuated image of a psychic situation, incompatible with the habitual attitude of consciousness, with inner coherence and a relatively high degree of autonomy. It is under conscious control only to a limited extent. [Primary Jung: CW 8, sec. 201]

The word **hijacking** is not Jung's technical term. In this file it is permitted only in the following form:

**[LABORATORY SHORTHAND: COMPLEX HIJACKING]**

It means: **a temporary state in which a constellated, affectively charged organization substantially narrows reflective consciousness, perception, and behavioural flexibility.** This shorthand is compatible with Jung's descriptions of disturbed consciousness, impeded will, memory disturbance, compulsive thinking and acting, and temporary identification with a complex. It is not a neuroscience claim, an amygdala claim, a diagnosis, proof of dissociation, or proof that responsibility disappeared. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 200-204; Laboratory synthesis]

## How to use this file

Use this file after or during reflection on an episode. Start with what can be observed. Separate fact from interpretation. Reconstruct sequence before assigning theory. Compare several frameworks. Look for recurrence before inferring a stable pattern. Treat interpretations about another person's inner state as hypotheses, not findings. Distinguish explanation from excuse. Where there is coercion, threat, violence, stalking, severe impairment, psychosis, intoxication, self-harm risk, or immediate danger, safety and appropriate professional assessment take priority over symbolic interpretation.

## What this file cannot establish

This file cannot establish that a particular real person has a complex, attachment style, trauma disorder, dissociative disorder, personality disorder, inferior-function problem, shadow issue, or any other diagnosis or structural label. It cannot determine intent from behaviour. It cannot determine whether memories are historically accurate merely because they arise with strong affect. It cannot establish that intense emotion is disproportionate without a sufficiently clear account of the actual event. It cannot convert a partner's criticism, lying, withdrawal, breach of agreement, or aggression into an internal problem simply because a historical pattern is also active.

---

# Part I - Complexes and Constellation

## 1. Why complex theory is useful and easy to overuse

Complex theory is useful because it begins with a common observation: people sometimes react in patterned ways that exceed what they consciously intended. Attention narrows, associative material comes rapidly, memory becomes selective, bodily activation rises, and a familiar story seems self-evidently true. Afterward, the person may see alternatives that were unavailable in the moment.

Jung's account gives these episodes a structure without requiring the ego to be the sole author of every psychic event. But that usefulness creates an overreach risk. "A complex" can become a vague synonym for having issues, being upset, being triggered, being unreasonable, or behaving badly. That flattening removes what is theoretically specific in Jung: affective charge, organization around a nucleus, associative recruitment, relative autonomy, interference with consciousness, and recurrence.

[Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 18-24, 198-211]

## 2. What Jung means by a complex

In Jung's energetic account, complexes are constellations of psychic elements grouped around feeling-toned contents. He describes a nuclear element and secondarily constellated associations. The nucleus has both an experiential component related to the environment and a dispositional component. Its feeling-tone expresses affective intensity. The greater its energetic value, the greater its constellating power. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 18-20]

In the later concise formulation, a feeling-toned complex is an emotionally accentuated image of a psychic situation that conflicts with the habitual attitude of consciousness. It has internal coherence and relative autonomy. This is more than a single thought. It is more like an organized affect-association pattern that can temporarily compete with conscious intention. [Primary Jung: CW 8, sec. 201]

A useful applied paraphrase is therefore:

> A complex is a relatively autonomous, affectively charged organization of associated meanings, memories, images, expectations, and tendencies, structured around a psychologically significant nucleus.

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS based on Primary Jung: CW 8]

The word "organization" is used here to avoid the misleading idea that a complex is one isolated memory or emotion. It is also intentionally broader than "belief". Jung's experimental work showed reaction-time disturbance, slips, memory gaps, bodily changes, and associative effects. The complex has cognitive, affective, mnemonic, bodily, and behavioural expressions, though Jung did not formulate these components using contemporary cognitive-science vocabulary.

## 3. Complexes are normal psychic structures

Complex does not mean pathology. Jung explicitly argues that complexes are not entirely morbid and are characteristic expressions of the psyche. They occur across persons and epochs. Pathology concerns their organization, intensity, rigidity, dissociative effects, consequences, and relation to the total personality, not their mere existence. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 209-210]

This matters in applied work. Saying "a complex may be active" should not mean "something is clinically wrong with this person." A familiar competence complex, authority complex, abandonment complex, shame complex, or parental complex may operate within ordinary life without constituting a disorder. Conversely, normality does not make all effects harmless. A normal psychic structure can still contribute to destructive conduct when intensely constellated.

## 4. Complex versus "having issues"

Colloquial speech often uses "complex" to mean insecurity, hang-up, sensitivity, or problem. That is too loose for this Laboratory. A Jungian complex hypothesis should normally require several converging observations, especially recurrence, affective charge, stereotyped meaning, associative links, and reduced reflective flexibility.

A person can have a strong preference, conflict, bad habit, grievance, fear, or anger without a complex being the best explanation. The theoretical label adds value only when it explains more than the ordinary description.

## 5. What "constellated" means

Constellation is event-like. A latent or less active organization becomes operational in a situation. Jung's definition emphasizes that an external situation can release a psychic process in which certain contents gather and prepare for action. Once constellated, the person occupies a position from which a fairly definite reaction can be expected. [Primary Jung: CW 8, sec. 198]

Applied cautiously, this suggests three components:

- a **cue or situation**;
- an **organized readiness** that gathers relevant associations and affect;
- a **biased field of possible reaction**.

The cue need not be objectively large. Its psychological significance depends on the activated organization. But this does not mean the cue is irrelevant or unreal.

## 6. Trigger versus constellation

"Trigger" in ordinary contemporary language usually identifies the precipitating event: a delayed text, raised voice, criticism, facial expression, sexual rejection, broken promise, public correction, or memory. "Constellation" identifies the organization that becomes activated around such an event.

| Feature | Ordinary trigger | Jungian constellation |
|---|---|---|
| Primary question | What set the reaction off? | What organized psychic pattern gathered and prepared for action? |
| Status | Descriptive event language | Jungian theoretical construct |
| Requires recurrence? | No | Recurrence and patterned organization strengthen the hypothesis |
| Requires strong affect? | No | Feeling-tone is central, though intensity varies |
| Explains meaning? | Usually not | Aims to explain why this cue recruits a particular pattern |
| Diagnostic? | No | No |

A trigger may occur without a complex. A complex may be constellated by very different triggers that share the same psychological meaning.

## 7. External cue versus internal constellation

The actual cue and the complex must be kept separate. If a partner lies, the lie is an event. If the event also activates an older betrayal pattern, both may matter. "It is a complex" must never become a device for erasing current reality.

A useful distinction is:

- **event validity:** what occurred in the present;
- **affective amplification:** how much older organization was recruited;
- **behavioural consequence:** what the person then did.

These are separable questions.

## 8. Same event, different constellations

The same criticism can activate different organizations. One person hears "I am incompetent" and collapses into shame. Another hears "You are controlling me" and becomes angry. Another hears "You are leaving me" and pursues reassurance. Another evaluates the criticism, decides it is accurate, and adjusts. The event alone therefore does not reveal the underlying organization.

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

## 9. Same complex, different triggers

A recurring complex may be activated by superficially different events that share an associative theme. A complex organized around humiliation may be constellated by a partner's teasing, a manager's correction, losing a game, a forgotten appointment, or not understanding a technical discussion. The common thread is not the surface event but the meaning carried by it.

This remains a hypothesis until recurrence is demonstrated. One striking episode is insufficient.

---

# Part II - Affect and Relative Autonomy

## 10. Affect and complexes

Affect is not an accessory to Jung's complex theory. The nucleus is "feeling-toned" and its value is expressed through affective intensity. Jung's association experiments used disturbances of reaction and bodily indicators as indirect evidence that certain contents carried unusual psychic value. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 19-24]

Affect can therefore be understood as one way the energetic importance of a complex becomes visible. Yet affect alone does not identify which theory is correct. Strong fear may be an adaptive response to danger, attachment activation, a Greenbergian primary maladaptive emotion, trauma-related activation, a constellated complex, or some combination.

## 11. Affect versus emotion

Jung's vocabulary is historically variable, and modern emotion theories draw distinctions that are not identical to his. For this file, **affect** refers broadly to the charged, embodied intensity accompanying psychic activation. **Emotion** refers to identifiable experiential patterns such as fear, anger, sadness, shame, disgust, tenderness, or joy. This is a Laboratory working distinction, not a claim that Jung maintained a single modern affect-emotion taxonomy.

Greenberg's model is different. Emotion-Focused Therapy treats emotion as an adaptive information and action system and differentiates primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, and instrumental emotions. Those categories must not be imported back into Jung. [Greenberg comparison: *Emotion-Focused Therapy*, chs. 2-3]

## 12. Strong affect is not proof of a complex

Strong affect can be proportionate. Fear during danger, anger at a serious violation, grief after loss, and shame after a genuine moral failure can be intense without a complex hypothesis adding anything. Strong affect becomes more supportive of a complex hypothesis when it is recurrently organized in a stereotyped pattern, recruits older associative material, narrows access to contradictory evidence, and produces reactions the person later recognizes as exceeding conscious intention.

## 13. Disproportionate affect

"Disproportionate" is useful only after the current event is accurately represented. It is not a synonym for "I disagree with the reaction." A reaction can appear disproportionate to an observer who does not know the event, history, stakes, power asymmetry, or cumulative pattern.

A responsible inquiry asks:

- What exactly happened?
- What reasonable meanings could that event carry now?
- What was the actual harm or threat?
- Was there a cumulative context?
- What older meanings were recruited?
- Did the response remain flexible and reality-based?

## 14. Bodily activation

Jung's early association work linked complex disturbance with measurable changes in reaction time and physiological curves such as pulse, respiration, and psychogalvanic response. These historical observations support the modest proposition that affectively charged complexes can have bodily correlates. They do not license a claim about a specific neural circuit or modern physiological mechanism. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 22-25]

In episode reconstruction, bodily data are therefore observational: heat, pressure, shaking, shallow breathing, collapse, numbness, muscular tension, urge to flee, or urge to move toward. They are not themselves diagnostic markers of a complex.

## 15. Relative autonomy

Jung's most important claim is not that complexes are independent personalities in a literal sense. It is that they show enough independence from ego intention to challenge the naive idea of a unitary conscious subject fully governed by will. In active form, complexes can interrupt intention, influence speech, alter memory, and narrow the conscious field. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 200-205]

"Relative" matters. A complex is not an autonomous person living inside a person. Nor should metaphorical personification be reified. Jung himself treats some personifying language as a metaphorical paraphrase and keeps open the question of whether complexes possess consciousness of their own. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 202-203]

## 16. Relative autonomy does not mean an independent person

The language of "parts," "voices," "splinter psyches," or "possession" can be phenomenologically useful, but it easily becomes ontological overclaim. This file uses such language only to describe functional independence from conscious intention. It does not assert multiple literal persons, entities, demons, or agents.

## 17. "Possession" by a complex

Jung explicitly links extreme identification with a complex to an older language of possession. In CW 8 he describes unconsciousness of complexes as giving them greater freedom and says that the complex may assimilate the ego, resulting in a momentary and unconscious alteration of personality that he calls identification with the complex. He then notes that earlier cultures used the language of possession. [Primary Jung: CW 8, sec. 204]

In this Laboratory, "complex possession" should therefore be used sparingly and preferably when discussing Jung's text. In applied self-analysis, "high-intensity constellation" is usually safer. Describing another person from the outside as "possessed by a complex" is not warranted.

## 18. Degrees of activation

[LABORATORY HEURISTIC: COMPLEX ACTIVATION CONTINUUM]

A useful continuum is:

1. **background sensitivity:** theme is recognizable but does not dominate;
2. **mild constellation:** affect and associative bias are present, reflection remains broad;
3. **moderate constellation:** attention narrows, familiar narrative and defensive tendency strengthen;
4. **high-intensity constellation:** alternative perspectives are difficult to access, behaviour becomes repetitive and less flexible;
5. **peak identification:** the complex-organized interpretation feels identical with reality and the person's behavioural repertoire is substantially narrowed;
6. **recovery:** affect reduces enough for competing perspectives and accountability to return.

This is not Jung's scale. It is a practical observational heuristic.

---

# Part III - [LABORATORY SHORTHAND: COMPLEX HIJACKING]

## 19. Defining the shorthand

**[LABORATORY SHORTHAND: COMPLEX HIJACKING]** refers to the high-intensity end of complex constellation where affectively charged organization substantially narrows reflective consciousness, perception, and behavioural flexibility.

The shorthand is justified only when several features converge. The strongest support comes from recurrence of the same theme across contexts, rapid associative recruitment, high affect, narrowed perception, marked difficulty taking in counterevidence, stereotyped defensive behaviour, and a later recognition that the reaction exceeded the person's broader intention or values.

## 20. What the shorthand is not

It is not:

- Jung's formal technical term;
- a claim about the amygdala or limbic system;
- proof of dissociation;
- proof of trauma;
- proof of a particular attachment style;
- proof of an inferior-function eruption;
- proof that the person "was not themselves" in a literal sense;
- proof that responsibility disappeared;
- an excuse for aggression, intimidation, deception, coercion, stalking, retaliation, or abandonment of obligations.

## 21. Possible signs of affective narrowing

The following signs are non-specific and become meaningful only in combination and over time:

- rapid onset of a familiar affect;
- stereotyped interpretation such as "I am being abandoned," "I am being controlled," "I am worthless," or "I must win";
- narrowed attention to confirming cues;
- inability to register contradictory evidence;
- sudden certainty about another person's motive;
- selective retrieval of old grievances or memories;
- strong bodily activation;
- pressure to act immediately;
- reduced range of responses;
- repetitive attack, retreat, appease, pursue, or self-attack;
- memory gaps or distorted recall around the exchange;
- later surprise at the strength or form of one's own reaction;
- re-emergence of the same pattern despite prior intellectual insight.

None proves a complex.

## 22. Before, during, and after

An episode should be reconstructed temporally.

**Before:** baseline state, current stress, fatigue, relationship context, known sensitivities, unresolved practical issue.

**During:** cue, first affect, body, immediate meaning, narrowing, action tendency, behaviour, feedback from others.

**After:** physiological settling, return of alternative perspectives, changed memory of the event, shame or justification, repair behaviour, and what remains true about the original problem.

The before-during-after frame reduces the temptation to treat the peak state as the whole person.

## 23. Attention narrowing

A constellated complex can make certain cues highly salient. In Jung's experimental frame, association becomes biased around affectively valued material. In applied terms, attention may become disproportionately allocated to tone of voice, delay, facial expression, status cues, sexual cues, signs of rejection, or signs of criticism. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 18-24, 198-200]

Attention narrowing does not mean that the selected cue is imaginary. It means competing data receive less processing.

## 24. Confirmation of existing meaning

Once a complex is constellated, new information can be assimilated to its organizing theme. Jung's language of assimilation is important: the complex can take up the topic or situation as a whole. [Primary Jung: CW 8, sec. 199]

A contemporary paraphrase would be: the episode starts to be read through an already charged template. This is a comparison, not an assertion that a complex is identical to a cognitive schema or internal working model.

## 25. Loss of alternative perspectives

A high-intensity episode often involves reduced access to "maybe". Possibilities collapse into certainty: "They meant to humiliate me," "She does not care," "He will leave," "I have failed," "I must fix this now." The defining issue is not whether one interpretation is emotionally vivid but whether reflection can still compare it with alternatives.

## 26. Memory selectivity

Jung's complex theory explicitly includes memory disturbance. In the association experiment, complex-laden material produced gaps and falsifications on repetition. In ordinary dialogue he notes that constellated complexes may interfere with the speakers' intentions and even with later recall of what was said. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 199-200]

Applied implication: do not treat high confidence during or immediately after activation as proof of memory accuracy. Nor should memory uncertainty be treated as proof of dissociation. Reconstruct from messages, timestamps, witnesses, contemporaneous notes, and other external evidence where stakes are material.

## 27. Certainty about the other

A strong warning sign for applied analysis is a jump from "I felt rejected" to "you intended to reject me," or from "I felt controlled" to "you are controlling by nature." The first describes experience. The second makes a claim about another mind or enduring character.

Knox's discussion of reflective function is useful here: mature reflection includes awareness of oneself and others as psychological agents with minds that cannot simply be read off from behaviour. [Post-Jungian development: Knox, ch. 6]

The guardrail is simple: **subjective certainty is evidence about the intensity of one's experience, not direct evidence about another person's private intention.**

---

# Part IV - Projection, Shadow, and Type

## 28. Complex-driven projection

Projection can become one mechanism by which a constellated complex organizes the interpersonal field. Jung's account in *Aion* is clear that projection is not a deliberate act by the conscious subject. Unconscious contents are encountered as though they belonged to the object, and this can displace a more direct relation with the actual person. [Primary Jung: CW 9ii, secs. 6-9]

In an activated episode, a projection hypothesis becomes more plausible when the other person is experienced with unusual certainty, totalization, or symbolic weight, especially when the same qualities are repeatedly encountered in different people. Examples include "every authority is contemptuous," "every delayed reply means abandonment," or "every disagreement is domination." Still, the real person may also be contemptuous, unreliable, or controlling. Projection and external fact can coexist.

## 29. Projection is not required for complex activation

A complex may shape attention, memory, affect, or behaviour without requiring a strong projection onto another person. A performance-related complex may activate before an exam. A grief-related complex may activate through an anniversary. A shame complex may arise from remembering one's own error. Projection is therefore one possible expression or mechanism, not a defining criterion of every complex.

## 30. Projection versus complex

| Dimension | Complex | Projection |
|---|---|---|
| Core construct | Affectively charged organization with relative autonomy | Unconscious attribution or location of psychic content in an object |
| Main evidence | Recurring affect-association pattern, constellation, interference with intention | Object carries qualities or meanings insufficiently differentiated from the subject's unconscious contribution |
| Can exist without the other? | Yes | Yes, though projections are often organized by complexes or archetypal factors |
| Typical applied question | What recurring organization is active? | What am I taking as simply "out there" that may partly belong to my own psyche? |
| Main error | Calling any strong emotion a complex | Calling any mistaken judgment a projection |

[Primary Jung: CW 8; CW 9ii]

## 31. Projection withdrawal during recovery

Projection withdrawal does not mean deciding that the other person is innocent, good, or irrelevant. It means differentiating the actual object from the psychic material attached to it. A more mature post-episode question is not "Was it all projection?" but:

- What did the person actually do?
- What did I add, infer, magnify, or repeat from elsewhere?
- Which judgment remains valid after affect settles?
- What boundary, repair, or action is still required?

Jung's clinical writings repeatedly treat withdrawal of projection as difficult because the projected content does not vanish; it must be recognized as psychic material that still requires relation and integration. [Primary Jung: CW 16, secs. 212-219; CW 9ii, secs. 6-9]

## 32. Complex versus shadow

Shadow and complex are related but not equivalent. In *Aion*, Jung presents the shadow as those personal qualities, often morally difficult or inferior from the standpoint of consciousness, that the ego has not adequately recognized. The shadow has affective and relatively autonomous qualities, and projections can protect the ego from recognizing it. [Primary Jung: CW 9ii, secs. 3-9]

A complex, by contrast, is the wider theoretical category of an affectively charged psychic organization. A shadow quality may be organized within a complex. A complex can also be built around attachment, power, loss, parental imago, sexuality, achievement, illness, or other themes that cannot simply be reduced to "dark traits."

| Feature | Complex | Shadow |
|---|---|---|
| Primary reference | Feeling-toned, relatively autonomous psychic organization | Unrecognized or insufficiently integrated aspects of the personality, especially those incompatible with ego attitude |
| Moral dimension | Not necessarily moral | Often explicitly moral in Jung's account |
| Projection | Possible | Frequently central to recognition difficulty |
| Normality | Normal psychic structure | Normal structural problem of consciousness and unconsciousness |
| Applied risk | Overdiagnosing any emotion | Reducing shadow to "bad traits" or treating criticism as proof of shadow projection |

## 33. Moralised affect

A complex can recruit moral language. Shame may say, "I am bad." Anger may say, "You are evil." Injury may become a total indictment. This does not establish shadow activation, but moral absolutism during affective narrowing is worth observing.

Shadow work is especially relevant when qualities condemned in others are repeatedly disowned in oneself. Yet the aim is not moral equivalence. Recognizing one's aggression does not mean another person's aggression is acceptable. Recognizing one's need for control does not invalidate a legitimate objection to coercion.

## 34. Positive shadow and complexes

What is excluded from consciousness is not always negative. Competence, assertiveness, sensuality, tenderness, dependence, ambition, creativity, or capacity for leadership may be disowned and then encountered with fascination or irritation in others. A complex organized around inferiority may constellate around someone who embodies a disowned positive capacity.

This is a useful hypothesis in attraction and envy, but it should not be used to reduce genuine admiration to projection. The other person may actually possess the admired quality.

## 35. Type as baseline organization

Jungian psychological type concerns relatively enduring patterns in conscious orientation: introversion or extraversion and differentiated use of thinking, feeling, sensation, and intuition. It is a theory of consciousness and habitual adaptation, not a theory of episodic affective takeover. [Primary Jung: CW 6]

Type can shape what a person notices, values, communicates, and finds difficult. It can therefore influence the form an activated episode takes. But type is not a complex, and type difference is not a causal explanation for harmful behaviour.

## 36. Complex versus inferior-function activation

Jung and von Franz both describe one-sided differentiation as leaving other functions less developed. Von Franz later emphasizes the inferior function as relatively unadapted, closely linked with the unconscious, and often emotionally awkward. [Primary Jung: CW 6, secs. 109-115, 130-131; Post-Jungian development: von Franz, *The Inferior Function*, ch. 1]

| Dimension | Complex activation | Inferior-function vulnerability |
|---|---|---|
| Basic construct | Affectively charged autonomous organization | Least differentiated function within typological organization |
| Time form | Episodic constellation, though complex persists | Relatively enduring structural asymmetry |
| Main marker | Recurring theme with affect and associative recruitment | Primitive, awkward, overcharged, or poorly differentiated use of a function |
| Relationship to unconscious | Complex belongs to unconscious organization | Inferior function is especially close to unconscious material in classical/post-Jungian type theory |
| Same thing? | No | No |

## 37. Can a complex recruit the inferior function?

Possibly, as an applied hypothesis, but the constructs should remain distinct. An activated authority complex might push an intuitive type into rigid, over-literal sensation or an intellectualized person into poorly differentiated feeling. Beebe's later model makes more explicit links between function-attitudes and archetypal complexes, but that is a post-Jungian development, not Jung's original complex theory. [Post-Jungian development: Beebe, chs. 3, 8-9]

A safer formulation is: **a complex may alter the person's access to and use of their typological functions, including exposing inferior-function vulnerability.** It is stronger than saying "the complex is the inferior function."

## 38. Stress is not proof of inferior-function activation

Fatigue, overload, alcohol, illness, pain, urgency, conflict, unfamiliar tasks, and fear can degrade anyone's functioning. A normally thoughtful person can become concrete under stress; a normally relational person can become blunt; an intuitive person can become detail-fixated. These shifts do not by themselves establish inferior-function activation.

---

# Part V - Attachment Activation versus Complex Activation

## 39. Attachment-system activation

Mikulincer and Shaver describe the attachment behavioural system as organized around the goal of felt security. Threat or distress, including perceived unavailability or unresponsiveness of an attachment figure, activates proximity-seeking. When protection and felt security are achieved, the system deactivates and attention can return to other activities. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, chs. 1-2]

This is not complex theory. Attachment activation is functional and threat-regulatory. It can occur in secure as well as insecure people. It is not a sign of pathology and does not require a Jungian complex.

## 40. Complex activation versus attachment activation

| Dimension | Complex activation | Attachment-system activation |
|---|---|---|
| Framework | Jungian analytical psychology | Attachment theory |
| Primary organizing principle | Feeling-toned psychic organization with relative autonomy | Regulation of safety, proximity, availability, and felt security |
| Typical cue | Any psychologically meaningful cue related to the complex | Threat, distress, separation, unavailability, or attachment-relevant danger |
| Core process | Constellation of associated contents and affect; interference with ego intention | Proximity seeking and appraisal of attachment-figure availability |
| Memory/expectation | Associations recruited around complex nucleus | Working models bias expectations about self, other, and support |
| Behaviour | Varies by complex and person | Secure proximity seeking, or secondary hyperactivating/deactivating strategies when security is not achieved |
| Proof from pursuit/withdrawal? | No | No; behaviour alone is insufficient to infer orientation |
| Empirical status | Historical-clinical Jungian theory | Large empirical adult-attachment research tradition, though this corpus uses the 2007 synthesis and is not current to all later research |

The same episode may fit both frameworks. That does not make the constructs identical.

## 41. Both can coexist

A delayed reply from a partner may activate the attachment system because contact with an attachment figure is uncertain. It may also constellate a rejection complex shaped by prior humiliation, abandonment, or family experience. The attachment formulation explains why proximity and availability become urgent. The complex formulation explains the broader affect-association organization and its recurrent symbolic or biographical theme.

Coexistence should be preferred to forced translation when each framework explains a different layer.

## 42. Attachment hyperactivation versus [LABORATORY SHORTHAND: COMPLEX HIJACKING]

Mikulincer and Shaver describe hyperactivating strategies as intensified efforts to obtain attention, protection, and support when proximity seeking has not reliably produced security. Vigilance to threat, persistent proximity seeking, rumination, and strong protest can follow. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2]

[LABORATORY SHORTHAND: COMPLEX HIJACKING] describes narrowed reflective consciousness under a constellated complex. It can include pursuit, but it can also include rage, shame collapse, perfectionistic overwork, paralysis, contempt, or compulsive proving. Hyperactivation is therefore more specifically tied to attachment regulation.

| Feature | [LABORATORY SHORTHAND: COMPLEX HIJACKING] | Attachment hyperactivation |
|---|---|---|
| Core concern | Dominance of affectively charged complex organization | Failure to achieve felt security through ordinary proximity seeking |
| Typical direction | Any stereotyped response linked to complex | Intensification of attachment-system activation and proximity-seeking efforts |
| Relationship-specific? | Not necessarily | Usually attachment-relevant, though attachment system can be activated by broader threat |
| Same behaviour possible? | Yes, for example repeated reassurance seeking | Yes |
| Can be distinguished from behaviour alone? | Rarely | Rarely |

## 43. Complex withdrawal versus attachment deactivation

Mikulincer and Shaver describe deactivating strategies as efforts to turn down the attachment system when closeness, need, or vulnerability has become associated with frustration or unavailability. Suppression of attachment needs, distancing, and compulsive self-reliance can result. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2]

A complex-based withdrawal can have a different organization: shame, fear of criticism, contempt, power struggle, moral injury, sensory overload, conflict avoidance, or a parental complex. The outward behaviour "withdrawal" does not tell us which mechanism applies.

| Feature | Complex withdrawal | Attachment deactivation |
|---|---|---|
| Organizing meaning | Depends on complex | Reduce attachment activation and frustration from unavailable attachment figure |
| Inner state | Could be shame, anger, fear, contempt, helplessness, overload | Often distancing from attachment need/vulnerability, though subjective experience varies |
| Behaviour | Silence, leaving, delay, intellectualization, shutdown | Distance, suppression, self-reliance, reduced proximity seeking |
| Diagnostic from withdrawal alone? | No | No |

## 44. Pursuit and withdrawal are not diagnoses

Susan Johnson's Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy (EFCT) commonly maps distressed cycles such as pursue/criticize and withdraw/avoid, while also recognizing attack-attack and withdraw-withdraw patterns. These are positions in an interactional cycle, not fixed identities. [Johnson EFCT comparison: *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, chs. 1, 4-6]

Therefore:

**withdrawal != avoidant attachment**

**pursuit != anxious attachment**

**pursuit or withdrawal != complex proof**

The same person may occupy different positions with different partners or at different moments.

---

# Part VI - Emotion Schemes versus Complexes

## 45. Greenberg's emotion scheme

Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy proposes emotion schemes as fundamental units of emotional processing. They integrate emotion, motivation, cognition, bodily response, memory, meaning, and action tendency into an automatically activated experiential organization. Emotional learning links cues with embodied and affective responses, so present input can reactivate a learned emotional organization. [Greenberg comparison: *Emotion-Focused Therapy*, ch. 2]

This resembles complex theory phenomenologically in important ways: both involve organized, affect-laden, associative processes that can be activated by cues and shape perception, body, thought, and action. But resemblance is not identity.

## 46. Emotion scheme versus complex

| Dimension | Jungian complex | Greenberg emotion scheme |
|---|---|---|
| Lineage | Analytical psychology | Emotion-Focused Therapy / emotion-process theory |
| Core definition | Feeling-toned, relatively autonomous psychic organization | Integrated emotion, motivation, cognition, memory, bodily response, meaning, and action organization |
| Central theoretical issue | Autonomy, constellation, unconscious organization, interference with conscious attitude | Emotional processing, adaptive/maladaptive emotional learning, transformation |
| Relation to consciousness | Often partly unconscious; may act independently of ego intention | Often activated preconsciously and becomes conscious as emotion, need, thought, action tendency |
| Required historical meaning | Frequently has biographical and potentially archetypal dimensions in Jungian theory | Built through emotional learning and lived experience |
| Therapeutic emphasis | Differentiation, relation to unconscious contents, integration | Awareness, symbolization, regulation, accessing adaptive emotion, transforming maladaptive emotion |
| Same construct? | No | No |

## 47. Primary maladaptive emotion versus complex

Greenberg calls an emotion primary maladaptive when it is an immediate response but reflects unresolved past learning rather than functioning adaptively in the present. This can look very similar to a complex-driven reaction. For example, present criticism may evoke immediate global shame rooted in older experience. [Greenberg comparison: *Emotion-Focused Therapy*, ch. 3]

The frameworks ask different questions. Emotion-Focused Therapy asks what kind of emotion is occurring and whether it is adaptive in the current context. Complex theory asks what affectively charged organization has been constellated, what associations belong to it, how autonomous it is, and how it relates to consciousness. Neither needs to be translated into the other.

## 48. Secondary emotion versus complex defence

In Greenberg's model, a secondary emotion is reactive or defensive and obscures another primary emotional response. Anger can cover fear or hurt; despair can cover anger; anxiety can arise in response to another emotion. But anger is not automatically secondary. Any emotion must be assessed in context. [Greenberg comparison: *Emotion-Focused Therapy*, ch. 3]

A complex can recruit secondary emotion or defensive behaviour, but "secondary emotion" is not a Jungian complex category.

| Feature | Secondary emotion | Complex-driven defensive expression |
|---|---|---|
| Framework | Greenberg | Laboratory integration of Jungian complex theory with defence language |
| Function | Reaction to another emotional process, often obscuring primary emotion | Protection or action organized within a constellated complex |
| Example | Anger covering fear of loss | Anger recruited by an abandonment complex to force contact or avoid helplessness |
| Same thing? | No | No |

---

# Part VII - Defences and Protective Responses

## 49. What counts as defence in this file

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

**Defence** is used as a broad comparative umbrella for a psychological operation that reduces, redirects, avoids, controls, disowns, or transforms threatening affect, meaning, need, conflict, or relationship exposure. This is not presented as Jung's formal definition and not as a complete psychoanalytic defence taxonomy.

The applied value of the term is functional: **what is this response protecting against or trying to regulate?**

## 50. Jung and defence

Jung discusses repression, dissociation, projection, compensation, resistance, one-sidedness, and the autonomy of complexes, but he does not give the systematic defence-mechanism taxonomy associated with later psychoanalytic traditions. Complex theory should therefore not be retrofitted into a modern defence manual.

In Jung's own account, the conscious attitude may try to suppress a complex, make it unreal, assimilate it to ego control, or project unconscious material. The complex itself may then influence conscious behaviour. These are dynamic relations, not a single defence list. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 201-211; CW 9ii, secs. 4-9]

## 51. Fordham and developmental Jungian defences

Fordham's developmental revision brings analytical psychology into closer dialogue with psychoanalytic accounts of early psychic organization. His chapter on "Defences of the self" explicitly engages splitting, projective identification, idealization, isolation, undoing, and the preservation of psychic organization under severe pressure. [Post-Jungian development: Fordham, *Explorations into the Self*, ch. 11]

This is a later developmental Jungian extension. It should not be attributed to Jung as though his original complex theory contained the same defence structure.

## 52. Knox and defence

Knox explicitly compares psychoanalytic, analytical-psychological, and attachment models of defence. Her developmental account places substantial weight on actual relationship experience, internal working models, emotional avoidance, trauma, and the development of reflective function. [Post-Jungian development: Knox, chs. 4-6]

Knox is particularly useful for a reality-testing guardrail: unconscious patterns do not imply that external reality is unimportant. Internal working models are formed in interaction with actual experience and can become rigid or outdated when emotional avoidance prevents updating.

## 53. Johnson's protective strategies

Susan Johnson repeatedly frames attack, clinging, distancing, and shutdown as protective positions within attachment threat. Her clinical approach validates the protective logic before helping clients access vulnerability and create different interactions. [Johnson comparison: *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 2-3, 6; Johnson EFCT: chs. 4-8]

These are attachment formulations. They are not evidence that a Jungian complex exists.

## 54. Greenberg's self-interruption and secondary processes

Greenberg describes self-interruption as active, often partly automatic constriction of emotional experience: holding back tears, tightening the body, silencing oneself, going numb, changing topic, or suppressing expression. He also differentiates secondary emotional reactions from primary emotional experience. [Greenberg comparison: *Emotion-Focused Therapy*, ch. 5 and later task chapters]

These concepts are useful when an apparent "complex defence" may instead be better described as emotional interruption or secondary emotional processing.

## 55. Defence versus complex

| Dimension | Defence | Complex |
|---|---|---|
| Basic question | What threatening experience is being avoided, controlled, redirected, or regulated? | What affectively charged organization has been constellated? |
| Status in this file | Comparative functional umbrella | Jungian theoretical construct |
| Can one organize the other? | Yes, a defence may be recruited within a complex | Yes, a complex may include habitual defensive operations |
| Identical? | No | No |

A defence is usually better understood as an operation or function. A complex is an organization. The distinction prevents phrases such as "withdrawal is the complex" or "the defence caused the complex" from becoming empty circular explanations.

## 56. Common protective responses

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Common observable protective responses include attack, criticism, pursuit, reassurance seeking, withdrawal, silence, appeasement, over-explanation, intellectualization, humour, minimization, self-attack, compulsive problem-solving, numbing, control, perfectionism, and abrupt exit.

The same behaviour can serve different functions. Function must be inferred from sequence, subjective experience, and recurrence, not assumed from appearance.

## 57. Attack

Attack may protect against shame, helplessness, fear, loss of status, attachment panic, vulnerability, or actual danger. It may also be deliberate intimidation. A complex formulation is most useful when attack arises rapidly within a stereotyped theme and the person later recognizes reduced flexibility.

The protective function never makes harmful impact irrelevant.

## 58. Withdrawal

Withdrawal may regulate overstimulation, protect against escalation, create time to think, avoid shame, punish the other, deactivate attachment need, escape actual danger, or express loss of interest. The behaviour itself cannot distinguish these functions.

A responsible analysis asks whether withdrawal was communicated, time-limited, coercive, retaliatory, safety-oriented, or simply avoidant.

## 59. Appeasement

Appeasement can be ordinary conflict management, strategic de-escalation, fear-based compliance, a learned relational strategy, or a complex-linked response to authority or abandonment. It may preserve immediate safety while increasing longer-term resentment or self-alienation.

## 60. Self-attack

Self-attack may appear as global shame, harsh internal criticism, catastrophizing one's error, or taking excessive responsibility to restore a sense of control. Greenberg's work on self-critical splits offers one distinct formulation; Jungian work may examine the complex organization and shadow dynamics; attachment theory may examine models of self as unworthy or unlovable. These are alternatives, not synonyms.

---

# Part VIII - Dissociation, Trauma, and Differential Limits

## 61. Jung, complexes, and dissociability

Jung's complex theory grew in a psychiatric context where dissociation was central. In CW 8 he explicitly compares complexes with fragmentary personalities and discusses the dissociability of the psyche. He argues that psychic processes are not perfectly unified and that smaller fragments can show relative independence. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 202-204, 365]

Historical proximity does not mean a modern dissociative disorder can be inferred from a constellated complex.

## 62. Complex versus dissociation

| Dimension | Complex activation | Dissociation formulation |
|---|---|---|
| Jungian meaning | Relative autonomy, constellation, disturbed consciousness | Jung historically uses dissociation broadly for splitting or loosening of psychic unity |
| Modern clinical status | Not a diagnosis | Contemporary dissociation includes specific phenomena and disorders requiring appropriate assessment |
| Memory effects possible? | Yes | Yes |
| Feeling unreal or unlike oneself possible? | Yes | Yes |
| Can one prove the other? | No | No |
| Applied rule | Use complex language only as a hypothesis about organization | Do not diagnose dissociation from shutdown, numbness, memory uncertainty, or "I wasn't myself" alone |

The Laboratory deliberately leaves modern diagnostic adjudication outside this file.

## 63. Shutdown is not automatically dissociation

Shutdown can reflect exhaustion, conflict avoidance, attachment deactivation, emotional suppression, self-interruption, freezing under threat, shame, depressive withdrawal, medication effects, illness, or deliberate refusal to engage. "I went blank" is an observation requiring clarification, not a diagnosis.

## 64. Trauma versus complex

Jung states that the origin of complexes is frequently connected with trauma or emotional shock, but he also identifies moral conflict and other forms of psychic incompatibility. Trauma is therefore one possible aetiological route in his account, not the definition of a complex. [Primary Jung: CW 8, sec. 204]

Knox's later work gives actual relational trauma a much more explicit developmental role in internal working models, defence, and reflective function. [Post-Jungian development: Knox, chs. 4-6]

For applied use:

**complex != trauma response**

A trauma formulation requires its own evidence. Conversely, if a person has a trauma history, every later intense reaction should not be automatically attributed to it.

---

# Part IX - Recovery, Reflection, and Reality Testing

## 65. "Coming out" of a complex

"Coming out of a complex" is a useful ordinary phrase if it means that the person is no longer strongly identified with the constellated organization and can again discriminate among affect, thought, memory, external fact, and alternative response. It should not imply that the complex has vanished.

Jung's account suggests that complexes cannot simply be argued away. Their relative autonomy and recurrence mean that the developmental task is better conceived as changing the ego's relation to them than as eradicating them. [Primary Jung: CW 8, secs. 201, 209-211]

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Recovery can therefore involve:

- reduced bodily arousal;
- wider attention;
- ability to entertain uncertainty;
- renewed access to contradictory evidence;
- recovery of the actual sequence of events;
- ability to distinguish felt meaning from external fact;
- recognition of one's own behaviour and impact;
- renewed curiosity about the other person's perspective;
- capacity to choose rather than repeat the first impulse.

## 66. Insight after activation

Retrospective insight is useful but should not be romanticised. A person may understand a pattern perfectly after every conflict and still enact it unchanged. The relevant marker is not only "I now see why I did that" but whether future recognition, behavioural choice, repair, and boundary-setting improve.

Insight also has a timing problem. Immediately after an episode, a new theory can become another form of certainty. The safest reconstruction begins with event sequence and external evidence before moving to historical meaning.

## 67. Shame after activation

Post-episode shame can support responsibility or obstruct it. Useful remorse remains specific: "I shouted, made an accusation without evidence, and frightened you." Global shame collapses behaviour into identity: "I am terrible; there is no point." The latter can shift attention away from the injured person and produce reassurance seeking rather than repair.

A complex formulation should therefore avoid turning shame into proof of depth. It may be another part of the cycle.

## 68. Justification after activation

The opposite problem is retrospective rationalisation. After behaviour driven by intense affect, the mind can build a coherent account that makes every action seem necessary. The presence of reasons does not establish that the reaction was proportionate.

Reality testing asks:

- Which facts would a neutral observer confirm?
- Which interpretations were made in the moment?
- What evidence was ignored?
- What did I do that I would regard as unacceptable if someone else did it?
- What part of the original grievance remains valid even after removing exaggeration?

## 69. Repair is not the same as analysis

A brilliant explanation of a complex does not repair an insult, broken agreement, damaged property, frightening outburst, prolonged stonewalling, deception, or breach of trust. Behavioural repair has its own requirements: acknowledgement, stopping the harmful behaviour, restitution where relevant, changed boundaries, credible follow-through, and time.

| Understanding a complex | Behavioural repair |
|---|---|
| Explains recurring psychic organization | Addresses concrete impact |
| May identify historical amplification | Requires present accountability |
| May improve early recognition | Requires changed action |
| Can be private self-reflection | Usually involves the affected relationship where safe and appropriate |
| Does not prove forgiveness is owed | Does not require the other person to accept the formulation |

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

---

# Part X - Complex-to-Complex Interaction and Relational Dynamics

## 70. Reciprocal constellation

[LABORATORY HEURISTIC: RECIPROCAL COMPLEX CONSTELLATION]

Two people can enter a feedback loop in which one person's activated pattern becomes a cue for the other's. Jung's observation that ordinary dialogue can constellate complexes in both parties provides a primary theoretical basis for considering reciprocal activation, but the detailed cycle below is a Laboratory construction. [Primary Jung: CW 8, sec. 199]

A typical sequence is:

**A cue -> A complex -> A protective behaviour -> impact on B -> B complex or other activation -> B protective behaviour -> new impact on A.**

Once the loop is running, each person receives new evidence that appears to confirm the organizing expectation. The pursuer experiences withdrawal as proof of abandonment. The withdrawing person experiences pursuit as proof of engulfment or criticism. The critic experiences defensiveness as proof of incompetence. The defensive person experiences criticism as proof of contempt.

## 71. Mutual activation does not mean equal responsibility

A reciprocal process is not a moral equation. Two people can influence each other while having very different power, freedom, intent, behavioural severity, and consequences. One person's fear can activate another's anger; that does not make fear and intimidation equivalent. One person's criticism can precede another person's violence; that does not distribute responsibility equally for violence.

The Laboratory therefore separates:

- **causal contribution:** what influenced the sequence;
- **behavioural responsibility:** what each person chose or enacted within their capacity;
- **impact:** what harm occurred;
- **power:** who had greater structural or situational control;
- **safety:** whether ordinary reciprocal dialogue was possible.

## 72. Confirmation loops

An activated complex can help create the interpersonal conditions it expects. This does not mean the feared outcome is invented. It means behaviour may increase its probability.

Examples:

- reassurance demands exhaust a partner, whose delayed response then confirms abandonment fear;
- anticipatory contempt evokes defensiveness, which confirms an expectation of incompetence;
- withdrawal to avoid criticism leaves issues unresolved, which increases later criticism;
- appeasement prevents honest disagreement, then resentment confirms a belief that intimacy is unsafe.

The key phrase is **may help create**. A partner can also independently behave badly. Do not use circularity to blame the more vulnerable person for another's choices.

## 73. Complex cycle versus EFCT negative cycle

Susan Johnson's Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy treats recurring interactional sequences such as pursue/criticize and withdraw/avoid as self-reinforcing cycles shaped by attachment threat, protective positions, and unmet needs for safe connection. [Johnson EFCT comparison: *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, chs. 4-8]

A complex cycle is a different model. It foregrounds the activation of relatively autonomous affect-association organizations in one or both partners.

| Dimension | Complex cycle | EFCT negative cycle |
|---|---|---|
| Primary unit | Constellated complex and its interpersonal effects | Recurring interactional sequence between partners |
| Main organizing theory | Jungian complex autonomy and projection | Attachment threat, emotional accessibility, responsiveness, engagement |
| Typical map | Cue -> complex -> defence -> impact -> reciprocal activation | Trigger -> emotion/meaning -> protective move -> partner response -> feedback |
| Requires attachment threat? | No | Attachment significance is central |
| Requires complex? | Yes as hypothesis | No |
| Clinical aim | Greater differentiation, ownership, reality testing, integration | De-escalation, access to vulnerability, restructuring interaction, secure bonding |
| Same construct? | No | No |

The two models can describe different layers of the same conflict without becoming interchangeable.

## 74. Reciprocal complex activation versus Schwartz-Salant's relational field

Schwartz-Salant's "interactive field" is a later post-Jungian model of a third area or field between people, with dynamics not reducible to either individual's conscious subjectivity. He draws heavily on alchemical imagery, transference-countertransference, projective identification, and the experience of opposites. [Post-Jungian development: Schwartz-Salant, chs. 1-5]

The Laboratory's reciprocal complex cycle is deliberately more modest. It is a sequence model of mutual triggering and protective response. It does not assert an autonomous third field.

| Dimension | Reciprocal complex activation | Interactive field |
|---|---|---|
| Status | Laboratory heuristic | Schwartz-Salant's post-Jungian theory |
| Focus | Sequential mutual activation | Experiential third area with its own field dynamics |
| Main use | Episode reconstruction and feedback mapping | Analytic understanding of relational transformation and field phenomena |
| Projective identification required? | No | Can be important but field is not reducible to it |
| Same as EFCT cycle? | No | No |

## 75. Transference context

Jung treats transference as a critical but variable psychotherapeutic phenomenon closely related to projection. In some texts he describes it as an attempt to establish psychological rapport and in others emphasizes that it consists in projections that may substitute for a real relationship. He also insists that transference is not always indispensable or uniformly beneficial. [Primary Jung: CW 16, secs. 276-289, 359-363]

For this file, transference is relevant when the analytic or therapeutic relationship itself becomes the carrier of constellated expectations, parental imagos, or other unconscious material.

## 76. Everyday relationship is not automatically transference

It is tempting to call every repetition with a partner "transference." That weakens the term. In intimate life, actual history with the partner accumulates. Expectations may be based on what this partner has repeatedly done, not merely on a transferred earlier template.

A good sequence is:

1. establish the partner's actual pattern;
2. identify what the present episode means to the person;
3. compare this with older patterns;
4. consider transference-like repetition only when the historical resemblance explains more than present evidence alone.

## 77. Therapist counterreaction is not objective proof

Fordham and Schwartz-Salant both take analyst counterreaction seriously, but neither warrants a simple inference that "what I feel proves what the patient put into me." Fordham emphasizes the analyst's own self-analysis and the difficulty of disentangling contributions. Schwartz-Salant explicitly explores uncertainty about whose affect is being experienced and sometimes suspends ownership judgments within the field. [Post-Jungian development: Fordham, ch. 10; Schwartz-Salant, chs. 4-5]

Therefore a therapist's irritation, fear, attraction, blankness, or pressure is data about the encounter, not objective evidence about the client's essence or motive.

## 78. What actually happened

The first relational question is factual. Write the episode in behavioural terms that both a camera and an audio recorder could approximately capture where possible.

Weak: "She abandoned me."

Stronger: "She read the message at 7:10 pm and did not reply until 10:40 pm."

Weak: "He disrespected me."

Stronger: "He interrupted three times, called my proposal stupid, and left while I was speaking."

The stronger version does not settle meaning. It creates a stable base for interpreting meaning.

## 79. Fact, meaning, and memory

| Layer | Question | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Observable fact | What can be externally checked? | Reply arrived 3.5 hours later |
| Immediate perception | What did I notice? | Silence after a difficult conversation |
| Felt meaning | What did it mean to me? | "I do not matter" |
| Memory/association | What did it remind me of? | Waiting for an unpredictable parent |
| Inference about other | What did I conclude about motive? | "She is punishing me" |
| Behaviour | What did I do? | Sent 14 messages and accused her |
| Later evidence | What became known afterward? | She was in an emergency meeting |

This table is not designed to invalidate emotion. It prevents emotion, memory, and inferred motive from being treated as the same evidentiary category.

## 80. Current reality may be sufficient

A complex hypothesis should not be preferred when present evidence already explains the reaction adequately. Repeated lying can create mistrust without requiring a betrayal complex. Ongoing contempt can produce anger without shadow projection. Chronic unreliability can make delayed contact threatening without attachment pathology.

Theoretical depth is not automatically explanatory superiority.

## 81. Historical amplification and present reality can both be true

A partner may actually behave dismissively **and** the response may be amplified by an older humiliation complex. A manager may genuinely be unfair **and** a parental authority complex may narrow the person's options. A delayed reply may be ordinary **and** attachment anxiety or an abandonment complex may intensify meaning.

The mature formulation is often additive rather than either/or.

## 82. Evidence against a complex hypothesis

A complex hypothesis becomes less plausible when:

- the reaction is well proportioned to the event and resolves with it;
- the theme does not recur across time or contexts;
- reflective capacity remains broad throughout;
- behaviour is deliberate, flexible, and strategically adapted;
- there is no unusual affective charge or associative recruitment;
- the response changes promptly with new evidence;
- the explanation requires ignoring clear present facts;
- another framework explains the episode more directly;
- the pattern appears only under a specific medical, substance, sleep, or situational condition.

"Less plausible" does not mean disproved. This file uses comparative formulation rather than categorical diagnosis.

---

# Part XI - Accountability, Agency, and Safety

## 83. Complex autonomy does not remove agency

Jung goes unusually far in describing the power of complexes. He says a constellated complex can disturb consciousness, impede will, affect memory, and produce a state of duress or compulsive thinking and acting. In the same passage he uses the legal analogy of diminished responsibility under certain conditions. [Primary Jung: CW 8, sec. 200]

That historical-theoretical statement must not be converted into a legal or moral conclusion about a real episode. A psychological formulation cannot determine legal responsibility, capacity, or excuse. Nor does reduced reflective flexibility mean zero choice in every case.

[LABORATORY ACCOUNTABILITY CONTROL]

A safer applied formulation is: **activation may reduce the range and quality of available reflection, but responsibility for impact, repair, safety, and future prevention remains a separate question.**

## 84. Explanation versus excuse

Explanation asks: what organization made this reaction likely?

Excuse asks: should the behaviour be exempted from ordinary accountability?

The first does not answer the second. A complex formulation can increase responsibility by showing where prevention is needed. If someone knows that particular states repeatedly precede harmful behaviour, that knowledge creates a stronger obligation to recognize warning signs, pause, leave safely, seek help, change routines, or establish boundaries before the peak.

## 85. Intention versus impact

"I did not mean to" may be relevant to intention but does not erase impact. Conversely, harmful impact does not prove malicious intent. Episode reconstruction should retain both.

Useful repair language is behaviour-specific:

- what I did;
- what effect it had;
- what I understand now;
- what I will do differently;
- what boundary or safeguard is needed.

The complex belongs in the explanatory background, not as the grammatical subject that replaces the person: "my complex made me do it" is usually a poor accountability statement.

## 86. Repeated activation increases responsibility for prevention

A first surprising episode may be difficult to anticipate. A recurrent pattern is different. Once triggers, bodily signs, narratives, and high-risk behaviours are known, future planning becomes part of responsibility.

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Prevention can include earlier interruption of the interaction, agreed cooling-off procedures, limits on messaging or alcohol during conflict, avoiding high-stakes discussion when exhausted, written follow-up, therapy, medical review where indicated, or explicit non-negotiable behavioural boundaries.

## 87. Do not psychologise abuse

Complex theory must not be used to convert coercive control, threats, intimidation, stalking, sexual coercion, physical violence, financial control, or repeated degradation into a romantic drama of unconscious mutuality.

A person can have complexes and also abuse another person. The presence of reciprocal activation does not make abuse reciprocal. A victim's fear, appeasement, or reactive anger is not evidence of equal responsibility.

## 88. Safety before symbolic interpretation

When safety is uncertain, ask practical questions first:

- Is anyone afraid to say no?
- Is there threat of harm?
- Is someone prevented from leaving?
- Are weapons, intoxication, stalking, or escalating violence present?
- Is there suicidal or homicidal risk?
- Is there severe disorganization, psychosis, or medical impairment?

If yes or possibly yes, symbolic interpretation is secondary. Seek appropriate immediate support and professional or emergency assistance according to the actual risk.

## 89. "They triggered me" guardrail

"They triggered me" describes a sequence but can obscure agency. A more precise sentence is:

**"That event activated a strong reaction in me; I am responsible for what I did next, while the event itself can still be evaluated on its own merits."**

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

This preserves both relational causality and personal accountability.

---

# Part XII - Episode Reconstruction and Differential Formulation

## 90. The Episode Reconstruction Protocol

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

This protocol is designed for disciplined retrospective analysis. It is not a diagnostic interview and not a substitute for therapy or assessment. It deliberately delays interpretation until the event has been described.

### Phase A - Establish the observable event

1. **What happened that an audio or video recording could approximately confirm?** Separate action from inferred motive.
2. **What happened immediately before the episode?** Include conversation topic, setting, timing, and practical stakes.
3. **What did the other person actually say or do?** Use specific words or actions where accurately remembered.
4. **What did I actually say or do?** Include messages sent, volume, leaving, silence, threats, reassurance seeking, appeasement, or self-attack.

### Phase B - Establish subjective experience

5. **What was the first moment I noticed a shift in myself?** Do not start with the peak.
6. **What did the situation mean to me in the first seconds?** Examples: rejection, humiliation, danger, control, failure, abandonment, betrayal, engulfment.
7. **What did I most fear would happen next?**
8. **What did I most urgently want from the other person or from myself?**

### Phase C - Identify affect and body

9. **What was the first emotion or affect I can identify?** Fear, anger, hurt, shame, sadness, disgust, excitement, helplessness, or another state.
10. **What happened in my body?** Heat, tension, constriction, collapse, numbness, shaking, pressure, urge to move, urge to freeze.
11. **How quickly did the intensity rise?** Seconds, minutes, or slowly over hours?
12. **Did the affect feel familiar in quality, not merely in intensity?**

### Phase D - Reconstruct thought and interpretation

13. **What sentence seemed unquestionably true at the peak?** Write it exactly if possible.
14. **What did I assume about the other person's intention?**
15. **What evidence supported that assumption at the time?**
16. **What evidence did I discount, fail to notice, or find impossible to consider?**

### Phase E - Reconstruct behavioural narrowing

17. **What action felt compulsory or urgent?** Attack, explain, pursue, leave, freeze, appease, prove, confess, punish, fix, check, or self-attack.
18. **What alternative actions could I access at the time?** If none were available subjectively, record that without treating it as proof of incapacity.
19. **Did I repeat a familiar behavioural sequence?**
20. **What immediate consequence did my behaviour create in the other person or situation?**

### Phase F - Examine recurrence and associative material

21. **Where else has this same affect-meaning combination appeared?**
22. **Does it occur with different people who occupy a similar psychological role?** Authority, lover, critic, dependent person, rival, parent-like figure.
23. **What earlier memories, images, dreams, phrases, or family situations come to mind?**
24. **Is the historical association spontaneous, or am I forcing it because a theory says it should be there?**

### Phase G - Test the complex hypothesis

25. **Was the reaction substantially more intense or rigid than the present event alone appears to require?** State why, not just yes or no.
26. **Did a stereotyped interpretation recur despite different surface circumstances?**
27. **Did contradictory evidence become difficult to access during activation?**
28. **Afterward, did I recognize a gap between my broader intention and what I actually did?**

A "yes" pattern across these questions supports, but does not prove, a complex hypothesis.

### Phase H - Test alternatives

29. **Would ordinary emotion adequately explain the episode?**
30. **Was attachment threat specifically central: availability, rejection, separation, reassurance, or safe connection?**
31. **Would a Greenberg emotion-scheme formulation better capture the automatic emotional learning involved?**
32. **Was typological overload or inferior-function vulnerability relevant without requiring a complex explanation?**
33. **Could trauma, dissociation, medical factors, medication, substances, sleep deprivation, pain, hormonal change, or acute stress materially contribute?**
34. **Was the other person's actual behaviour sufficient to explain my response?**

### Phase I - Accountability and reality testing

35. **What remains objectively true about the event after I am calmer?**
36. **Which part of my interpretation now looks amplified, uncertain, or projected?**
37. **What was the impact of my behaviour regardless of intention?**
38. **What repair, boundary, restitution, or changed behaviour is required?**

### Phase J - Learning and prevention

---

# Applied Attachment, Complexes, and Emotion Regulation

## Source Status and Evidence Note

This is a **Mode D source-grounded applied synthesis**. It is not a reconstruction of one author and it does not propose a new unified psychological theory. Its task is narrower and more practical: to help a reader distinguish several explanatory models that can all appear relevant when an intimate relationship episode carries strong affect, threat, repetition, old meaning, and current interpersonal consequences.

The source hierarchy follows the Laboratory's governing architecture. For claims about Jungian complex theory, Jung's own published work controls, especially *The Structure and Dynamics of the Psyche*, with *Two Essays on Analytical Psychology* used for individuation, differentiation, and the ego-unconscious relation. [JUNG PRIMARY: Jung, CW 8, especially “A Review of the Complex Theory,” §§194-219; Jung, CW 7, especially §§240 and 266-269.] Jean Knox is used as a later developmental and attachment-informed Jungian bridge, not as evidence that Jung himself held attachment theory. [POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT: Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, especially chs. 4, 6, and 7.] Adult attachment structure and dynamics are taken primarily from Mario Mikulincer and Phillip R. Shaver's *Attachment in Adulthood: Structure, Dynamics, and Change*, the 2007 Project edition. Susan M. Johnson's *Attachment Theory in Practice* supplies the attachment-centred clinical account of emotional accessibility, responsiveness, engagement, protective strategies, co-regulation, and corrective emotional interaction. Leslie S. Greenberg's *Emotion-Focused Therapy: Coaching Clients to Work Through Their Feelings*, second edition, supplies the emotion-scheme and emotion-classification framework. [ATTACHMENT THEORY: Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, especially chs. 1, 2, 9, and 10. Johnson, *Attachment Theory in Practice*, especially chs. 1-3 and 6-7. EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY: Greenberg, *Emotion-Focused Therapy*, especially chs. 2, 3, and 6-9.]

The Project copy of Mikulincer and Shaver is the 2007 first edition. It is therefore an authoritative feeder for this corpus but not an exhaustive statement of attachment research published after 2007. Johnson's source is the 2019 Guilford Press volume. Greenberg's source is the 2015 second edition published by the American Psychological Association. Knox's source was first published in 2003 by Brunner-Routledge; the Project copy identifies the Taylor & Francis e-Library edition of 2005. These edition limits matter when interpreting claims about the evidence base.

This file uses detailed paraphrase rather than transcription. Where a precise Jung paragraph is given, it was recoverable from the Project source. For later authors, chapter-level provenance is used where that is the most reliable portable reference. No missing page number, quotation, study result, or causal claim is reconstructed from memory.

## Coverage of This File

The file is organised around one practical problem: **how to formulate a relationship episode when several explanatory layers are plausible at once**. It therefore covers attachment-system activation; internal working models; attachment anxiety and avoidance; hyperactivating and deactivating strategies; Jungian complex constellation; projection; emotion schemes; primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, and instrumental emotion; Johnson's language of core vulnerability and reactive protection; self-regulation; co-regulation; pursuit; withdrawal; shame; anger; fear; longing; current partner behaviour; recursive interaction; differentiation; responsibility; power; safety; and longitudinal pattern testing.

The central method is differential formulation. Each framework is asked to answer the question it is designed to answer. Attachment theory asks about perceived security, availability, responsiveness, proximity, support, and regulation. Jungian complex theory asks whether an affectively charged, relatively autonomous psychic organisation has been constellated. Greenberg's model asks how an emotional response is organised, what function it has, and whether it is primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, or instrumental. Projection asks whether subjective psychic material is being attributed to the other in a way that exceeds what current evidence warrants. Behavioural reality asks what actually happened.

These are not translations of one another. A single episode can support more than one formulation, but overlap is not identity. A delayed reply can activate an attachment system without constellating a major complex. A criticism can constellate an inadequacy complex without being primarily an attachment event.
