A person can experience primary adaptive anger at a genuine boundary violation without that anger being a defence against fear. A partner can be objectively unavailable while the perceiver also brings an old expectation of abandonment to the situation. The aim is to preserve that complexity without allowing “depth” language to outrun the evidence.

## Purpose

The principal purpose is to make reflective inquiry more disciplined. In ordinary relationship conflict, psychological language can easily become a shortcut: “I am anxious,” “they are avoidant,” “my complex took over,” “that was projection,” “the anger is really fear.” Sometimes such formulations are useful. Sometimes they are premature. The same outward behaviour may serve different functions across people and contexts, and the same person may behave differently across relationships or even across episodes in one relationship.

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

This file therefore treats formulation as a routing exercise:

1. Establish the observable event.
2. Describe the subjective experience.
3. Identify the immediate meaning and emotional response.
4. Ask whether attachment security is at stake.
5. Ask whether an old affective organisation appears constellated.
6. Ask how the emotion is functioning.
7. Test for projection or amplification.
8. Consider ordinary contextual explanations.
9. Preserve responsibility, boundaries, power, and safety.
10. Hold the resulting formulation provisionally and test it longitudinally.

The point is not to name the deepest cause. The point is to improve explanatory precision while retaining uncertainty.

## Integration without Conceptual Collapse

The governing distinction is simple: **similar phenomenology does not imply theoretical equivalence**.

A person who becomes highly distressed when a partner is unavailable may be showing attachment-system activation. If the distress includes intensified proximity seeking, vigilance, rumination, and difficulty disengaging from attachment-related cues, a hyperactivating strategy may be plausible. [ATTACHMENT THEORY: Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.] If the episode also evokes a recurrent affective theme with disproportionate charge, stereotyped interpretation, narrowed conscious freedom, and a sense that “something in me takes over,” a complex formulation may add explanatory value. [JUNG PRIMARY: Jung, CW 8, §§198-203.] If the person reacts with anger that protects against or follows another emotional response, Greenberg's secondary-emotion concept may be useful, but the anger could instead be primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, instrumental, attachment protest, complex-associated affect, or proportionate anger at current conduct. [EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY: Greenberg, ch. 3.] None of these labels can be assigned from intensity alone.

Likewise, an internal working model is not a Jungian complex. In attachment theory, working models are cognitive-affective representations and procedural expectations built from attachment-relevant experience that guide attention, prediction, interpretation, memory, and regulatory action. [ATTACHMENT THEORY: Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.] Jung's complex is a feeling-toned psychic organisation with a strong affective centre, associative material, relative autonomy, and the capacity to interfere with conscious intention. [JUNG PRIMARY: Jung, CW 8, §§198-203.] Knox shows why developmental and Jungian perspectives can fruitfully meet, especially around implicit relational organisation, meaning, defence, and reflective function, but her bridge does not abolish the conceptual differences between the systems. [POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT: Knox, chs. 4-7.]

## How to Use This File

Use the file as a **hypothesis discipline**, not a labelling system. It is most useful after a concrete episode has been described in behavioural terms. Begin with what could in principle have been recorded by a camera or verified by both parties: what was said, what was done, what agreement existed, what changed, what boundary was crossed, what response followed. Then add private experience: emotion, body state, fear, longing, impulse, image, memory, and immediate interpretation. Only then introduce theory.

For self-analysis, the emphasis should be on one's own observable behaviour and directly accessible experience. Claims about another person's attachment orientation, complex, unconscious motive, developmental history, or trauma should usually remain low-confidence or speculative unless there is direct evidence. The fact that a behaviour fits a familiar psychological pattern does not establish why the other person did it.

Repeated use should be longitudinal. A single episode can establish that distress occurred. It rarely establishes a stable attachment orientation, recurrent complex, enduring emotion scheme, or fixed relational position. Patterns become more credible when the same trigger-meaning-affect-response organisation recurs across time and contexts, and when alternative explanations become less adequate.

## What This File Cannot Establish

This framework cannot, from an isolated episode, establish a person's attachment style, another person's attachment style, a childhood attachment history, trauma, a Jungian complex, an unconscious motive, compatibility, prognosis, or whether a relationship should continue or end. It cannot convert a clinical model into a moral verdict. It cannot infer equal responsibility merely because interaction is recursive. It cannot make harmful conduct less harmful by redescribing it as dysregulation.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

A formulation should be rejected or suspended when it requires facts that are not available, when an ordinary contextual explanation is sufficient, when it obscures the current partner's actual conduct, or when it shifts attention away from safety and responsibility. Psychological explanation adds value only when it explains more than the facts already show without contradicting them.

# Part I - Core Models and Distinctions

## Attachment-System Activation

[ATTACHMENT THEORY]

Mikulincer and Shaver organise adult attachment around a behavioural system whose broad goal is protection and felt security in the face of threat. The system is not a personality label. It is a regulatory system that can be activated by danger, distress, separation, uncertainty, or cues of attachment-figure unavailability. When an attachment figure is perceived as accessible and responsive, proximity or support seeking can restore security and allow attention to return to exploration and other goals. When availability is doubtful or repeatedly unsuccessful, secondary regulatory strategies become more likely. [Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, chs. 1-2.]

The attachment model is therefore inherently relational and conditional. It asks not merely, “What trait does this person have?” but “What threat is present, which attachment figure is relevant, how available does that figure seem, what regulatory strategy is being used, and what happens next?” The Project edition explicitly treats relationship-specific working models as important. A person can carry different expectations and regulatory tendencies in different attachment relationships, while more generalized representations can also develop through repeated experience. This is one reason a single episode cannot justify assigning a global attachment category.

### Security, safe haven, and secure base

The safe-haven and secure-base functions describe two related aspects of attachment. Under distress, an attachment figure can be sought as a source of comfort, protection, or regulation. When felt security is available, that relationship can also support exploration, autonomy, and engagement with the wider world. Security is therefore not the same as permanent closeness or constant reassurance. In the underlying control-system model, successful proximity and support reduce activation and help the person return to other tasks. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.]

### Attachment anxiety and avoidance

Attachment anxiety and attachment avoidance are best treated here as dimensions of attachment-related organisation, not as shorthand diagnoses. High attachment anxiety is associated in this model with concerns about rejection, abandonment, or insufficient availability, and with greater reliance on hyperactivating regulation. High attachment avoidance is associated with discomfort with dependence or closeness and with greater reliance on deactivating regulation. These dimensions do not determine every moment of behaviour. They describe tendencies in attachment-relevant cognition, affect, and regulation whose expression depends on context, partner, and activation state. [Mikulincer & Shaver, chs. 2-4.]

### Hyperactivation and deactivation

Hyperactivating strategies intensify attachment-related attention and action when security is not achieved. They can include heightened vigilance to signs of unavailability, persistent proximity seeking, rumination, intensified bids for reassurance, and difficulty disengaging from threat-related cues. Deactivating strategies move in the other direction: they inhibit attachment-related needs, suppress or distance from vulnerability, minimize reliance on others, and promote defensive self-reliance. Mikulincer and Shaver describe these as secondary strategies after the primary strategy of seeking security through an available attachment figure does not work reliably. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.]

The behavioural consequences can look dramatic, but neither strategy can be inferred from one behaviour alone. Repeated texting can be hyperactivation, practical urgency, anger, habit, or a response to actual unreliability. Silence can be deactivation, overwhelm, fear of escalation, deliberation, contempt, exhaustion, or a legitimate boundary. A theory of strategy requires functional and longitudinal evidence, not visual resemblance.

### Internal working models

Internal working models are central to the attachment account of continuity and change. In Mikulincer and Shaver's formulation, significant attachment interactions are represented in cognitive-affective structures that include memories, beliefs, expectations, and procedural knowledge about how attachment figures respond and how one should regulate attachment needs. Models can become chronically accessible, but alternative models can also be activated by context and relationship. They influence what a person anticipates, attends to, interprets, remembers, and does. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.]

Knox uses the internal-working-model concept as part of her bridge between attachment theory, cognitive science, and psychodynamic thinking. She stresses that cumulative interpersonal experience is encoded into unconscious or implicit representations that guide expectations and perception. She also insists on the informational and representational character of the process: “internalization” need not mean that another person's psyche is literally inserted into one's own. [POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT: Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, ch. 4.]

An internal working model may therefore bias current relationship perception without making that perception imaginary. A model can lead someone to expect unavailability, but the present partner may also be unavailable. The working-model hypothesis becomes most useful when the expectation is recurrent, anticipatory, rapid, and relatively resistant to disconfirming experience, or when the same current cue is interpreted through a familiar self-other template across situations.

## Jungian Complex Constellation

[JUNG PRIMARY]

Jung's complex theory begins from a different unit of analysis. In “A Review of the Complex Theory,” he describes **constellation** as an automatic and involuntary process in which a situation releases a psychic process and brings together contents prepared for action. The constellated contents are complexes with their own specific energy. [Jung, CW 8, §198.] This does not mean that every emotional reaction is a complex. It means that certain affectively organised psychic contents can become activated as relatively coherent units.

In Jung's definition, a feeling-toned complex is an image of a psychic situation that is strongly emotionally accentuated and incompatible, to some degree, with the habitual attitude of consciousness. It has inner coherence and relative autonomy. [Jung, CW 8, §201.] Once activated, a complex can disturb consciousness, interfere with intention and memory, and narrow freedom of response. Jung's deliberately provocative claim that “complexes can have us” captures the experience that conscious intention is no longer fully in command. [Jung, CW 8, §200.] He compares strongly autonomous complexes with dissociated psychic fragments and repeatedly stresses that they can behave as if they were partial personalities. [Jung, CW 8, §§202-203.]

Three qualifications matter for applied use.

First, complex theory is not a synonym for pathology. Jung treats complexes as normal constituents of psychic life and as among the living units of the unconscious psyche. [Jung, CW 8, §§210-218.] The practical question is not whether a person “has complexes” but how a particular complex is organised, what constellates it, how autonomous it becomes, and what relation consciousness can establish with it.

Second, constellation is broader than attachment activation. A complex may be organised around themes of humiliation, authority, inferiority, achievement, family, sexuality, morality, rivalry, loss, or many other affectively charged constellations. Attachment threat can be one trigger among many, but Jung's construct is not defined by the attachment behavioural system.

Third, intensity alone is insufficient. Strong affect after a proportionate present-day event may be entirely understandable without invoking a complex. A complex formulation gains value when the response shows repeated thematic organisation, disproportionate or rapidly amplified affect, stereotyped meaning, narrowed perspective, difficulty integrating contradictory evidence, recurrence across contexts, or a marked gap between reflective intention and actual behaviour.

### Complex influence without total possession

Applied language should be graded. “A complex may have been constellated” is often more accurate than “the complex possessed the person.” Jung's text supports degrees of autonomy and interference. Full language of possession can overstate a common episode and encourage the reader to externalize responsibility. Even when a complex is active, the person's conduct remains behaviour for which consequences and responsibility can still be assessed.

## Emotion Schemes and Emotional Processing

[EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY]

Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy begins from a third level of explanation. His “emotion scheme” is an integrated organisation of affective, cognitive, motivational, bodily, memory, and action-related processes. Emotion schemes are shaped by lived experience and can be activated rapidly, often before elaborate conscious reflection. They organise how a situation is appraised, what is felt, what need is salient, and what action tendency becomes available. [Greenberg, *Emotion-Focused Therapy*, ch. 2.]

The emotion-scheme concept is therefore broader than a single consciously named emotion. It links embodied experience, appraisal, memory, meaning, motivation, and response. Greenberg's clinical interest lies not only in reducing distress but in differentiating what kind of emotional process is occurring and in changing maladaptive emotional organisation through new emotional experience. [Greenberg, chs. 2-3, 6-9.]

### Primary adaptive emotion

Primary adaptive emotion is the immediate emotional response that fits the current situation and helps organise adaptive action. Fear can support protection, anger can support boundary defence, sadness can support grieving and withdrawal from what is lost, and other emotions can carry similarly useful information. “Primary” does not mean morally superior; “adaptive” refers to the fit and function of the response in the current context. [Greenberg, ch. 3.]

### Primary maladaptive emotion

Primary maladaptive emotion is also an immediate, core response, but one that is shaped by unresolved learning such that it no longer fits the present situation well. Greenberg gives special attention to enduring shame, fear, sadness, or anger that can be rapidly triggered and feel familiar or compelling even when current circumstances differ from the original learning context. [Greenberg, ch. 3.] This category may phenomenologically resemble aspects of a Jungian complex or attachment working model, but the theoretical constructs are not identical.

### Secondary emotion

Secondary emotion is a response to another emotion or to the person's thoughts about that emotion. It can obscure or defend against a more primary emotional state. Anger, for example, can sometimes arise in response to fear, shame, sadness, or helplessness. But Greenberg explicitly treats category as functional rather than tied to a particular emotion label. Anger can also be primary and adaptive. The applied question is therefore “What is this emotion doing in this sequence?” rather than “What emotion is underneath anger by definition?” [Greenberg, ch. 3.]

### Instrumental emotion

Instrumental emotion describes emotional expression that has become a way of influencing others or achieving an interpersonal end, consciously or outside awareness. The category does not imply deliberate deception in every case. A person may have learned that a particular emotional display reliably elicits care, retreat, reassurance, or compliance. [Greenberg, ch. 3.]

### Regulation and transformation

Greenberg distinguishes simply controlling emotion from learning to tolerate, symbolise, understand, regulate, and transform it. When emotional arousal is overwhelming, regulation is necessary before productive processing. When maladaptive primary emotion is central, the longer-term aim is not merely suppression but transformation through access to new adaptive emotional resources, needs, meanings, and action tendencies. [Greenberg, chs. 6-9.]

## Knox as Developmental Bridge

[POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT]

Knox is especially useful because she directly asks how analytical psychology can engage developmental psychology, attachment theory, cognitive science, memory research, and contemporary psychoanalytic thinking. Her book should not be read as a glossary that translates Jung into attachment terms. Its importance is methodological: it shows how development and implicit relational experience can be brought into a post-Jungian model without claiming that Jung's original concepts already contained those later theories.

In chapter 4, Knox emphasises that attachment theory explains how cumulative experiences with key attachment figures are encoded into internal working models that guide expectations, perception, and behaviour in later emotionally important relationships. She treats these models as forms of implicit relational organisation. [Knox, ch. 4.] This gives a developmental account of how recurring self-other expectations can arise.

In chapter 6, Knox develops reflective function as the capacity to experience one's own and other people's minds as having distinct thoughts, emotions, desires, and intentions. For the present file, the key contribution is differentiation of subjectivities. Under strong affect, the ability to imagine that the other may have a mind different from one's immediate representation of them can weaken. Reflection is therefore relevant to reality testing, perspective taking, and the ability to hold one's own interpretation as a hypothesis rather than a fact. [Knox, ch. 6.]

Knox also links development to therapeutic change. New understanding alone may be insufficient if automatic protective patterns remain organised at an implicit relational level. New relational experience, together with reflection and interpretation, can matter because it offers experience inconsistent with older automatic expectations. [Knox, ch. 7.] For applied relationship analysis, this suggests a cautious bridge: a recurrent attachment expectation can contribute to the conditions under which an affective complex is repeatedly constellated, and repeated experience can reinforce both. It does **not** follow that the working model and complex are the same psychic structure.

## Projection

[JUNG PRIMARY]

Projection is used here narrowly. In Jungian terms, unconscious content can be experienced as if it belongs primarily or wholly to an external object. In *Two Essays*, Jung describes transference initially in terms of projection of unconscious contents and notes that early projections can be connected with personal memories. [Jung, CW 7, §98.] The broader Jungian tradition treats withdrawal or recognition of projection as requiring the subject to recover some psychological content previously located in the other.

For differential formulation, projection is not the same as having an expectation. “I fear you will leave” can be an attachment-related expectation without projection. “You are deliberately trying to humiliate me” may involve projection if the attribution greatly exceeds the available evidence and repeats a personally charged theme, but it may also be accurate. The first task is therefore to compare attribution with evidence. The existence of subjective amplification does not prove that the other person provides no objective basis for the perception.

## Why the Models Are Not Redundant

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

The models answer questions at different levels. Attachment theory is strongest when the issue concerns security, attachment-figure availability, proximity, support, and regulatory strategy. Complex theory is strongest when the issue concerns affective autonomy, thematic recurrence, narrowed consciousness, and the reactivation of organised unconscious material. Emotion-Focused Therapy is strongest when the issue concerns the functional organisation of emotion, its relation to needs and action tendencies, and whether an emotional response is adaptive, maladaptive, secondary, or instrumental. Projection adds a subject-object question: which qualities attributed to the other are supported by present evidence, and which may carry subjective material? Behavioural analysis remains prior to all of them because theories cannot substitute for the event.

| Construct or model | Central question | Typical evidence | What it may explain | Distinct prediction | What it cannot establish by itself |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Attachment-system activation | Is felt security or attachment-figure availability under threat? | Threat, separation, distress, proximity or support seeking, change with responsiveness | Why security-related cues become highly salient | Reliable responsiveness should often reduce activation and restore exploration | Presence of a Jungian complex; motive of the partner |
| Internal working model | What learned self-other expectation is shaping prediction and regulation? | Recurrent expectations, selective attention, relationship-specific or generalized patterns | Anticipation of availability, rejection, comfort, or dependence | New consistent attachment experience can modify accessibility and expectation | That an attribution is a projection; that a complex is constellated |
| Hyperactivation | Is the person escalating attachment-related monitoring and proximity seeking when security is uncertain? | Vigilance, persistent bids, rumination, difficulty disengaging | Intensified attachment protest and threat monitoring | Clear dependable responsiveness should reduce the need to intensify | Global “anxious personality”; why every pursuit occurs |
| Deactivation | Is the person inhibiting attachment needs and dependence to manage expected unavailability? | Suppression, distancing, avoidance of reliance, defensive self-reliance | Reduced conscious access to attachment needs and distance from vulnerability | Increased threat to dependence may increase suppression or distancing | Introversion; why every withdrawal occurs |
| Complex constellation | Has an affectively charged autonomous psychic organisation become active? | Thematic recurrence, strong charge, narrowed perspective, stereotyped meaning, interference with intention | Why a response feels larger, older, repetitive, and partly autonomous | Similar symbolic or interpersonal cues may reactivate the same theme across contexts | That the issue is attachment-based; moral innocence; trauma history |
| Projection | Is subjective material being attributed to the other beyond current evidence? | Recurrent attribution, contradiction by evidence, strong certainty, later recognition of own contribution | Distortion or amplification of subject-object perception | Better differentiation should increase capacity to separate attribution from observation | That the other has no relevant behaviour; that all criticism is subjective |
| Emotion scheme | What embodied emotional organisation is being activated? | Body state, appraisal, memory, need, action tendency, recurring emotional sequence | Moment-to-moment emotional organisation and learned affective meaning | Changing emotional processing may alter meaning and action tendency | Attachment orientation; Jungian complex; objective truth of attribution |
| Primary or secondary emotion process | What function does the current emotional response have? | Temporal sequence, context fit, relation to other emotion, needs and action tendency | Whether emotion fits present conditions or responds to another emotion | Accessing a more primary process may alter secondary distress when that formulation is correct | That vulnerability is always “truer”; partner motive |
| Ordinary contextual distress | Is the response adequately explained by present circumstances? | Clear current trigger, proportional affect, limited recurrence, resolution when event changes | Normal reaction to loss, disrespect, uncertainty, fatigue, or conflict | Correcting the current problem should substantially reduce distress | Deep developmental structure; enduring pattern |

The most useful integrated formulation may contain several rows. For example, actual partner inconsistency can activate attachment threat, an abandonment-themed complex, and primary maladaptive fear, while secondary anger increases interpersonal pressure. That integrated account remains a set of linked hypotheses, not a claim that all constructs name one mechanism.

# Part II - Working Models, Complexes and Emotion Schemes

## Internal Working Models

[ATTACHMENT THEORY]

An internal working model is best understood as a set of attachment-relevant representations and procedures rather than as a miniature person inside the mind. In the Mikulincer and Shaver model, working models contain information about significant attachment interactions, expectations about self and others, and procedural knowledge about how to regulate attachment needs. They can exist at relationship-specific and more general levels. Their chronic accessibility helps produce continuity, but they can also be revised by new experience and different models can become active in different contexts. [Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, ch. 2.]

Knox gives the concept special importance because it offers a developmental account of unconscious relational meaning. She describes cumulative experiences with attachment figures as encoded and stored in implicit representations that influence later perception and behaviour. She also stresses that the representational model is built from actual interpersonal experience even though later retrieval can bias the interpretation of new experience. [Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, ch. 4.]

The applied implication is that a working model is neither a transparent record of the past nor an arbitrary fantasy. It is a predictive organisation. Its evidentiary signature is therefore expectation: what does the person anticipate about availability, rejection, comfort, dependence, and their own worth or efficacy in attachment situations? What information is noticed or discounted? How is ambiguous partner behaviour interpreted? Does the expectation shift when experience is consistently different?

## Internal Working Model versus Complex

The two constructs overlap phenomenologically in several ways. Both can operate outside focal awareness. Both can shape perception and behaviour. Both can be activated by cues and can contribute to recurrent relationship patterns. Both can connect current events with prior experience. These similarities are enough to make comparison useful, but not enough to make them identical.

A working model is defined within attachment theory by its relationship to attachment experience, expectations about self and attachment figures, and regulation of the attachment behavioural system. A Jungian complex is defined by affective charge, associative organisation, relative autonomy, and its capacity to disturb or narrow conscious functioning. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2; Jung, CW 8, §§198-203.]

The predictions differ. If a working model is central, variation should track attachment context and perceived availability particularly strongly. If a complex is central, thematically similar cues may constellate the same affective organisation in domains that are not primarily attachment-related. An inferiority complex, for example, might appear in intimate relationships, work evaluation, status competition, and encounters with authority. An attachment working model of unavailability is more specifically concerned with the accessibility and responsiveness of attachment figures, even if it later generalises.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

A developmental attachment history may contribute material to a later complex. A complex may in turn amplify attachment threat. This is a plausible integrative hypothesis. It is not evidence that the constructs are theoretically interchangeable.

## Internal Working Model versus Projection

An internal working model influences anticipation and interpretation. Projection concerns the location of subjective psychic content in the object. The distinction can be operationalised by asking two different questions.

**Working-model question:** “What did I expect would happen between me and this attachment figure before enough current evidence was available?”

**Projection question:** “What motive, quality, attitude, or psychic content am I experiencing as belonging to the other, and how well does that attribution fit observable evidence?”

A person can expect rejection because of an attachment working model without projecting a specific motive. Conversely, a person can project hostility onto another in a situation that is not especially attachment-related. The two processes can interact: an expectation of rejection may increase readiness to attribute contempt or abandonment motives to ambiguous behaviour. That interaction still does not collapse them.

## Internal Working Model versus Emotion Scheme

Greenberg's emotion scheme and the attachment working model are both experience-shaped organising structures, but their domains differ. The working model is attachment-specific: it organises expectations about significant others, self in attachment relationship, and attachment regulation. The emotion scheme is an experiential-emotional organisation involving bodily feeling, appraisal, memory, need, motivation, and action tendency. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2; Greenberg, *Emotion-Focused Therapy*, ch. 2.]

An emotion scheme can therefore concern experiences that are not primarily attachment events, such as shame around performance or anger around injustice. An attachment working model can influence emotional processing without itself being an emotion classification. In a given episode, the working model may help explain why a cue is read as abandonment, while the emotion-scheme formulation explains how that reading activates fear, bodily arousal, a need for comfort, and a particular action tendency.

## Complex versus Emotion Scheme

Complexes and emotion schemes can look especially similar because both integrate affect, memory, meaning, and action. The difference lies in theoretical architecture and emphasis. Jung's complex belongs to a model of a plural, partially autonomous psyche. Its hallmark is feeling-tone, associative organisation, and relative autonomy from the ego. Greenberg's emotion scheme belongs to an experiential information-processing account of emotion. Its hallmark is rapid embodied appraisal and organisation of feeling, meaning, need, and action tendency. [Jung, CW 8, §§198-203; Greenberg, ch. 2.]

A useful practical discriminator is what the theory predicts. Complex theory predicts thematic constellations with a degree of autonomy and recurrence that may cut across situations and can be explored symbolically as well as biographically. Emotion-scheme theory predicts patterned emotional appraisal and action tendencies that can be accessed, differentiated, regulated, and transformed through emotional processing. Both may fit the same event. Neither becomes redundant merely because both include affect and memory.

# Part III - Regulation and Protective Strategies

## Attachment Regulation

[ATTACHMENT THEORY]

In attachment theory, regulation is inseparable from relationship. The attachment system evolved, in this model, to organise proximity and access to a protective other under threat. Adult regulation therefore includes both intrapersonal and interpersonal processes. A person can calm through their own learned strategies, through actual contact with an attachment figure, through an internalised expectation of availability, or through a combination of these. [Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, chs. 2 and 9.]

Mikulincer and Shaver's model gives a useful sequence. Threat activates the attachment system. The person evaluates whether a relevant attachment figure is available and responsive. If availability is experienced as adequate, security-based strategies can operate: support can be sought, distress can be acknowledged without dominating all attention, and engagement with non-attachment goals can resume. If availability is uncertain or expected to fail, secondary strategies become more prominent. Hyperactivation intensifies efforts to obtain security. Deactivation attempts to reduce distress by inhibiting attachment needs and reliance on others. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.]

This sequence is a model, not a universal script. A person can use more than one strategy, shift strategies within an episode, or show different organisation with different attachment figures. A person may also use a behaviour that looks “hyperactivating” for reasons unrelated to attachment. Functional evidence matters more than form.

Johnson's clinical attachment model adds an explicit emphasis on emotional accessibility, responsiveness, and engagement. In her framework, security in close relationships depends substantially on whether partners can reach one another emotionally and respond in ways that convey availability and importance. Attachment threat can therefore be organised around questions such as: “Are you there for me?”, “Will you respond when I need you?”, and “Do I matter to you?” [Johnson, *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 1-2.]

Johnson also places co-regulation at the centre of human functioning. In her account, effective attachment does not require becoming independent of relational need. She treats secure dependence as compatible with resilience and autonomy, and regards successful self-regulation as developing within, not outside, a history of interpersonal regulation. [Johnson, chs. 1-3.]

## Self-Regulation and Co-Regulation

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

**Self-regulation** is the capacity to modulate and organise one's own affect, attention, impulses, meaning-making, and behaviour sufficiently to preserve choice. It can include pausing, naming emotion, tolerating bodily arousal, reality testing, delaying action, seeking information, setting a boundary, using internalised support, or deliberately seeking interpersonal support.

**Co-regulation** is regulation that occurs through interaction with another person. It can include comfort, attunement, clarification, physical proximity, reassurance, collaborative problem solving, responsive listening, or simply the felt availability of a trusted attachment figure. Johnson explicitly treats co-regulation as a baseline feature of attachment rather than as a developmental failure that mature adults should outgrow. [Johnson, ch. 3.]

Co-regulation is not the same as fusion. Fusion implies insufficient psychological differentiation or inability to maintain a distinct perspective, agency, and responsibility in relationship. Co-regulation can occur between differentiated people who remain separate subjects. Nor is co-regulation automatically dependency in a pejorative sense. Human beings routinely use relationships to regulate stress. The practical issue is not whether regulation is interpersonal, but whether the pattern supports flexibility, reciprocity, reality testing, and functioning, or instead becomes coercive, rigid, one-sided, or incapable of tolerating ordinary separation.

Self-regulation is likewise not the same as self-sufficiency. A person who suppresses need, refuses comfort, or exits every vulnerable interaction may appear highly independent while actually relying on a rigid deactivating strategy. Conversely, a person who asks directly for comfort and can settle when comfort is available may show more regulatory flexibility even though the strategy is openly relational.

### A practical distinction

A useful test is to ask what happens **after** support is available.

- If distress falls, perspective broadens, and autonomous functioning returns, interpersonal support is working as regulation.
- If reassurance must be repeated without durable effect, hyperactivation, a competing complex, an entrenched emotion scheme, actual ongoing threat, or another process may be maintaining arousal.
- If support is rejected automatically, deactivation may be plausible, but so are mistrust based on current behaviour, shame, anger, sensory overload, a need for space, or a legitimate wish not to be comforted by the person who caused the harm.

This is hypothesis testing, not diagnosis.

## Hyperactivation

[ATTACHMENT THEORY]

Hyperactivation is a secondary attachment strategy. It becomes useful as a concept when the person appears to increase monitoring, proximity seeking, signalling, worry, and attachment-related action because felt security has not been achieved. Mikulincer and Shaver describe it as maintaining the attachment system in a heightened state. Attention remains oriented toward threat and availability. The person's regulatory effort is directed toward obtaining a response from the attachment figure. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.]

Possible manifestations include repeated checking, reassurance seeking, intensified communication, protest, inability to disengage from ambiguous cues, catastrophic anticipation of abandonment, or persistent attempts to restore closeness. None of these behaviours is specific to hyperactivation. Repeated contact after a partner disappears without explanation may be proportionate to genuine uncertainty. Persistent checking during a medical emergency is not evidence of attachment insecurity. The same behaviour becomes more suggestive of hyperactivation when it reliably appears under attachment threat, exceeds practical information needs, and remains difficult to settle even when reasonable reassurance is available.

Hyperactivation is not synonymous with attachment anxiety as a whole. Attachment anxiety is a broader dimensional tendency. Hyperactivation is a regulatory strategy or process. A person high in attachment anxiety may use hyperactivation frequently, but a person who is not globally high in anxiety can hyperactivate in a specific relationship or under unusual threat.

Hyperactivation is also not “complex possession.” A complex can intensify or shape hyperactivation, but the concepts explain different aspects. Attachment theory explains the security-seeking function. Complex theory may explain why a particular cue evokes a highly charged, stereotyped, historically resonant meaning that persists beyond what attachment regulation alone predicts.

## Deactivation

[ATTACHMENT THEORY]

Deactivation is the contrasting secondary strategy. When closeness or reliance on an attachment figure is expected to be ineffective, unavailable, humiliating, intrusive, or dangerous, the person may inhibit attachment-related awareness and action. Mikulincer and Shaver describe deactivation in terms of distancing from attachment needs, suppressing vulnerability, and maintaining self-reliance. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.]

Possible manifestations include emotional suppression, minimising need, reluctance to seek support, distancing during conflict, dismissing dependency, or shifting attention away from attachment-relevant information. Again, form is not function. A person can withdraw because they are deactivating attachment, because they are flooded and need time, because they are reflecting, because they fear aggression, because the conversation is unproductive, or because a boundary has been crossed.

Deactivation is not introversion. Introversion in Jung's typological sense concerns the habitual orientation of libido toward the subject rather than the object; it is not a theory of attachment need suppression. A quiet, inwardly oriented person can be securely attached. An extraverted person can use deactivation. Treating social style as attachment evidence collapses two unrelated classifications.

## Pursuit and Withdrawal

[ATTACHMENT THEORY / EFCT COMPARISON]

Johnson's couple work often describes interactional positions in which one partner pursues, protests, criticises, or presses while the other withdraws, defends, shuts down, or distances. These positions are useful descriptions of a recursive interactional pattern. They are not direct attachment diagnoses. [Johnson, *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 6-7; see also Johnson, *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*.]

Pursuit can be driven by attachment protest and hyperactivation, but it can also reflect anger at a concrete problem, insistence on accountability, different conflict style, poor impulse control, practical urgency, or a learned communication habit. Withdrawal can express deactivation, but it can also reflect fear, overwhelm, shame, exhaustion, deliberate self-control, contempt, strategic stonewalling, or a necessary safety boundary.

The distinction matters because treating behaviour as a direct readout of attachment style can obscure what the behaviour is doing in the episode. A pursuer may be relatively secure but responding to repeated unreliability. A withdrawer may be highly anxious internally while behaviourally shutting down. The same person can pursue in one relationship and withdraw in another.

### Observable form versus psychological function

| Observable behaviour | Possible attachment reading | Possible complex reading | Possible emotion-process reading | Ordinary or contextual alternatives | Evidence needed |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Repeated reassurance seeking | Hyperactivation under perceived unavailability | Rejection or abandonment theme constellated | Primary maladaptive fear; secondary anxiety; need for comfort | Current partner actually inconsistent; acute stress | Does reassurance settle distress? Does pattern recur across attachment contexts? |
| Silence during conflict | Deactivation; inhibition of attachment need | Shame, inferiority, authority, or conflict complex | Overwhelm; secondary shutdown; primary fear | Need for time; fatigue; safety strategy; deliberate boundary | Internal experience, timing, return to conversation, relationship pattern |
| Emotional numbness | Deactivation or suppression of attachment cues | Complex-related dissociation or defensive narrowing, if supported | Under-regulated or over-regulated emotional process | Exhaustion, medication, depression, shock, ordinary uncertainty | Broader clinical and contextual data; not inferable from one episode |
| Intense anger after rejection | Attachment protest; hyperactivation | Abandonment, humiliation, or inferiority complex | Primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, or secondary anger | Proportionate anger at mistreatment | Sequence, present facts, recurrence, action tendency, underlying emotion if any |
| Leaving the room | Distancing under attachment threat | Flight from constellated affect | Regulation of overwhelming arousal | Sensible timeout, fear of violence, boundary enforcement | Whether departure is temporary, communicated, and followed by repair |
| Clinging or repeated contact | Proximity seeking intensified by threat | Loss or abandonment complex | Fear, grief, instrumental distress, or adaptive seeking | Real emergency or imminent loss | Context, proportionality, response to availability |
| Intellectualising | Possible distancing from vulnerable attachment affect | Defence against complex affect | Avoidance of embodied emotion | Natural cognitive style, professional habit, need to think | Whether cognition blocks or assists emotional processing |
| Refusing comfort | Deactivation; low trust in availability | Complex involving dependence, shame, or betrayal | Anger, fear, shame, or lack of fit with offered comfort | Comforter caused harm; wrong form of support; desire for autonomy | Reason for refusal and response to alternative support |

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

The table is a differential router. It does not say which reading is correct.

## Dependency, Secure Dependence, Fusion and Self-Sufficiency

[ATTACHMENT THEORY]

Johnson explicitly rejects the idea that mature functioning requires emotional independence from significant others. Her attachment-centred account treats constructive dependence as part of human resilience. A secure bond can provide a safe haven under threat and a secure base for exploration. [Johnson, chs. 1-2.] This position is compatible with Mikulincer and Shaver's account in which felt security supports exploration rather than trapping the person in proximity seeking. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.]

That does not mean all dependency is secure. The useful distinction is functional:

- **Secure dependence** permits asking for support, receiving it, returning to autonomous activity, and tolerating the other as a separate person.
- **Rigid dependency** may involve inability to function without repeated external regulation, extreme intolerance of separation, or coercive demands for availability.
- **Fusion** involves insufficient psychological differentiation, including difficulty maintaining distinct beliefs, emotions, needs, or responsibility.
- **Defensive self-sufficiency** may minimise legitimate relational needs and treat reliance itself as dangerous or shameful.

These are not diagnostic categories. They are questions about how regulation, agency, and relationship operate together.

# Part IV - Emotional Organisation

## Johnson's Reactive and Vulnerable Emotion

[ATTACHMENT THEORY / JOHNSON CLINICAL FORMULATION]

Johnson's 2019 model repeatedly distinguishes surface, automatic, or reactive responses from deeper engagement with core vulnerability. In her clinical process, anger, antagonism, numbness, or defensive distance may sometimes organise interaction while fears, longings, shame, sadness, or helplessness remain less directly expressed. The therapist works to deepen emotional experience and help the client own and communicate attachment-relevant vulnerability in a way that can create new interaction. [Johnson, *Attachment Theory in Practice*, ch. 3.]

This vocabulary belongs to Johnson's attachment-centred experiential model. “Vulnerable” is relationally and clinically organised around what is difficult to risk in connection, not a claim that one emotion category is metaphysically deeper or always more truthful. A reactive response may accurately register a present problem even if it also obscures a vulnerable need.

## Greenberg's Emotion Categories

[EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY]

Greenberg's classification asks a different question: what is the function and temporal status of the emotional response?

- **Primary adaptive emotion** is the first core response that fits the present situation and supports adaptive action.
- **Primary maladaptive emotion** is also primary and automatic but is organised by learning that no longer fits the present situation well.
- **Secondary emotion** arises in response to another emotion or to thoughts about emotion and can obscure or defend against a more primary process.
- **Instrumental emotion** is emotional expression that has become a means of influencing the interpersonal environment, consciously or outside awareness. [Greenberg, ch. 3.]

These categories are functional. Anger is not inherently secondary; sadness is not inherently primary; fear is not inherently maladaptive. Classification depends on sequence, context, fit, need, action tendency, and the person's learned emotional organisation.

## Johnson versus Greenberg

The two systems overlap because both attend closely to emotional process, defensive organisation, and transformation through new experience. They differ in theoretical centre of gravity.

| Question | Johnson attachment-centred formulation | Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy formulation |
|---|---|---|
| Primary organising problem | Attachment security, emotional connection, accessibility, responsiveness, engagement | Emotional processing, emotion schemes, emotion function, adaptive versus maladaptive response |
| Surface distress | Often tracked as reactive protection, escalation, shutdown, or numbing within attachment threat | May be secondary emotion, instrumental emotion, or a primary emotion depending on function |
| Deeper process | Core attachment vulnerability, fears, longings, shame, loss, need for connection | Primary adaptive or primary maladaptive emotional process, associated needs and action tendencies |
| Clinical aim | Reshape emotional engagement and attachment-related interaction; increase secure connection and regulation | Access, regulate, differentiate, symbolise, and transform emotional experience |
| Key caution | Vulnerability is not automatically factually correct about the partner | Primary emotion is not automatically adaptive; emotion category cannot be inferred from label alone |

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

Do not translate “reactive” into “secondary” or “vulnerable” into “primary.” Sometimes the concepts will point to a similar sequence; sometimes they will not. The source traditions define their categories differently.

## Anger, Fear, Shame and Longing

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

These emotions are especially vulnerable to overinterpretation in relationship analysis.

**Anger** may be a primary adaptive response to violation, a primary maladaptive response shaped by earlier learning, a secondary response to fear or shame, an attachment protest, affect carried by a constellated complex, an instrumental attempt to control interaction, or an ordinary proportionate response to current behaviour. Strong anger is not evidence that fear “must” be underneath it.

**Fear** may be adaptive danger detection, attachment fear of loss or unavailability, primary maladaptive fear based on old learning, complex-associated fear, or realistic anticipation based on current conduct. Fear of abandonment is not sufficient to infer attachment anxiety.

**Shame** may be a primary maladaptive emotion, an adaptive social signal in some contexts, an affect in an inferiority or humiliation complex, or a response to present rejection or criticism. Its presence does not by itself locate the cause in childhood.

**Longing** may be attachment-related proximity need, grief, erotic or relational desire, a wish for recognition, or part of an idealised projection. The same word can name different processes. What matters is the sequence and function.

The discipline across all four is the same: describe first, classify second, and prefer the least elaborate theory that adequately explains the evidence.

# Part V - Projection, Development and Relationship Reality

## Projection under Attachment Threat

[JUNG PRIMARY / LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Projection becomes especially tempting as an explanatory shortcut when attachment threat is high. Strong fear, shame, anger, or longing can increase confidence in an interpretation precisely when perspective has narrowed. Jungian projection theory offers a useful warning: unconscious or insufficiently recognised subjective content can be experienced as if it belongs to the object. [Jung, CW 7, §98.] Attachment theory adds a different warning: working models can bias anticipation, attention, interpretation, and memory in attachment-relevant situations. [Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, ch. 2.]

The overlap is real but the mechanisms are not the same. An attachment working model may prime the expectation “important people become unavailable when I need them.” Projection concerns a stronger subject-object attribution, for example “you are deliberately withholding affection because you want me to suffer.” The first is an expectation about relational availability. The second attributes intention or psychic quality to the partner. Both can be wrong, partly right, or accurate.

The observation-first rule is critical here. Before describing projection, ask:

1. What did the other person actually say or do?
2. What intention or quality was attributed to them?
3. What evidence supports that attribution?
4. What evidence contradicts it?
5. Does the same attribution recur across different people or situations?
6. Does confidence in the attribution rise with affective arousal?
7. After arousal falls, does the person revise the interpretation?

A repeated pattern in which ambiguous events rapidly become proof of the same hostile motive may strengthen a projection hypothesis. But current reality remains decisive. The partner may genuinely be dismissive, deceptive, critical, unavailable, or controlling. Psychological interpretation must not erase observable conduct.

### The “hook” problem

A projected attribution often attaches to something in the current object that makes it plausible. The practical task is therefore not to decide between “all projection” and “all reality.” A better formulation may be: **real cue + learned expectation + affective amplification + attribution**. The proportions can vary from episode to episode.

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

For example, a partner responds to a difficult disclosure with a brief, flat answer. The observable fact is limited responsiveness in that moment. One person interprets this as contempt and impending abandonment. The partner may in fact be tired and emotionally clumsy, or may be dismissive, or may be distancing intentionally. The perceiver may also carry a working model of unavailability and a humiliation complex. The correct formulation cannot be obtained by choosing the most psychologically elaborate explanation. It requires more evidence.

## Current Partner versus Old Expectation

[ATTACHMENT THEORY / POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT]

Attachment and developmental models make it possible to understand why old relational expectations can organise present perception without claiming that present relationships are merely repetitions of childhood. Knox's chapter on internal working models emphasises that cumulative interpersonal experience becomes implicit representational organisation that influences later expectations and perception. [Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, ch. 4.] Mikulincer and Shaver similarly describe working models as shaping attention, memory, appraisal, and regulatory strategy. [Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 2.]

The key distinction is between **perceived** responsiveness and **actual** responsiveness.

Perceived responsiveness is the person's experience of whether the other is available, attentive, caring, and emotionally reachable. Actual responsiveness refers to observable behaviour in context. The two interact but are not identical. An insecure expectation can make adequate responsiveness difficult to register. Conversely, a person with a generally secure history can be distressed by a partner who is chronically unreliable.

A rigorous formulation therefore asks two questions simultaneously:

- What does the person expect and perceive?
- What does the partner actually do over time?

The first without the second risks psychologising away real relationship problems. The second without the first misses the role of personal history and affective organisation.

### Disconfirmation and persistence

One useful longitudinal test is what happens when reality repeatedly contradicts the old expectation. If a partner is consistently reliable, does the expectation gradually soften? Attachment theory permits change in working models through new experience, although deeply accessible patterns may be slow to update. If the expectation remains rigid despite sustained contradictory evidence, a more entrenched working model, complex, emotion scheme, or other process may be relevant. If the partner's behaviour is inconsistent, the persistence of the expectation may be rational rather than pathological.

## Attachment Injury, Insecurity, Trauma and Complex

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

These terms should be kept separate because each carries different theoretical and evidentiary commitments.

**Attachment insecurity** refers broadly to patterns of attachment-related expectation and regulation in which felt security is less reliable and secondary strategies such as hyperactivation or deactivation become more prominent. It is not synonymous with trauma.

**Attachment injury**, in Johnson's couple framework, refers to relationship events in which an attachment figure is experienced as critically unavailable or betraying at a moment of significant need, with enduring consequences for trust and the bond. The concept belongs to an attachment-centred couple-treatment framework. It should not be expanded into a generic label for every painful relationship event.

**Trauma** requires a separate assessment of event, response, and consequences. Strong affect, abandonment fear, emotional numbing, or recurrent relationship conflict is not enough to establish trauma. Knox discusses trauma and defence in a developmental context, but that does not justify retrospectively naming all rigid relational patterns traumatic. [Knox, ch. 5.]

**Complex** is Jung's construct for an affectively charged psychic organisation with relative autonomy. A complex can be related to traumatic experience, attachment experience, ordinary developmental conflict, family patterns, or other sources. The theory does not define complex as a trauma imprint. [Jung, CW 8, §§198-218.]

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

An episode may involve several of these layers. A past relational betrayal can become part of an attachment-related working model, contribute to an enduring emotional organisation, and provide material around which a complex is constellated. That is a multi-level formulation. It does not license using the labels interchangeably.

## Secure Attachment versus Individuation

[JUNG PRIMARY / ATTACHMENT THEORY]

Secure attachment and individuation belong to different theoretical systems and should not be treated as equivalent developmental achievements.

Attachment security concerns the operation of attachment relationships and regulatory expectations. In Mikulincer and Shaver's model, security supports effective proximity seeking, trust in availability, emotion regulation, and the ability to return to exploration. Johnson similarly presents secure connection as a basis for resilience, emotional engagement, and constructive dependence. [Mikulincer & Shaver, chs. 2 and 16; Johnson, chs. 1-2.]

Jung's individuation concerns becoming a psychological individual, a process of becoming one's own self through differentiation from collective identifications and a changed relation between ego and unconscious. Jung distinguishes individuation from individualism and presents it as a process that should enhance, not abolish, collective relationship. [Jung, CW 7, §§266-269.] He also stresses the need for differentiation from the collective psyche and warns against forms of identification that ignore the individuality of others. [Jung, CW 7, §240.]

Secure attachment may create conditions that make reflection, exploration, and psychological development easier. Individuation may improve a person's capacity to participate in relationship without excessive projection or collective identification. Those are possible interactions. Neither construct is a measure of the other.

A securely attached person is not thereby individuated in Jung's sense. An individuating person is not necessarily secure in attachment. A relationship can be emotionally safe yet collusive or developmentally constraining; a psychologically differentiating process can temporarily unsettle an attachment bond. The developmental goals overlap only partially.

# Part VI - Recursive Relationship Process

## Reciprocal Activation

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Relationship episodes are recursive because each person's behaviour changes the conditions faced by the other. One partner's threat response becomes the other's stimulus. This does not require assuming identical psychology.

A simple sequence is:

**event → Person A meaning → A affect/regulation → A behaviour → Person B perception → B meaning → B affect/regulation → B behaviour → changed conditions for A**.

Attachment theory can describe how A's proximity seeking or distancing affects B's security. Complex theory can describe how B's response becomes a cue that constellates an old affective organisation in A, and vice versa. Emotion-process theory can describe the sequence of primary and secondary affect within each person. Projection can alter what each person believes the other's behaviour means.

The loop becomes self-reinforcing when protective strategies inadvertently confirm the partner's fear. A person who fears abandonment may press for reassurance in a way that overwhelms the other. The other may withdraw to regulate. Withdrawal then looks like proof of abandonment, increasing pursuit. Or a person who fears engulfment may distance; the partner intensifies bids for contact; intensified bids then confirm the fear of being controlled, increasing distance. These are generic interactional possibilities, not attachment diagnoses.

## Individual Contribution versus Interactional Process

An interactional account and an individual account answer different questions.

The interactional account asks how behaviours link together in sequence and how each response changes the next cue. The individual account asks what each person brings to that sequence: working models, complexes, emotion schemes, values, habits, stress, capacities for reflection, and current motives.

It is possible for a cycle to be jointly generated while contributions are unequal. One person may contribute 80 percent of the observable escalation and the other 20 percent. One may be reacting to a genuine breach. One may have more power to stop the pattern. One may be acting coercively while the other's behaviour is primarily defensive. Circularity describes temporal influence, not moral equivalence.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

“Both trigger each other” is too vague to be useful. Replace it with behavioural sequence:

- A interrupts repeatedly.
- B stops speaking and looks away.
- A raises voice and moves closer.
- B leaves the room.
- A sends repeated messages.

Then ask what each step means and what function it may serve. The more concrete the sequence, the less likely psychological language is to hide responsibility.

## Power, Responsibility and Safety

Psychological formulation cannot be permitted to neutralise power. Attachment and emotion models are designed to explain regulation and relational process, not to excuse coercion, intimidation, betrayal, manipulation, violence, stalking, threats, contempt, chronic deception, or systematic boundary violation.

A person can be dysregulated and still responsible for harmful conduct. A complex can be constellated and still leave consequences that require repair. Attachment fear can help explain protest without making controlling behaviour acceptable. A deactivating strategy can help explain withdrawal without making chronic stonewalling harmless. Primary adaptive anger can support a boundary, but aggression remains behaviour that must be evaluated separately.

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Every formulation should therefore retain a **responsibility ledger**:

1. What did each person actually do?
2. What foreseeable impact did it have?
3. What choices were available at the time?
4. What repair is required regardless of motive?
5. What boundary remains legitimate?
6. Is either person unsafe?
7. Does one person have materially greater power, control, or capacity to impose consequences?

If the relationship contains coercive control or violence, an ordinary reciprocal-cycle formulation may be misleading because it suggests symmetry where there is not symmetry. Safety assessment takes priority over interpretive depth.

## Why Circularity Does Not Mean Equal Blame

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

A feedback loop can be asymmetric. In systems language, two variables can influence each other while one influence is stronger, more deliberate, or more damaging. The same applies to couples.

Consider two cases:

**Case A:** One partner criticises harshly; the other becomes defensive; criticism escalates; defensiveness increases. Both influence the cycle, but the initial criticism may be the more modifiable and damaging behaviour.

**Case B:** One partner lies repeatedly about a major issue; the other becomes vigilant and interrogative; secrecy increases; vigilance increases. The vigilance may now help maintain conflict, but describing a “mutual trust cycle” without foregrounding repeated deception would flatten responsibility.

The proper conclusion is not that one partner is always innocent. It is that **causal circularity, psychological formulation, and moral responsibility are separate analytical questions**.

## Differentiation as a Regulatory Resource

[JUNG PRIMARY / POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT]

Jung's individuation framework and Knox's reflective-function discussion converge at one practical point without becoming the same theory: a person benefits from being able to experience self and other as psychologically distinct. Jung's individuation requires differentiation from collective identifications and unconscious contents; Knox's reflective function includes awareness that other people have their own thoughts, emotions, desires, and intentions. [Jung, CW 7, §§240, 266-269; Knox, ch. 6.]

In applied relationship work, differentiation means being able to say:

- “This is what I feel,” without “therefore this is what you intended.”
- “This is what I fear,” without “therefore it will happen.”
- “This is what I need,” without “therefore you must provide it in this form.”
- “This is what you did,” without “therefore I know your entire character.”
- “My history may be active,” without “therefore the present does not matter.”
- “Your behaviour may be problematic,” without “therefore none of my own organisation matters.”

That capacity is not emotional detachment. It is the ability to remain in relationship while preserving subject-object distinction, evidence, agency, and responsibility.

# Part VII - Applied Differential Formulation

## Observation-First Episode Reconstruction

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

The Laboratory method begins with an asymmetry: **observations have priority over interpretations**. This is not because behaviour is self-explanatory, but because every deeper formulation depends on an adequate account of what occurred. If the event is poorly specified, theory simply elaborates uncertainty.

Use the following sequence.

### A. Observable reality

Record what a reasonably neutral observer could identify.

- What happened?
- What was said or done?
- What was the timing?
- What prior agreement or expectation existed?
- Was information missing?
- Was a boundary crossed?
- Was there actual unavailability, criticism, deception, contempt, rejection, threat, or practical incompatibility?
- What did each person do next?

Avoid motive language at this stage. “They did not reply for eight hours” is an observation. “They ignored me to punish me” is an interpretation. “I asked the same question four times in ten minutes” is an observation. “I was hyperactivating” is already theoretical.

### B. Subjective experience

Then describe first-person experience without deciding what caused it.

- What emotion appeared first, if recoverable?
- What happened in the body?
- What image, memory, or phrase came to mind?
- What immediate meaning was assigned?
- What was feared?
- What was wanted from the other?
- What impulse appeared?
- What action followed?

This section may include contradictory experience. A person can feel anger and fear, want contact and distance, or feel numb while also noticing bodily activation. Do not force coherence prematurely.

### C. Attachment hypothesis

Ask whether the episode is centrally organised around security in relation to an attachment figure.

Supporting questions:

- Was separation, rejection, unavailability, loss, or unreliable responsiveness salient?
- Did the person seek proximity, contact, reassurance, support, or confirmation of importance?
- Did they instead suppress need, distance, or insist on self-reliance when dependence felt risky?
- Did a clear responsive signal reduce activation?
- Is the pattern stronger with attachment figures than in unrelated contexts?
- Does the same person show different regulation with different partners?

Evidence against an attachment formulation matters equally. If the conflict concerns a practical disagreement with little security significance, if the emotion settles when the practical issue is resolved, or if the pattern appears mainly in performance or authority contexts rather than attachment relationships, attachment may not be the best primary lens.

### D. Complex hypothesis

Ask whether the episode has the phenomenology of constellation described by Jung.

Possible supporting signs:

- a recurrent affective theme appears rapidly;
- emotional charge is high relative to the immediate cue;
- interpretation becomes stereotyped or absolute;
- contradictory evidence becomes difficult to hold;
- conscious intention is disrupted;
- the same theme appears across different relationships or life domains;
- after the episode, the person experiences the response as partly foreign, compulsive, or “not like myself”;
- associated memories, images, fantasies, or symbolic material cluster around the same theme.

[JUNG PRIMARY: Jung, CW 8, §§198-203.]

Evidence against a complex formulation includes proportionate affect, good access to alternative perspectives, limited recurrence, clear current cause, and rapid resolution after the concrete problem changes.

### E. Emotion-process hypothesis

Ask how the emotion is functioning rather than assuming the emotion label provides the answer.

- Does the response fit the current situation and support adaptive action?
- Is it immediate but organised by old learning that no longer fits well?
- Is it a response to another emotion or to thoughts about that emotion?
- Is the emotional expression functioning interpersonally to obtain a response?
- What need and action tendency accompany it?
- Does deeper processing broaden action or merely increase arousal?

[EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY: Greenberg, chs. 2-3.]

A classification should remain provisional until temporal sequence and function are reasonably clear.

### F. Projection hypothesis

Ask what was attributed to the other.

- What motive, attitude, or quality was assumed?
- Was it observed or inferred?
- What evidence supports it?
- What evidence contradicts it?
- Is the attribution recurrent across people?
- Does it intensify with affect?
- Is there evidence that the same disowned quality also exists in the subject?

A projection hypothesis should be used cautiously because it is especially easy to weaponise. “That is your projection” is not evidence. It is an interpretation requiring support.

### G. Ordinary and contextual alternatives

Before settling on a depth formulation, consider ordinary causes:

- stress;
- fatigue;
- illness;
- time pressure;
- misunderstanding;
- poor communication;
- different conflict preferences;
- temperament;
- cultural expectations;
- practical disagreement;
- values conflict;
- sexual or relational preference difference;
- competing priorities;
- actual unreliability;
- actual incompatibility.

The ordinary explanation is not psychologically inferior. It should be preferred when it explains the facts adequately with fewer assumptions.

### H. Responsibility and safety

Separate explanation from accountability.

- What did I actually do?
- What impact did it have?
- What repair is required even if my reaction was understandable?
- What did the other person actually do?
- What belongs to their responsibility?
- What boundary remains legitimate?
- Is there coercion, intimidation, violence, stalking, deception, or another safety issue that makes a symmetrical cycle formulation misleading?

### I. Integration

End with a provisional statement, not a verdict.

A useful form is:

> The strongest current evidence supports X because A, B, and C. Y may also contribute because D and E, but confidence is lower. Z is currently unnecessary because the observable facts are adequately explained without it. The key uncertainty is Q. The next discriminating observation would be R.

That format forces the theory to earn its place.

## Competing Hypotheses

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

For any consequential episode, generate at least three plausible explanations before choosing a preferred one. A disciplined set might include:

1. **Current-reality hypothesis:** the distress is proportionate to what the partner actually did.
2. **Attachment hypothesis:** the event activated concerns about availability, security, or dependence and elicited a secondary attachment strategy.
3. **Complex hypothesis:** the event constellated a recurrent affective organisation that amplified meaning and narrowed freedom.
4. **Emotion-process hypothesis:** the response is best understood through primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, or instrumental emotion.
5. **Projection hypothesis:** part of the attributed motive or quality may belong to the subject's own unconscious material.
6. **Context hypothesis:** stress, fatigue, habit, conflict style, or practical circumstances explain most of the episode.

Not every episode needs all six. The purpose is to prevent a preferred theory from becoming self-confirming.

### Evidence for and against

Every hypothesis should have a disconfirmation condition. If no possible observation could count against the theory, the theory is not functioning as a disciplined formulation.

Examples:

- **Hyperactivation hypothesis:** strengthened if repeated reassurance temporarily reduces distress but uncertainty rapidly reactivates monitoring; weakened if the partner remains objectively unavailable and the person's concern resolves when reliable information appears.
- **Deactivation hypothesis:** strengthened if attachment need is repeatedly suppressed specifically when dependence becomes salient; weakened if withdrawal is limited to unsafe or unproductive interactions and the person seeks support readily elsewhere.
- **Complex hypothesis:** strengthened by recurrent thematic activation across contexts and narrowed conscious flexibility; weakened by proportionate, context-specific affect with little recurrence.
- **Projection hypothesis:** strengthened when attribution persists despite contradictory evidence and the same quality appears as disowned material in the subject; weakened when independent evidence consistently confirms the attribution.
- **Primary maladaptive emotion hypothesis:** strengthened when an immediate emotional response is familiar, rigid, history-linked, and poorly fitted to current conditions; weakened when the emotion is well fitted to present events and supports effective action.

## Parsimony and Counterevidence

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

**Prefer the least elaborate explanation that adequately explains the facts.** This is not a rule against depth psychology. It is a rule against using depth language as prestige.

If a person is angry because a partner broke an explicit agreement for the third time, the first formulation should be “anger in response to repeated agreement breach.” Attachment threat or complex activation may also be present, but they add value only if they explain features left unexplained by the current event.

If someone becomes convinced that a partner will leave after a minor scheduling change, experiences intense panic, recalls several earlier abandonment experiences, repeatedly seeks reassurance, and remains unable to register reassurance, additional attachment, complex, or emotion-scheme hypotheses become more useful.

### Depth is not truth

A complex, archetypal, developmental, or attachment explanation is not more accurate merely because it reaches farther back in time or sounds more sophisticated. A deep explanation can be wrong. A mundane explanation can be right. The quality of a formulation depends on fit, evidence, predictive usefulness, and its capacity to remain open to correction.

## Confidence Discipline

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Use four confidence levels.

**HIGH**: recurring observable pattern supported directly by longitudinal evidence, with important alternatives tested and found less adequate.

**MODERATE**: plausible mechanism with recurrent supporting evidence, but meaningful alternatives remain.

**LOW**: sparse, relationship-specific, or indirect evidence. The formulation is useful mainly as a question.

**SPECULATIVE**: inference about unconscious motive, childhood history, another person's attachment organisation, trauma, or a complex without direct supporting evidence.

Confidence applies to each claim separately. One episode can support a high-confidence behavioural description and a speculative psychological explanation at the same time.

Example:

- “The partner ended the conversation three times when voices rose”: HIGH, if observed or agreed.
- “Withdrawal functions to reduce attachment vulnerability”: MODERATE or LOW depending on repeated evidence.
- “The partner is avoidantly attached because of childhood neglect”: SPECULATIVE unless independently assessed.

This discipline is especially important when analysing an absent partner. Most claims about another person's attachment orientation, complex, unconscious motive, or developmental history should remain low-confidence or speculative.

## Longitudinal Pattern Analysis

One episode is a sample, not a structure. Stable formulations require repeated observation.

[REFLECTIVE APPLICATION]

Use the following template over time:

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
