| Date / context | When and where did the episode occur? |
| Actual event | Behavioural description without motive language |
| Prior agreement or issue | What expectation, boundary, or practical issue existed? |
| Emotion | Named feelings, allowing more than one |
| Body | Arousal, numbness, tension, heat, collapse, agitation, etc. |
| Immediate meaning | What did the event seem to mean? |
| Perceived attachment threat | Was availability, rejection, loss, dependence, or importance at stake? |
| Desired response | What did I want the other person to do? |
| Own behaviour | What I actually did |
| Partner's observable response | What they actually did next |
| Working-model hypothesis | What self-other expectation may have shaped perception? |
| Complex hypothesis | What recurrent affective theme, if any, appears? |
| Projection hypothesis | What attribution may exceed evidence? |
| Emotion-process hypothesis | Primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, instrumental, or unclear? |
| Ordinary alternatives | Stress, habit, misunderstanding, values conflict, current unreliability, etc. |
| Counterevidence | What does not fit my preferred explanation? |
| Outcome | What happened after the episode? |
| Confidence | High / Moderate / Low / Speculative for each hypothesis |
| What to watch next | Observation that would discriminate among hypotheses |

After several entries, review patterns rather than memorable peaks. Ask which triggers recur, which responses settle with responsiveness, which themes generalise across domains, which emotions repeatedly precede others, and which interpretations remain accurate when calm.

## Generic Cases

The following cases are hypothetical. They demonstrate formulation discipline and are not diagnostic templates.

### Case 1: Delayed reply and panic

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

**Observable facts:** A person sends a message about evening plans. Their partner does not reply for six hours. There was no explicit agreement to reply sooner. The person sends seven additional messages, checks social media repeatedly, and later reports panic and certainty that the relationship is ending. The partner eventually replies that work ran late.

**Attachment hypothesis:** Moderate. The cue involves perceived unavailability, and the response includes intensified proximity and monitoring. Hyperactivation is plausible. It would be strengthened if similar patterns recur when attachment figures are unavailable and if reliable responsiveness generally reduces activation.

**Complex hypothesis:** Low to moderate. An abandonment-themed complex is plausible only if a recurrent, highly charged theme appears across contexts and narrows perspective beyond the attachment episode itself.

**Emotion-process hypothesis:** Moderate. Immediate fear may be primary maladaptive if the intensity and certainty largely reflect old learning rather than present evidence. Anger after the reply could be secondary, primary adaptive, or both at different moments.

**Projection hypothesis:** Low. The thought “they are deliberately ignoring me because they want to leave” would require evidence. The observable delay alone does not establish intention.

**Current-reality hypothesis:** Also relevant. Six hours of silence may be unusual or inconsiderate depending on the relationship's normal communication. More context is needed.

**What remains unknown:** prior communication norms, recurrence, partner reliability, history, and whether reassurance settles the pattern.

### Case 2: Criticism, shame, and anger

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

**Observable facts:** During a discussion about household spending, one partner says, “You never think things through.” The other becomes flushed, says, “You think I am useless,” raises their voice, and leaves.

**Current-reality hypothesis:** Strong. The criticism is global and demeaning rather than limited to the spending decision. Anger and hurt may be proportionate.

**Complex hypothesis:** Moderate if “useless/inadequate” is a recurrent theme across work, family, and intimate relationships, especially if global shame rapidly takes over consciousness.

**Attachment hypothesis:** Possible but not required. If criticism is experienced as withdrawal of love or evidence of impending rejection, attachment threat may be involved. If the episode is mainly about status, competence, or humiliation, complex theory may explain more.

**Emotion-process hypothesis:** Shame may be primary maladaptive if it reflects an entrenched self-condemning organisation. Anger may be primary adaptive boundary defence, secondary protection against shame, or a mixture over time. Sequence must be established rather than assumed.

**Projection hypothesis:** The statement “you think I am useless” goes beyond the partner's exact words but is partly cued by them. Amplification is plausible; pure projection is not established.

### Case 3: Silence during conflict

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

**Observable facts:** A disagreement becomes louder. One person stops speaking, looks down, and says, “I need twenty minutes.” They return after twenty-five minutes and restart the conversation.

**Deactivation hypothesis:** Low from this episode alone. Withdrawal occurred, but the person named a limit and returned. The behaviour may be effective self-regulation.

**Complex hypothesis:** Low unless a recurrent theme and affective narrowing are evident.

**Emotion-process hypothesis:** Overwhelm or fear may be present, but there is insufficient evidence to classify it.

**Ordinary explanation:** Strong. A time-limited pause can be a practical regulation strategy.

The case illustrates why “withdrawal = avoidant attachment” is poor reasoning.

### Case 4: Reassurance that does not land

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

**Observable facts:** One partner repeatedly says they want the relationship and describes concrete future plans. The other reports believing the reassurance for only a few minutes before becoming certain again that abandonment is imminent. This pattern repeats across months despite generally consistent behaviour.

**Attachment hypothesis:** Moderate to high. Chronic threat monitoring and rapid loss of felt security despite responsive signals fit a hyperactivating pattern.

**Working-model hypothesis:** Moderate to high. A stable expectation of unavailability may be dominating interpretation of new evidence.

**Complex hypothesis:** Moderate if abandonment is also an affectively autonomous theme with strong associative material, recurring beyond attachment-specific contexts or accompanied by marked loss of reflective flexibility.

**Emotion-process hypothesis:** Primary maladaptive fear is plausible if the immediate emotional organisation reflects old learning and remains poorly fitted to present circumstances.

**Projection hypothesis:** Specific accusations of hidden intent would need separate testing. Fear of abandonment alone is not projection.

### Case 5: Feeling engulfed by closeness

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

**Observable facts:** After a relationship becomes more intimate, one person reduces contact and says they feel crowded. The partner has recently begun asking for daily calls and more shared weekends. No threats or coercion are reported.

**Attachment hypothesis:** Deactivation is plausible but low-confidence without longitudinal evidence. Increased dependence may be activating discomfort with closeness.

**Current-reality hypothesis:** Equally plausible. The partners may simply prefer different levels of contact.

**Complex hypothesis:** Possible if closeness repeatedly constellates themes of engulfment, loss of autonomy, or control across relationships.

**Emotion-process hypothesis:** Fear, irritation, or anger requires functional analysis. Anger could be adaptive boundary defence rather than a cover for intimacy fear.

**Compatibility hypothesis:** Must remain visible. A mismatch in desired closeness can be real even if both partners are psychologically healthy.

The case illustrates a core rule of the Laboratory: formulation should not convert preference differences into pathology.

# Part VIII - Integrated Retrieval Tools

## Core Comparison Tables

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

The following comparisons are retrieval aids. They do not imply that the paired constructs occupy equivalent levels of theory.

| Pair | Similarity that can cause confusion | Critical difference | Evidence that helps discriminate |
|---|---|---|---|
| Attachment activation vs complex constellation | Both can involve sudden affect, narrowed attention, urgency, and altered behaviour | Attachment activation is defined by security/proximity in relation to an attachment figure; complex constellation is defined by activation of an affectively charged autonomous psychic organisation | Does the pattern track attachment availability specifically, or recur around the same affective theme across attachment and non-attachment domains? |
| Internal working model vs Jungian complex | Both can operate outside focal awareness and shape interpretation | Working model is an attachment-relevant predictive representation; complex is feeling-toned, associative, and relatively autonomous | Look for attachment-specific self-other expectations versus broad thematic constellation and interference with conscious intention |
| Internal working model vs projection | Both can bias perception of another person | Working model generates expectation; projection attributes subjective psychic content to the object | Separate “what I expected” from “what motive/quality I assigned to you” and compare both with evidence |
| Internal working model vs emotion scheme | Both are experience-shaped organising structures | Working model is attachment-specific; emotion scheme organises embodied affect, appraisal, need, motivation, and action | Ask whether the pattern is principally about attachment availability or about a broader emotional situation and action tendency |
| Complex vs emotion scheme | Both integrate affect, memory, meaning, and response | Jung stresses autonomy and plural psychic organisation; Greenberg stresses emotional appraisal, function, need, and transformation | Look for autonomy/thematic constellation versus emotion-process sequence and functional classification |
| Hyperactivation vs complex-driven escalation | Both can produce intensity and repetitive behaviour | Hyperactivation serves attachment-security seeking; complex escalation reflects activation of a charged theme that need not be attachment-specific | Does responsive attachment contact reliably reduce activation? Does the same theme recur outside attachment contexts? |
| Deactivation vs complex-related withdrawal | Both can look like distancing or numbness | Deactivation inhibits attachment needs and reliance; complex-related withdrawal may follow shame, humiliation, authority, inferiority, or other themes | Clarify what the person is avoiding and whether dependence/availability is central |
| Attachment anxiety vs rejection-related complex | Both can include fear of abandonment or rejection | Anxiety is a dimensional attachment orientation; a complex is a specific affective organisation with relative autonomy | Assess breadth of attachment-related expectations versus recurrence of a charged theme across domains |
| Attachment avoidance vs introversion | Both may superficially look reserved | Avoidance concerns dependence and attachment regulation; introversion is a Jungian attitude of psychic orientation | Observe support seeking, closeness, dependence, and regulation rather than sociability alone |
| Pursuit vs hyperactivation | Pursuit can be a behavioural expression of hyperactivation | Pursuit is observable interactional behaviour; hyperactivation is a regulatory function | Establish whether pursuit is security seeking, accountability seeking, practical urgency, anger, or something else |
| Withdrawal vs deactivation | Withdrawal can enact deactivation | Withdrawal is observable behaviour; deactivation is attachment need suppression/distancing | Ask whether withdrawal inhibits dependency/vulnerability, or instead regulates arousal, enforces a boundary, or protects safety |
| Co-regulation vs dependency | Both involve reliance on another | Co-regulation describes interpersonal regulation; dependency describes degree/form of reliance and can be secure or problematic | Does support restore functioning and agency, or is external regulation rigidly indispensable? |
| Co-regulation vs fusion | Both involve close interpersonal influence | Co-regulation can occur between differentiated persons; fusion weakens separateness and independent perspective | Can each person remain a distinct subject, tolerate disagreement, and take responsibility? |
| Self-regulation vs self-sufficiency | Both may reduce outward reliance | Self-regulation is flexible capacity; self-sufficiency can be a value, preference, or defensive inhibition of need | Can the person seek and receive support when useful without loss of agency? |
| Secure attachment vs individuation | Both can support autonomy and relationship | Security is attachment-system organisation; individuation is Jung's process of psychological differentiation and selfhood | Use attachment evidence for one, Jungian developmental evidence for the other |
| Attachment injury vs trauma vs complex | All can involve painful, lasting relational meaning | Attachment injury is an attachment-bond construct; trauma has separate event/response criteria; complex is a Jungian psychic organisation | Do not infer one from another; assess each on its own source criteria |
| Dysregulation vs harmful behaviour | Dysregulation can contribute to harmful action | Dysregulation explains state/process; harmful behaviour is conduct with consequences | Assess behaviour, impact, choice, repair, and safety separately from mechanism |
| Reciprocal activation vs equal responsibility | Both partners can affect the sequence | Causal feedback does not imply equivalent contribution, power, intention, or harm | Map behaviour step by step and assess power and impact independently |
| Insight vs repair | Insight can change understanding | Repair requires behavioural and relational action, accountability, and often changed experience | Ask what was acknowledged, changed, repaired, and sustained after insight |

## Johnson / Greenberg Terminology Table

[ATTACHMENT THEORY / EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY]

| Johnson attachment-centred language | Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy language | Relationship | Non-equivalence rule |
|---|---|---|---|
| Reactive or automatic surface response | Secondary emotion, primary emotion, or instrumental emotion depending on function | Both may describe emotionally organised responses that maintain difficulty | “Reactive” does not automatically mean “secondary” |
| Core emotional vulnerability | Primary emotional process may be adaptive or maladaptive | Both deepen attention beyond surface protection | “Vulnerable” does not automatically mean “primary” or “adaptive” |
| Attachment fear and longing | Emotion linked to need and action tendency | Both connect emotion to motivational significance | An attachment need is not the same construct as Greenberg's general emotion-related need |
| Numbing / suppression / distancing | Emotion avoidance or dysregulation; category depends on process | Both can identify reduced engagement with emotion | The same behaviour may have different functions and theoretical explanations |
| Corrective emotional experience and interaction | New emotional processing and transformation | Both depend on new experience rather than insight alone | Johnson centres attachment interaction; Greenberg centres emotion process and task |
| Secure connection / co-regulation | Regulation supported by relationship plus emotional processing | Both value relationship as change context | Security is not a Greenberg emotion category; regulation is not identical to bonding |

The practical consequence is that an integrated case note should keep both vocabularies intact. If Johnson's framework describes anger as reactive protection around attachment vulnerability, Greenberg's framework still requires a separate functional assessment before calling that anger secondary.

## Integrated Differential Formulation Table

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

| Observation | Attachment hypothesis | Complex hypothesis | Emotion-process hypothesis | Projection hypothesis | Ordinary/current-reality explanation | Evidence for | Evidence against | Confidence | What to observe next |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Panic after delayed reply | Threat to availability; hyperactivation | Abandonment complex possible | Primary maladaptive fear possible | “They are leaving to punish me” may be projection | Delay may actually violate normal communication expectations | Recurrent monitoring under unavailability; settles with reliable contact | Concern proportionate to actual repeated unreliability | Variable | Whether reassurance restores security; whether same theme recurs elsewhere |
| Strong shame after criticism | Rejection threat possible | Inferiority/humiliation complex plausible | Primary maladaptive shame possible | Attribution that partner sees whole self as worthless may exceed words | Criticism may genuinely be contemptuous | Theme recurs across evaluation contexts; rapid global self-condemnation | Criticism was specific, respectful, and response proportionate | Variable | Cross-context recurrence; effect of non-shaming feedback |
| Repeated reassurance seeking | Hyperactivation plausible | Loss/rejection theme possible | Fear/anxiety process; possible secondary distress | Assumptions about hidden abandonment motive | Partner may actually be inconsistent | Reassurance sought under attachment threat; relief brief | Reassurance objectively unreliable or contradictory | Moderate if recurrent | Duration of relief; relationship specificity |
| Silence during conflict | Deactivation possible | Shame/authority/conflict complex possible | Overwhelm, fear, or avoidance | Attribution cannot be inferred from silence | Timeout, fatigue, safety, reflective pause | Need/vulnerability consistently suppressed under closeness threat | Person names pause and reliably returns | Low from one episode | Internal experience and what happens after pause |
| Emotional numbness | Deactivation possible | Complex-related dissociation only if supported | Under-arousal/avoidance; classification unclear | None necessarily | Exhaustion, shock, depression, medication, uncertainty | Numbness specifically follows attachment dependency cues | Numbness broad and medically/contextually explained | Low | Broader pattern and clinical context |
| Intense anger after rejection | Attachment protest/hyperactivation possible | Rejection/humiliation complex possible | Primary adaptive, maladaptive, or secondary anger | Motive attribution may be amplified | Anger may be proportionate to actual rejection or betrayal | Recurrent attachment-linked escalation | Clear boundary violation adequately explains anger | Variable | Temporal sequence, need, action tendency, later appraisal |
| Fear of dependence | Deactivation possible | Dependence/control complex possible | Primary fear or shame | Other seen as controlling without evidence may involve projection | Partner may actually be intrusive | Reliance reliably triggers distancing despite trustworthy others | Fear appears only with one controlling partner | Variable | Cross-relationship pattern and response to respectful support |
| Reassurance never feels credible | Hyperactivation / insecure working model | Abandonment complex possible | Primary maladaptive fear or shame | Hidden-intent attribution possible | Reassurance may be contradicted by behaviour | Consistent partner conduct yet felt security rapidly collapses | Partner genuinely sends mixed signals | Moderate if longitudinal | Compare words, behaviour, and duration of settled state |
| Feeling engulfed by closeness | Deactivation possible | Engulfment/control complex possible | Fear or anger may be adaptive or maladaptive | Partner experienced as controlling beyond evidence | Real mismatch in desired contact; actual control | Similar reaction to reasonable closeness across relationships | Partner makes excessive demands | Variable | Cross-context pattern and negotiated-boundary response |
| Defensive intellectualising | Possible deactivation from attachment affect | Defence against charged complex material | Avoidance of embodied emotion possible | None necessarily | Normal cognitive processing style | Cognition consistently replaces felt experience under vulnerability | Thinking improves regulation and communication | Low to moderate | Whether emotional access becomes possible without loss of reflection |
| Contempt after vulnerability | Defensive distancing possible | Shame/inferiority complex possible | Secondary contempt/anger possible | Devaluation of other may carry projection | Actual loss of respect or unresolved values conflict | Contempt reliably follows exposure of need/shame | Contempt predates vulnerability and tracks real incompatibility | Low without pattern | Sequence and function; whether accountability changes it |

## Areas of Overlap

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

The frameworks converge on several broad observations even though they theorise them differently.

First, past experience can shape present perception without conscious deliberation. Attachment theory locates this partly in working models. Knox emphasises implicit developmental organisation. Greenberg locates learned emotional meaning in emotion schemes. Jung describes complexes as capable of autonomous activation. The convergence is that current consciousness does not start from a blank slate.

Second, strong affect can narrow flexible processing. Jung describes interference with intention and consciousness under constellation. Attachment hyperactivation can intensify monitoring and threat-related cognition, while deactivation can inhibit access to attachment need. Greenberg distinguishes productive emotional engagement from dysregulated or defensive emotional processing. The common observation is that emotion can reorganise attention and action, but the explanatory mechanisms differ.

Third, relationship experience can change psychological organisation. Attachment models allow working models to update. Johnson uses corrective emotional and interactional experience to reshape security. Greenberg emphasises transformation through new adaptive emotion. Knox stresses the importance of new relational experience alongside interpretation. Jungian work seeks a changed conscious relation to complexes and unconscious contents rather than mere suppression. The convergence is that insight alone is often insufficient.

Fourth, differentiation matters. Attachment security is not fusion. Knox's reflective function includes recognition of separate minds. Jung's individuation requires differentiation from collective and unconscious identifications. In applied terms, better functioning generally involves increased capacity to experience emotion and relationship without collapsing self into other or interpretation into fact.

## Genuine Theoretical Differences

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

The differences are not terminological trivia.

1. **Object of explanation.** Attachment theory explains a species-typical behavioural system and individual differences in its regulation. Complex theory explains affectively charged autonomous psychic organisations. Greenberg explains emotional processing and transformation. Projection theory examines subject-object attribution.

2. **Domain specificity.** Attachment constructs are specifically organised around attachment figures and security. Complexes and emotion schemes can be organised around many domains.

3. **Architecture of mind.** Jung's complex theory presupposes a psyche in which semi-autonomous organisations can interfere with the ego. Attachment theory is more representational and regulatory. Greenberg's model is experiential, process-oriented, and emotion-centred.

4. **Evidence traditions.** Mikulincer and Shaver draw heavily on experimental, self-report, observational, and social-personality research. Jung's complex theory arose from clinical psychiatry, association experiments, and analytical observation, then became embedded in a broader depth-psychological model. Greenberg's therapy model draws on experiential psychotherapy, task analysis, process research, and clinical theory. Knox explicitly attempts a bridge across such traditions.
